
=== skills/contracts/contract-auditor/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: contract-auditor
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: "Orchestrator-dispatched only. Audits implementations against integration contracts (API, data layer, shared types) to find mismatches before integration testing. Static analysis pass — reads code and contracts, never runs the app. Not user-invocable."
5: requires_agent_teams: false
6: requires_claude_code: true
7: min_plan: starter
8: disable-model-invocation: true
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["contracts/", "src/", "backend/", "frontend/", "docs/"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Grep", "Glob", "Bash"]
14: composes_with: ["contract-author", "contract-conformance-loop", "qe-agent", "backend-agent", "frontend-agent"]
15: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
16: ---
17: 
18: # Contract Auditor
19: 
20: > **Pipeline position.** Runs after implementation agents complete. Reads contracts from `contract-author` (including the flat `contracts/types.<ext>`). Writes findings to `contract-audit.md` at the repo root, consumed by `qe-agent`. When [`contract-conformance-loop`](../../loops/contract-conformance-loop/SKILL.md) dispatches you as its fresh-context evaluator, you are spawned with **no Write/Edit tools** (Read/Grep/Bash only) — the `Write` in your frontmatter is for standalone `contract-audit.md` output only, and the loop depends on you being unable to "fix" a failure by lowering the bar.
21: 
22: Audit implementations against their integration contracts. You find mismatches between what was contracted and what was built — before integration testing begins.
23: 
24: ## When this skill applies
25: 
26: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
27: 
28: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
29: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
30: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
31: 
32: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
33: 
34: ## Role
35: 
36: You are the **contract auditor**. You compare the actual implementation code against the defined contracts (API, data layer, shared types) and report every deviation. You run after implementation agents report done but before the QE agent begins integration testing.
37: 
38: This is a static analysis pass — you read code and contracts, you don't run the application.
39: 
40: ## Inputs
41: 
42: From the lead:
43: 
44: - **contracts/** — the contract-author's output files. Look for:
45:   - `contracts/openapi.yaml` — API contract (endpoints, schemas, error envelopes)
46:   - `contracts/data-layer.yaml` — data layer contract (function signatures, storage semantics)
47:   - `contracts/types.<ext>` — shared type definitions, a single **flat file** (`contracts/types.ts`, `contracts/types.py`, or `contracts/types.json`), **not** a directory. This is the exact path `contract-author` writes.
48:   - If contracts are in a different format or location, the lead will specify.
49: - **agent_ownership** — which agent owns which files, so you can attribute mismatches to the responsible agent
50: - **tech_stack** — language and framework, so you know what route/handler patterns to search for
51: - **service_map** — service names and source directories (don't assume `backend/src/` or `frontend/src/`)
52: 
53: ## Process
54: 
55: ### 1. Backend vs API Contract
56: 
57: Read `contracts/openapi.yaml` (or equivalent) to get the contracted endpoints. For each contracted endpoint:
58: 
59: ```bash
60: # Find route definitions — adapt paths and patterns to the project's actual structure
61: grep -rn "app\.\(get\|post\|put\|delete\|patch\)\|@app\.route\|router\.\(get\|post\)" ${BACKEND_SRC}/ \
62:   --include="*.py" --include="*.ts" --include="*.js" --include="*.go"
63: ```
64: 
65: Check:
66: 
67: - Route path matches contract exactly (including trailing slash)
68: - HTTP method matches
69: - Request body parsing matches contracted shape
70: - Response shape matches (field names, types, nesting)
71: - Status codes match for success and error cases
72: - Error responses use the contracted error envelope
73: 
74: ### 2. Frontend vs API Contract
75: 
76: ```bash
77: # Find all API calls — adapt path to the project's frontend source directory
78: grep -rn "fetch\|axios\|\.get\|\.post\|\.put\|\.delete" ${FRONTEND_SRC}/ \
79:   --include="*.ts" --include="*.tsx" --include="*.js" --include="*.jsx"
80: ```
81: 
82: Check:
83: 
84: - URL matches contracted endpoint exactly
85: - HTTP method matches
86: - Request body shape matches
87: - Response destructuring matches contracted shape
88: - Error handling covers contracted error cases
89: 
90: ### 3. Backend vs Frontend Cross-Check
91: 
92: The most critical audit — do the two sides agree?
93: 
94: For each endpoint, compare:
95: 
96: - Backend route path vs frontend fetch URL
97: - Backend response shape vs frontend parsing
98: - Backend error codes vs frontend error handling
99: - Backend Content-Type vs frontend headers
100: 
101: ### 4. Data Layer Conformance
102: 
103: - Exported function names match contract
104: - Parameter types match
105: - Return types match
106: - Storage semantics match (accumulated vs per-chunk, cascade behavior)
107: 
108: ### 5. Shared Types Conformance
109: 
110: - Backend models match `contracts/types.<ext>` (the flat file, e.g. `contracts/types.py`)
111: - Frontend types match `contracts/types.<ext>` (the flat file, e.g. `contracts/types.ts`)
112: - No camelCase vs snake_case mismatches (unless documented transform exists)
113: - Enum values identical on both sides
114: - **Are shared types actually imported and used?** If the contract provides Pydantic models or TypeScript interfaces as "single source of truth," verify the implementation imports them for validation and serialization rather than manually constructing dicts. Manual construction is the #1 cause of field-naming drift.
115: 
116: ### 6. Domain Rules Conformance
117: 
118: Check `contracts/README.md` for domain business rules and verify the implementation enforces them:
119: 
120: - Invariants (e.g., "sellers can't buy their own listings") — is the check in the code?
121: - Null handling — can nullable fields be set to null via PATCH? (common bug: conflating absent keys with explicit null)
122: - Transaction semantics — are atomic operations actually wrapped in transactions?
123: - Idempotency — do idempotent endpoints handle duplicates correctly?
124: 
125: ### 7. Contract Internal Consistency
126: 
127: Check the contracts themselves for contradictions — this is unique value the auditor provides that the qe-agent cannot:
128: 
129: - Does the README contradict the shared types file? (e.g., types say "use these models" but README says "don't import Pydantic")
130: - Do the OpenAPI response shapes match the shared types definitions?
131: - Are there endpoints in the OpenAPI spec that aren't covered by the data layer contract?
132: - Are there error codes used in the implementation that aren't defined in the contract?
133: 
134: Flag contradictions to the lead — don't assume the implementation is wrong when the contract is unclear.
135: 
136: ### 8. Generate Audit Report
137: 
138: Write the report to **`contract-audit.md`** at the repo root — alongside `contracts/` and the qe-agent's `qa-report.md`/`qa-report.json`. This is the concrete artifact `qe-agent` consumes (it reads `contract-audit.md` before starting integration testing). If the project already pins an audit-output path in `docs/agents/contract-format.md`, use that instead.
139: 
140: ```markdown
141: # Contract Audit Report
142: Generated: [timestamp]
143: 
144: ## Summary
145: | Area | Matches | Mismatches | Not Checked |
146: |------|---------|------------|-------------|
147: | API endpoints | X | Y | Z |
148: | Frontend calls | X | Y | Z |
149: | Data layer | X | Y | Z |
150: | Shared types | X | Y | Z |
151: 
152: ## Mismatches
153: ### MISMATCH-1: [endpoint/function]
154: - **Contract says:** [contracted behavior]
155: - **Implementation does:** [actual behavior]
156: - **Agent responsible:** [backend | frontend]
157: - **File:** [path:line]
158: 
159: ## Verified Matches
160: [List of everything that matched, for completeness]
161: ```
162: 
163: ### Severity Guidelines
164: 
165: - **CRITICAL** — will cause runtime integration failure (wrong field names on wire, missing endpoints, broken error envelope)
166: - **HIGH** — will cause edge-case failures (null handling bugs, error ordering, missing validation)
167: - **MEDIUM** — contract drift risk (shared types not imported, undocumented behavior)
168: - **LOW** — style or naming inconsistency with no functional impact
169: 
170: ## Pact Testing (Optional)
171: 
172: For projects that use consumer-driven contract testing, see `references/pact-setup.md` for Pact framework integration patterns.
173: 
174: ## Coordination Rules
175: 
176: - **Read-only** — you never modify code
177: - **Contract is truth** — if implementation differs, implementation is wrong
178: - **Report precisely** — include file paths, line numbers, exact differences
179: - **Flag ambiguities** — if the contract is ambiguous, flag it to the lead
180: - **You vs. qe-agent** — you do **static** contract verification (reading code against contracts). The qe-agent does **runtime** verification (executing requests and comparing responses). You run first; your `contract-audit.md` report feeds into QE's Phase 1. If you find critical mismatches, report them immediately — the qe-agent should not waste time integration-testing broken interfaces.
181: - **You vs. contract-author** — the contract-author *generates* contracts; you *verify* implementations match them. If you find a gap in the contract itself (ambiguous, incomplete, or contradictory), flag it to the lead — don't assume the implementation is wrong when the contract is unclear.

=== skills/contracts/contract-author/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: contract-author
3: version: 1.5.0
4: description: |
5:   Generate machine-readable integration contracts (API, data layer, shared types, events) before any implementation begins in a multi-agent build. This is orchestrator Phase 4 — contracts written here BEFORE any implementation agent is spawned. Use when authoring API contracts, OpenAPI specs, AsyncAPI specs, Pydantic models, TypeScript interfaces, JSON Schema definitions, data layer interfaces, shared type schemas, integration boundaries between agents, or domain business rules. Trigger on "write the API contract", "define the shared types", "spec out the endpoints", "create the OpenAPI", "author the contract", or when the orchestrator needs contracts authored for a plan. Bundles six templates (OpenAPI, AsyncAPI, Pydantic, TypeScript, JSON Schema, data-layer YAML) — pick the one matching the project's stack.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: ["contracts/", "schemas/"]
11:   patterns: ["openapi.yaml", "asyncapi.yaml"]
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with: ["backend-agent", "frontend-agent", "contract-auditor", "contract-conformance-loop", "qe-agent"]
15: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
16: ---
17: 
18: # Contract Author
19: 
20: > **Tradeoff:** Biases toward upfront design over fast iteration. For prototypes, skip contracts and use direct module imports.
21: 
22: ## When this skill applies
23: 
24: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
25: 
26: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
27: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
28: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
29: 
30: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
31: 
32: <what-to-do>
33: 
34: Generate machine-readable integration contracts before any implementation begins. Contracts are the foundation of reliable multi-agent builds — specification problems cause ~42% of multi-agent failures.
35: 
36: ## Role
37: 
38: You are the **contract author**. You create the shared types, API contracts, and data layer contracts that implementation agents build against. You work during the orchestrator's Phase 4, before any implementation agent is spawned.
39: 
40: ## Why Contracts Matter
41: 
42: Without contracts, agents independently invent their own endpoint URLs, response shapes, type definitions, and storage semantics. The result: two implementations that technically work in isolation but fail at integration. Contracts eliminate this by defining the interfaces upfront.
43: 
44: ## Process
45: 
46: ### 0a. Read project config — `docs/agents/contract-format.md`
47: 
48: Before extracting entities or touching a template, check for `docs/agents/contract-format.md`. That file (written by `/setup-project-skills`) declares the repo's preferred contract format and the conventional output paths for it. When present, use it as the source of truth for the rest of this skill:
49: 
50: - It names one format: **OpenAPI**, **Pydantic**, **TypeScript**, or **JSON Schema**.
51: - It names the output directory and filename convention (e.g. `contracts/openapi/<name>.openapi.yaml`).
52: - It may name per-agent consumption notes (which agent generates types from the spec, which agent gates conformance).
53: 
54: If the file is present, skip the "Pick the format that matches the project's primary language" choice in step 1 and use the declared one. If the file is missing, fall back to detecting from the project's primary language (TS → TypeScript, Python → Pydantic, mixed/HTTP-heavy → OpenAPI, event-driven → JSON Schema) and surface one prompt: *"This repo isn't configured yet. Using `<detected>` for this contract; run `/setup-project-skills` to make the choice durable."* Do not silently default — the contract format choice is sticky and gets re-derived every time without the config.
55: 
56: ### 0b. Extract Entities from the Plan
57: 
58: Before writing any contract, read the plan and extract:
59: 
60: - **Domain entities** — nouns that represent stored data (User, Product, Order, etc.)
61: - **Relationships** — which entities reference which (Order has many OrderItems, each referencing a Product)
62: - **Actions** — verbs that become endpoints (create, search, checkout, upload)
63: - **Domain rules** — business logic that crosses agent boundaries (e.g., "sellers can't buy their own listings", "stock decrements atomically at checkout")
64: - **Integration points** — external services (Stripe, S3, Redis) that need contract coverage
65: 
66: This extraction step prevents missing entities that only become apparent during implementation.
67: 
68: ### 1. Start with Shared Types
69: 
70: Always create the shared types file first — everything else references it. Write it as a **single flat file** named `contracts/types.<ext>` (e.g. `contracts/types.ts`, `contracts/types.py`, `contracts/types.json`) — **not** a directory `contracts/types/`. Consumers (`contract-auditor`, `frontend-agent`) read this one flat file.
71: 
72: Pick the format that matches the project's primary language:
73: 
74: - TypeScript → `references/typescript-template.ts` → write to `contracts/types.ts`
75: - Python → `references/pydantic-template.py` → write to `contracts/types.py`
76: - Multi-language → `references/json-schema-template.json` → write to `contracts/types.json`
77: 
78: Define every entity, enum, request shape, response shape, and the error envelope. Use the strongest type annotations available — `EmailStr` for emails, `HttpUrl` for URLs, `Decimal` for money (or integer cents with clear documentation). The richer the types, the fewer integration bugs.
79: 
80: ### 2. Author API Contract
81: 
82: Use `references/openapi-template.yaml` as the starting point. For every endpoint, specify:
83: 
84: - **Method + Path** (exact, including trailing slash convention)
85: - **Request body** (exact JSON shape with types)
86: - **Success response** (status code + exact JSON shape)
87: - **Error responses** (every possible error status + shape)
88: - **SSE/Streaming events** (if applicable, with exact event shapes)
89: 
90: Required sections (non-negotiable):
91: 
92: - Conventions (base URL, trailing slashes, Content-Type, date format, ID format)
93: - Error envelope (standard shape for all errors)
94: - CORS (allowed origin for browser consumers)
95: 
96: ### 3. Author Data Layer Contract
97: 
98: Use `references/data-layer-template.yaml` as the starting point. Define function signatures, return types, storage semantics:
99: 
100: - Streaming/chunked data handling (accumulated vs per-chunk)
101: - Cascade delete behavior
102: - Timestamp ownership (caller vs data layer vs DB)
103: - ID generation strategy
104: - Required indexes
105: 
106: ### 4. Author Event Contract (if applicable)
107: 
108: For event-driven systems, use `references/asyncapi-template.yaml`:
109: 
110: - Channel/topic names
111: - Message schemas
112: - Delivery guarantees
113: - Error handling
114: 
115: ### 5. Assign Cross-Cutting Concerns
116: 
117: Explicitly assign each concern to exactly one agent:
118: 
119: - URL conventions → backend
120: - Response envelope → backend
121: - Error format → backend
122: - CORS configuration → backend
123: - Streaming storage → backend/data layer
124: - Accessibility → frontend
125: 
126: Add project-specific concerns as they arise (e.g., Stripe webhook validation → backend, image upload storage → backend, client-side validation → frontend).
127: 
128: ### 6. Document Domain Business Rules
129: 
130: Capture cross-cutting business logic that agents need to enforce consistently. These are rules that don't belong in a single contract file but affect multiple agents:
131: 
132: - Invariants (e.g., "sellers cannot buy their own listings")
133: - Transaction semantics (e.g., "checkout must atomically decrement stock and create order")
134: - Idempotency requirements (e.g., "webhook handler must tolerate duplicate delivery")
135: - State machine transitions (e.g., "order status can only go pending → paid → shipped → delivered")
136: 
137: Document these in `contracts/README.md` under a "Domain Rules" section.
138: 
139: ### 7. Define File Ownership Boundaries
140: 
141: In `contracts/README.md`, include a table mapping which agent owns which files/directories. This prevents conflicts during parallel implementation:
142: 
143: ```
144: | Agent    | Owns                              |
145: |----------|-----------------------------------|
146: | Backend  | src/api/, src/services/, src/models/ |
147: | Frontend | src/components/, static/, templates/ |
148: | Neither  | contracts/ (read-only)             |
149: ```
150: 
151: ### 8. Add Per-Agent Implementation Notes
152: 
153: For each agent that will consume the contracts, include brief implementation guidance in the README:
154: 
155: - Which libraries/frameworks to use for contract compliance
156: - How to import or generate types from the shared types file
157: - Any agent-specific patterns (e.g., "frontend should generate TypeScript types from openapi.yaml using openapi-typescript")
158: 
159: These notes save agents from independently rediscovering the same decisions.
160: 
161: ### 9. Quality Checklist
162: 
163: Before handing contracts to the orchestrator:
164: 
165: - [ ] URLs are exact (method + path, no ambiguity)
166: - [ ] Response shapes are explicit JSON, not prose
167: - [ ] All status codes specified (success AND error)
168: - [ ] SSE event types have exact JSON shapes
169: - [ ] Storage semantics explicit
170: - [ ] Shared types defined once and referenced everywhere
171: - [ ] Trailing slash convention stated
172: - [ ] Error envelope defined
173: - [ ] Cross-cutting concerns each assigned to one agent
174: - [ ] CORS origin specified
175: - [ ] Every contract versioned (start at v1)
176: - [ ] Domain business rules documented
177: - [ ] File ownership boundaries defined for each agent
178: - [ ] Per-agent implementation notes included
179: - [ ] Field names consistent across ALL contract files (types, OpenAPI, data layer)
180: - [ ] Complexity matches project scope (simple projects don't need JWT, AsyncAPI, etc.)
181: - [ ] TypeScript projects: `contracts/tsconfig.json` exists alongside `src/` and extends a workspace base where applicable. Verify by running `tsc --noEmit` from the contracts package — it should typecheck cleanly, not print the tsc help screen.
182: 
183: ## Contract Versioning
184: 
185: All contracts start at v1. When changes are needed during the build:
186: 
187: 1. Increment version (v1 → v2)
188: 2. Write the full updated contract (not just a diff)
189: 3. Notify all affected agents with explicit change description
190: 4. Get acknowledgment from each affected agent
191: 
192: **The changelog lives in `contracts/CHANGELOG.md` ONLY.** A generated types/contract file (`contracts/types.ts`, `contracts/openapi.yaml`, etc.) carries at most a single one-line `// — vX.Y.Z` version marker near the top — **NEVER** an inline multi-line version-history block. "Bump the header" means updating that one version line, not appending a history entry to the file. An inline changelog at the top of a widely-imported file (`types.ts` is read on nearly every task) is pure read-tax for zero runtime value, and it self-propagates: each agent that edits the file pattern-matches the block and appends to it, so it only grows. Keep version history in `CHANGELOG.md`, not in source headers. If you find an inline history block while editing, that's a finding (file a cleanup item), not a thing to extend.
193: 
194: ## Right-Sizing
195: 
196: Match contract complexity to the project. A personal habit tracker with SQLite doesn't need JWT auth schemas, AsyncAPI specs, or elaborate security middleware contracts. Ask:
197: 
198: - Does this project need auth? If not, omit security schemes entirely.
199: - Is there a real-time component? If not, skip AsyncAPI.
200: - How many entities? A 2-entity project needs a simpler data layer than a 10-entity one.
201: - What DB? SQLite projects use auto-increment IDs, not UUIDs. PostgreSQL projects can use UUID v4.
202: 
203: Over-engineered contracts waste agent time implementing unnecessary complexity.
204: 
205: ## Anti-Pattern
206: 
207: > **Forbidden:** Authoring contracts speculatively for future features. Author only contracts the current dispatch needs.
208: 
209: </what-to-do>
210: 
211: <supporting-info>
212: 
213: ## Output
214: 
215: Your deliverables (machine-readable formats — not markdown narratives):
216: 
217: - `contracts/types.[ts|py|json]` — shared type definitions. This is a single **flat file** (`contracts/types.ts` / `contracts/types.py` / `contracts/types.json`), **never** a directory `contracts/types/`. The consumer (`contract-auditor`) and `frontend-agent` read this exact flat-file path.
218: - `contracts/tsconfig.json` — REQUIRED when language is TypeScript. Must extend the workspace base tsconfig if one exists (e.g. `../../tsconfig.base.json`). Without this, `tsc --noEmit` runs with no project config and silently prints its help text instead of typechecking, which gets discovered late. (Skip this file for Python/Go/other-language projects.)
219: - `contracts/openapi.yaml` — API contract (OpenAPI 3.1 spec)
220: - `contracts/data-layer.yaml` — data layer interface (use `references/data-layer-template.yaml`)
221: - `contracts/asyncapi.yaml` — event-driven interface (if applicable)
222: - `contracts/README.md` — human-readable summary including:
223:   - Conventions (base URL, trailing slashes, date format, ID format, CORS)
224:   - Naming transform table (snake_case ↔ camelCase field mappings)
225:   - Cross-cutting concern assignments
226:   - Domain business rules
227:   - File ownership boundaries
228:   - Per-agent implementation notes
229:   - Endpoint quick reference
230: 
231: - `contracts/CHANGELOG.md` — the single system of record for contract version history. Every version bump's history entry goes here. A code/contract file gets only the one-line `// — vX.Y.Z` marker plus (optionally) a pointer to this file; it never carries an inline multi-line history block. See "Contract Versioning" above.
232: 
233: The `schemas/` directory is for standalone JSON Schema files when the project uses schema-based validation outside the API context (e.g., config file validation, message queue payloads).
234: 
235: ## Naming Convention Rule
236: 
237: When the API uses camelCase (OpenAPI/TypeScript) but the backend uses snake_case (Python), document the transform explicitly in `contracts/README.md`. The Pydantic template includes `alias_generator=to_camel` for this — both sides must agree on the wire format.
238: 
239: </supporting-info>

=== skills/git/git-commit/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: git-commit
3: version: 1.3.0
4: description: >
5:   Guide for creating git commits in this repository: conventional commit format,
6:   allowed types, and branch naming conventions.
7:   Use when staging changes and writing a commit message, creating a branch,
8:   or naming a branch. Trigger on: "commit", "create a commit", "git commit",
9:   "stage changes", "write a commit message", "make a commit", "branch name",
10:   "new branch", "create branch". Also use proactively whenever you are about
11:   to run `git commit` — even if the user did not explicitly mention this skill.
12: requires_agent_teams: false
13: requires_claude_code: false
14: min_plan: starter
15: owns:
16:   directories: []
17:   patterns: []
18:   shared_read: []
19: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Bash"]
20: composes_with: ["git-pr", "git-pr-feedback", "git-post-merge-cleanup"]
21: spawned_by: []
22: ---
23: 
24: # Git Commit Conventions
25: 
26: ## Commit Message Format
27: 
28: ```text
29: type: short description in imperative mood
30: 
31: Optional body paragraph.
32: ```
33: 
34: - **No scope** — use `feat: add X`, not `feat(module): add X`.
35: - **Lowercase** type and description.
36: - **Imperative mood**: "add X", "fix Y", "remove Z" — not "added", "adding", "fixes".
37: - **No period** at the end.
38: - **Max ~72 characters** on the subject line.
39: - Optional body (blank line after subject): use for context, not mechanics.
40: 
41: ### Allowed Types
42: 
43: | Type | When to use |
44: |---|---|
45: | `feat` | New feature or behaviour |
46: | `fix` | Bug fix |
47: | `test` | Adding or fixing tests only |
48: | `docs` | Documentation only |
49: | `refactor` | Code change with no feature/fix |
50: | `chore` | Build, tooling, config, deps |
51: | `build` | Build system changes |
52: | `perf` | Performance improvement |
53: | `style` | Formatting, whitespace — no logic change |
54: | `revert` | Reverts a previous commit |
55: 
56: ### Examples
57: 
58: A complete commit with body:
59: 
60: ```text
61: feat: add retry policy to HTTP client
62: 
63: Wrap outbound calls in a retry with exponential backoff so
64: transient failures from downstream services don't fail the request.
65: ```
66: 
67: A minimal commit:
68: 
69: ```text
70: fix: handle null input in prompt builder
71: ```
72: 
73: Subject-only quick reference:
74: 
75: ```text
76: test: add unit tests for retry backoff logic
77: chore: update dependencies
78: docs: document skill frontmatter spec
79: refactor: extract connection string builder to helper
80: ```
81: 
82: ---
83: 
84: ## Branch Naming
85: 
86: ```text
87: {type}/{short-description}
88: ```
89: 
90: - Use the commit type directly (`feat`, `fix`, `chore`, etc.) — not expanded forms.
91: - Description uses hyphens to separate words, kept to 3–5 meaningful words.
92: 
93: ### Branch Name Examples
94: 
95: ```text
96: feat/add-query-meter-api
97: fix/api-client-retry-policy
98: chore/update-skill-frontmatter
99: docs/add-orchestrator-guide
100: ```
101: 
102: ---
103: 
104: ## Merge Strategy
105: 
106: PRs merge with a **squash merge** by default unless the branch has a meaningful commit history worth preserving.
107: 
108: ---
109: 
110: ## Co-Authored-By Trailer
111: 
112: Commits in this repo end with a `Co-Authored-By: Claude ... <noreply@anthropic.com>` trailer and a `Claude-Session:` line. These are applied automatically from the agent's environment/system instructions — do **not** hand-type them into the message body, and do not invent or alter the email or session URL. Write your `type: description` subject and optional body as normal; the trailers are appended below the body, separated by a blank line, as the last lines of the message.
113: 
114: ---
115: 
116: ## Quick Checklist
117: 
118: - [ ] Subject line: `type: description` (lowercase, imperative, no scope, no period)
119: - [ ] Type is one of the allowed types above
120: - [ ] Optional body explains the *why*, not the *what*
121: - [ ] Branch: `type/short-description`
122: 
123: ---
124: 
125: ## Anti-Pattern
126: 
127: > **Forbidden:** Amending commits unless the user explicitly asks. After a pre-commit hook failure, create a NEW commit.

=== skills/git/git-post-merge-cleanup/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: git-post-merge-cleanup
3: version: 1.2.0
4: description: >
5:   Clean up everything stale after merges in one pass: local branches fully
6:   merged into the default branch, remote-tracking refs whose remote is gone,
7:   and worktrees that look "modified" but are already on main. Scans, classifies,
8:   presents a clear plan, and only acts on confirmation. Supports `--dry-run`
9:   and `--yes`. Use whenever the user mentions cleaning up branches OR worktrees,
10:   tidying git state after a batch of PRs landed, or noticing stale debris.
11:   Trigger on: "clean up branches", "delete merged branches", "prune branches",
12:   "stale branches", "branch cleanup", "remove old branches",
13:   "clean up worktrees", "remove old worktrees", "worktree confusion", "phantom
14:   modified files", "post-merge cleanup".
15:   Also trigger proactively when `git worktree list` shows entries beyond the
16:   main worktree, or after a batch of PRs land.
17: requires_agent_teams: false
18: requires_claude_code: false
19: min_plan: starter
20: owns:
21:   directories: []
22:   patterns: []
23:   shared_read: []
24: allowed-tools: ["Bash", "Read", "Write"]
25: composes_with: ["git-commit", "git-pr", "git-pr-feedback"]
26: spawned_by: []
27: ---
28: 
29: # Git Post-Merge Cleanup
30: 
31: After a batch of PRs land, the local repo accumulates debris: fully merged
32: branches, tracking refs whose remote was deleted by GitHub, and worktrees
33: that still show "modified" files even though those changes are already on
34: main. This skill cleans all of it in one pass — scan, classify, present a
35: plan, confirm, execute. The scan is cheap; always do both classes.
36: 
37: ## Flags
38: 
39: - `--dry-run` — scan and present the plan, but do not delete anything.
40: - `--yes` — skip the confirmation prompt. Still presents the plan first.
41: 
42: Default (no flags): scan, present plan, wait for confirmation.
43: 
44: ## Step 1 — Refresh remote state
45: 
46: ```bash
47: git fetch origin --prune
48: ```
49: 
50: Without `--prune`, stale `origin/*` refs linger for branches GitHub already
51: deleted on merge. Also scan for orphan tags (e.g. `freebuff-snapshot/*`) that
52: were left behind when their source branches were deleted — there is no
53: `--prune` equivalent for tags, so remove them explicitly:
54: 
55: ```bash
56: git tag -d <tag-name>                        # delete locally
57: git push origin --delete refs/tags/<tag-name> # delete remotely
58: ```
59: 
60: List all tags with `git tag` and decide which are orphaned by checking if
61: they reference a deleted branch (the tag name often embeds the branch UUID).
62: 
63: ## Step 2 — Detect the default branch
64: 
65: Do not assume `main`:
66: 
67: ```bash
68: git symbolic-ref -q --short refs/remotes/origin/HEAD 2>/dev/null | sed 's@^origin/@@'
69: ```
70: 
71: If that fails, fall back to checking whether `main` or `master` exists
72: locally. Use the result as `$DEFAULT`.
73: 
74: ## Step 3 — Scan and classify
75: 
76: Run both classifiers. Results feed the single plan in Step 4.
77: 
78: ### Branches
79: 
80: Cross-reference: `git branch -vv` (tracking status), `git branch --merged
81: origin/$DEFAULT` (local merged), `git branch -r --merged origin/$DEFAULT`
82: (remote merged), `git branch -r --no-merged origin/$DEFAULT` (context).
83: 
84: Bucket every branch into one of:
85: 
86: - **fully-merged-local** — in `--merged`, not the default branch. Safe to `git branch -d`.
87: - **fully-merged-remote** — in `-r --merged`, not `origin/HEAD` and not `origin/$DEFAULT`. Safe to `git push origin --delete`.
88: - **orphan-tracking** — `git branch -vv` shows tracking remote as `gone` AND branch is not in `--merged`. Unmerged work; flag for user attention, do not delete.
89: - **in-flight** — everything else. Leave alone.
90: 
91: See `references/branch-classification.md` for edge cases (squash-merged
92: branches that don't appear in `--merged`, detached HEAD, etc).
93: 
94: ### Worktrees
95: 
96: ```bash
97: git worktree list
98: ```
99: 
100: The first entry is the **main worktree** (the repo root) — never a candidate.
101: For each secondary worktree, classify as either:
102: 
103: - **safe-worktree** — branch is fully merged into `$DEFAULT` OR squash-merged with empty diff vs `$DEFAULT` OR uncommitted changes all match `$DEFAULT`. Safe to `git worktree remove --force`.
104: - **unsafe-worktree** — genuinely unmerged work. Flag for user attention, do not remove.
105: 
106: The detection commands and the exact "safe" definition live in
107: `references/worktree-cleanup-rules.md`. Use them — do not eyeball.
108: 
109: ## Step 4 — Present the plan
110: 
111: Show one combined plan, grouped by category, with counts and reasons:
112: 
113: ```text
114: Post-merge cleanup plan ($DEFAULT = main)
115: 
116: Local branches to delete (fully merged) — N:
117:   - feat/add-retry
118:   - chore/bump-deps
119: 
120: Remote branches to delete (fully merged) — M:
121:   - origin/feat/add-retry  (or: already pruned by GitHub)
122: 
123: Worktrees to remove (safe) — P:
124:   - .claude/worktrees/heuristic-northcutt  [claude/heuristic-northcutt] — branch merged
125:   - .worktrees/auth-feature                [feat/auth]                 — squash-merged (empty diff)
126: 
127: Needs attention (will NOT touch) — K:
128:   - branch: experimental             — remote gone, unmerged work
129:   - worktree: .worktrees/spike-foo   — 3 files differ from main
130: 
131: Keeping — J active branches, 1 main worktree.
132: ```
133: 
134: If a category is empty, say so explicitly ("No orphaned branches.") rather
135: than omitting it — the user should see every category was checked.
136: 
137: ## Step 5 — Confirm
138: 
139: - Default: wait for the user to confirm before any destructive command runs.
140: - `--dry-run`: print the plan and stop.
141: - `--yes`: proceed without confirmation. Still print the plan first.
142: 
143: ## Step 6 — Execute
144: 
145: Order matters: remove worktrees before deleting their branches, otherwise
146: `git branch -d` refuses because the branch is checked out elsewhere.
147: 
148: Remove safe worktrees first:
149: 
150: ```bash
151: git worktree remove --force <worktree-path>
152: ```
153: 
154: `--force` is needed because the worktree shows local modifications relative
155: to its HEAD — those changes are already on `$DEFAULT`, which is why we
156: classified it safe. The classification IS the safety net.
157: 
158: Delete local merged branches:
159: 
160: ```bash
161: git branch -d branch1 branch2 branch3
162: ```
163: 
164: Lowercase `-d` refuses to delete unmerged branches as a backstop. Never use
165: `-D` unless a squash-merge was confirmed (empty diff vs `$DEFAULT`) or the
166: user explicitly asked to force-delete.
167: 
168: Delete remote merged branches:
169: 
170: ```bash
171: git push origin --delete branch1 branch2 branch3
172: ```
173: 
174: Use bare names — strip `origin/`. "remote ref does not exist" is expected
175: when GitHub already deleted on PR merge; note and move on.
176: 
177: Prune worktree metadata and tracking refs:
178: 
179: ```bash
180: git worktree prune
181: git fetch origin --prune
182: ```
183: 
184: ## Step 7 — Summary
185: 
186: Show the final state with `git branch -vv` and `git worktree list`. Report:
187: local branches deleted, remote branches deleted, worktrees removed, items
188: flagged for attention, and what remains.
189: 
190: ### Non-interactive merge commit pitfall
191: 
192: When resolving a merge conflict in an automated/non-interactive context,
193: `git merge --continue` opens Vim for the commit message and hangs indefinitely.
194: Use one of these instead:
195: 
196: ```bash
197: GIT_EDITOR=true git merge --continue       # accept auto-generated message
198: git commit -m "Merge branch 'X' into Y"   # explicit message
199: ```
200: 
201: `git merge --continue` does NOT accept `--no-edit` (it takes no arguments),
202: so the `GIT_EDITOR` env var is the reliable path.
203: 
204: ## Safety rules
205: 
206: - Never remove the main worktree (the repo root). Ever.
207: - Never delete `$DEFAULT`, `develop`, `staging`, or `production`.
208: - Never delete a branch or remove a worktree with unmerged work without
209:   explicit user confirmation. The "needs attention" bucket is the signal.
210: - Lowercase `-d` before uppercase `-D`. Always.
211: - The classification is the safety net — do not eyeball.
212: 
213: ## Edge cases
214: 
215: - **Nothing to clean up** — report "Already tidy" and stop.
216: - **Permission errors on remote delete** — report cleanly; suggest the
217:   GitHub UI or a permissions check.
218: - **Squash-merge not detected by `--merged`** — covered by the empty-diff
219:   check in `references/worktree-cleanup-rules.md`; same check applies to
220:   branches without worktrees.
221: - **Large counts (>20 in a category)** — list all but group by prefix
222:   (e.g., "12 feat/*, 5 chore/*") so the plan stays readable.

=== skills/git/git-pr/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: git-pr
3: version: 1.4.0
4: description: >
5:   Guide for creating and updating GitHub pull requests in this repository:
6:   PR title format, body structure, clean descriptions, and the gh CLI workflow.
7:   Use when creating a pull request, writing a PR description, updating a PR,
8:   or preparing changes for review. Trigger on: "create a PR", "open a PR",
9:   "pull request", "PR description", "PR body", "ready for review",
10:   "gh pr create", "open pull request", "submit PR", "PR template".
11:   Also use proactively whenever you are about to run `gh pr create` —
12:   even if the user did not explicitly mention this skill.
13: requires_agent_teams: false
14: requires_claude_code: false
15: min_plan: starter
16: owns:
17:   directories: []
18:   patterns: []
19:   shared_read: []
20: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Bash"]
21: composes_with: ["git-commit", "git-pr-feedback"]
22: spawned_by: []
23: ---
24: 
25: # GitHub Pull Request Conventions
26: 
27: ## PR Title Format
28: 
29: ```text
30: type: Short description of the change
31: ```
32: 
33: - Use the same type conventions as git-commit (`feat`, `fix`, `docs`, etc.).
34: - Sentence case for the description (capital first letter, no trailing period).
35: - Keep it concise (under 70 characters is ideal).
36: - The description should summarize the *intent* of the whole PR, not list individual commits.
37: 
38: ### Title examples
39: 
40: ```text
41: feat: Add retry policy to HTTP client
42: fix: Handle null input in prompt builder
43: docs: Add platform comparison document
44: chore: Update skill frontmatter spec
45: ```
46: 
47: ---
48: 
49: ## PR Body Structure
50: 
51: Use this template when writing the PR description. Adjust sections as needed
52: for the size and complexity of the change — small fixes may only need the summary.
53: 
54: ```markdown
55: ## Summary
56: 
57: <1–3 bullet points explaining what changed and why>
58: 
59: ## Changes
60: 
61: <Grouped list of notable changes.>
62: 
63: ## Test plan
64: 
65: <How was this tested? What should reviewers verify?>
66: ```
67: 
68: ### Body guidelines
69: 
70: - **Lead with the why.** Reviewers already have the diff — tell them what problem this solves.
71: - **Keep it scannable.** Bullet points and short paragraphs. No walls of text.
72: - **Mention risks.** If the change has migration steps or deployment ordering constraints, call them out.
73: 
74: ---
75: 
76: ## Workflow
77: 
78: ### Creating a new PR
79: 
80: 1. **Ensure the branch is pushed** with tracking set:
81: 
82:    ```bash
83:    git push -u origin HEAD
84:    ```
85: 
86: 2. **Check for an existing PR first** — the branch may already have a PR from
87:    a previous push or another session:
88: 
89:    ```bash
90:    gh pr view --head <branch> --json number,state,title 2>/dev/null
91:    ```
92: 
93:    If a PR already exists, update it with `gh pr edit` instead of creating
94:    a duplicate. If it's in a MERGED state, note that the work is already
95:    shipped — do not create a new PR.
96: 
97: 3. **Create the PR** using `gh pr create`. Pass the body via a HEREDOC to
98:    preserve formatting. Keep HEREDOC content flush-left so GitHub renders
99:    the markdown correctly:
100: 
101:    ```bash
102:    gh pr create --title "type: Description here" --body "$(cat <<'EOF'
103:    ## Summary
104: 
105:    - <what changed and why>
106: 
107:    ## Changes
108: 
109:    - <notable change 1>
110:    - <notable change 2>
111: 
112:    ## Test plan
113: 
114:    - [ ] <verification step>
115:    EOF
116:    )"
117:    ```
118: 
119:    Use `--base <branch>` if targeting a branch other than the repository default.
120: 
121: 4. **Return the PR URL** to the user so they can review it.
122: 
123: ### Updating an existing PR
124: 
125: ```bash
126: gh pr edit {number} --title "type: Updated description"
127: gh pr edit {number} --body "$(cat <<'EOF'
128: …updated body…
129: EOF
130: )"
131: ```
132: 
133: ### Checking PR status
134: 
135: ```bash
136: gh pr status          # overview of your PRs
137: gh pr view {number}   # details of a specific PR
138: gh pr checks {number} # CI check status
139: ```
140: 
141: ---
142: 
143: ## Pre-Push Checklist
144: 
145: - [ ] PR title: `type: Description` (sentence case, under 70 chars)
146: - [ ] PR body has a Summary section explaining the why
147: - [ ] Test plan describes how to verify the change
148: - [ ] CI checks pass (or failures are understood and noted)

=== skills/git/git-pr-feedback/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: git-pr-feedback
3: version: 1.3.0
4: description: >
5:   Fetch, triage, and address PR review comments from GitHub Copilot and human
6:   reviewers. Use when the user asks to check PR feedback, review comments,
7:   address reviewer suggestions, or fix PR issues. Trigger on: "check PR
8:   comments", "what did copilot say", "address PR feedback", "fix review
9:   comments", "handle PR suggestions", "review feedback", "PR comments",
10:   "copilot review", "reviewer feedback", or any mention of responding to pull
11:   request comments. Also trigger when the user returns to a PR after some time
12:   and wants to handle accumulated feedback.
13: requires_agent_teams: false
14: requires_claude_code: false
15: min_plan: starter
16: owns:
17:   directories: []
18:   patterns: []
19:   shared_read: ["*"]
20: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep"]
21: composes_with: ["git-pr", "git-commit", "git-post-merge-cleanup", "babysit"]
22: spawned_by: []
23: ---
24: 
25: # PR Feedback Handler
26: 
27: Fetch review comments on a GitHub PR, triage them, fix what's clear, ask about
28: what's ambiguous, and reply to each comment on GitHub with what was done.
29: 
30: ## Workflow
31: 
32: ### 1. Identify the PR
33: 
34: Determine the PR number from context:
35: 
36: ```bash
37: # Current branch's PR
38: gh pr view --json number -q .number
39: 
40: # Or from a URL the user provided
41: # Or from a specific PR number they mentioned
42: ```
43: 
44: ### 2. Fetch All Comments
45: 
46: Pull both review comments (inline on code) and issue-level comments:
47: 
48: ```bash
49: # Inline review comments (from Copilot and reviewers)
50: gh api --paginate repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{pr}/comments
51: 
52: # General PR comments (not on specific lines)
53: gh api --paginate repos/{owner}/{repo}/issues/{pr}/comments
54: 
55: # Review summaries
56: gh api --paginate repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{pr}/reviews
57: ```
58: 
59: Use `--paginate` to ensure all comments are returned — the default page size
60: is 30, which can silently miss comments on large PRs.
61: 
62: Parse each comment to extract:
63: 
64: - **who**: `user.login` — distinguish `Copilot`, `copilot-pull-request-reviewer[bot]`, and human reviewers
65: - **where**: `path` and `line` — the file and line the comment targets
66: - **what**: `body` — the feedback text
67: - **id**: `id` — needed for replying
68: - **replied**: to detect already-handled comments, scan all comments for
69:   replies where `in_reply_to_id == <top_level_id>` and the reply author is
70:   the PR author.
71: 
72: ### 3. Triage Each Comment
73: 
74: Classify every comment into one of these categories:
75: 
76: | Category | Action | Example |
77: |----------|--------|---------|
78: | **Bug** | Fix immediately | "This glob won't match broken symlinks" |
79: | **Improvement** | Fix if straightforward, ask if complex | "Exit code should be non-zero for errors" |
80: | **Doc issue** | Fix immediately | "The doc says X but the code does Y" |
81: | **Style/nit** | Fix if trivial, skip if subjective | "Consider renaming this variable" |
82: | **Noise/false positive** | Dismiss with explanation | "This is intentional because..." |
83: | **Ambiguous/tricky** | Present to user with options | "This could be fixed multiple ways..." |
84: 
85: **Triage guidelines:**
86: 
87: - Read the actual code the comment references before deciding. The diff context
88:   in the comment may be stale after pushes.
89: - Copilot comments are usually technically accurate but sometimes miss context.
90:   Verify before acting.
91: - Human reviewer comments carry more weight. Treat these as higher priority.
92: - If a comment suggests a significant design decision, always escalate to the user.
93: 
94: ### 4. Present the Triage
95: 
96: Before making changes, show the user a summary:
97: 
98: ```text
99: PR #34 — 3 review comments:
100: 
101: 1. [BUG] Copilot on sync-skills.sh:426
102:    Broken symlink glob — --clean uses */ which skips broken symlinks
103:    → Will fix: change glob to /*
104: 
105: 2. [IMPROVEMENT] Copilot on sync-skills.sh:101
106:    usage exits 0 on invalid options
107:    → Will fix: exit 1 for unknown options
108: 
109: 3. [DOC] Copilot on SKILL.md:78
110:    Doc claims non-repo skills untouched but name collisions get replaced
111:    → Will fix: clarify the wording
112: 
113: Ready to fix all 3? Or want to discuss any of them?
114: ```
115: 
116: For items classified as **ambiguous/tricky**, present the options and wait for
117: the user's decision before proceeding.
118: 
119: ### 5. Implement Fixes
120: 
121: For each actionable comment:
122: 
123: 1. Read the file at the referenced path and line
124: 2. Make the fix
125: 3. Stage the changed files by name (not `git add -p` — interactive staging
126:    doesn't work in automated environments)
127: 
128: After all fixes are applied, commit following git-commit conventions:
129: 
130: ```bash
131: git add path/to/fixed-file1 path/to/fixed-file2
132: git commit -m "$(cat <<'EOF'
133: fix: address review feedback on {context}
134: 
135: {brief description of changes}
136: EOF
137: )"
138: 
139: git push
140: ```
141: 
142: ### 6. Reply to Comments on GitHub
143: 
144: For each comment, post a reply using the GitHub API:
145: 
146: ```bash
147: # Reply to an inline review comment (threaded)
148: gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{pr}/comments/{comment_id}/replies \
149:   -f body="Fixed in {sha} — {brief description of what changed}."
150: 
151: # Reply to a general PR comment (post a new issue comment)
152: gh api repos/{owner}/{repo}/issues/{pr}/comments \
153:   -f body="Re: {summary of original comment} — Fixed in {sha}."
154: ```
155: 
156: Reply content by category:
157: 
158: - **Bug/Improvement/Doc**: "Fixed in {short sha} — {what changed}."
159: - **Noise/false positive**: "This is intentional — {reason}."
160: - **Style adopted**: "Good catch, fixed in {sha}."
161: - **Style declined**: "Keeping as-is — {reason}."
162: 
163: ### 7. Check for New Comments
164: 
165: After pushing fixes, Copilot may generate new comments on the updated code.
166: Don't block waiting for them — let the user know they can ask to check again
167: later.
168: 
169: ## Handling Specific Reviewer Types
170: 
171: ### GitHub Copilot
172: 
173: - Copilot review is *requested* immediately when a PR is created or updated,
174:   but comments can take 30 seconds to several minutes to appear.
175: - Usually catches real issues: bugs, edge cases, doc inconsistencies.
176: - Sometimes suggests changes that miss project-specific context — verify first.
177: - May not comment at all on markdown-only or trivial PRs.
178: 
179: ### Human Reviewers
180: 
181: - May not appear immediately — check back when the user asks.
182: - Comments may be conversational rather than prescriptive.
183: - Look for approval/changes-requested status in the reviews endpoint.
184: - If a reviewer requested changes, prioritize their comments.
185: 
186: ## Edge Cases
187: 
188: - **Already-replied comments**: Skip any top-level comment that has a reply
189:   from the PR author (`in_reply_to_id` points back to the parent).
190: - **Outdated comments**: After pushing fixes, read the file fresh — don't
191:   trust the diff context in the comment.
192: - **Multiple commits**: Batch fixes into one commit unless the fixes are
193:   logically independent enough to warrant separate commits.
194: - **No comments yet**: If fetching after PR creation and getting zero comments,
195:   that's normal — let the user know and offer to check again later.

=== skills/loops/babysit/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: babysit
3: version: 1.1.0
4: description: >-
5:   Keep one of your open PRs healthy on a schedule: poll for new review activity,
6:   auto-address bot/Copilot nits and routine rebases, re-run the project gate, and
7:   reconcile the PR thread — looping until the PR has zero blocking findings and is
8:   mergeable, with HITL checkpoints on anything irreversible. This is the
9:   scheduled, guardrailed loop around git-pr-feedback (which does the per-pass
10:   fetch/triage/fix). Run it as Boris Cherny's daily-driver "/loop 5m /babysit",
11:   or invoke /babysit once. Use when you want a PR babysat while you work
12:   elsewhere, want review comments auto-handled on a cadence, or want a PR kept
13:   rebased and green. Trigger on "babysit my PR", "babysit prs", "watch this PR",
14:   "keep my PR green", "auto-address review comments on a schedule", "loop 5m
15:   babysit", "keep the PR rebased", "poll the PR for new feedback". Never
16:   auto-merges and never force-pushes a shared branch unattended. A
17:   configuration of loop-controller.
18: requires_claude_code: true
19: min_plan: starter
20: disable-model-invocation: true
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
22: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "git-pr-feedback", "git-pr", "git-commit", "loop"]
23: spawned_by: []
24: ---
25: 
26: # babysit
27: 
28: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
29: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
30: > externalization. This skill supplies the three things specific to "keep a PR
31: > healthy": the **per-pass recipe** (it schedules [`git-pr-feedback`], it does
32: > not re-implement it), the **mechanical proof** (zero open blocking findings +
33: > mergeable), and the **HITL boundary** that decides what's AFK-safe vs what
34: > pauses. Read `loop-controller` for the guardrails; they're inherited, not
35: > repeated here.
36: >
37: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop commits, pushes, rebases, and
38: > touches a live PR thread on its own, on a clock. It is user-driven — you want
39: > to *type* `/loop 5m /babysit` (or `/babysit`), not have Claude silently start
40: > tending a PR because a reviewer happened to comment.
41: 
42: ## The 5-part contract
43: 
44: | Part | This loop |
45: |---|---|
46: | **trigger** | an open PR of yours with potential review activity; scheduled via `/loop 5m /babysit`, or an explicit `/babysit` (optionally a PR number) |
47: | **action** | ONE pass: fetch new review findings (Copilot + human) via [`git-pr-feedback`] → triage → address **one root-cause finding** (or rebase if base moved) → re-run the project gate → push → reconcile the PR thread. One change per pass; re-check the whole PR state after |
48: | **proof** | the `gh` PR review/status query returns **zero OPEN actionable/blocking comments AND `mergeable == "MERGEABLE"`** (up to date with base, CI green) — default-FAIL: assume findings are unaddressed until the fetch proves otherwise (treat `mergeable == "UNKNOWN"` as not-yet-proven and re-check) |
49: | **memory** | a per-PR fix log (findings addressed, findings deferred + why), git history, and the PR thread itself — all durable across the session-scoped `/loop` fires |
50: | **stop** | zero blocking findings **AND** mergeable **OR** poll/iteration cap **OR** no-progress for 3 rounds (same finding unaddressed 3×) **OR** budget cap **OR** an HITL checkpoint is hit |
51: 
52: ## The proof: zero blocking findings AND mergeable, default-FAIL
53: 
54: "Healthy" is not "I replied to the last comment." It is **two conditions
55: observed together from `gh`**: the PR has zero *open, actionable, blocking*
56: review comments, **and** GitHub reports it mergeable (rebased onto base, CI
57: green). Assume the PR is **not** healthy until a fresh fetch proves both — that's
58: the default-FAIL stance, and it's why every pass *re-fetches* rather than
59: trusting last pass's verdict. A loop that stops after answering one comment ships
60: a PR that picked up three new ones and went stale behind a base push.
61: 
62: Name the artifact explicitly: a `gh pr view --json mergeable,reviewDecision`
63: plus the open-comments count from `git-pr-feedback`'s fetch (see
64: `references/scheduling.md` for the exact query). Both must read clean *in the
65: same pass*.
66: 
67: ## Step 1 — One pass = one git-pr-feedback cycle
68: 
69: The per-pass work **is** [`git-pr-feedback`]: identify the PR, fetch inline +
70: issue comments + review summaries (paginated), triage each by author and
71: category, address what's clear, reply on GitHub. **Do not re-document or
72: re-implement that** — invoke it. babysit adds only the loop discipline around it:
73: one root-cause change per pass, then re-check the *whole* PR state. If the base
74: branch moved and the only blocker is staleness, the pass is a routine rebase (not
75: a finding fix). The full per-pass algorithm and the handoff are in
76: `references/scheduling.md`.
77: 
78: ## Step 2 — Schedule it with /loop (the watcher, not /goal)
79: 
80: Per `loop-controller` Step 1: this is a **watch/poll** job — you wait for review
81: activity to *change* on a cadence — so the primitive is **`/loop`**, the
82: scheduler, **not** `/goal` (which pushes to a finish line). The recipe is the
83: documented daily driver:
84: 
85: ```
86: /loop 5m /babysit
87: ```
88: 
89: `/loop` is **session-scoped, expires after ~3 days, and does not catch up** on
90: missed intervals — those mechanics (and the `loop.md` self-paced variant) live in
91: `loop-controller`'s `references/primitives.md`; don't re-document them. What goes
92: in `loop.md` and how to scope the run to one PR are in
93: `references/scheduling.md`.
94: 
95: ## HITL is load-bearing for this loop
96: 
97: babysit runs **only inside the reversible boundary** unattended. The irreversible
98: or human-facing actions are **HITL checkpoints — never autonomous**:
99: 
100: - **Force-push / rebasing a shared branch** — pause. A fast-forward push of your
101:   own commits is fine; a history rewrite of a branch others may have pulled is
102:   not.
103: - **Resolving or replying to a *human* reviewer's conversation** — pause. A
104:   bot/Copilot nit can be answered and resolved unattended; a human's thread is a
105:   conversation, and **you do not argue with a reviewer inside a loop.** On any
106:   substantive disagreement with a human reviewer, stop and surface it.
107: - **Merging** — never. babysit does **not** auto-merge, full stop. Reaching the
108:   proof (zero blocking + mergeable) means *ready for the human to merge*, not
109:   "merge it."
110: 
111: Within the boundary — addressing Copilot/bot nits, routine fast-forward rebases,
112: re-running the gate, fast-forward pushes — it is AFK-safe. The full AFK-safe vs
113: pause table is in `references/scheduling.md`.
114: 
115: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
116: 
117: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
118: this loop sets:
119: 
120: - **Poll / iteration cap** — `/loop`'s ~3-day session expiry is the outer bound;
121:   set an inner per-PR pass cap (default ~20) so a wedged PR doesn't burn the
122:   whole window. Hitting it is a *stop-and-escalate*, not a reason to loosen the
123:   proof.
124: - **No-progress detection** — if the **same blocking finding** survives **3
125:   consecutive passes** (addressed but the reviewer/check still flags it, or it
126:   can't be resolved within the reversible boundary), stop and surface it. Three
127:   passes on one finding means it needs a human, not a fourth attempt.
128: - **Budget cap** — a watch loop that fires every 5 minutes for days adds up;
129:   enforce a token/cost ceiling that *terminates* the loop (read it from
130:   `.claude/profile.yaml` when present), not just warns.
131: - **Never cheat the proof.** Don't mark a thread resolved without addressing it,
132:   don't dismiss a human reviewer's comment to clear the count, and don't merge to
133:   make "mergeable" moot. A green that came from silencing a reviewer is a
134:   *finding*, not a win.
135: 
136: ## Choosing the driver primitive
137: 
138: `/loop 5m /babysit` is the scheduler. The *per-pass* exit (this pass made one
139: clean, reversible change and re-checked the PR) is provable from `gh` output, so a
140: pass can run under auto mode unattended within the HITL boundary above. The loop
141: as a whole has **no `/goal` finish line** — it watches until you stop it or a stop
142: condition fires.
143: 
144: ## Long-run hygiene (wired per loop-controller Step 6)
145: 
146: This loop runs unattended for days, so the Claude 5 long-run rules are part of
147: its contract, not inherited ambience (drop-in text: `model-adaptation` →
148: `references/long-run-hygiene.md`):
149: 
150: - **Evidence-backed progress** — every per-pass report line (finding addressed,
151:   rebase done, gate green) traces to a tool result from *that pass*: the `gh`
152:   query output, the gate's exit code, the push result. Never narrate a fix the
153:   re-fetch didn't confirm.
154: - **Don't end a pass on a promise** — a pass ends with the change made and the
155:   whole PR state re-checked, or at an explicit HITL stop; never with "I'll
156:   rebase next pass" and no action. The autonomous-operation reminder applies
157:   while the user is away — the HITL checkpoints above are the only sanctioned
158:   pauses.
159: - **Budget is a harness decision** — the cost ceiling reads from externalized
160:   state (`.claude/profile.yaml`); don't surface a remaining-token countdown to
161:   the working pass, and never summarize-and-quit on a phantom context worry.
162: - **Effort per pass** — routine nit/rebase passes run at `medium`/`high`;
163:   reserve `xhigh` for a genuinely hard finding (tiering: `model-adaptation`).
164: 
165: ## Reference files
166: 
167: - `references/scheduling.md` — the per-pass algorithm and the `git-pr-feedback`
168:   handoff, the scheduling recipe (`/loop 5m /babysit`, what to put in `loop.md`,
169:   scoping to one PR, the exact `gh` proof query), and the HITL boundary table
170:   (what's AFK-safe vs what pauses).
171: 
172: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
173: [`git-pr-feedback`]: ../../git/git-pr-feedback/SKILL.md
174: [`git-pr`]: ../../git/git-pr/SKILL.md
175: [`git-commit`]: ../../git/git-commit/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/codebase-exploration-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: codebase-exploration-loop
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: >-
5:   Build a working mental model of an unfamiliar codebase by looping: fan out
6:   read-only subagents to map subsystems, synthesize, find the gaps in your
7:   understanding, and repeat until a written architecture summary answers every
8:   seed question — each answer citing where in the code it comes from. The
9:   deep-research fan-out shape applied to the LOCAL codebase (Read/Grep, no web),
10:   with a subagent-parallelism cap and a loop-until-dry stop on understanding
11:   gaps. Use when onboarding to a new repo,
12:   reverse-engineering someone else's code, or answering "I don't understand how X
13:   works here" before you touch it.
14:   Trigger on "help me understand this codebase", "onboard me to this repo", "how
15:   does X work in this code", "build a mental model of this project", "explore
16:   this codebase", "map this codebase", "I don't understand this code", "explore
17:   until I understand". A configuration of loop-controller.
18: requires_claude_code: true
19: min_plan: starter
20: disable-model-invocation: true
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Grep", "Glob", "Bash", "Agent", "Workflow"]
22: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "project-profiler", "repo-deep-dive", "orchestrator"]
23: spawned_by: []
24: ---
25: 
26: # codebase-exploration-loop
27: 
28: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
29: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
30: > externalization. This skill supplies the three things specific to "understand
31: > this codebase": the **seed-question proof** (a default-FAIL question list that
32: > only flips on a code citation), the **read-only fan-out** that maps subsystems
33: > in parallel, and the **loop-until-dry** stop on remaining understanding gaps.
34: > Read `loop-controller` for the guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated here.
35: >
36: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop spends tokens fanning out a wave
37: > of subagents per iteration, repeatedly, until your questions are answered. It
38: > is user-driven — you want to *type* `/codebase-exploration-loop` with your
39: > questions, not have Claude silently launch an exploration swarm because you
40: > asked a passing question about a file.
41: 
42: ## The 5-part contract
43: 
44: | Part | This loop |
45: |---|---|
46: | **trigger** | "I don't understand X" about an unfamiliar repo — onboarding, reverse-engineering, or an explicit `/codebase-exploration-loop` with a list of seed questions |
47: | **action** | ONE round: read the gap list → fan out read-only subagents (one per open subsystem/question, parallelism capped) → each returns findings + file:line citations → synthesize into the architecture summary → mark answered questions, surface newly-discovered gaps |
48: | **proof** | the architecture summary answers **every** seed question, each answer **citing a `path:line` (or symbol) in the code**, with zero open "I don't understand X" gaps — default-FAIL: every question starts `answered: false` and flips only on a citation |
49: | **memory** | `seed-questions.json` (the default-FAIL question list + the new gaps each round surfaces) and `ARCHITECTURE-SUMMARY.md` (the growing synthesis) — durable across rounds; no reliance on conversation memory |
50: | **stop** | every question `answered: true` with a citation **OR** loop-until-dry (a round surfaces no new gaps and answers nothing new) **OR** iteration/round cap **OR** no-progress for 2 rounds **OR** budget cap |
51: 
52: ## The proof: seed questions answered with citations, default-FAIL
53: 
54: "Understood" is not "I read a lot of files and it feels familiar." It is **every
55: seed question on the list marked `answered: true`, and each answer points at the
56: `path:line` (or symbol) in the actual code that backs it**. Assume *not*
57: understood until that citation exists — that's the default-FAIL stance, and it's
58: what stops the loop from declaring victory on a vibe. An answer with no code
59: citation is an unanswered question.
60: 
61: Name the artifact: `seed-questions.json` (one entry per question, each defaulting
62: `answered: false`) plus `ARCHITECTURE-SUMMARY.md` (the prose synthesis that cites
63: the code per answer). Both must agree before the loop is done — a question can't
64: be `true` in the JSON without a citation in the summary.
65: 
66: ## Step 1 — Capture the seed questions (default-FAIL)
67: 
68: Before any fan-out, write the seed questions to `seed-questions.json`, every one
69: `answered: false`. These are *your* questions — "where does a request enter?",
70: "how is auth enforced?", "what's the data model?", "where's the build
71: configured?" — not a generic checklist. If the user gave questions, use them
72: verbatim; otherwise propose a starter set and confirm. The list grows: when a
73: round reveals you didn't understand something you thought you did, add it as a
74: new default-FAIL entry. The loop is done only when the list is fully `true` *and*
75: stops growing. The JSON schema and the gap-discovery rule are in
76: `references/exploration.md`.
77: 
78: ## Step 2 — Fan out read-only subagents (cap the parallelism)
79: 
80: This is the `deep-research` fan-out shape pointed at the **local codebase**:
81: one subagent per open subsystem or question, dispatched in parallel, each given
82: **Read/Grep/Glob/Bash-to-inspect only — no Write/Edit, no web**. A read-only
83: mapper cannot accidentally change the code it's studying, and (per
84: `loop-controller`'s evaluator pattern) a fresh subagent reports what's *there*
85: rather than what it hopes is there. Each returns a short findings memo with
86: `path:line` citations.
87: 
88: Cap the fan-out width per round (default ~4-6 concurrent) — per `loop-controller`
89: Step 5, more parallel readers do not equal faster understanding, they equal a
90: synthesis you can't reconcile and a budget you can't predict. The dispatch
91: recipe (and the `/deep-research`-vs-this distinction) is in
92: `references/exploration.md`.
93: 
94: ## Step 3 — Synthesize, then find the next gaps
95: 
96: Merge the round's memos into `ARCHITECTURE-SUMMARY.md`: update the section per
97: subsystem, attach each citation to the question it answers, and flip those
98: questions to `answered: true`. Then do the load-bearing step — **read the
99: summary as a whole and ask what you still can't explain.** A subsystem memo that
100: answers "how does X work" usually exposes "...but what calls X, and where does
101: its data come from?" Those become new default-FAIL entries. This is the
102: loop-until-dry engine: the loop runs until a round both answers nothing new and
103: surfaces no new gaps.
104: 
105: ## How this differs from its neighbors
106: 
107: This loop's whole reason to exist is the **convergence on YOUR questions** — it
108: is the lightweight, iterative member of a family of heavier one-shot tools, and
109: it *delegates* to them rather than re-implementing them.
110: 
111: - **vs [`repo-deep-dive`]** — that is a **one-shot, heavyweight deliverable**: a
112:   comprehensive 12-14 document reference series for a whole repo, grounded in a
113:   pre-run deep-research markdown. This loop is the **lighter, iterative,
114:   seed-question-driven** path that stops the moment *your* questions are answered
115:   — it may never document a subsystem you didn't ask about. When the user wants
116:   the full series, **hand off to `repo-deep-dive`** (the boundary and handoff
117:   trigger are in `references/exploration.md`); don't grow into it.
118: - **vs [`project-profiler`]** — that produces a fixed one-shot artifact
119:   (`CLAUDE.md` + `profile.yaml`) on a standard template. This loop's deliverable
120:   is the **question-answering summary plus the loop discipline**. It can *feed*
121:   project-profiler — invoke it to refresh `CLAUDE.md` from what the loop learned
122:   — but profiling is not its proof.
123: - **vs `deep-research`** — same fan-out → verify → synthesize *shape*, but
124:   deep-research goes to the **web with citations to sources**; this goes to the
125:   **local code with citations to `path:line`**, read-only, no network.
126: 
127: If after those boundaries this still reads like "deep-research on local code,"
128: that is exactly the point: the genuine contribution is the **loop-until-the-seed-
129: questions-are-answered convergence** and the **read-only fan-out discipline** —
130: not a new documentation format.
131: 
132: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
133: 
134: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
135: this loop sets:
136: 
137: - **Round / iteration cap** — default ~6-8 rounds (read from
138:   `.claude/profile.yaml` if set). Hitting it is a *stop-and-report what's still
139:   unanswered*, not a license to mark questions `true` without a citation.
140: - **No-progress detection** — if a round answers no new question **and** surfaces
141:   no new gap, the loop is **dry** — stop. If the **same** gap survives **2
142:   consecutive rounds** with no new citation, surface it to the human; some
143:   questions need the code's author, not a third fan-out.
144: - **Budget cap** — a fan-out of subagents per round multiplies token spend; per
145:   `loop-controller` enforce a ceiling that *terminates* the loop, and keep the
146:   per-round width capped (Step 2).
147: - **Read-only is the boundary.** Mapper subagents get no Write/Edit and no web —
148:   full stop. This loop *studies* code; it does not modify it. Refreshing
149:   `CLAUDE.md` is a separate, explicit `project-profiler` invocation, not
150:   something a mapper does mid-round. There is no irreversible action inside the
151:   loop, so it is AFK-safe within that read-only boundary.
152: - **Never cheat the proof.** Don't flip a question to `answered: true` without a
153:   real code citation, and don't drop a hard question off the list to empty it. A
154:   summary that "answers" by hand-waving is a *finding*, not understanding.
155: 
156: ## Choosing the driver primitive
157: 
158: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, this is a **dynamic-workflow / fan-out** loop, not
159: a `/goal` finish-line race — each round *is* a parallel dispatch:
160: 
161: - **Default — dynamic workflow / fan-out** (the `/deep-research` engine over
162:   local code): each round fans read-only mappers across subsystems, then
163:   synthesizes. Compose with the [`orchestrator`]'s Workflow mode when you want
164:   the fan-out managed as a workflow with an explicit token budget.
165: - **`/goal`** can drive the *outer* convergence — condition: "`seed-questions.json`
166:   has every entry `answered: true` with a citation, or stop after N rounds" — with
167:   each turn doing one fan-out-and-synthesize round. Remember `/goal` has no native
168:   budget; embed the round cap.
169: 
170: ## Reference files
171: 
172: - `references/exploration.md` — the `seed-questions.json` schema and default-FAIL
173:   / gap-discovery rule, the read-only fan-out dispatch pattern (and how it differs
174:   from `/deep-research`), and the hand-off-to-`repo-deep-dive` boundary.
175: 
176: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
177: [`repo-deep-dive`]: ../../workflows/repo-deep-dive/SKILL.md
178: [`project-profiler`]: ../../workflows/project-profiler/SKILL.md
179: [`orchestrator`]: ../../orchestrator/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/contract-conformance-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: contract-conformance-loop
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: >-
5:   Drive an implementation until EVERY criterion of an authored contract holds —
6:   a Plan-Generate-Evaluate (PGE / GAN-style) loop where the builder implements
7:   toward one not-yet-passing criterion and a FRESH-CONTEXT evaluator subagent
8:   (contract-auditor, spawned with no Write/Edit tools, default-FAIL) grades all
9:   criteria against evidence and never lets the builder self-grade. This is the
10:   loop form of the contract-author / contract-auditor pair. Use when a contract
11:   or acceptance-criteria spec exists and an implementation must be built or
12:   verified against it. Trigger on "build until the contract passes", "implement
13:   the spec until all criteria hold", "conform to the contract", "drive this to
14:   spec", "make the implementation match the contract", "loop until acceptance
15:   criteria pass", "build-until-spec", "PGE loop", "evaluate against the
16:   contract", "/contract-conformance-loop". A configuration of loop-controller.
17: requires_claude_code: true
18: min_plan: starter
19: disable-model-invocation: true
20: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob", "Agent"]
21: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "contract-author", "contract-auditor", "orchestrator", "fix-until-green"]
22: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
23: ---
24: 
25: # contract-conformance-loop
26: 
27: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
28: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
29: > externalization. This skill supplies the two things specific to
30: > "build until the spec holds": a **fresh-context evaluator** as the proof
31: > (the [`contract-auditor`] graded against an authored contract, default-FAIL)
32: > and the **generator/evaluator split** that keeps the builder from grading its
33: > own work. Read `loop-controller` for the guardrails; they're inherited, not
34: > repeated here.
35: >
36: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop edits and commits code on its
37: > own and spends tokens spawning an evaluator subagent each round, until every
38: > contract criterion passes. You want to *type* `/contract-conformance-loop` (or
39: > have the orchestrator dispatch it) — not have Claude silently start an
40: > autonomous build loop because a contract happened to exist.
41: 
42: ## The 5-part contract
43: 
44: | Part | This loop |
45: |---|---|
46: | **trigger** | an authored contract exists ([`contract-author`] output in `contracts/`) plus an implementation to build or verify against it, or an explicit `/contract-conformance-loop` (optionally scoped to one contract) |
47: | **action** | ONE iteration: the **generator** (the builder / role agent) implements or fixes toward **one** not-yet-passing criterion → a **fresh-context evaluator subagent** ([`contract-auditor`], spawned with **no Write/Edit**) checks **ALL** criteria against evidence and returns a default-FAIL verdict with per-criterion pass/fail + feedback → failing criteria + feedback feed the next iteration |
48: | **proof** | every entry in the criteria JSON has passing **evidence confirmed by the fresh-context evaluator** (not the builder) — default-FAIL: each criterion starts `"passed": false` and only the evaluator's verdict, citing evidence, flips it |
49: | **memory** | `criteria.json` (the default-FAIL criteria, one entry per contract criterion), `PROGRESS.md` (what's done / next), a git checkpoint per criterion turned green |
50: | **stop** | the evaluator confirms **all** criteria pass **OR** iteration cap **OR** no-progress for 3 rounds **OR** budget cap |
51: 
52: ## The proof: a fresh evaluator's verdict, default-FAIL
53: 
54: "Done" is **not** "the builder thinks the feature is built." It is **every
55: criterion in `criteria.json` flipped to `"passed": true` by the fresh-context
56: evaluator, each citing concrete evidence** — and re-confirmed against the *whole*
57: criteria set on the final round, not just the criterion last touched.
58: 
59: Two rules make this convergent rather than a rubber-stamp:
60: 
61: - **The grader is not the doer.** The builder is a pathological optimist about
62:   its own work; a same-context critic approves mediocre output. The evaluator is
63:   a *fresh* subagent that sees only the artifact + the contract, never the build
64:   reasoning. This is the PGE / GAN pattern from `loop-controller` Step 2.
65: - **The evaluator cannot edit.** It is spawned with **no Write/Edit tools** — so
66:   it can only *report* a failure, never "fix" one by quietly lowering the bar. It
67:   inspects (Read / Grep / Bash-to-run-tests) and returns a verdict.
68: 
69: This maps onto the existing pair: [`contract-author`] writes the spec the loop
70: builds against; [`contract-auditor`] **is** the evaluator. This loop does not
71: re-implement either — it sequences them in a bounded, default-FAIL loop. The full
72: generator/evaluator split, the `criteria.json` schema, and the evaluator dispatch
73: prompt are in [`references/pge-loop.md`](references/pge-loop.md).
74: 
75: ## Step 1 — Build the default-FAIL criteria JSON
76: 
77: Read the authored contract from `contracts/` (the [`contract-author`] output:
78: `openapi.yaml`, `data-layer.yaml`, `types.<ext>`, and the Domain Rules in
79: `contracts/README.md`). Decompose it into **one testable criterion per checkable
80: fact** and write each as a JSON entry with `"passed": false`. Store it at the
81: profile-defined path (default `criteria.json`). A criterion the evaluator cannot
82: check against evidence is not a criterion — split it until it is. Schema and a
83: worked example: [`references/pge-loop.md`](references/pge-loop.md).
84: 
85: ## Step 2 — Generator: implement toward ONE failing criterion
86: 
87: Pick the highest-leverage `"passed": false` criterion and have the builder
88: implement or fix **just that one**. One criterion per iteration — don't batch
89: features; batching destroys the signal about which change moved which criterion
90: (`loop-controller` Step 5). Update `PROGRESS.md` with what was attempted.
91: 
92: ## Step 3 — Evaluator: grade ALL criteria from fresh context
93: 
94: Spawn the evaluator subagent ([`contract-auditor`]) **fresh, with no Write/Edit
95: tools**, blind to the builder's reasoning. Hand it only the contract + the
96: implementation + `criteria.json`. It re-checks **every** criterion against
97: evidence (re-verify the whole, not just the one touched — `loop-controller`
98: Step 5) and returns the criteria JSON with each entry either still `false` (plus
99: feedback) or flipped to `true` (plus the evidence that flipped it). The builder
100: never edits `criteria.json`; only the evaluator's verdict does.
101: 
102: ## Step 4 — Feed failures back, checkpoint, repeat
103: 
104: Merge the evaluator's verdict into `criteria.json`. On a green-ward round (a
105: criterion newly `true`, nothing regressed), **commit a checkpoint** naming the
106: criterion satisfied — the git trail is the loop's undo and post-mortem. Feed the
107: still-failing criteria + their feedback into the next generator pass. When the
108: evaluator confirms **all** criteria pass on a single whole-set re-check, the loop
109: is done; report the final `criteria.json` as evidence.
110: 
111: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
112: 
113: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The
114: caps this loop sets:
115: 
116: - **Iteration cap** — default ~10–15 build/evaluate rounds (read from
117:   `.claude/profile.yaml` if set). Hitting the cap is a *stop-and-escalate*, not a
