118:   license to weaken the contract or have the builder self-pass a criterion.
119: - **No-progress detection** — if **the same set of criteria stays `false`** for
120:   **3 consecutive rounds**, stop and escalate. Three rounds with the criterion
121:   count unchanged means the approach is wrong, or the criterion is ambiguous —
122:   surface it (it may be a contract gap, which [`contract-auditor`] flags rather
123:   than failing the implementation against).
124: - **Budget cap** — the evaluator subagent costs a full fresh context **per
125:   round**; a long run is materially more expensive than a self-graded loop.
126:   Watch `/cost`; terminate at the ceiling, don't just warn.
127: - **Never let the builder grade itself.** Forbidden, and each is a *finding*:
128:   the builder flipping a `criteria.json` entry to `true`, softening a criterion,
129:   or the evaluator being reused with the builder's context (it must be fresh and
130:   Write/Edit-free). A green that the builder wrote is not proof — only the
131:   evaluator's verdict is.
132: - **HITL before irreversible.** If satisfying a criterion would touch something
133:   irreversible (a migration against a real DB, an external API), that's an HITL
134:   checkpoint — pause for the human (`loop-controller` guardrail 4).
135: 
136: ## Choosing the driver primitive
137: 
138: The canonical form is **a skill orchestrating a generator + an evaluator
139: subagent** — the evaluator subagent *is* the proof, so this loop always spawns it
140: (hence `Agent` in `allowed-tools`). Per `loop-controller` Step 1:
141: 
142: - **Canonical — generator + fresh-context evaluator subagent.** Use whenever any
143:   criterion is subjective or needs cross-file judgment (does the implementation
144:   *import* the shared types, do the domain rules actually hold). The evaluator's
145:   default-FAIL verdict is the stop signal.
146: - **`/goal` may substitute** *only* when **every** criterion is fully provable
147:   from command output (e.g. a contract reduced entirely to a test suite +
148:   schema-validation exit codes). Then `/goal "every criterion in criteria.json is
149:   passed:true, confirmed by the contract's own checks — or stop after N turns"`,
150:   and the loop degenerates toward [`fix-until-green`]. The moment a criterion
151:   needs judgment, fall back to the fresh evaluator.
152: 
153: ## Using it under the orchestrator
154: 
155: This is the **build-until-spec inner loop** (archetype 2). The orchestrator
156: authors the contract via [`contract-author`] (its Phase 4), then dispatches this
157: loop with the builder role as generator and [`contract-auditor`] as the
158: evaluator. The orchestrator does **not** override a stuck loop — if the loop
159: escalates after no-progress, that's a real conformance blocker or a contract gap,
160: not a number to paper over. A satisfied `criteria.json` informs the build; the
161: `qe-agent`'s `qa-report.json` still decides the gate (`loop-controller`'s rule:
162: the loop informs, the gate decides).
163: 
164: ## How this differs from its neighbors
165: 
166: - **vs. [`contract-auditor`]** — the auditor is a *one-shot* static audit that
167:   reports mismatches. This is the bounded *iterative* loop that drives them to
168:   zero, wrapping the auditor as its default-FAIL evaluator harness.
169: - **vs. [`fix-until-green`]** — that loop's proof is three exit codes; this
170:   loop's proof is per-criterion contract evidence graded by a *fresh evaluator*.
171:   Use fix-until-green when "done" is the test suite; use this when "done" is "the
172:   contract's criteria all hold," some of which no single exit code can prove.
173: 
174: ## Reference files
175: 
176: - [`references/pge-loop.md`](references/pge-loop.md) — the generator/evaluator
177:   split in full, the `criteria.json` default-FAIL schema with a concrete JSON
178:   example, and the fresh-context evaluator dispatch (no Write/Edit, blind to the
179:   build reasoning, returns a per-criterion verdict).
180: 
181: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
182: [`fix-until-green`]: ../fix-until-green/SKILL.md
183: [`contract-author`]: ../../contracts/contract-author/SKILL.md
184: [`contract-auditor`]: ../../contracts/contract-auditor/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/coverage-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: coverage-loop
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: >-
5:   Grow a test suite until a coverage target is met: run coverage, find the
6:   lowest-covered meaningful unit, add REAL behavior-checking tests for it, then
7:   re-measure the whole report — looping until total (and per-target) coverage is
8:   at or above the configured target AND the full suite is green. Reads the target
9:   from .claude/profile.yaml (no hard-coded 80 or 100). Forbids gaming the number
10:   with assertion-free tests or excluding files from the coverage config — both are
11:   findings. Use when coverage is below target, when a build wave needs more tests,
12:   or as the QE coverage inner loop under an orchestrated build. Trigger on:
13:   "raise coverage", "hit the coverage target", "add tests until coverage", "get
14:   to N percent coverage", "coverage is too low", "loop until coverage passes",
15:   "grow the test suite", "cover the untested code", "/coverage-loop". A
16:   configuration of loop-controller.
17: requires_claude_code: true
18: min_plan: starter
19: disable-model-invocation: true
20: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
21: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "fix-until-green", "qe-agent", "orchestrator"]
22: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
23: ---
24: 
25: # coverage-loop
26: 
27: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
28: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
29: > externalization. This skill supplies the two things specific to "grow the suite
30: > to a coverage number": a **mechanical proof** (the coverage report meets the
31: > configured target with a green suite) and the **anti-gaming discipline** that
32: > keeps the number honest. Read `loop-controller` for the guardrails; they're
33: > inherited, not repeated here.
34: >
35: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop writes test files and commits on
36: > its own and spends tokens re-running the full coverage suite each round, until
37: > the target is hit. You want to *type* `/coverage-loop` (or have the
38: > orchestrator dispatch it) — not have Claude start an autonomous test-writing
39: > loop because some file looked under-tested.
40: 
41: ## The 5-part contract
42: 
43: | Part | This loop |
44: |---|---|
45: | **trigger** | coverage below the configured target, a build wave needing more tests, or an explicit `/coverage-loop` (optionally scoped to a path or per-target) |
46: | **action** | ONE iteration: run coverage → find the **lowest-covered meaningful unit** → add **real, behavior-checking** tests for it → re-run coverage over the **whole** report (and the full suite) |
47: | **proof** | the coverage report shows total **AND** every configured per-target threshold **>=** the target **AND** the full suite exits 0 — default-FAIL: assume **below target** until the freshly-generated report proves otherwise, read from the named artifact (`coverage.xml` / `lcov.info` / the `N% covered` summary line), not from memory of a prior run |
48: | **memory** | `coverage_plan.md` (the live TODO of under-covered units + a per-round `unit → tests added → new %` log), plus a git checkpoint per coverage-raising round |
49: | **stop** | report meets target **with the suite green** **OR** iteration cap **OR** no-progress for 3 rounds **OR** budget cap |
50: 
51: ## The proof: a fresh coverage report at-or-above target, default-FAIL
52: 
53: "Done" is **not** "I added some tests" and **not** "the file I was looking at is
54: covered now." It is a **freshly-generated coverage report whose total (and every
55: configured per-target) percentage is at or above the target, with the full suite
56: green in the same run**. Assume **below target** until that report exists —
57: that's the default-FAIL stance. A loop that trusts a stale percentage, or that
58: raises one file while the global number slips, has not met the proof.
59: 
60: **Read the target from `.claude/profile.yaml`** (e.g. `coverage.target`,
61: `coverage.per_target`), falling back to the project's own coverage config
62: (`.coveragerc`, `jest.config` `coverageThreshold`, `pytest` `--cov-fail-under`,
63: `tarpaulin.toml`, …). **Never hard-code 80 or 100** — the target is the
64: project's, not this skill's. The per-stack coverage commands, how to read each
65: report format, and the anti-gaming rules are in
66: [`references/coverage.md`](references/coverage.md).
67: 
68: ## Step 1 — Resolve the target, the command, and the report artifact
69: 
70: Before looping, pin three things for *this* project and record them in
71: `coverage_plan.md` so every iteration re-measures identically:
72: 
73: 1. **The target** — from `.claude/profile.yaml`, else the project's coverage
74:    config. Capture both the total target and any per-target thresholds. **If
75:    neither declares a target, stop and ask** — a coverage loop with no target
76:    cannot converge; never invent one.
77: 2. **The coverage command** — prefer the project's own named script (`npm run
78:    coverage`, `make coverage`, `pytest --cov`); stack defaults are in
79:    [`references/coverage.md`](references/coverage.md). Run what CI runs.
80: 3. **The report artifact** — the file/summary the proof reads (`coverage.xml`,
81:    `lcov.info`, `coverage/coverage-summary.json`, the terminal `TOTAL … N%`
82:    line). The proof is read from this artifact, never asserted.
83: 
84: ## Step 2 — Run coverage, find the lowest-covered meaningful unit
85: 
86: Generate a fresh report and parse it. Pick the **lowest-covered *meaningful*
87: unit** — a function, branch, or module that represents real untested behavior —
88: not whichever file is alphabetically first and not trivial generated/boilerplate
89: lines. One unit per iteration (`loop-controller` Step 5); chasing ten files at
90: once destroys the signal about which tests moved the number. Log the chosen unit
91: and its current % in `coverage_plan.md`.
92: 
93: ## Step 3 — Add REAL tests for that unit
94: 
95: Write tests that **exercise the unit and assert on its observable behavior** —
96: inputs mapped to expected outputs, error paths, edge cases, branch conditions.
97: A test that calls a function purely to execute its lines without asserting on the
98: result is not a test; it is coverage theater (see the anti-cheat rules below).
99: Where the existing suite is itself red, **delegate to [`fix-until-green`]** to
100: restore green before adding more — coverage of a broken suite is meaningless, and
101: that loop owns the three-exit-code proof so this one doesn't re-implement it.
102: 
103: ## Step 4 — Re-measure the WHOLE report, checkpoint
104: 
105: Re-run coverage over the **entire** report — not just the unit you touched — and
106: re-run the full suite. A round that raises one file but drops the global total,
107: or greens coverage while reding a test, has made things worse; only a whole-report
108: re-measure catches it (`loop-controller` Step 5, "restart the streak"). On a
109: coverage-raising round (total moved up, nothing regressed, suite green),
110: **commit a checkpoint** naming the unit covered — the git trail is the loop's undo
111: and post-mortem. When total and every per-target threshold are at or above target
112: with the suite green, the loop is done; report the final coverage report as
113: evidence.
114: 
115: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
116: 
117: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
118: this loop sets:
119: 
120: - **Iteration cap** — default ~15–25 add-tests rounds (read from
121:   `.claude/profile.yaml` if set). Hitting the cap is a *stop-and-escalate*, not a
122:   license to game the number. The last percent toward a 100% target is often the
123:   cap-hitter — surface "stuck at X% on these units" rather than faking it.
124: - **No-progress detection** — if the coverage total **does not increase** for
125:   **3 consecutive rounds**, stop and escalate. Three rounds of flat coverage
126:   means the remaining gap is structurally hard (dead code, untestable glue,
127:   environment-bound paths) — a human call, surfaced with what was tried.
128: - **Budget cap** — re-running the full coverage suite **every round** is
129:   materially more expensive than a single test run; watch `/cost` and terminate
130:   at the ceiling, don't just warn.
131: - **Never game the coverage number.** Forbidden, and each is a *finding* if you
132:   catch it (mirrors [`fix-until-green`]'s never-cheat rule, points back to
133:   `loop-controller` guardrail 6): writing **assertion-free "tests"** that execute
134:   lines without checking behavior; **excluding files / adding ignore pragmas**
135:   (`.coveragerc` `omit`, `/* istanbul ignore */`, `# pragma: no cover`,
136:   `coveragePathIgnorePatterns`) to make the denominator shrink instead of covering
137:   the code; **lowering the configured threshold** to meet it; or deleting/skipping
138:   hard-to-cover tests. When the percentage jumps, read the diff that did it — a
139:   jump from new excludes, not new assertions, is the cheat.
140: - **AFK-safe within the reversible boundary.** Writing test files + running
141:   coverage is reversible and has a hard verifier — fine unattended. A test that
142:   would touch something irreversible (a real DB, an external API) is an HITL
143:   checkpoint — pause for the human (`loop-controller` guardrail 4).
144: 
145: ## Choosing the driver primitive
146: 
147: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, the coverage % is provable from command output, so
148: the default is **`/goal`**:
149: 
150: - **Default — `/goal`:** `/goal "the coverage report shows total and every
151:   per-target threshold at or above the project's configured target, and the full
152:   suite exits 0, with no file excluded and no assertion-free test added — or stop
153:   after N rounds."` The Haiku evaluator reads the coverage summary you surface
154:   each turn; remember `/goal` has no native budget, so the turn cap and `/cost`
155:   are the backstops.
156: - **Stop-hook gate** when you want a coverage floor to ship *with* the build and
157:   block exit deterministically (an orchestrator wave gate that fails the wave if
158:   coverage regresses below target). The gate script runs the coverage command and
159:   parses the artifact; wire it per `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`
160:   (`stop_hook_active` guard included).
161: 
162: ## Using it under the orchestrator
163: 
164: This is the **QE coverage inner loop** and a natural **wave gate**. The
165: orchestrator dispatches it after a build wave to raise coverage on the wave's new
166: code, or wires the Stop-hook as a coverage floor. Under-covered code routes back
167: to the owning agent **by file** (`loop-controller`'s by-file routing): a thin
168: coverage report in `src/api/` is the backend agent's gap, not a generic "add
169: tests." The orchestrator does **not** override a stuck loop — if coverage-loop
170: escalates after flat coverage, that's a real testability blocker, not a number to
171: paper over. As always, the loop informs; the [`qe-agent`]'s `qa-report.json`
172: still decides the gate.
173: 
174: ## How this differs from its neighbors
175: 
176: - **vs. [`fix-until-green`]** — that loop drives an **existing** suite to GREEN
177:   (three exit codes, a binary stop). This loop **grows** the suite to a coverage
178:   NUMBER. They compose: coverage-loop calls fix-until-green to keep the suite
179:   green while it adds tests, then measures the number fix-until-green can't see.
180: - **vs. the [`qe-agent`]** — the role agent owns the QA gate and `qa-report.json`
181:   one-shot. This is the bounded *iterative* loop that drives coverage up to the
182:   target before that gate is evaluated.
183: 
184: ## Reference files
185: 
186: - [`references/coverage.md`](references/coverage.md) — per-stack coverage
187:   commands (node/jest+vitest+c8, python/coverage.py+pytest-cov, go, rust/tarpaulin,
188:   ruby/simplecov, java/jacoco, …), how to read each report format and per-target
189:   threshold, and the full anti-gaming rule set (assertion-free tests, exclude/omit
190:   config, threshold-lowering — and how to detect each from the diff).
191: 
192: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
193: [`fix-until-green`]: ../fix-until-green/SKILL.md
194: [`qe-agent`]: ../../roles/qe-agent/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/dependency-health-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: dependency-health-loop
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: >-
5:   Keep a project's dependencies healthy on a schedule: audit for known
6:   vulnerabilities and over-stale pins, apply ONE safe update (or a vuln fix) per
7:   pass, run the full gate to prove the update is non-breaking, and open or update
8:   a PR — looping on a sprint cadence with HITL on every major version bump and
9:   never auto-merging. Use when you want deps watched while you build, want
10:   security advisories acted on a cadence, want lockfiles kept fresh without
11:   breaking the build, or want a dependency audit run every 30 minutes during a
12:   sprint. Trigger on "audit my dependencies", "keep deps up to date", "dependency
13:   health", "check for vulnerable packages", "npm audit on a schedule", "pip-audit
14:   loop", "cargo audit loop", "update dependencies safely", "watch for CVEs",
15:   "keep the lockfile fresh", "dependabot-style loop", "bump deps and test". Routes
16:   major/breaking bumps to a human and never merges. A configuration of
17:   loop-controller.
18: requires_claude_code: true
19: min_plan: starter
20: disable-model-invocation: true
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
22: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "security-agent", "infrastructure-agent", "fix-until-green", "git-pr", "loop"]
23: spawned_by: []
24: ---
25: 
26: # dependency-health-loop
27: 
28: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
29: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
30: > externalization. This skill supplies the three things specific to "keep deps
31: > healthy": the **per-pass recipe** (audit → one safe bump → gate → PR), a
32: > **mechanical proof** that combines a security/freshness audit with a green gate
33: > after every update, and the **HITL boundary** that holds majors for a human.
34: > Read `loop-controller` for the guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated
35: > here.
36: >
37: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop edits the lockfile, commits, and
38: > pushes branches on its own, on a clock. It is user-driven — you want to *type*
39: > `/loop 30m /dependency-health-loop` (or `/dependency-health-loop`), not have
40: > Claude silently start bumping packages because an advisory landed.
41: 
42: ## The 5-part contract
43: 
44: | Part | This loop |
45: |---|---|
46: | **trigger** | a sprint in flight (deps drifting / new advisories), scheduled via `/loop 30m /dependency-health-loop`, or an explicit `/dependency-health-loop` |
47: | **action** | ONE pass: run the ecosystem audit → pick **one** safe update (a security fix first, else one over-stale patch/minor) → apply it → run the **whole** [`fix-until-green`] gate → open or update a PR. One bump per pass; re-audit the whole tree after |
48: | **proof** | **no known vulnerabilities AND no pinned dep stale beyond policy AND the gate exits green** — all three observed in the same pass, from the audit report + gate exit codes. Default-FAIL: assume **unhealthy** until the audit proves otherwise |
49: | **memory** | `dep-health.md` (audit snapshot, the bump→result log, deferred majors + why), the PR branch, the lockfile diff, and git history — durable across the session-scoped `/loop` fires |
50: | **stop** | proof holds (clean audit + in-policy freshness + green gate) **OR** poll/iteration cap **OR** no-progress for 3 passes (same advisory unfixable within the reversible boundary) **OR** budget cap **OR** an HITL checkpoint is hit (a major bump, or a lockfile-semantics change) |
51: 
52: ## The proof: clean audit + in-policy freshness + green gate, default-FAIL
53: 
54: "Healthy" is not "the last bump installed." It is **three conditions read
55: together in one pass**: the ecosystem audit reports **zero known
56: vulnerabilities** at or above the policy severity, **no pinned dependency is
57: stale beyond the freshness policy**, and the full gate (`fix-until-green`'s three
58: exit codes) is **green after the update**. Assume the tree is **unhealthy** until
59: a fresh audit proves all three — that is the default-FAIL stance, and it is why
60: every pass *re-audits* rather than trusting the last verdict. A loop that stops
61: after one bump ships a tree that picked up a new CVE and a red typecheck.
62: 
63: This is the only loop whose proof fuses a **security/freshness audit** with a
64: **green gate after each bump** — neither half alone is the proof. Name the
65: artifacts: the audit report (`npm audit --json` / `pip-audit` / `cargo audit` /
66: `govulncheck`) plus the gate's exit codes. Both must read clean *in the same
67: pass*. Per-ecosystem audit + freshness commands are in
68: `references/ecosystems.md`.
69: 
70: ## Step 1 — Audit, pick exactly one update
71: 
72: Run the ecosystem audit and the freshness check (the precise commands per stack
73: are in `references/ecosystems.md`). Then pick **one** target, in priority order:
74: 
75: 1. **A security fix** for the highest-severity known vulnerability (a patch/minor
76:    that resolves an advisory).
77: 2. Else **one over-stale dependency** past the freshness policy — smallest safe
78:    bump first (patch before minor).
79: 
80: One bump per pass. Batching destroys the signal about which change broke (or
81: fixed) the gate, and it makes the PR un-reviewable. If the only available fix for
82: an advisory is a **major** bump, that is an HITL checkpoint — do not apply it;
83: record it as a deferred major and surface it.
84: 
85: ## Step 2 — Apply, then verify with the whole gate
86: 
87: Apply the single bump, regenerate the lockfile, and re-run the **entire**
88: [`fix-until-green`] gate (test + lint + typecheck) from scratch — not just the
89: package's own tests. A bump that installs cleanly but reds typecheck has made the
90: tree worse, and only a full re-run catches it. **Do not re-implement the gate** —
91: invoke `fix-until-green`; this loop adds only the dependency discipline around it.
92: If the gate goes red and the fix is a mechanical adaptation to the new API,
93: `fix-until-green` resolves it; if it can't be made green within the bump, **roll
94: the bump back** (the lockfile diff is the undo) and record it as needing a human.
95: 
96: ## Step 3 — Open or update the PR, re-audit
97: 
98: On a green bump, commit a checkpoint naming the package and version, then open or
99: update a single dependency-health PR via [`git-pr`]. Then **re-audit the whole
100: tree** — the proof is about the whole, re-checked after every change. When the
101: audit is clean, freshness is in policy, and the gate is green together, the
102: loop's proof holds; report the audit report + gate output as evidence. The loop
103: prepares the PR for a human to merge — it never merges.
104: 
105: ## HITL is load-bearing for this loop
106: 
107: This loop runs **only inside the reversible boundary** unattended. The
108: irreversible or breaking actions are **HITL checkpoints — never autonomous**:
109: 
110: - **A major version bump** — pause, always. A breaking major is never merged
111:   unattended; record it as a deferred major and surface it for a human, with the
112:   changelog/migration link.
113: - **Anything that changes lockfile semantics** beyond a clean add/bump — a
114:   resolution override, a peer-dependency force, a registry/source swap, removing
115:   a transitive pin — pause. These change *what resolves*, not just a version.
116: - **Merging** — never. Reaching the proof means *ready for the human to merge*,
117:   not "merge it." This loop does not auto-merge, full stop.
118: 
119: Within the boundary — a patch/minor security update or in-policy freshness bump
120: with a green gate, on its own branch/PR — it is AFK-safe. The full AFK-safe vs
121: pause table is in `references/ecosystems.md`.
122: 
123: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
124: 
125: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
126: this loop sets:
127: 
128: - **Poll / iteration cap** — `/loop`'s ~3-day session expiry is the outer bound;
129:   set an inner per-run pass cap (default ~20) so a tree full of advisories
130:   doesn't burn the window. Hitting it is a *stop-and-escalate*, not a license to
131:   loosen the proof.
132: - **No-progress detection** — if the **same advisory** survives **3 consecutive
133:   passes** (only fixable by a major, or the bump can't be made green), stop and
134:   surface it. Three passes on one advisory means it needs a human decision, not a
135:   fourth attempt.
136: - **Budget cap** — a watch loop firing every 30 minutes adds up; enforce a
137:   token/cost ceiling that *terminates* the loop (read from `.claude/profile.yaml`
138:   when present), not just warns.
139: - **Never cheat the proof.** Forbidden, each a *finding*: pinning around an
140:   advisory with an audit-ignore/allowlist entry instead of fixing it, downgrading
141:   the policy severity to clear the count, suppressing a deprecation rather than
142:   resolving it, or merging to make the audit moot. A clean audit that came from
143:   silencing the auditor is not health.
144: 
145: ## How this differs from its neighbors
146: 
147: This loop is **not** a one-shot dependency update, and it is not a re-skin of an
148: existing skill — it draws three explicit boundaries:
149: 
150: - **vs a one-shot `npm update` / dependabot bump:** those apply versions; they do
151:   not *prove* health. This loop's substance is the **loop discipline** — a
152:   default-FAIL proof that fuses audit + freshness + a green gate, one bump per
153:   pass with full re-verification, no-progress detection, scheduling, and an HITL
154:   gate on majors. The bump is the easy part; the proof and the boundary are the
155:   product.
156: - **vs [`security-agent`]:** the security role authors the **policy** — which
157:   severities block, which advisories are accepted, the SBOM/audit expectations.
158:   This loop **consumes** that policy as its proof threshold and *acts on a
159:   cadence*; it does not define security posture. On a vuln it can't resolve
160:   within the reversible boundary, it escalates to the human (and, under an
161:   orchestrated build, to the security role).
162: - **vs [`infrastructure-agent`]:** infra owns the dependency manifests and
163:   lockfiles as files. This loop proposes single, gated bumps **as PRs** for the
164:   owner to merge — it never force-merges into infra-owned files, and majors route
165:   to a human exactly because they may change runtime/build semantics infra owns.
166: - **vs [`fix-until-green`]:** that loop's proof is three exit codes. This loop's
167:   proof *contains* a green gate but is strictly larger (audit + freshness), and
168:   it **invokes** `fix-until-green` to verify each bump rather than re-implementing
169:   the gate.
170: 
171: ## Choosing the driver primitive
172: 
173: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, this is a **watch/poll** job — you wait for the
174: dependency tree and advisory feed to *change* on a cadence — so the primitive is
175: **`/loop`** (sprint cadence) or **`/schedule`** (a longer, e.g. nightly,
176: cadence), **not** `/goal` (which pushes to a finish line). The daily-driver
177: recipe:
178: 
179: ```
180: /loop 30m /dependency-health-loop
181: ```
182: 
183: The *per-pass* exit (this pass made one clean, reversible, gated bump and
184: re-audited) is provable from the audit report + gate exit codes, so a pass can
185: run unattended within the HITL boundary above. `/loop`'s session-scope, ~3-day
186: expiry, and no-catch-up mechanics live in `loop-controller`'s
187: `references/primitives.md`; don't re-document them.
188: 
189: ## Reference files
190: 
191: - `references/ecosystems.md` — per-ecosystem audit + freshness + update commands
192:   (npm/pnpm/yarn, pip/poetry/uv, cargo, go, bundler, composer), the
193:   major-vs-minor HITL policy, the lockfile-safety rules (what's a clean bump vs a
194:   semantics change), and the AFK-safe vs pause table.
195: 
196: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
197: [`fix-until-green`]: ../fix-until-green/SKILL.md
198: [`security-agent`]: ../../roles/security-agent/SKILL.md
199: [`infrastructure-agent`]: ../../roles/infrastructure-agent/SKILL.md
200: [`git-pr`]: ../../git/git-pr/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/fix-until-green/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: fix-until-green
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: >-
5:   Drive a failing build to passing in a disciplined loop: run the project's full
6:   gate (tests + lint + typecheck), fix one root cause, re-run the whole gate, and
7:   repeat until all three exit clean — with a hard iteration cap, no-progress
8:   detection, and a rule against cheating the gate green. Use when tests/CI are
9:   red and you want them green, when a build wave fails its checks, or as the QE
10:   inner loop under an orchestrated build. Trigger on: "fix until green", "make
11:   the tests pass", "get CI green", "fix the failing tests", "make it green",
12:   "drive the build to passing", "red to green", "loop until tests pass", "fix
13:   lint and type errors", "keep fixing until the suite is clean", "make the wave
14:   gate pass". A configuration of loop-controller — it supplies the harness; this
15:   skill supplies the proof (three exit codes) and the fix discipline.
16: requires_claude_code: true
17: min_plan: starter
18: disable-model-invocation: true
19: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
20: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "orchestrator-task-loop", "qe-agent", "diagnose-loop", "git-commit", "orchestrator"]
21: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
22: ---
23: 
24: # fix-until-green
25: 
26: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
27: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
28: > externalization. This skill supplies the two things specific to "get the build
29: > green": a **mechanical proof** (three exit codes) and the **fix discipline**
30: > that keeps the loop from cheating its way there. Read `loop-controller` for the
31: > guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated here.
32: >
33: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop edits and commits code on its own
34: > until a gate passes. You want to *type* `/fix-until-green` (or have the
35: > orchestrator dispatch it) — not have Claude start an autonomous edit loop
36: > because a test happened to fail.
37: 
38: ## The 5-part contract
39: 
40: | Part | This loop |
41: |---|---|
42: | **trigger** | red suite / CI, a failed build wave, or an explicit `/fix-until-green` (optionally scoped to a path) |
43: | **action** | run the whole gate → read the highest-signal failure → fix **one root cause** → re-run the **whole** gate |
44: | **proof** | `test` exits 0 **AND** `lint` exits 0 **AND** `typecheck` exits 0 — default-FAIL (assume red until all three are observed clean **in the same run**) |
45: | **memory** | `fix_plan.md` (live TODO of remaining failures + the failure→fix log) once the loop runs past one iteration or has to escalate — a single trivial mechanical fix doesn't need the ceremony; plus a git checkpoint per green-ward step |
46: | **stop** | all three clean **OR** iteration cap **OR** no-progress for 3 rounds **OR** budget cap |
47: 
48: ## The proof: three exit codes, default-FAIL
49: 
50: "Green" is not "the test I was looking at passes." It is **all three of test,
51: lint, and typecheck exiting 0 in the same run**, with no test removed or weakened
52: to get there. Assume **red** until you have observed all three clean together —
53: that's the default-FAIL stance. A loop that calls it done after fixing the one
54: visible failure ships the other two broken.
55: 
56: Some stacks fold lint/typecheck into the test command or omit one (a dynamically
57: typed project may have no typecheck step). Detect what actually exists (Step 1);
58: a step that genuinely doesn't exist passes vacuously, but **don't assume** —
59: absence must be confirmed, not guessed.
60: 
61: ## Step 1 — Detect the gate commands
62: 
63: Find the three commands for *this* project before looping. Precedence:
64: 
65: 1. **`.claude/profile.yaml`** if present — it may declare `test` / `lint` /
66:    `typecheck` explicitly. Use those verbatim.
67: 2. **Declared scripts** — `package.json` `scripts`, `Makefile` targets,
68:    `pyproject.toml` / `tox.ini`, `Cargo.toml`, `go.mod`, `composer.json`. Prefer
69:    the project's own named script (`npm run test`, `make check`) over a raw tool
70:    invocation, so you run what CI runs.
71: 3. **Stack default** — only if nothing is declared. The per-stack table (node,
72:    python, rust, go, ruby, java, …) is in `references/gate-commands.md`.
73: 
74: Record the three resolved commands in `fix_plan.md` so every iteration runs the
75: identical gate. Running a *different* command than CI is the most common way a
76: "green" loop produces a still-red PR.
77: 
78: ## Step 2 — Run the whole gate, read the real failure
79: 
80: Run all three commands, capture exit codes and output. Then **pick one
81: root-cause failure to fix** — not the first line of the stack trace, the *cause*.
82: Twenty failures with one shared cause is one fix, not twenty. Don't shotgun
83: edits across many failures in a single iteration; that destroys the signal about
84: what actually moved the gate.
85: 
86: ## Step 3 — Fix one root cause
87: 
88: - **Mechanical failures** (a clear type error, a missing import, an off-by-one
89:   the assertion points straight at) — fix directly.
90: - **Hard failures** (flaky tests, "passes locally fails in CI," a performance
91:   regression, intermittent races, "I can't reproduce it") — **invoke
92:   [`diagnose-loop`]** rather than guessing. Its Phase-1 discipline (build a fast,
93:   deterministic, binary-signal reproduction first) is exactly what a fix loop
94:   needs and exactly what gets skipped under pressure to go green.
95: - **Change one variable per iteration** so the re-run tells you whether *that*
96:   fix worked. This is what makes the loop a binary search instead of guessing.
97: 
98: ## Step 4 — Re-verify the whole gate, checkpoint
99: 
100: Re-run **all three** commands from scratch — not just the one you touched. A fix
101: that greens the failing test but reds typecheck has made the gate worse, and only
102: a full re-run catches it. This is the "restart the streak" rule: the proof is
103: about the *whole*, re-checked after every change.
104: 
105: On a green-ward step (strictly fewer failures, nothing new broken), **commit a
106: checkpoint** with a message naming the root cause fixed — the git trail is the
107: loop's undo and its post-mortem. When all three exit 0 together, the loop is
108: done; report the final gate output as evidence.
109: 
110: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
111: 
112: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The
113: caps this loop sets:
114: 
115: - **Iteration cap** — default ~15–25 (read from `.claude/profile.yaml` if set).
116:   Hitting the cap is a *stop-and-escalate*, not a license to weaken the gate.
117: - **No-progress detection** — if the **same set of failures** survives **3
118:   consecutive iterations**, stop and escalate. The same failure three times means
119:   the approach is wrong, not that it needs a fourth try. Surface the stuck
120:   failure to the human with what was tried.
121: - **Never cheat the gate green.** Forbidden, and each is a *finding* if you catch
122:   it: deleting or `skip`-ping a failing test, weakening an assertion, adding an
123:   ignore directive (`// eslint-disable`, `# type: ignore`, `@ts-expect-error`) to
124:   silence rather than fix, or relocating a violation into the checker's blind
125:   spot. If a test is genuinely wrong, that's a human decision — surface it, don't
126:   unilaterally delete it. (Anthropic's harness, verbatim: *"It is unacceptable to
127:   remove or edit tests."*)
128: - **Green against a mock is green-on-a-proxy — not the same as done.** This one
129:   isn't cheating (you weakened nothing) — but `test+lint+typecheck` exiting 0
130:   against a stubbed DB, a fake API, or a mock provider proves the *mocked* build
131:   is internally consistent, not that the product works. That's a real result
132:   worth having (it compiles, the wiring type-checks), and a perfectly good gate
133:   for *this* loop. It is **not** "done" for anything whose value depends on the
134:   real path. When that's the situation, say so out loud — "three exit codes green
135:   **against the mock**; the real `<X>` path is unexercised" — and route the gap to
136:   `qe-agent` / `coverage-loop`; don't let a green badge imply the product runs.
137:   See [`loop-controller`] Step 2, *measure the goal, not a stand-in*.
138: - **AFK-safe only within the reversible boundary.** Editing source + running the
139:   gate is reversible and has a hard verifier — fine unattended. If a fix would
140:   touch something irreversible (a migration against a real DB, an external API,
141:   anything destructive), that's an HITL checkpoint — pause for the human.
142: 
143: ## Choosing the driver primitive
144: 
145: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, by how you're running it:
146: 
147: - **Default — `/goal`:** the proof is provable from command output, so
148:   `/goal "the test, lint, and typecheck commands for this project all exit 0 in
149:   the same run, with no test removed or weakened — or stop after N turns."` The
150:   Haiku evaluator reads the gate output you surface each turn. Remember the
151:   embedded turn cap — `/goal` has no native budget.
152: - **Stop-hook gate** when you want the check to ship *with* the build and block
153:   exit deterministically (the orchestrator wave gate, a per-skill gate other role
154:   skills inherit). The gate script + `stop_hook_active` guard are in
155:   `references/stop-hook.md`.
156: - **`claude -p` (Ralph)** for a long, greenfield, fresh-context-per-iteration run
157:   — sandboxed, with an external iteration/token counter.
158: 
159: ## Using it under the orchestrator
160: 
161: This is the **QE inner loop** and a natural **wave gate**. When the orchestrator
162: dispatches it (or wires the Stop-hook into a wave), failures route back to the
163: owning agent **by file**: a red typecheck in `src/api/` goes to the backend
164: agent, not a generic "fix it." The orchestrator does **not** override a stuck
165: loop — if fix-until-green escalates after no-progress, that's a real blocker for
166: the QA gate, not a number to paper over. The relationship to the QA gate is
167: exactly `loop-controller`'s rule: the loop informs, the `qe-agent`'s
168: `qa-report.json` still decides.
169: 
170: ## Reference files
171: 
172: - `references/gate-commands.md` — per-stack detection of the test / lint /
173:   typecheck commands (node, python, rust, go, ruby, java, php, …) and how to
174:   compose three exit codes into one pass/fail.
175: - `references/stop-hook.md` — the Stop-hook gate that ships with this skill: the
176:   script, the `stop_hook_active` guard, and how to wire it into a build or have a
177:   role skill inherit it.
178: 
179: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
180: [`diagnose-loop`]: ../../workflows/diagnose-loop/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/loop-controller/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: loop-controller
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: >-
5:   Wrap any task in a verifiable stop condition plus a mandatory guardrail stack
6:   so an autonomous loop converges instead of thrashing or burning the budget —
7:   the foundation harness every loop skill composes on. Use whenever you want
8:   Claude to keep working until something is provably true (tests pass, coverage
9:   hits a target, a contract's criteria hold, a queue is empty), to schedule a
10:   recurring check, or to pick the right loop primitive (/goal vs /loop vs
11:   Stop-hook vs a bash Ralph loop vs a dynamic workflow). Trigger on: "loop
12:   until", "keep going until", "run until green", "work until done", "autonomous
13:   loop", "agentic loop", "ralph loop", "/goal", "iterate until", "loop safely",
14:   "iteration cap", "loop budget", "runaway agent", "overnight build". Read it
15:   first when authoring any new loop skill.
16: requires_claude_code: true
17: min_plan: starter
18: disable-model-invocation: true
19: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep", "Agent", "Workflow"]
20: composes_with: ["orchestrator", "fix-until-green", "orchestrator-task-loop", "contract-conformance-loop", "babysit", "coverage-loop", "perf-loop", "self-healing-loop", "migration-loop", "nightly-docs-and-changelog", "dependency-health-loop", "codebase-exploration-loop", "repo-cleanup-loop", "qe-agent", "contract-auditor", "diagnose-loop", "context-manager", "model-adaptation", "find-unknowns", "loop", "schedule"]
21: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
22: ---
23: 
24: # loop-controller
25: 
26: > **Why this is a deliberate (`disable-model-invocation`) skill.** A loop edits,
27: > commits, and spends tokens on its own. You want to *type* `/loop-controller`
28: > (or have the orchestrator dispatch it), not have Claude silently start an
29: > autonomous loop because a test happened to fail. The discipline below is what
30: > makes that safe.
31: 
32: ## The one rule that governs every loop
33: 
34: **A loop is only as good as its stopping condition.** Loops *converge* when
35: "done" is an external signal the agent cannot argue with — a test exit code, a
36: coverage percentage, an empty queue, a fresh evaluator's verdict. They *diverge*
37: — oscillate, thrash, or run the budget to zero — when "done" is a subjective
38: judgment the agent grades itself on. Agents are pathological optimists about
39: their own work; left to self-assess, they declare victory early.
40: 
41: There is a subtler failure an objective proof does **not** cure: a signal that
42: is mechanical, default-FAIL, and un-gameable can still measure the **wrong
43: thing**. A test suite green against a mock, a stub, or a fixture is all three of
44: those — and proves nothing about whether the real product works. That is
45: *convergence on a fiction*, and because it wears the costume of rigor (an exit
46: code! a number!) it slips past review more easily than a subjective claim would.
47: 65 green tests against a mocked backend look exactly like 65 green tests against
48: the real one. So the proof must measure the **real goal**, not a stand-in for it
49: (Step 2) — un-gameable *and* pointed at the thing that actually matters.
50: 
51: So the entire job of this skill is to turn a fuzzy "keep working on X" into a
52: **convergent** loop: a mechanical proof *of the real goal*, the right execution
53: primitive, and a guardrail stack that stops the loop whether or not the proof is
54: ever met.
55: 
56: Everything else here — fix-until-green, coverage-loop, perf-loop — is a *config*
57: of this harness: a specific proof plugged into the same machinery. Author new
58: loops against this skill; don't reinvent the loop engine.
59: 
60: ## First — does a concrete loop already exist?
61: 
62: Twelve configs of this harness already ship in `skills/loops/`. Route the intent
63: to one of them before authoring anything — Steps 0–6 below are for the loop that
64: *doesn't* exist yet. (Every one of these is `disable-model-invocation: true`:
65: dispatch it by its slash command, or let the orchestrator dispatch the build
66: loops. None auto-fires.)
67: 
68: | The intent is… | Dispatch |
69: |---|---|
70: | "make tests / lint / typecheck pass", red CI, "run until green" | `fix-until-green` |
71: | Drain a multi-agent build's shared task list until every task passes its gate (Agent Teams) | `orchestrator-task-loop` |
72: | Build until an authored contract's criteria all hold (build-until-spec) | `contract-conformance-loop` |
73: | "Keep my PR green / rebased / answer review comments while I'm away" | `babysit` |
74: | "Raise test coverage to N%" | `coverage-loop` |
75: | "Get this metric under its budget" (latency, bundle size, memory) | `perf-loop` |
76: | "Watch the logs / CI and fix what breaks" | `self-healing-loop` |
77: | "Migrate / transform every file in this enumerated set" | `migration-loop` |
78: | "Keep the docs + changelog from rotting" (nightly) | `nightly-docs-and-changelog` |
79: | "Keep dependencies current and audited" | `dependency-health-loop` |
80: | "Map this unfamiliar codebase until my questions are answered" | `codebase-exploration-loop` |
81: | "Weekly repo hygiene — stale branches, PRs, worktrees" | `repo-cleanup-loop` |
82: 
83: No row fits → continue below and author the new loop against this harness.
84: 
85: ## Step 0 — Should this even be a loop?
86: 
87: A loop has real setup cost and its own failure modes; the mistake is looping
88: things that don't earn one. Before building anything, put the task through four
89: questions — a "no" on any of them is a strong signal to just do the work manually
90: (or once) instead:
91: 
92: 1. **Does it repeat?** A one-off is usually faster by hand. Loops pay off on work
93:    that comes back — every PR, every night, every new ticket.
94: 2. **Can "done" be verified mechanically?** If nothing but a human can reject a
95:    bad result, the human is still the real gate and the loop saves little. This is
96:    the same requirement as Step 2 — no mechanical proof, no convergent loop.
97: 3. **Can the agent act end-to-end?** If it must stop for permission or missing
98:    context every few minutes, that's assisted manual work, not a loop.
99: 4. **Is "done" objective?** "Tests pass" / "queue empty" loops; "the design feels
100:    right" / "the strategy is sound" does not. Subjective, high-stakes, judgment-
101:    heavy work (architecture rewrites, auth, payments, prod deploys) is where a
102:    human stays in the driver's seat — keep it manual, or gate it hard (guardrail 4).
103: 
104: Pass all four and the 5-part contract below will fill in cleanly. Fail one and
105: the honest move is to not build the loop.
106: 
107: ## The 5-part loop contract (required)
108: 
109: Before running anything, write the loop's contract. A loop that can't fill in
110: all five lines isn't ready — the blank is the bug.
111: 
112: | Part | Question it answers | Example |
113: |---|---|---|
114: | **trigger** | What starts this loop? | "red CI on branch X" / "every 30m" / "user runs `/fix-until-green`" |
115: | **action** | What does one iteration *do*? | "run the gate, fix one root-cause failure, re-run the whole gate" |
116: | **proof** | What mechanical signal proves done? | "`npm test` exits 0 **and** lint exits 0 **and** typecheck exits 0" |
117: | **memory** | What state survives between iterations? | "`PROGRESS.md`, the feature-list JSON, git history" |
118: | **stop** | Every way this loop ends | "proof passes **OR** 20 iterations **OR** no-progress for 3 rounds **OR** budget cap" |
119: 
120: The `proof` and `stop` lines are load-bearing. Keep this contract in the loop
121: skill's body (or at the top of the run) so any reviewer can audit convergence at
122: a glance. This is the Forward Future "loop library" 5-part schema, adopted as the
123: required contract. The taxonomy of common loop shapes (fix-until-green,
124: build-until-spec, refine-until-quality, research, review, migration, coverage,
125: perf, self-healing, exploration) lives in `references/authoring.md`.
126: 
127: ## Step 1 — Choose the primitive
128: 
129: The first decision is *which engine runs the loop*. Get this wrong and you burn
130: money: a scheduler pointed at finishable work re-runs blindly on the clock; a
131: "work until done" wrapper pointed at an external poll spins forever.
132: 
133: **The decision rule:** *Are you pushing work to a finish line, or watching for
134: something to change?*
135: 
136: | If the job is… | Use | Because |
137: |---|---|---|
138: | Push to a finish line, proof is **provable from Claude's own output** (test exits 0, git clean) | **`/goal`** | A small fast model judges the transcript each turn; "no" feeds the reason back. The closest native "loop until done." |
139: | Push to a finish line, proof needs a **script/file/tool check** the model can't just assert | **Stop-hook gate** | A hook runs the real check and blocks exit until it passes — ships *with* the skill. |
140: | **Watch / poll** for an external change on a cadence | **`/loop`** | A thin scheduler that re-runs a prompt or slash command on an interval; it does not push to a finish line. |
141: | One big mechanical change across **many files in parallel** | **`/batch` / dynamic workflow** | Fans out across worktree-isolated subagents; compose with the `orchestrator`'s Workflow mode. |
142: | **Greenfield**, want a fresh context window every iteration | **`claude -p` bash loop (Ralph)** | State lives on disk; each pass starts clean. Needs a sandbox (`--dangerously-skip-permissions`). |
143: 
144: Do not conflate `/goal` (finish line) with `/loop` (watch). They are different
145: primitives with different failure modes. Full mechanics, constraints, and the
146: exact invocations for each — including `/goal`'s evaluator-can't-read-files
147: limit and `/loop`'s session-scope/expiry/no-catch-up rules — are in
148: `references/primitives.md`. **Read it before authoring.**
149: 
150: ## Step 2 — Make "done" mechanical and default-FAIL
151: 
152: A convergent loop needs a proof signal the agent cannot rationalize past.
153: 
154: - **Phrase "done" as an observable.** "all tests in `test/auth` pass and lint is
155:   clean," not "the auth code looks correct." If the proof is a number, name the
156:   artifact that produces it (coverage report, benchmark output, the gate's exit
157:   code).
158: - **Default-FAIL.** Every criterion starts `false` and only flips on *evidence*.
159:   Store the criteria as JSON, not prose — a model is far less likely to quietly
160:   rewrite a JSON `"passed": false` than to soften a sentence. (Anthropic's
161:   long-running-agent harness stores the feature list as JSON for exactly this
162:   reason.)
163: - **Measure the goal, not a stand-in.** An objective signal is necessary but not
164:   sufficient — it also has to exercise the *real* thing the loop is for. If the
165:   goal depends on a real dependency (a live service, real data, an integration, a
166:   deploy) and the proof only touches a mock / stub / fixture of it, the loop will
167:   converge — green, confident, and wrong. Either the proof exercises the real
168:   path at least once, or it is *explicitly* labelled scaffold-level ("the mocked
169:   build is internally consistent") with a separate goal-level proof named. A
170:   green that came from measuring the stand-in instead of the goal is a coverage
171:   gap wearing a green badge — the same class of bug as a green that came from
172:   moving the number instead of fixing the cause (Step 3, guardrail 6), and just
173:   as much a *finding*.
174: - **Separate the grader from the doer for any subjective bar.** When "done"
175:   can't be reduced to an exit code (UI quality, doc clarity, API ergonomics),
176:   the proof is a **fresh-context evaluator subagent** — spawned with **no
177:   Write/Edit tools** so it cannot "fix" a failure by lowering the bar, and
178:   blind to how the work was built so it can't rubber-stamp its own reasoning.
179:   This is the GAN / Plan-Generate-Evaluate pattern. A same-model critic that
180:   saw the build approves mediocre work; an external signal (tests) or a fresh
181:   evaluator is what stops the rubber-stamp. See `references/authoring.md`.
182: 
183: ## Step 3 — Install the guardrail stack (mandatory)
184: 
185: Every loop ships with all of these. They are not optional polish — a documented
186: multi-agent loop ran **11 days and burned tens of thousands of dollars** because
187: it had observability but no *enforcement*. Alerting is not a guardrail;
188: termination is.
189: 
190: 1. **Iteration cap.** A hard `--max-iterations` (or "stop after N turns" baked
191:    into the `/goal` condition — `/goal` has no native cap). This is the primary
192:    backstop when the proof is never met.
193: 2. **Token / cost budget with enforcement.** A ceiling that *terminates* the
194:    loop, not just warns. `/goal` has no built-in budget — embed a turn cap and
195:    watch `/cost`; dynamic workflows take an explicit token budget; bash loops
196:    need an external counter. A 50-iteration run on a large codebase can cost
197:    $50–100+. The number that actually tells you whether the loop is worth running
198:    is **cost per *accepted* result**, not tokens spent or iterations run: a loop
199:    that opens five PRs where you merge one, or emits a daily report no one reads,
200:    can cost more than doing the work by hand. Track yield, not spend — a low
201:    accept rate means the loop is manufacturing review debt, and the fix is a
202:    tighter proof (Step 2), not a bigger budget.
203: 3. **No-progress / oscillation detection.** Stop if iterations stop changing
204:    state, or if output repeats (≥~90% similarity to a recent iteration), or if
205:    token use grows quadratically rather than linearly. Thrashing and budget
206:    exhaustion are the two dominant non-convergence modes — detect both.
207: 4. **HITL checkpoint before anything irreversible.** Pause for a human before a
208:    DB write, a deploy, an external API call, a force-push. Unattended loops run
209:    only what is reversible and has a hard verifier.
210: 5. **Checkpoint commits.** Commit working state every iteration with a
211:    descriptive message. On a wedged codebase, `git reset --hard` to the last
212:    green checkpoint and re-loop is usually cheaper than rescuing it.
213: 6. **Never let the loop weaken its own gate.** Forbid editing or deleting tests
214:    to make them pass, silencing a check with an ignore directive, or relocating
215:    a violation into the checker's blind spot. A green that came from moving the
216:    number instead of fixing the cause is a *finding*, not a win. When a gate
217:    flips red→green, read the diff that did it.
218: 
219: The full stack — including the `stop_hook_active` guard for Stop-hook loops, the
220: oscillation thresholds, and the staged-adoption / rollback ladder — is in
221: `references/safety.md`.
222: 
223: ## Step 4 — Externalize state (so iterations are stateless)
224: 
225: A loop that remembers across iterations only through the conversation breaks the
226: moment context compacts or a fresh-context pass starts. Externalize to
227: **path-addressable files**:
228: 
229: - a **progress file** (`PROGRESS.md` / `claude-progress.txt`) — what's done, what's next;
230: - a **task / feature-list JSON** — the default-FAIL criteria;
231: - a live **plan/TODO** (`fix_plan.md`) — rewritten freely each pass;
232: - an **`init.sh`** — how to build and run;
233: - **git history** — the durable checkpoint trail.
234: 
235: Each iteration starts by *reading* these, does one increment, then *updates*
236: them and commits. Read the caps (max-iterations, budget) and the state-file
237: paths from `.claude/profile.yaml` when present, so the same loop skill works
238: across projects without hard-coding. (This is the orchestrator/role-skill
239: convention; loops follow it.)
240: 
241: ## Step 5 — Run, watch for divergence, know when to kill
242: 
243: - **One task per iteration.** Trust the loop to pick the most important next
244:   thing; don't batch. Spawn subagents for expensive search/verification, but
245:   **cap build/test parallelism at 1** — two agents building at once destroy the
246:   backpressure signal.
247: - **Re-verify the whole, not just the change.** After each fix, re-run the
248:   *entire* proof (full suite, every page, the whole rubric), not only the unit
249:   you touched. "Restart the streak" is how you catch a fix that broke something
250:   else.
251: - **Roll a loop back to attended (or kill it) when** any of: token spend grows
252:   non-linearly, output similarity >90% across iterations, a fresh evaluator and
253:   your own judgment disagree, or it's about to touch an irreversible resource
254:   without a checkpoint. A suspiciously easy convergence is itself a finding —
255:   inspect before trusting.
256: 
257: ## Step 6 — Long-run behavioral hygiene (Claude 5 family)
258: 
259: Steps 1–5 make the loop *converge*. This step keeps the **model's behavior** honest over
260: a long run on the Claude 5 family (Fable 5 / Mythos 5), where a single turn can run for
261: minutes and an autonomous run for hours. These are prompt-level additions to the loop's
262: brief — the harness enforces convergence; these keep the agent from lying, quitting early,
263: or panicking about context on the way there. Drop-in instruction text and the 5-part
264: mapping for each are in `model-adaptation/references/long-run-hygiene.md`; wire what the
265: loop needs:
266: 
267: - **Evidence-backed progress (anti-fabrication).** Instruct the agent to audit each
268:   progress claim against an actual tool result before reporting it. This is *distinct from*
269:   guardrail 6: guardrail 6 stops the loop from gaming the mechanical *gate*; this stops the
270:   model from *narrating* work it never verified. Both matter; neither substitutes.
271: - **Don't end a turn on a promise.** Deep in a run the model can say "I'll now run X" with
272:   no tool call, or pause to ask when it already has enough. `/goal` + Stop-hooks catch this
273:   mechanically (Step 1); add the prompt-level last-paragraph self-check and, for unattended
274:   runs, the autonomous-operation reminder so the agent doesn't lean on the hook.
275: - **Don't surface the budget countdown to the model.** Guardrail 2 watches `/cost` for
276:   *enforcement* — but that number is a **harness** stop signal, not something to show the
277:   model. Seeing a remaining-token countdown makes the Claude 5 family prematurely summarize,
278:   offer to hand off, or trim its own work. Read the budget from externalized state (Step 4)
279:   and decide in the harness; if a count must be visible, add the "you have ample context,
280:   continue" reassurance.
281: - **Model & effort per iteration.** Effort is the primary intelligence/latency/cost dial: `high`
282:   default, `xhigh` for the hardest proof/verify steps, `medium`/`low` for routine passes.
283:   Lower effort on the Claude 5 family often beats `xhigh` on prior models — reduce it if a
284:   loop converges but each iteration runs longer than the work needs. Model tiers the same
285:   way — pick both per `model-adaptation`'s *Model & effort tiering* policy: workers and
286:   routine passes tier down, but the fresh-context evaluator (Step 2) stays on the top tier.
287: - **Send-to-user for verbatim mid-turn output.** A loop otherwise only speaks by *ending*
288:   its turn for HITL. For long async loops that must surface a deliverable or a direct answer
289:   *without* stopping, give the agent a client-side `send_to_user` tool plus the elicitation
290:   line — never route the model's reasoning through it (that's the `reasoning_extraction`
291:   refusal landmine; see `model-adaptation`).
292: 
293: The fresh-context evaluator (Step 2) is itself one of these patterns — the guide's
294: "fresh verifiers beat self-critique" — so it's already wired; just run it *periodically*
295: on a long build, not only at the end.
296: 
297: ## Comprehension debt — the human-side stop condition
298: 
299: Steps 1–6 keep the *machine* honest. There is one failure they don't catch,
300: because it lands on the human, not the loop: a loop that ships correct, green
301: diffs faster than anyone reads them. Each merge feels like progress, but the
302: codebase starts moving faster than the team's understanding of it — the tests
303: pass, yet nobody can say *why* the code is shaped the way it is. That's
304: **comprehension debt**, and it's the antidote's mirror image of convergence-on-a-
305: fiction: there the proof was too weak, here the proof was fine but human
306: understanding silently fell behind. The bill arrives at the next bug, in a module
307: no one can reason about.
308: 
309: The guardrails don't fix this because it isn't a convergence problem — it's a
310: review problem. Keep it in check by keeping loops on **small, readable diffs**,
311: having a human actually read the diff a red→green flip produced (guardrail 6
312: already asks for this), and — for any substantial or fully agent-driven change —
313: running a **comprehension quiz before merge**: `find-unknowns` owns that move
314: (explain the diff and what it touches, then test that you can pass a quiz on the
315: non-obvious behavior). Autonomy that outruns understanding isn't a faster team;
316: it's a deferred debugging session.
317: 
318: ## Using it as the harness other loops compose on
319: 
320: Concrete loop skills don't re-implement any of the above — they *fill in the
321: contract* and inherit the machinery:
322: 
323: - **`fix-until-green`** — proof = test+lint+typecheck all exit 0; primitive =
324:   `/goal` or a Stop-hook gate; the canonical first instance.
325: - A new loop = a new `skills/loops/<name>/` whose SKILL.md states its 5-part
326:   contract, names its proof artifact, and points back here for the guardrails.
327:   The authoring walkthrough (with the loop taxonomy and a frontmatter template)
328:   is `references/authoring.md`.
329: 
330: Under the **orchestrator**, loops slot in at two levels — and both are built:
331: `orchestrator-task-loop` is the outer loop over the shared task list (re-assign
332: until every task passes its gate), while the inner loops run per role —
333: `fix-until-green` at the wave/QA gates, and `contract-conformance-loop` /
334: `coverage-loop` / `perf-loop` / `migration-loop` dispatched when the mission
335: calls for them (see the orchestrator's `references/phase-guide.md`, *Optional
336: build loops*).
337: 
338: ## Reference files
339: 
340: - `references/primitives.md` — `/goal`, `/loop`, `/batch`, dynamic workflows,
341:   Stop-hooks, and the bash-Ralph loop: exact mechanics, constraints, invocations,
342:   and the implementation-approach tradeoff table. Read before choosing a primitive.
343: - `references/safety.md` — the full guardrail stack, `stop_hook_active`,
344:   oscillation/no-progress detection, the cost footguns, and the staged-adoption
345:   and rollback ladder.
346: - `references/authoring.md` — the 10-archetype loop taxonomy, the fresh-context
347:   evaluator / default-FAIL pattern in detail, and a step-by-step template for
348:   writing a new loop skill that composes on this one.

=== skills/loops/migration-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: migration-loop
3: version: 1.1.0
4: description: >-
5:   Migrate a known, enumerated set of targets one at a time until EVERY one is
6:   done — one iteration picks the next un-migrated target from a checklist,
7:   transforms it, verifies behaviour-identical (the suite stays green), commits a
8:   checkpoint, and marks it migrated, with a hard exit that BOTH the suite is
9:   green AND a grep for the old pattern returns zero. The only loop over a fixed
10:   target set whose proof is "no legacy pattern remains," not just "tests pass."
11:   Use for a codemod across a repo, a Jest-to-Vitest swap, an API version bump, a
12:   framework migration, an import/dependency rename, or any mechanical transform
13:   applied to a finite list of files. Trigger on "migrate everything from
14:   X to Y", "run the codemod across the repo", "bump every call site", "swap the
15:   framework", "migrate all the tests", "finish the migration", "no old pattern
16:   left", "/migration-loop". A configuration of loop-controller.
17: requires_claude_code: true
18: min_plan: starter
19: disable-model-invocation: true
20: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob", "Agent"]
21: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "orchestrator", "fix-until-green", "db-migration-agent"]
22: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
23: ---
24: 
25: # migration-loop
26: 
27: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
28: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
29: > externalization. This skill supplies the two things specific to "migrate a
30: > fixed set of targets to completion": a **default-FAIL target checklist** driven
31: > one entry per iteration, and a **proof that no legacy pattern remains** (a grep
32: > for the old pattern returning zero), not merely "tests pass." Read
33: > `loop-controller` for the guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated here.
34: >
35: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop edits and commits code on its own
36: > — a checkpoint commit per migrated target — until the whole set is done. You
37: > want to *type* `/migration-loop` (or have the orchestrator dispatch it) — not
38: > have Claude silently start rewriting every file because it noticed an old API.
39: 
40: ## The 5-part contract
41: 
42: | Part | This loop |
43: |---|---|
44: | **trigger** | a finite, enumerable set of targets needs the *same* transform (a codemod, a Jest-to-Vitest swap, an API version bump, a framework/import rename), plus a way to verify behaviour is preserved — or an explicit `/migration-loop` (optionally scoped to one path) |
45: | **action** | ONE iteration: pick the next `"migrated": false` target from the checklist → apply the transform to **just that target** → run the suite to confirm behaviour-identical → on green, **commit a checkpoint** naming the target → flip its checklist entry to `true` |
46: | **proof** | **EVERY** checklist entry `"migrated": true` **AND** the full suite exits 0 **AND** a grep for the legacy pattern returns **zero** matches — default-FAIL: assume incomplete until all three artifacts (the checklist, the suite exit code, the grep count) are observed together |
47: | **memory** | `migration-checklist.json` (one default-FAIL entry per target + the old→new mapping), `PROGRESS.md` (what's migrated / next), a git checkpoint commit per migrated target |
48: | **stop** | all three proof artifacts hold **OR** iteration cap **OR** no-progress for 3 rounds **OR** budget cap **OR** an HITL checkpoint for an irreversible target (see Guardrails) |
49: 
50: ## The proof: checklist + grep-for-old-pattern, default-FAIL
51: 
52: "Done" is **not** "the suite is green." A green suite proves the targets you
53: *touched* still behave — it says nothing about the ones you *missed*. The thing
54: that distinguishes a real migration from a partial one is the third artifact: a
55: **grep for the legacy pattern returning zero**. Three signals, all required, all
56: default-FAIL (assume not-done until each is observed):
57: 
58: 1. **The checklist is exhausted** — every entry in `migration-checklist.json` is
59:    `"migrated": true`. Each starts `false` and only flips when *that* target is
60:    transformed and its checkpoint commit lands.
61: 2. **The full suite exits 0** — re-run the *whole* suite, not just the touched
62:    file's tests; the migration is behaviour-preserving only if nothing else
63:    regressed (`loop-controller` Step 5, "re-verify the whole").
64: 3. **`grep -r '<legacy-pattern>'` returns zero** across the migrated scope —
65:    the mechanical proof that no call site, import, or usage was skipped. Pin the
66:    exact pattern(s) in the checklist so every iteration (and the final check)
67:    greps the *identical* string. A non-zero count is a *finding*: a missed
68:    target, not a passing migration.
69: 
70: Building the checklist/mapping, choosing the pattern(s), and the
71: "no legacy pattern remains" verification are detailed in
72: [`references/migration-checklist.md`](references/migration-checklist.md).
73: 
74: ## Step 1 — Enumerate the targets and build the default-FAIL checklist
75: 
76: Discover the full target set *before* looping — a migration over a set you
77: haven't fully enumerated cannot prove completion. Use `Grep`/`Glob` to find every
78: occurrence of the legacy pattern, dedupe to a target list, and write each as a
79: JSON entry with `"migrated": false`, the old→new mapping, and the exact grep
80: pattern. Store it at the profile-defined path (default
81: `migration-checklist.json`). The checklist *is* the loop's scope: if a target
82: isn't on it, the loop won't migrate it, and the final grep will catch the gap.
83: 
84: ## Step 2 — Decide sequential vs fan-out
85: 
86: - **Sequential (`/goal`, default)** — one target per iteration. Use when targets
87:   are interdependent, the transform is subtle, or the set is small enough that
88:   per-target review matters. The Haiku evaluator reads the suite output and the
89:   grep count you surface each turn.
90: - **Parallel (`/batch` / dynamic workflow)** — for large, *mechanically uniform*
91:   sets (hundreds of identical codemod sites). Fan out across **worktree-isolated**
92:   subagents (compose the [`orchestrator`]'s Workflow mode), each migrating a slice
93:   and committing in its own worktree. **Cap build/test parallelism at 1 per
94:   worktree** (`loop-controller` Step 5) — two builds at once destroy the
95:   backpressure signal. The whole-set grep + suite still run once after the merge
96:   as the single proof. The decision rule and the fan-out shape are in
97:   [`references/migration-checklist.md`](references/migration-checklist.md).
98: 
99: ## Step 3 — Migrate one target, verify behaviour-identical, checkpoint
100: 
101: Apply the transform to **one** target (or one worktree slice). Then run the
102: **full** suite — a migration is only valid if behaviour is preserved, so the test
103: gate is the per-iteration verifier. If the suite goes red, the transform changed
104: behaviour: fix it (or **invoke [`fix-until-green`]** if it's a non-trivial gate
105: failure) before moving on — never advance the checklist over a red suite. On
106: green, **commit a checkpoint** naming the migrated target, then flip its entry to
107: `true`. The commit-per-target trail is the loop's undo: `git reset --hard` to the
108: last green target is cheaper than rescuing a wedged transform.
109: 
110: ## Step 4 — Re-run the whole proof, repeat
111: 
112: After the last target, run all three proof artifacts together: the checklist must
113: be fully `true`, the full suite must exit 0, and the legacy-pattern grep must
114: return **zero**. A non-zero grep with a "fully migrated" checklist means a target
115: was missed during enumeration — add it and loop. When all three hold on one pass,
116: the loop is done; report the final checklist, the suite exit code, and the
117: `grep -c` output (zero) as evidence.
118: 
119: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
120: 
121: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
122: this loop sets:
123: 
124: - **Iteration cap** — default ~1 per target plus slack (read from
125:   `.claude/profile.yaml` if set); for fan-out, a hard cap on concurrent worktrees.
126:   Hitting the cap is a *stop-and-escalate*, not a license to relax the grep or
127:   mark targets done without migrating them.
128: - **No-progress detection** — if the same target stays `"migrated": false` (or the
129:   legacy grep count stops dropping) for **3 consecutive rounds**, stop and
130:   escalate. The same target failing three times means the transform is wrong, not
131:   that it needs a fourth try.
132: - **Never fake completion.** Forbidden, each a *finding*: marking a target
133:   `"migrated": true` without transforming it, narrowing the grep pattern so it
134:   reports zero while usages remain, or deleting/skipping a test to keep the suite
135:   green over a behaviour change. A zero grep that came from a weakened pattern is
136:   not a migration.
137: - **Reversible code transforms are AFK-safe; irreversible resources are HITL.** A
138:   code/codemod migration behind a test gate with checkpoint commits per target is
139:   reversible — fine unattended. A migration that touches an **irreversible
140:   resource** — a real DB schema migration against live data, a destructive data
141:   backfill, an external system — is an **HITL checkpoint** (`loop-controller`
142:   guardrail 4): pause for the human, and route the schema-migration case to
143:   [`db-migration-agent`], which owns up/down migrations, dry-runs, and rollback.
144: 
145: ## Choosing the driver primitive
146: 
147: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, by the target set:
148: 
149: - **Default — `/goal`** for sequential one-target-per-iteration: the proof is
150:   provable from the suite output + the grep count you surface, so
151:   `/goal "every entry in migration-checklist.json is migrated:true, the full
152:   suite exits 0, and a grep for the legacy pattern returns zero — or stop after N
153:   turns."` Remember the embedded turn cap — `/goal` has no native budget.
154: - **`/batch` / dynamic workflow** for large parallel sets with worktree isolation
155:   (hence `Agent` in `allowed-tools`), composing the [`orchestrator`]'s Workflow
156:   mode. The whole-set grep + suite run once after merge as the proof.
157: 
158: ## Using it under the orchestrator
159: 
160: This is the **Migration inner loop** (archetype 6). The orchestrator dispatches
161: it for a planned migration, routing per-file failures back to the owning role
162: **by file** (a red suite in `src/api/` goes to the backend agent). The
163: orchestrator does **not** override a stuck loop — if migration-loop escalates
164: after no-progress, that's a real migration blocker, not a number to paper over. A
165: fully-migrated checklist informs the build; the `qe-agent`'s `qa-report.json`
166: still decides the gate (`loop-controller`'s rule: the loop informs, the gate
167: decides).
168: 
169: ## Expand–contract mode (when one change breaks everything at once)
170: 
171: A single mechanical change whose blast radius breaks thousands of call sites in
172: one motion — rename a column, retype a shared symbol, change a core signature —
173: can't run as the normal migrate-and-verify loop: every batch is red until the
174: last one lands. Sequence it **expand → migrate → contract** instead:
175: 
176: 1. **Expand** — add the new form *beside* the old (new column, overloaded
177:    signature, aliased export). Nothing breaks; the suite stays green.
178: 2. **Migrate** — drive call sites over in blast-radius-sized batches with the
179:    normal checklist loop; each batch stays green because the old form still
180:    exists.
181: 3. **Contract** — when the legacy-pattern grep hits zero *call sites*, delete
182:    the old form. The final proof adds a grep for the old *definition*.
183: 
184: Wire the stages as three checklist phases — the contract stage is its own target
185: whose transform is the deletion. When batches can't stay green alone (deeply
186: entangled sites), fall back to a shared integration branch that merges only when
187: the whole set is green. (Adapted from mattpocock `engineering/to-tickets`
188: v1.1.0; CB-9.)
189: 
190: ## Long-run hygiene (wired per loop-controller Step 6)
191: 
192: A long migration is exactly where the Claude 5 long-run rules earn their keep
193: (drop-in text: `model-adaptation` → `references/long-run-hygiene.md`):
194: 
195: - **Evidence-backed progress** — a checklist entry flips `true` only on the
196:   observed pair (suite exit 0 + checkpoint commit hash) for *that* target; a
197:   `PROGRESS.md` line without both is fabrication, not progress.
198: - **Don't end an iteration on a promise** — each iteration ends with a target
199:   migrated, committed, and flipped, or at a declared stop condition; never with
200:   "next I'll migrate X" and no edit.
201: - **Budget is a harness decision** — read caps from `.claude/profile.yaml`;
202:   don't show the model a countdown, and don't let a long set trigger
203:   summarize-and-quit — the checklist externalizes exactly where to resume.
204: - **Effort per iteration** — mechanical transforms run at `medium`/`low`;
205:   reserve `high`/`xhigh` for a transform that reds the suite (tiering:
206:   `model-adaptation`).
207: 
208: ## How this differs from its neighbors
209: 
210: - **vs. [`fix-until-green`]** — that loop drives a *failing* suite to green; this
211:   loop drives a *fixed target set* to fully migrated. The suite green is only one
212:   of three proof artifacts here; the load-bearing one is the legacy-pattern grep.
213:   This loop *invokes* fix-until-green when a transform reds the gate.
214: - **vs. [`db-migration-agent`]** — that role owns a single irreversible schema
215:   migration (up/down, dry-run, rollback) as an HITL operation; this loop drives a
216:   *set* of reversible code transforms to completion and *delegates* the schema
217:   case to it at the HITL checkpoint.
218: 
219: ## Reference files
220: 
221: - [`references/migration-checklist.md`](references/migration-checklist.md) —
222:   building the target checklist/mapping (the `migration-checklist.json`
223:   default-FAIL schema with a worked example), the sequential-vs-`/batch`-fan-out
224:   decision and the worktree-isolation shape, and the "no legacy pattern remains"
225:   verification (choosing the grep pattern, scoping it, and reading the count).
226: 
227: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
228: [`fix-until-green`]: ../fix-until-green/SKILL.md
229: [`orchestrator`]: ../../orchestrator/SKILL.md
230: [`db-migration-agent`]: ../../roles/db-migration-agent/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/nightly-docs-and-changelog/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: nightly-docs-and-changelog
3: version: 1.0.1
4: description: >-
5:   Run a nightly sweep that keeps an existing repo's docs and changelog from
6:   rotting: diff the code since the last run, bring the docs for the changed
7:   surface back in line with the implementation, append every user-relevant
8:   change to CHANGELOG.md (or record a no-change night), and open ONE reviewable
9:   PR — never auto-merging. Scheduled to survive laptop-off via a cloud routine /
10:   Desktop scheduled task, NOT a session-scoped loop. Use when you want docs and
11:   the changelog kept current automatically while you sleep, when docs keep
12:   drifting behind code, when the changelog is always stale, or when you want a
13:   morning PR of doc+changelog updates to review. Trigger on: "keep the docs
14:   current", "nightly docs sweep", "update the changelog nightly", "docs keep
15:   drifting", "stale changelog", "overnight docs", "stop the docs rotting",
16:   "nightly doc PR". Drafts AFK; merges HITL. A configuration of
17:   loop-controller.
18: requires_claude_code: true
19: min_plan: starter
20: disable-model-invocation: true
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
22: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "docs-agent", "git-pr", "schedule"]
23: spawned_by: []
24: ---
25: 
26: # nightly-docs-and-changelog
27:
