28: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
29: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
30: > externalization. This skill supplies the three things specific to "keep an
31: > existing repo's docs+changelog from rotting on a nightly cadence": the
32: > **per-night recipe** (it diffs since the last run and authors the doc +
33: > changelog updates for that surface itself, following the documentation
34: > conventions [`docs-agent`] defines — docs-agent is the orchestrator's
35: > build-phase role and is not user-invocable on a schedule), the
36: > **mechanical proof** (docs match the changed surface AND every user-relevant
37: > change is recorded, ending in a reviewable PR), and the **HITL boundary** (it
38: > opens a PR and stops — it never merges docs). Read `loop-controller` for the
39: > guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated here.
40: >
41: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop edits docs, writes the changelog,
42: > commits, and opens a PR on its own, on a schedule. It is user-scheduled — you
43: > *set it up* once (or type `/nightly-docs-and-changelog`), not have Claude
44: > silently start rewriting docs because some code changed during the day.
45: 
46: ## The 5-part contract
47: 
48: | Part | This loop |
49: |---|---|
50: | **trigger** | a nightly cron fire from the scheduled routine (default ~02:00 local), or an explicit `/nightly-docs-and-changelog` to run one sweep now |
51: | **action** | ONE sweep: read the last-run marker → `git diff <marker>..HEAD` → for the changed surface, author the doc updates yourself (following [`docs-agent`]'s conventions) → classify each change user-relevant vs internal → append user-relevant ones to `CHANGELOG.md` (or write a no-change record) → advance the marker → open ONE PR via [`git-pr`] |
52: | **proof** | the docs for the changed surface match the current implementation **AND** every user-relevant change since the marker is recorded in `CHANGELOG.md` (or a no-change night is recorded), surfaced as a reviewable **docs/changelog diff + PR** — default-FAIL: assume docs are stale and the changelog incomplete until verified against the diff since the last run |
53: | **memory** | the **last-run marker** (a git tag/ref or `.claude/docs-sweep-last`), `CHANGELOG.md`, git history, and the open PR thread — all durable across nightly fires and laptop-off gaps |
54: | **stop** | proof met → PR opened (then **HITL** — stop, never merge) **OR** no diff since the marker (record a no-change night, no PR) **OR** iteration/pass cap **OR** no-progress for 3 nights (same surface still drifting) **OR** budget cap **OR** an HITL checkpoint is hit |
55: 
56: ## The proof: docs current AND changelog complete, default-FAIL
57: 
58: "Current" is not "the docs looked fine last week." It is **two conditions
59: observed together against the diff since the last run**: the docs covering the
60: *changed surface* match the implementation, **and** every *user-relevant* change
61: since the marker has a `CHANGELOG.md` entry (a quiet night gets an explicit
62: no-change record so a skipped night is distinguishable from a missed one).
63: Assume **stale** until a fresh sweep proves both — that's the default-FAIL stance,
64: and it's why every night re-diffs from the marker rather than trusting yesterday.
65: 
66: Name the artifact explicitly: the **docs/changelog diff plus the PR** the sweep
67: opens. The PR *is* the proof a human reviews; an empty diff with a no-change
68: record is a valid (and common) outcome.
69: 
70: ## Step 1 — Diff since the last run
71: 
72: The whole sweep is scoped by the last-run marker. Read it, diff `marker..HEAD`,
73: and that diff — nothing else — defines tonight's work surface. No marker yet
74: (first run) means bootstrap from the last release tag or a sensible recent
75: baseline, not the whole history. The marker mechanism (git tag vs `.claude/`
76: file), how to advance it atomically only after the PR opens, and how to recover a
77: missed night (the marker, not the clock, defines the window — a laptop-off gap is
78: just a wider diff next run) are in `references/scheduling.md`.
79: 
80: ## Step 2 — Classify user-relevant vs internal, then write the updates
81: 
82: Two distinct outputs come off the same diff:
83: 
84: - **Docs that must track the code** — for the changed surface, author the doc
85:   updates yourself (you hold Write/Edit), following the documentation conventions
86:   [`docs-agent`] establishes for this repo. docs-agent is the orchestrator's
87:   build-phase doc role — `disable-model-invocation`, dispatched only inside a
88:   contract-first build with a lead, contracts, and an ownership map — so a
89:   user-scheduled nightly sweep on an already-shipped repo reuses its
90:   *conventions*, not its dispatch. This loop adds the *scheduling, diff-scoping,
91:   and proof*, and matches docs-agent's house style rather than inventing a second
92:   one.
93: - **The changelog** — only **user-relevant** changes get an entry. A new flag,
94:   a behavior change, a fixed user-facing bug, a breaking change: yes. An internal
95:   refactor, a test-only change, a lint fix, a dependency bump with no user-visible
96:   effect: no. The exact user-relevant-vs-internal rubric (with examples) is in
97:   `references/scheduling.md`.
98: 
99: ## Step 3 — Open ONE PR, then stop (HITL)
100: 
101: Commit the docs+changelog changes and open **one** PR via [`git-pr`] titled for
102: the sweep date and summarizing what moved. Then **stop**. This loop **never
103: auto-merges** — docs and changelog wording is a human judgment call, and a PR
104: opened at 02:00 is meant to be reviewed at 09:00, not merged in the dark. One PR
105: per night keeps the review unit small; don't batch multiple nights into one PR.
106: 
107: ## How this differs from its neighbors
108: 
109: - **vs [`docs-agent`]** — docs-agent is a **build-phase role**: it writes the docs
110:   *for a build in progress*, dispatched by the orchestrator alongside the other
111:   role agents. This skill is the **recurring nightly sweep** that keeps an
112:   *already-shipped* repo's docs and changelog from rotting over time. It owns the
113:   *cadence, the diff-since-last-run window, the changelog discipline, the
114:   default-FAIL proof, and the PR/HITL boundary* — and authors the doc updates
115:   itself in docs-agent's house style (docs-agent is orchestrator-only and not
116:   user-invocable, so this loop reuses its *conventions*, not its dispatch).
117:   Because the nightly sweep runs **outside** a parallel orchestrated build —
118:   there is never a concurrent docs-agent — editing the `docs/` + `CHANGELOG.md`
119:   that docs-agent owns *at build time* creates no live ownership conflict; the
120:   two never run at once, and this loop follows the same conventions. Remove this
121:   skill and docs-agent still can't keep your repo current on its own; nothing
122:   schedules it or scopes it to "what changed since last night."
123: - **vs [`git-pr`]** — git-pr is the PR mechanics; this loop calls it as its final
124:   step, it doesn't reinvent PR creation.
125: - **vs a bare `/loop`** — see Step 4: `/loop` is the wrong primitive for a nightly
126:   cadence, which is the whole reason this skill schedules differently.
127: 
128: ## Step 4 — Schedule it to survive laptop-off (the routine, NOT /loop)
129: 
130: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, the primitive choice is load-bearing here and it is
131: **not `/loop`**. `/loop` is session-scoped, expires after ~3 days, and does not
132: catch up on missed fires — a laptop closed overnight kills it, which is exactly
133: the window this loop must run in. So the primitive is a **cloud routine /
134: Desktop scheduled task** (via `/schedule`) that fires on a real cron independent
135: of any open session and survives the machine being off. The full `/loop`-vs-
136: scheduled-routine reasoning lives in `loop-controller`'s
137: `references/primitives.md` — don't re-document it. The exact `/schedule` setup,
138: the cron line, and what the routine prompt invokes are in
139: `references/scheduling.md`.
140: 
141: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
142: 
143: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
144: this loop sets:
145: 
146: - **Pass cap per night** — one sweep = one PR; cap the within-night iterations
147:   (default ~10) so a pathological diff can't burn the whole budget before
148:   morning. Hitting it is a *stop-and-open-a-partial-PR-and-flag*, not a license to
149:   hand-wave the docs.
150: - **No-progress detection** — if the **same changed surface** keeps drifting
151:   across **3 consecutive nights** (docs re-edited but never converge, or the same
152:   file shows up undocumented night after night), stop and surface it. Three nights
153:   on one surface means it needs a human, not a fourth automated pass.
154: - **Budget cap** — a nightly routine runs unattended for weeks; enforce a
155:   token/cost ceiling that *terminates* the sweep (read it from
156:   `.claude/profile.yaml` when present), not just warns.
157: - **HITL is the exit, not a checkpoint mid-loop.** Opening the PR is where this
158:   loop hands off to a human — it **never merges**, never force-pushes, and never
159:   rewrites a published CHANGELOG entry. Drafting docs + changelog and opening a PR
160:   is reversible and AFK-safe; merging is the human's call.
161: - **Never cheat the proof.** Don't advance the marker without opening a PR (that
162:   silently swallows a night's changes), don't drop a user-relevant change to keep
163:   the changelog short, and don't mark docs "current" without checking them against
164:   the diff. A clean night that came from skipping the diff is a *finding*, not a
165:   win.
166: 
167: ## Choosing the driver primitive
168: 
169: The scheduled routine (`/schedule`) is the outer engine — it fires the sweep on
170: cron and survives laptop-off. Within a night, the sweep pushes to a finish line
171: (docs current + changelog complete + PR opened), provable from the diff and the
172: `gh` PR result, so the inner pass can run under auto mode unattended **inside the
173: reversible boundary** (draft + PR only). The loop as a whole has no `/goal`
174: finish line across nights — it watches the repo evolve and sweeps each night
175: until you unschedule it.
176: 
177: ## Reference files
178: 
179: - `references/scheduling.md` — the per-night algorithm and the doc-writing
180:   conventions; the last-run marker mechanism (git tag vs `.claude/docs-sweep-last`,
181:   advancing it atomically, recovering a missed night); the user-relevant-vs-
182:   internal changelog rubric with examples; the no-change-night record format; and
183:   the `/schedule` routine setup (cron line, routine prompt, what survives
184:   laptop-off).
185: 
186: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
187: [`docs-agent`]: ../../roles/docs-agent/SKILL.md
188: [`git-pr`]: ../../git/git-pr/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/orchestrator-task-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: orchestrator-task-loop
3: version: 1.0.1
4: description: >-
5:   The outer orchestration loop over the Agent Teams shared task list: the lead
6:   loops the board until EVERY task is completed AND each passes its TaskCompleted
7:   gate, feeding idle workers via the TeammateIdle hook so the team never stalls.
8:   One pass = read the board, assign the highest-priority unblocked task to each
9:   idle teammate, gate each completed task (pass counts it; fail rolls it back),
10:   re-verify the whole board. Experimental — gated behind
11:   CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1; falls back to the orchestrator's
12:   subagent/sequential path when off. Use to drain a task board or keep the team
13:   working until done under a build. Trigger on: "loop over the task list",
14:   "drain the task board", "keep the team working until done", "agent teams
15:   loop", "orchestrator task loop", "keep idle teammates fed". A configuration
16:   of loop-controller.
17: requires_claude_code: true
18: requires_agent_teams: true
19: min_plan: starter
20: disable-model-invocation: true
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep", "Agent", "Task", "Workflow"]
22: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "orchestrator", "fix-until-green", "qe-agent", "contract-auditor", "context-manager", "diagnose-loop", "git-commit", "loop", "schedule"]
23: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
24: ---
25: 
26: # orchestrator-task-loop
27: 
28: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
29: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
30: > externalization. This skill supplies the two things specific to draining the
31: > task list: a **mechanical proof** (every task `completed` **and** passing its
32: > `TaskCompleted` gate) and the **assignment/supervision discipline** that keeps
33: > the loop from declaring victory on a half-drained list. Read `loop-controller`
34: > for the guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated here.
35: >
36: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop spawns teammates and drives an
37: > entire task list to completion on its own — assigning, supervising, gating, and
38: > re-triggering its own stop. You want to *type* `/orchestrator-task-loop` (or
39: > have the orchestrator dispatch it) — not have Claude silently start an unbounded
40: > team-supervision loop because a task board happened to exist.
41: >
42: > **Experimental.** Agent Teams is disabled by default and gated behind
43: > `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`, with known limitations (no session
44: > resumption for in-process teammates, lagging task-state propagation, slow
45: > shutdown). This is a prototype-behind-the-flag skill. If the flag is unset, it
46: > refuses to start and points you at the orchestrator's subagent/sequential path
47: > (see Step 1 + the degradation note).
48: 
49: ## The 5-part contract
50: 
51: | Part | This loop |
52: |---|---|
53: | **trigger** | A populated Agent Teams shared task list (tasks `pending`/`in_progress`, team spawned) **and** `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` — or an explicit `/orchestrator-task-loop`. Dispatched by the orchestrator after contracts exist and the board is seeded (one task per contract-bounded unit, `blockedBy` edges from the dependency graph). |
54: | **action** | **One supervision pass:** read the board → for every `idle` teammate (surfaced by `TeammateIdle`), assign the highest-priority `pending` task whose `blockedBy` set is fully `completed` → let teammates work in their own context windows and claim tasks → on `completed`, run that task's `TaskCompleted` gate (**pass → done; fail → rolled back to `pending` with the failure as feedback**) → surface blocked/ambiguous tasks for HITL. **Assignment, not implementation** — the loop routes and supervises; teammates do the work. |
55: | **proof** | The board is **drained**: every task `completed` **AND** each passed its `TaskCompleted` gate **AND** no `blockedBy` edge points at a non-`completed` task — observed in one full read. Default-FAIL: assume undrained until that whole-board predicate holds. (See "The proof".) |
56: | **memory** | The **shared task list file** is the durable state (task states + `blockedBy` + gate verdicts survive across passes and fresh contexts) — this loop's "feature-list JSON," default-FAIL. Plus a **checkpoint commit per task**, the team's **peer-message log**, and an optional `PROGRESS.md` roll-up. No cross-iteration state lives in the lead's conversation. |
57: | **stop** | board drained (success) **OR** hard iteration (pass) cap **OR** the **3-failure circuit breaker** (board/task unchanged across 3 consecutive passes, or a task ping-pongs `completed`→gate-fail→`pending` ≥3×) **OR** enforced token/cost budget cap **OR** an HITL-blocked task with no unblocked work left. |
58: 
59: ## The proof: a fully drained task list, default-FAIL
60: 
61: "Done" is **not** "every teammate said they finished." It is a mechanical,
62: default-FAIL predicate over the *whole* board, re-evaluated after every pass
63: ("restart the streak"):
64: 
65: **`DONE` ⟺ for every task `t` on the board:**
66: 1. `t.state == completed`, **and**
67: 2. `t` passed its `TaskCompleted` gate (last verdict PASS — a `completed` task whose gate is unrun or failed does **not** count), **and**
68: 3. every `b ∈ t.blockedBy` is itself `completed` and gate-passing (no dangling dependency), **and**
69: 4. no `pending`/`in_progress` tasks remain, and no `idle` teammate has assignable work.
70: 
71: Each clause starts `false` and flips only on evidence read from the task-list
72: file. The proof is over the **board**, not the last task touched: completing task
73: N while a `TaskCompleted` rollback re-opened task M means the board is **not**
74: drained — only a full re-read catches it. This is exactly `loop-controller`'s
75: default-FAIL + re-verify-the-whole rule applied to a task board instead of one
76: exit code, and it is where this loop differs from a single-task loop: the verifier
77: is the *board predicate*, evaluated every pass.
78: 
79: ## Step 1 — Detect the Agent Teams runtime, or degrade
80: 
81: Before assigning anything, confirm the substrate. Precedence:
82: 
83: 1. **`CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`** must be set. If it is unset, this
84:    loop **refuses to start** — tell the user to set the flag, or hand back to the
85:    orchestrator's subagent/sequential path (the orchestrator's runtime tree:
86:    subagents via Task/Agent, else sequential). The shared task list + Teammate/Task
87:    hooks this loop relies on only exist under native Agent Teams.
88: 2. **A spawned team + a populated shared task list.** If contracts exist but the
89:    board isn't seeded, that's the orchestrator's job, not this loop's — seed one
90:    task per contract-bounded unit of work, with `blockedBy` edges from the
91:    dependency graph, *then* loop.
92: 
93: Read caps (max passes, budget), state-file paths, and the seed source from
94: `.claude/profile.yaml` / the orchestrator's plan when present — don't invent tasks
95: the plan already defines. Substrate mechanics (states, `blockedBy`, TeammateTool,
96: peer messaging, the flag, known limitations) are in `references/agent-teams.md`.
97: 
98: ## Step 2 — Assign ready work, keep idle workers fed
99: 
100: One pass routes work; it does not write code:
101: 
102: - **`TeammateIdle` = "assign new work."** When a teammate goes idle, pick the
103:   **highest-priority `pending` task whose `blockedBy` is fully `completed`** and
104:   assign it. This feeder is what keeps the loop converging instead of stalling
105:   with idle workers and undrained tasks.
106: - **Teammates work in their own context windows** and claim tasks
107:   (`pending`→`in_progress`). All coordination flows through the shared file +
108:   peer-message log — no central bottleneck.
109: - **Cap build/test parallelism per `loop-controller` Step 5.** Fan out search and
110:   read-only verification freely, but don't let two teammates run the integrated
111:   build at once — concurrent builds destroy the backpressure signal.
112: 
113: The assignment algorithm in full (priority + unblocked-set selection) is in
114: `references/hooks.md` under the `TeammateIdle` feeder.
115: 
116: ## Step 3 — Gate each completed task (the inner loop hooks in here)
117: 
118: When a teammate marks a task `completed`, its **`TaskCompleted` gate** fires —
119: this is the proof's atom and the loop's backpressure edge:
120: 
121: - **Pass → the task counts toward the proof.**
122: - **Fail (exit 2) → the task is rolled back** to `pending`/`in_progress` with the
123:   failure surfaced as feedback. A task does **not** count until its gate is green.
124: - **Each task's gate can be driven by a [`fix-until-green`] inner loop.** The
125:   teammate working a code task runs `fix-until-green` to drive its
126:   tests+lint+typecheck red→green; only then does `TaskCompleted` pass and this
127:   outer loop counts it. The outer loop **never reaches into** a task's gate — it
128:   consumes the verdict. (This is the inner/outer composition; see "Under the
129:   orchestrator".)
130: - The gate may be a **deterministic script** (mechanical bars) or an
131:   **agent-type/prompt hook** — a fresh-context evaluator subagent with **no
132:   Write/Edit tools** (`loop-controller` Step 2) for subjective bars; for
133:   build-until-spec tasks, [`contract-auditor`] is the natural evaluator.
134: - **QA-gated tasks consult the qe-agent's `qa-report.json`** (`gate_decision.proceed`,
135:   CRITICAL blockers, contract_conformance/security ≥ 3) — the same gate the
136:   orchestrator already uses. The loop **informs, it does not decide**.
137: 
138: ## Step 4 — Re-verify the whole board, checkpoint
139: 
140: After any task flips, re-scan the **entire** board against the §proof predicate —
141: not just the task that moved. "Restart the streak": a fix that completed task N
142: but a rollback that re-opened task M means the board is still undrained, and only a
143: full re-read catches it. On each task that passes its gate, **commit a checkpoint**
144: naming the task — the git trail is the loop's undo (`git reset --hard` to the last
145: green task + re-loop) and post-mortem. When the predicate holds for the whole
146: board, the loop is done; report the drained board as evidence.
147: 
148: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
149: 
150: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
151: this loop sets, mapped onto the team substrate:
152: 
153: - **Hard iteration cap** — a max number of supervision passes (read from
154:   `.claude/profile.yaml` if set). Hitting it is **stop-and-escalate**, never
155:   "lower the gate."
156: - **Enforced token/cost budget** — a ceiling that **terminates**, not just warns.
157:   Agent Teams uses 3–5×+ a single session's tokens; the documented 11-day /
158:   tens-of-thousands-of-dollars runaway had observability but no enforcement. Set
159:   per-teammate budget caps **and** a board-level ceiling.
160: - **No-progress / oscillation = the orchestrator's 3-failure circuit breaker.** If
161:   board state (or a single task) is unchanged across **3 consecutive passes**, or a
162:   task ping-pongs `completed`→gate-fail→`pending` ≥3×, **stop and escalate**. This
163:   *is* `loop-controller`'s guardrail and the orchestrator's existing breaker — one
164:   vocabulary, not a new one. See `skills/orchestrator/references/circuit-breaker.md`.
165: - **Checkpoint commit per task** — commit working state each time a task passes its
166:   gate (Step 4).
167: - **HITL for ambiguous/blocked tasks** — pause and surface any task that's blocked
168:   with no path forward, ambiguous, or about to touch something irreversible
169:   (deploy, prod DB write, external API, force-push). Don't let the loop guess past a
170:   real block.
171: - **Sandbox** for any unattended run — workspace/worktree isolation so one
172:   teammate's failure can't contaminate the board or other teammates.
173: - **Never weaken the gate.** Forbidden, each a *finding*: editing the
174:   `TaskCompleted` gate, the task list, or `qa-report.json` to mark a task done. A
175:   task that went `completed` by relaxing its gate is not progress — read the diff
176:   that flipped it.
177: 
178: **Promote to unattended/overnight ONLY when every loop in the build has all five**
179: (`safety.md` Stage 3): a hard external verifier, an *enforced* token budget,
180: no-progress detection, checkpoint commits, and a sandbox. Until then, run
181: **attended** — and because Agent Teams is experimental, stay attended until the
182: loop completes consecutive clean runs (the staged-adoption ladder in
183: `loop-controller/references/safety.md`). **Roll back to attended / kill** when:
184: token spend grows non-linearly, board state is unchanged across passes, a
185: `TaskCompleted` gate and human judgment disagree, or a task is about to touch an
186: irreversible resource without a checkpoint.
187: 
188: **Degradation (required, `requires_agent_teams: true`).** When
189: `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS` is unset, this loop does not run — fall back
190: to the orchestrator's subagent path (Task/Agent fan-out with the wave gate driving
191: each wave red→green via `fix-until-green`) or sequential mode. The proof and the
192: discipline survive; only the shared-task-list substrate and the Teammate/Task hooks
193: are unavailable.
194: 
195: ## Choosing the driver primitive
196: 
197: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, this loop's primitive is the **Stop/SubagentStop
198: hook + TeammateIdle hook combination**: the Stop hook blocks the lead's exit while
199: the board predicate (§proof) is not yet `DONE`; `TeammateIdle` keeps workers fed so
200: the board actually converges between blocks. Secondary, for interactive runs: a
201: `/goal`-style condition over the whole board ("every task `completed` and passing
202: its `TaskCompleted` gate, or stop after N passes"). The hook scripts, the
203: `stop_hook_active` guard, and the auto-conversion-to-SubagentStop note are in
204: `references/hooks.md`.
205: 
206: ## Under the orchestrator (the wiring this unblocks)
207: 
208: `loop-controller` already notes the two-level structure — an inner loop per role,
209: an outer loop over the shared task list — and says "that orchestrator wiring is a
210: separate, later skill." **This is that skill.**
211: 
212: - **Outer = this skill.** Loops over the **whole board** (assignment + supervision):
213:   drain every task to `completed` + gate-passing.
214: - **Inner = [`fix-until-green`].** Drives **one task's** gate red→green (three exit
215:   codes, no cheating). They **compose**: a task's `TaskCompleted` gate is satisfied
216:   by dispatching `fix-until-green`; the outer loop consumes the verdict.
217: - **`qe-agent` still decides the build gate.** Echoing `fix-until-green`: the loop
218:   informs, the `qe-agent`'s `qa-report.json` / `gate_decision` decides — a rollback
219:   or a circuit-breaker escalation is a real blocker, not a number to paper over.
220: - **Generalizes ralph-orchestrator:** orchestrator decomposes → spawns workers →
221:   loops the board; `validation.failed` is a worker re-trigger. Here the orchestrator
222:   is the event router and `TaskCompleted` is the `validation.passed/failed` edge,
223:   onto Agent Teams' native shared task list.
224: 
225: ## Reference files
226: 
227: - `references/agent-teams.md` — the shared-task-list substrate: states
228:   (`pending`→`in_progress`→`completed`), `blockedBy` edges, seeding one task per
229:   contract-bounded unit, teammates claiming/working in their own context windows,
230:   peer-message coordination, the drained-board predicate as a checkable routine, the
231:   `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` flag, and the known-limitations caveats.
232: - `references/hooks.md` — the three hooks in full: the `TaskCompleted` gate (payload,
233:   exit-2 rollback, backing it with `fix-until-green` or `qa-report.json`), the
234:   `TeammateIdle` feeder (assignment algorithm), and the `Stop`/`SubagentStop`
235:   re-trigger (script + `stop_hook_active` guard + auto-conversion).
236: 
237: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
238: [`fix-until-green`]: ../fix-until-green/SKILL.md
239: [`contract-auditor`]: ../../contracts/contract-auditor/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/perf-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: perf-loop
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: >-
5:   Drive a metric under its budget in a disciplined profile-optimize-reprofile
6:   loop run under REPEATABLE conditions: benchmark the whole measured set under a
7:   fixed environment, find the single highest-leverage hotspot or regression,
8:   optimize one thing, re-benchmark the WHOLE set, and repeat until the metric is
9:   under target on every measured path with no functional regression. The proof is
10:   a measured number from a named benchmark artifact, not a hunch. Use for latency,
11:   throughput, page-load, bundle-size, memory, or test-suite-speed targets, or as
12:   the performance-role inner loop under an orchestrated build. Trigger on:
13:   "optimize until under Nms", "get page load under budget", "profile and speed
14:   this up", "make it faster until target", "reduce latency loop", "performance
15:   budget", "shrink the bundle", "speed up the test suite", "benchmark until
16:   green", "perf loop", "/perf-loop". A configuration of loop-controller.
17: requires_claude_code: true
18: min_plan: starter
19: disable-model-invocation: true
20: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
21: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "fix-until-green", "performance-agent", "orchestrator"]
22: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
23: ---
24: 
25: # perf-loop
26: 
27: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
28: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
29: > externalization. This skill supplies the two things specific to "make it hit
30: > the budget": a **measured proof under repeatable conditions** (a named
31: > benchmark artifact, default-FAIL) and the **measurement discipline** that keeps
32: > a noisy benchmark from lying to the loop. Read `loop-controller` for the
33: > guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated here.
34: >
35: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop edits and commits code on its own
36: > and spends real time re-running benchmarks until a metric is under target. You
37: > want to *type* `/perf-loop` (or have the orchestrator dispatch it) — not have
38: > Claude start an autonomous optimize loop because a number looked slow.
39: 
40: ## The 5-part contract
41: 
42: | Part | This loop |
43: |---|---|
44: | **trigger** | a metric is over its budget (a slow path, a perf regression, a too-big bundle), or an explicit `/perf-loop` (optionally scoped to one path/benchmark) |
45: | **action** | ONE iteration: **benchmark the whole measured set under fixed conditions** → identify the **single highest-leverage** hotspot/regression → optimize **one thing** → **re-benchmark the WHOLE set** → re-run the functional suite |
46: | **proof** | the metric is **under target on EVERY measured path** in the benchmark output **AND** the functional suite still exits 0 — default-FAIL: assume over-budget until the benchmark, run under repeatable conditions, proves otherwise |
47: | **memory** | `perf_baseline.json` (the per-path measured numbers + variance, the durable baseline), `PROGRESS.md` (hotspot → change → delta log), a git checkpoint per measured improvement |
48: | **stop** | metric under target on every path AND suite green **OR** iteration cap **OR** no-progress for 3 rounds **OR** budget cap |
49: 
50: ## The proof: a measured number under repeatable conditions, default-FAIL
51: 
52: "Fast enough" is **not** "the profiler looked better" or "the one path I touched
53: sped up." It is **every measured path under its target in the benchmark output,
54: re-measured across the whole set, with the functional suite still green** — and
55: the benchmark must be reproducible, or the number is noise, not proof. Assume
56: **over-budget** until you have that artifact. A loop that declares victory off a
57: single fast run on a warm cache ships a regression the next cold start exposes.
58: 
59: Two rules make the proof trustworthy:
60: 
61: - **A noisy benchmark is a broken proof.** Before any number counts, the
62:   measurement must be *repeatable*: warm-up runs discarded, a fixed environment
63:   (same machine/container, no competing load, pinned data set and inputs),
64:   multiple runs, and reported **variance**. If run-to-run spread is larger than
65:   the improvement you're claiming, you have measured nothing — tighten the
66:   harness before optimizing. The repeatable-benchmark checklist and per-stack
67:   profiling/benchmark tools are in [`references/benchmark-discipline.md`].
68: - **A speedup that breaks behavior is rejected.** Every optimization re-runs the
69:   functional suite — **delegate to [`fix-until-green`]**, don't re-implement the
70:   gate. A change that drops latency but reds a test is not an improvement; it's a
71:   regression that happens to be fast. The functional gate is part of the proof,
72:   not a separate concern.
73: 
74: ## Step 1 — Read the budget and the benchmark command
75: 
76: Resolve, for *this* project, three things before looping — from
77: `.claude/profile.yaml` when present (so the same loop works across projects
78: without hard-coding):
79: 
80: 1. **the metric** (p95 latency, page-load ms, bundle KB, peak RSS, suite wall-clock);
81: 2. **the target** (the budget the metric must come in under, per measured path);
82: 3. **the benchmark command** that produces the metric under repeatable conditions.
83: 
84: If the profile doesn't declare them, detect the stack's benchmark/profiling
85: tooling ([`references/benchmark-discipline.md`] has the per-stack table) and
86: **confirm the target with the human** — an unstated budget is not a proof. Record
87: all three in `perf_baseline.json` so every iteration measures the identical thing
88: the identical way.
89: 
90: ## Step 2 — Benchmark the whole set, establish/refresh the baseline
91: 
92: Run the benchmark across **every** measured path under the fixed conditions from
93: Step 1 (warm-up discarded, multiple runs, variance recorded), and write the
94: per-path numbers to `perf_baseline.json`. This whole-set baseline is what "no
95: regression elsewhere" is checked against — a single-path benchmark cannot prove
96: the loop didn't slow down a neighbor.
97: 
98: ## Step 3 — Identify the single highest-leverage hotspot
99: 
100: From the profile, pick the **one** change with the best expected delta-per-effort
101: — the dominant hotspot or worst regression against baseline, not the first slow
102: line you see. Optimize **one thing per iteration** (`loop-controller` Step 5):
103: batching destroys the signal about which change moved the number, and perf changes
104: routinely interact. Log the chosen hotspot + hypothesis in `PROGRESS.md`.
105: 
106: ## Step 4 — Re-benchmark the WHOLE set, re-run the suite, checkpoint
107: 
108: Re-run the **entire** benchmark from scratch — not just the path you touched.
109: This is `loop-controller`'s "restart the streak": an optimization that speeds one
110: path while regressing another has made the set worse, and only a whole-set
111: re-measure catches it. Then re-run the functional suite via [`fix-until-green`]:
112: no green suite, no accepted improvement.
113: 
114: On a measured improvement (a path closer to budget, nothing regressed, suite
115: green), **commit a checkpoint** naming the hotspot fixed and the before/after
116: delta — the git trail is the loop's undo and its post-mortem. When every measured
117: path is under target and the suite is green, the loop is done; report the final
118: `perf_baseline.json` and benchmark output as evidence.
119: 
120: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
121: 
122: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The
123: caps this loop sets:
124: 
125: - **Iteration cap** — default ~10–20 optimize/re-benchmark rounds (read from
126:   `.claude/profile.yaml` if set). Hitting the cap is a *stop-and-escalate*: some
127:   budgets aren't reachable without an architectural change, which is a human
128:   decision, not a license to fake the number.
129: - **No-progress detection** — if the metric **doesn't improve** across **3
130:   consecutive iterations** (improvement smaller than the benchmark's own
131:   variance counts as no improvement), stop and escalate. Three rounds inside the
132:   noise floor means the approach is wrong or the budget needs revisiting.
133: - **Budget cap** — re-benchmarking the whole set every round is *slow* (wall-clock
134:   and, for cloud runners, money). A long perf loop is materially more expensive
135:   than a quick fix loop; terminate at the ceiling, don't just warn.
136: - **Never fake the metric.** Forbidden, and each is a *finding* if you catch it:
137:   weakening the benchmark (fewer iterations, a warmer cache, a smaller data set,
138:   dropping the slow path from the measured set), loosening the target without
139:   human sign-off, or reporting a single lucky run instead of the variance-aware
140:   number. Moving the measuring stick is not a speedup. (`loop-controller`
141:   guardrail 6, specialized to a measured proof.)
142: - **Never trade correctness for speed silently.** A faster path that reds a test
143:   is rejected by the [`fix-until-green`] re-run — that gate is non-negotiable.
144: - **HITL before irreversible.** Editing source + re-benchmarking is reversible with
145:   a hard verifier — fine unattended. A change touching an irreversible perf lever
146:   (a prod-affecting index migration, a cache-infra change) is an HITL checkpoint —
147:   pause for the human (`loop-controller` guardrail 4).
148: 
149: ## Choosing the driver primitive
150: 
151: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, by how you're running it:
152: 
153: - **Default — `/goal`:** the proof is provable from the benchmark output you
154:   surface each turn, so `/goal "the benchmark reports every measured path under
155:   its target with run-to-run variance below the margin, and the functional suite
156:   exits 0 — or stop after N turns."` The evaluator reads the benchmark numbers you
157:   surface; remember `/goal` has no native budget, so embed the turn cap.
158: - **Stop-hook gate** when you want the budget check to ship *with* the build and
159:   block exit deterministically — a perf budget enforced as a wave gate (the
160:   benchmark script runs, exit non-zero if any path is over target). The
161:   `stop_hook_active` guard pattern is in `loop-controller`'s `references/safety.md`.
162: 
163: ## Using it under the orchestrator
164: 
165: This is the **performance-role inner loop** (archetype 8). The orchestrator
166: dispatches it to drive a path under budget; it delegates the functional gate to
167: [`fix-until-green`] and the deep profiling judgment to [`performance-agent`]. The
168: orchestrator does **not** override a stuck loop — if perf-loop escalates after
169: no-progress or the cap, that's a real budget blocker (often an architectural one),
170: not a number to paper over. A satisfied `perf_baseline.json` informs the build;
171: the `qe-agent`'s `qa-report.json` still decides the gate (`loop-controller`'s
172: rule: the loop informs, the gate decides).
173: 
174: ## How this differs from its neighbors
175: 
176: - **vs. [`fix-until-green`]** — its proof is exit codes (correctness); this loop's
177:   is a *measured number under repeatable conditions* (speed). perf-loop *composes*
178:   fix-until-green as its no-regression check — speed on top of, never instead of,
179:   green.
180: - **vs. a coverage loop** — both prove a measured artifact, but perf is the only
181:   loop whose proof is trustworthy *only when the measurement itself is repeatable*
182:   — variance is a first-class concern, not a footnote.
183: 
184: ## Reference files
185: 
186: - [`references/benchmark-discipline.md`] — the repeatable-benchmark checklist
187:   (warm-up, fixed environment, multiple runs, variance, the noise floor), the
188:   per-stack profiling/benchmark tooling table (web/page-load, backend latency,
189:   bundle size, memory, test-suite speed), and the no-functional-regression rule
190:   (how perf-loop delegates to `fix-until-green`).
191: 
192: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
193: [`fix-until-green`]: ../fix-until-green/SKILL.md
194: [`performance-agent`]: ../../roles/performance-agent/SKILL.md
195: [`references/benchmark-discipline.md`]: references/benchmark-discipline.md

=== skills/loops/repo-cleanup-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: repo-cleanup-loop
3: version: 1.0.1
4: description: >-
5:   Keep a repo's git state intentional on a weekly cadence: scan every branch,
6:   PR, commit trail, and worktree, classify each as current / owned / safely
7:   removable-with-evidence, RECOVER any valuable work before deleting, then remove
8:   exactly one class of stale items with evidence — looping until every item is
9:   accounted for, with an HITL checkpoint on anything ambiguous and never a
10:   force-delete of unmerged work. The broad recurring evidence-gated loop that
11:   delegates the post-merge-branch case to git-post-merge-cleanup. Use when you
12:   want a repo kept tidy on a schedule, stale branches and worktrees pruned safely
13:   after sprints, or a weekly hygiene sweep that recovers before it deletes.
14:   Trigger on "clean up the repo weekly", "weekly repo hygiene", "prune stale
15:   branches and worktrees on a schedule", "tidy git state every week", "scheduled
16:   repo cleanup", "sweep stale PRs and worktrees". A configuration of
17:   loop-controller.
18: requires_claude_code: true
19: min_plan: starter
20: disable-model-invocation: true
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
22: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "git-post-merge-cleanup", "loop"]
23: spawned_by: []
24: ---
25: 
26: # repo-cleanup-loop
27: 
28: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
29: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
30: > externalization. This skill supplies the three things specific to "keep the
31: > repo state intentional": the **per-class recipe** (it delegates the
32: > post-merge-branch case to [`git-post-merge-cleanup`], it does not re-implement
33: > it), the **mechanical proof** (a classified inventory where nothing is
34: > "safely removable" until evidence flips it), and the **recover-before-delete
35: > HITL boundary** that makes deletion safe. Read `loop-controller` for the
36: > guardrails; they're inherited, not repeated here.
37: >
38: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop deletes branches, prunes refs,
39: > and removes worktrees on its own, on a clock — irreversible acts. It is
40: > user-driven: you want to *type* `/loop` it weekly (or run `/repo-cleanup-loop`
41: > once), not have Claude silently start pruning because it noticed debris.
42: 
43: ## The 5-part contract
44: 
45: | Part | This loop |
46: |---|---|
47: | **trigger** | a weekly cadence (`/loop` or `/schedule`) over the whole repo, or an explicit `/repo-cleanup-loop` |
48: | **action** | ONE pass: scan repo state (branches + PRs + commits + worktrees) → classify each item → **recover** anything valuable → remove **one class** of safely-removable items **with evidence** → re-scan |
49: | **proof** | the repo state is intentional — **every** branch / PR / worktree is `current` OR `owned` OR `safely-removed-with-evidence` — default-FAIL: each inventory item starts `"reviewed": false` and only flips on classification + evidence |
50: | **memory** | the classified inventory JSON (per-item: kind, name, class, evidence, `"reviewed"`, `"recovered"`), a recovery log (what was salvaged + where), and git history / reflog as the durable undo trail |
51: | **stop** | every item `reviewed` and intentional (proof) **OR** iteration/pass cap **OR** no-progress for 3 passes (same item unresolved) **OR** budget cap **OR** an HITL checkpoint on an ambiguous item |
52: 
53: ## The proof: a classified inventory, default-FAIL
54: 
55: "Tidy" is not "I deleted some merged branches." It is **every item in the repo's
56: git state accounted for**: each branch, open PR, dangling commit trail, and
57: worktree is classified `current` (active work), `owned` (someone's in-flight
58: work — leave it), or `safely-removable` **with recorded evidence** that it was
59: merged or its work recovered. Assume every item is **unreviewed** until the scan
60: classifies it — that's the default-FAIL stance, and it's why the inventory JSON
61: stores `"reviewed": false` per item rather than a prose summary. A model is far
62: less likely to quietly flip a JSON `false` than to soften a sentence.
63: 
64: Name the artifact explicitly: the inventory JSON written each pass (one record
65: per branch / PR / worktree). The pass is only done when no record reads
66: `"reviewed": false` and no record is `safely-removable` without an `evidence`
67: field. The classify / recover / remove algorithm and the exact evidence rules
68: are in `references/cleanup-algorithm.md`.
69: 
70: ## Step 1 — One pass = scan → classify → recover → remove one class → re-scan
71: 
72: The per-pass work is a single sweep, not a free-for-all:
73: 
74: 1. **Scan** the whole repo's git state — local + remote branches, open PRs (via
75:    `gh`), worktrees, and stale/dangling refs (`git worktree list`,
76:    `git branch -vv`, `git for-each-ref`, `gh pr list`).
77: 2. **Classify** each item into the inventory (default-FAIL). Evidence rules and
78:    the bucket definitions live in `references/cleanup-algorithm.md`.
79: 3. **Recover before you delete.** Any item that *looks* removable but holds work
80:    not on the default branch goes through the recovery step first — see the HITL
81:    section. This is the load-bearing safety act.
82: 4. **Remove exactly one class** of safely-removable items with evidence (e.g.
83:    "fully-merged local branches" this pass, "gone remote-tracking refs" next).
84:    One class per pass keeps the signal about what each removal changed.
85: 5. **Re-scan** and re-classify. The proof is about the *whole* state, re-checked
86:    — never trust last pass's inventory.
87: 
88: **Delegate the post-merge-branch case — do not re-implement it.** For the
89: fully-merged-local-branch, gone-remote-tracking-ref, and safe-worktree classes,
90: invoke [`git-post-merge-cleanup`] (its `--dry-run` for the scan, then its
91: confirmed execute). That skill already owns the branch/worktree classification
92: and the destructive-command ordering. This loop adds the *discipline around* it:
93: the default-FAIL whole-repo inventory, the recover-before-delete gate, the
94: one-class-per-pass cadence, and the weekly schedule. The exact delegation
95: boundary — what this loop hands off versus what it keeps — is in
96: `references/cleanup-algorithm.md`.
97: 
98: ## Step 2 — Schedule it with /loop or /schedule (the watcher, not /goal)
99: 
100: Per `loop-controller` Step 1: this is a **watch/poll** job on a weekly cadence —
101: you keep the repo's state intentional as work accrues, you do not push it to a
102: single finish line — so the primitive is **`/loop`** (session-scoped) or
103: **`/schedule`** (a cloud cron that survives laptop-off — the better fit for a
104: *weekly* cadence), **not** `/goal`. `/loop`'s session-scope / ~3-day-expiry /
105: no-catch-up mechanics live in `loop-controller`'s `references/primitives.md`.
106: 
107: ## How this differs from its neighbors
108: 
109: The one real overlap is **[`git-post-merge-cleanup`]**, and the boundary is
110: deliberate:
111: 
112: - **`git-post-merge-cleanup`** is a **focused one-shot tidy**: after a batch of
113:   PRs land, scan → classify → present a plan → act on confirmation, for three
114:   specific classes (merged local branches, gone remote-tracking refs, stale
115:   worktrees). It runs once, when you ask, and it is the *expert* on the branch
116:   and worktree classification.
117: - **`repo-cleanup-loop`** is the **broader, recurring, evidence-gated weekly
118:   loop over the whole repo** — branches *and* open PRs *and* commit trails *and*
119:   worktrees — that **recovers valuable work before deleting**, removes one class
120:   per pass, and re-scans until the whole inventory is intentional.
121: 
122: It is **not** a thin scheduled wrapper: the substance is the loop discipline, not
123: the branch logic it borrows — the default-FAIL whole-repo inventory, the
124: recover-before-delete step, one-class-per-pass, and the weekly schedule. When a
125: pass hits the merged-branch / gone-ref / safe-worktree classes it **delegates**
126: to `git-post-merge-cleanup` rather than re-implementing them. What it owns that
127: the one-shot does not: the recurring cadence, the PR + commit-trail scan, and the
128: recover-first gate.
129: 
130: ## HITL is load-bearing for this loop
131: 
132: Deletion is the irreversible act, and **recovering valuable work before deleting
133: is the load-bearing safety step.** This loop runs only inside the reversible
134: boundary unattended; anything ambiguous is a **human checkpoint — never
135: autonomous**:
136: 
137: - **Never delete a branch / worktree / ref without evidence it is safely
138:   removable** — merged into the default branch, or its work demonstrably
139:   recovered. No evidence = not removable, full stop.
140: - **Recover before delete.** An item that holds commits not on the default
141:   branch is *valuable work*, not debris. Salvage it first (a `recovery/<name>`
142:   ref or tag, the commits cherry-picked or noted, the diff archived) and log
143:   where it went — *then* it is eligible for removal. Recovery happens before the
144:   delete, every time.
145: - **Never force-delete unmerged work.** Lowercase `git branch -d` (refuses
146:   unmerged) before uppercase `-D`; `-D` only after merge is confirmed or the
147:   human explicitly asks. No `git push --force`, no `--force` worktree removal of
148:   genuinely-unmerged work, unattended.
149: - **Ambiguous item = pause.** Squash-merged-but-shows-unmerged, a stale open PR
150:   with unpushed local work, a worktree with uncommitted diffs that don't match
151:   the default branch, a long-lived `develop`/`staging`/`production` branch —
152:   surface it, do not act. The "needs attention" bucket is the signal.
153: 
154: The full AFK-safe vs pause table and the recovery recipe are in
155: `references/cleanup-algorithm.md`.
156: 
157: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
158: 
159: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
160: this loop sets:
161: 
162: - **Pass / iteration cap** — default ~10 passes per run (read from
163:   `.claude/profile.yaml` when present). One class removed per pass; hitting the
164:   cap is a *stop-and-report*, not a license to bulk-delete the rest.
165: - **No-progress detection** — if the **same item** stays `"reviewed": false`
166:   across **3 consecutive passes** (can't be classified, or can't be safely
167:   removed within the boundary), stop and surface it. Three passes on one item
168:   means it needs a human, not a fourth scan.
169: - **Budget cap** — a weekly scheduled sweep adds up over months; enforce a
170:   token/cost ceiling that *terminates* the run, not just warns.
171: - **Never cheat the proof.** Don't mark an item `safely-removable` without an
172:   `evidence` field, don't `git branch -D` to clear an unmerged item from the
173:   count, and don't skip the recovery step to "tidy faster." A clean inventory
174:   that came from force-deleting unrecovered work is a *finding*, not a win.
175: 
176: ## Choosing the driver primitive
177: 
178: `/schedule` (preferred for weekly) or `/loop` is the scheduler. The *per-pass*
179: exit — this pass classified the inventory, recovered what was valuable, and
180: removed one class with evidence — is provable from `git`/`gh` output and the
181: inventory JSON, so a pass runs unattended **within the HITL boundary above**. The
182: loop as a whole has **no `/goal` finish line**; it watches and tidies on a
183: cadence until you stop it or a stop condition fires.
184: 
185: ## Reference files
186: 
187: - `references/cleanup-algorithm.md` — the scan → classify → recover → remove
188:   algorithm, the per-class evidence rules and inventory-JSON shape, the
189:   recover-before-delete recipe, the AFK-safe vs pause table, and the exact
190:   delegation boundary to `git-post-merge-cleanup` (what to hand off, what to keep).
191: 
192: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
193: [`git-post-merge-cleanup`]: ../../git/git-post-merge-cleanup/SKILL.md

=== skills/loops/self-healing-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: self-healing-loop
3: version: 1.1.0
4: description: >-
5:   Watch a production or CI error signal on a cadence and, when an ACTIONABLE
6:   error appears, trace its root cause, fix it in a branch, verify the failing
7:   signal now passes, and open a PR for a human to merge — never auto-deploy and
8:   never auto-merge a prod hotfix. One pass polls the error source: actionable
9:   error present means hand the hard diagnosis to diagnose-loop, fix the cause,
10:   verify, open or update a PR; no actionable error means record a clean-log
11:   confirmation. Use when you want an unattended watcher over CI status, error
12:   logs, alerts, or an observability feed that heals reversible failures and
13:   escalates the rest. Trigger on "watch the logs and fix errors", "self-heal CI",
14:   "production error sweep", "poll for failures and open a fix PR", "auto-fix the
15:   red build", "watch CI and heal it", "error sweep loop", "auto-heal production
16:   errors", "/self-healing-loop". A configuration of loop-controller.
17: requires_claude_code: true
18: min_plan: starter
19: disable-model-invocation: true
20: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
21: composes_with: ["loop-controller", "observability-agent", "diagnose-loop", "fix-until-green", "loop"]
22: spawned_by: []
23: ---
24: 
25: # self-healing-loop
26: 
27: > **A configuration of [`loop-controller`].** That skill supplies the loop
28: > machinery — primitive selection, the full guardrail stack, state
29: > externalization. This skill supplies the three things specific to "watch and
30: > heal": a **poll cadence** over an external error signal, a **default-FAIL proof
31: > of the error gone** (a re-run, not a hope), and a **hard HITL boundary** at any
32: > prod-touching or irreversible fix. Read `loop-controller` for the guardrails;
33: > they're inherited, not repeated here.
34: >
35: > **Why `disable-model-invocation`:** this loop edits and commits code on its own
36: > and opens PRs on a schedule. You want to *type* `/self-healing-loop` (or wire
37: > it under `/loop`) — not have Claude silently start an autonomous heal loop
38: > because a log line looked alarming.
39: 
40: ## The 5-part contract
41: 
42: | Part | This loop |
43: |---|---|
44: | **trigger** | a scheduled poll tick (via `/loop`) over an error source — red CI, an error-log query, an alert/Channel push — or an explicit `/self-healing-loop` |
45: | **action** | ONE pass: poll the source → triage actionable vs noise → if actionable, reproduce + root-cause (hand the hard diagnosis to [`diagnose-loop`]) → fix the cause in a branch → **verify** → open/update a PR; else record a clean-log confirmation |
46: | **proof** | the error is **resolved AND verified** — the re-run of the failing signal now passes / the log query is clean — default-FAIL: assume UNRESOLVED until the re-run proves it. Artifact = the green re-run output (or, on a quiet tick, a timestamped clean-log confirmation) |
47: | **memory** | `heal_log.md` (one entry per tick: signal seen, triage verdict, root cause, the PR opened, the verifying re-run) + the fix branch's git history; the last-seen error fingerprint so a known-open error isn't re-triaged each tick |
48: | **stop** | the verifying re-run passes and a PR is open (or the tick is clean) **OR** iteration cap **OR** no-progress for N ticks on the same error **OR** budget cap **OR** an HITL checkpoint is hit (a prod-touching / irreversible fix) — pause for the human, never proceed |
49: 
50: ## The proof: the error is gone, and a re-run says so
51: 
52: "Healed" is **not** "I edited the file the stack trace pointed at." It is the
53: **failing signal re-run and observed passing** — the same test re-run green, the
54: same log query returning zero matches, the same endpoint returning 200. Assume
55: **unresolved** until that re-run exists; that is the default-FAIL stance. A fix
56: that is plausible but never re-verified is a guess, and a guess shipped to a PR
57: wastes the reviewer's time and your budget.
58: 
59: On a quiet tick (no actionable error), the proof is a **clean-log confirmation**:
60: a timestamped `heal_log.md` entry recording the source polled, the query run, and
61: the zero/green result. A watcher that records nothing on quiet ticks can't prove
62: it was actually watching.
63: 
64: ## The HITL boundary is load-bearing
65: 
66: This loop runs unattended only inside the **reversible, hard-verifier-backed**
67: envelope: reproduce in a sandbox, fix on a branch, verify, open a PR. Everything
68: that touches live state is an **HITL checkpoint** — pause and surface, never act:
69: 
70: - **never auto-deploy** a hotfix (even a "one-line" one),
71: - **never auto-merge** a PR that fixes a prod error,
72: - **never** mutate production data, live infra, secrets, or external APIs to
73:   "heal" — those are human decisions.
74: 
75: The autonomous deliverable is *a PR a human merges*, not a healed production
76: system. When the only fix is irreversible (a data backfill, a rollback deploy),
77: the loop's job ends at: reproduced, diagnosed, fix proposed, **human paged**. The
78: full poll-source options, the actionable-vs-noise triage table, and the HITL
79: boundary table are in [`references/watch-and-heal.md`](references/watch-and-heal.md).
80: 
81: ## Step 1 — Poll the error source, fingerprint what you see
82: 
83: Each tick, read the configured source (precedence: `.claude/profile.yaml` if it
84: declares one, else the project's CI/log tooling — the same feeds the
85: [`observability-agent`] instruments): CI status (`gh run list`), an error-log
86: query, or an alert/Channel push. Compute a stable **fingerprint** for
87: each error (normalized message + top frame) and compare against the last-seen set
88: in `heal_log.md` so a known-open error isn't re-diagnosed every tick. Source
89: options and example queries: [`references/watch-and-heal.md`](references/watch-and-heal.md).
90: 
91: ## Step 2 — Triage: actionable vs noise
92: 
93: Not every red line earns a fix. **Actionable** = a reproducible defect in code
94: this repo owns, with a fix that fits the reversible envelope. **Noise** = a flaky
95: infra blip, a known-open error already in a PR, a third-party outage, or a signal
96: whose fix is inherently prod-touching (→ straight to HITL). Triage first; one
97: bad triage spends an entire diagnose cycle on a ghost. The triage table is in the
98: reference file. A noise tick still gets a `heal_log.md` entry.
99: 
100: ## Step 3 — Reproduce and root-cause (delegate the hard part)
101: 
102: For an actionable error, **build a fast deterministic reproduction first**, then
103: trace the *cause*, not the first frame. This is exactly [`diagnose-loop`]'s
104: discipline — **invoke it**, don't re-implement it. Its Phase-1 binary-signal
105: reproduction is the thing that gets skipped under "just ship the fix" pressure
106: and the thing that makes the verify in Step 4 meaningful. Record the cause in
107: `heal_log.md`.
108: 
109: ## Step 4 — Fix on a branch, verify, open the PR
110: 
111: Fix the root cause on a dedicated branch (never on the default branch). Then
112: **verify**: re-run the failing signal — for a red build, this is where you
113: delegate to [`fix-until-green`] to drive the whole gate (test + lint + typecheck)
114: clean, not just the one failing test. The verifying re-run output **is** the
115: proof. Commit a checkpoint, open or update a PR describing the cause, the fix,
116: and the green re-run, and **stop there** — the human merges. Append the tick's
117: outcome (PR link + verifying run) to `heal_log.md`.
118: 
119: ## Guardrails specific to this loop
120: 
121: Inherits the full stack from `loop-controller` → `references/safety.md`. The caps
122: this loop sets:
123: 
124: - **Iteration cap** — bound the *ticks* (the `/loop` cadence is itself a cap:
125:   it's session-scoped and expires) and the heal attempts *within* a tick (read
126:   from `.claude/profile.yaml` if set). Hitting either is a *stop-and-escalate*.
127: - **No-progress detection** — if the **same error fingerprint** survives **N
128:   consecutive ticks** without the verifying re-run going green, stop and surface
129:   it. The same production error un-healed three ticks running means the fix is
130:   wrong or the cause is misdiagnosed — page the human, don't keep re-trying.
131: - **Budget cap** — a polling watcher can run for hours; each actionable tick
132:   spends a full diagnose + fix cycle. Watch `/cost`; terminate at the ceiling.
133:   Quiet ticks should be cheap — don't re-diagnose known-open errors.
134: - **HITL before anything irreversible** — the load-bearing boundary above. Any
135:   prod-touching / irreversible fix pauses for the human (`loop-controller`
136:   guardrail 4). Never auto-deploy, never auto-merge a prod hotfix.
137: - **Never weaken the signal to make the tick clean.** Forbidden, each a *finding*:
138:   muting an alert, deleting the failing test, narrowing a log query to hide the
139:   error, or marking healed without the verifying re-run. A clean log that came
140:   from silencing the check is not a heal.
141: 
142: ## Choosing the driver primitive
143: 
144: Per `loop-controller` Step 1, this is a **watch / poll** loop, so the primitive is
145: **`/loop`**, not `/goal` — it watches an external signal change on a cadence
146: rather than pushing one task to a finish line:
147: 
148: - **Canonical — `/loop` poller.** `/loop 30m /self-healing-loop` (or the cadence
149:   the source warrants) runs one heal pass per tick. `/loop` is session-scoped,
150:   expires in ~3 days, and does no catch-up — re-arm it for a standing watcher
151:   (`loop-controller`'s `references/primitives.md`). For a true always-on schedule
152:   across sessions, promote to a scheduled cloud routine.
153: - **Inside a tick**, the *heal* step degenerates to a finish-line loop — that's
154:   where [`fix-until-green`] (a `/goal` or Stop-hook loop) drives the red build to
155:   green as this loop's verifier.
156: 
157: ## Long-run hygiene (wired per loop-controller Step 6)
158: 
159: A watcher that ticks for days needs the Claude 5 long-run rules in its own
160: contract (drop-in text: `model-adaptation` → `references/long-run-hygiene.md`):
161: 
162: - **Evidence-backed progress** — "healed" is claimed only with the verifying
163:   re-run's output in `heal_log.md`; a quiet tick is claimed only with the
164:   timestamped clean-log query result. No entry, no claim.
165: - **Don't end a tick on a promise** — a tick ends with a PR opened/updated, a
166:   clean-log entry recorded, or a human paged at the HITL boundary; never with
167:   "I'll diagnose this next tick" and no triage.
168: - **Budget is a harness decision** — a poll loop that fires around the clock
169:   adds up; enforce the ceiling from `.claude/profile.yaml` in the harness and
170:   keep countdowns away from the working tick.
171: - **Effort per tick** — quiet-tick polls run at `low`/`medium`; escalate to
172:   `high`/`xhigh` only inside an actionable diagnose/fix cycle (tiering:
173:   `model-adaptation`).
174: 
175: ## How this differs from its neighbors
176: 
177: - **vs. [`diagnose-loop`]** — diagnose-loop is the *inner* reproduce-and-root-cause
178:   discipline for one hard bug. This is the *outer* watch→heal→verify→PR cycle: it
179:   adds the polling cadence, the actionable-vs-noise triage, and the prod HITL
180:   boundary, and **invokes** diagnose-loop for the diagnosis it can't shortcut.
181: - **vs. babysit (review-and-revise)** — babysit polls a *PR's review comments* and
182:   revises the diff. This polls *production / CI error signals* and opens a *new*
183:   fix PR. Different source, different deliverable; both are `/loop` pollers.
184: 
185: ## Reference files
186: 
187: - [`references/watch-and-heal.md`](references/watch-and-heal.md) — the poll-source
188:   options (CI status, error-log queries, an alert/Channel/webhook push) with
189:   example commands, the actionable-vs-noise triage table, and the HITL boundary
190:   table (what runs unattended vs what always pages a human).
191: 
192: [`loop-controller`]: ../loop-controller/SKILL.md
193: [`fix-until-green`]: ../fix-until-green/SKILL.md
194: [`diagnose-loop`]: ../../workflows/diagnose-loop/SKILL.md
195: [`observability-agent`]: ../../roles/observability-agent/SKILL.md

=== skills/meta/madness/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: madness
3: version: 1.4.0
4: description: >-
5:   The front door to the whole toolkit — one reliable entry point that reads what
6:   you want, picks the RIGHT starting skill (orchestrator, plan-builder, a loop, a
7:   role or workflow skill, or skill-explorer), and launches it for you — so you
8:   stop reaching for `orchestrator` on everything just to be sure a skill fires.
9:   Use when you don't know which skill to start with, want to kick off work but
10:   aren't sure of the entry point, or just want to summon the toolkit. Confirms
11:   before anything expensive (agent swarms, autonomous loops, full builds) and
12:   fires cheap routes immediately. Trigger on: "/madness", "madness", "where do I
13:   start", "which skill do I use", "route me", "kick this off", "get me into the
14:   right workflow", "I don't know what skill", "use the toolkit", "what's the
15:   front door", "what should I run for this". It routes and hands off — it does
16:   not do the work itself and it is not a meta-orchestrator.
17: requires_agent_teams: false
18: requires_claude_code: false
19: min_plan: starter
20: owns:
21:   directories: []
22:   patterns: []
23:   shared_read: ["skills/"]
24: composes_with: ["skill-explorer", "orchestrator", "plan-builder", "loop-controller", "use-pxpipe"]
25: spawned_by: []
26: ---
27: 
28: # Madness — the front door
29: 
30: This is the one entry point you can reach for when you don't want to think about
31: which of the dozens of skills is the right one. You say what you're trying to do;
32: `madness` figures out the correct *starting* skill, confirms if it's about to
33: launch something heavy, and hands you off into it.
34: 
35: ## Why this exists
36: 
37: The toolkit under-triggers. With dozens of skills loaded every session,
38: descriptions blur and the model often answers a request directly instead of
39: reaching for the skill that would do it better. The usual workaround is to type
40: `orchestrator` as a panic button — but `orchestrator` is a *multi-agent build*
41: entry point (contracts, role agents, QA gates). On anything that isn't a
42: parallel build it either spins up machinery you don't need or bounces you with
43: "this isn't a multi-agent build." So the crutch works, but it's the wrong shape.
44: 
45: `madness` is the right-shaped crutch: a hair-trigger router whose only job is to
46: land you in the correct front door and get out of the way. Type `/madness`, or
47: ask "where do I even start with this," and it routes.
48: 
49: ## The prime directive: route, then hand off
50: 
51: **Pick the right starting skill, launch it, and stop.** `madness` is not a
52: meta-orchestrator and must never *become* the work. It does not write the code,
53: draft the plan, or run the loop itself — it identifies which skill does that and
54: invokes it. The moment the target skill is running, `madness` is done. If you
55: find yourself doing the task instead of dispatching it, you've drifted; route and
56: hand off.
57: 
58: This is the deliberate counterpart to `skill-explorer`, which *names* a skill but
59: will not invoke it. `madness` is the active version: it names **and** launches.
60: See "madness vs skill-explorer" below for when to use which.
61: 
62: ## How a dispatch goes
63: 
64: 1. **Get the intent.** If invoked as `/madness <task>`, the argument is the
65:    intent. If invoked bare (`/madness` with nothing after it), or the intent is
66:    too vague to route confidently, show the menu (below) and ask — one question,
67:    then route. Don't guess at a heavy route from a one-word prompt.
68: 
69: 2. **Classify to a front door.** Match the intent against the entry-point map
70:    below. Route to the *front door*, not the leaf skill — `orchestrator`,
71:    `plan-builder`, and `loop-controller` each do their own internal routing, so
72:    handing off to them is enough. For the long tail the map doesn't cover, lean
73:    on `skill-explorer`'s `references/routing-table.md` (the full intent->skill
74:    table) rather than reinventing it here.
75: 
76: 3. **Decide cheap or expensive.** See the confirm gate below. This decides
77:    whether you launch immediately or check first.
78: 
79: 4. **Announce, then launch.** State the pick in one line — "This is a multi-agent
80:    build -> launching `orchestrator`." For an expensive route, end that line with
81:    a question and *wait* ("...that spins up a team and runs autonomously — go?").
82:    For a cheap route, just invoke. Use the Skill tool (or the skill's trigger
83:    phrase) to actually fire it — naming it without invoking is `skill-explorer`'s
84:    job, not this one.
85: 
86: 5. **Hand off and get out of the way.** Once the target skill is active, let it
87:    drive. Don't narrate over it or duplicate its work.
88: 
89: ## The entry-point map
90: 
91: Route intent -> front door. Each front door owns the deeper routing from there.
92: Targets marked *Claude-Code-native* (`feature-dev:feature-dev`,
93: `frontend-design:frontend-design`, `orchestrator`, the loops, the role agents)
94: exist only on Claude Code; on any other host, route to the nearest portable
95: skill in the same row instead.
96: 
97: | When the intent is... | Front door | Cost |
98: |---|---|---|
99: | Multi-agent / parallel / "swarm" / "team" build, a `MISSION.md`, coordinate a build across components | `orchestrator` | expensive |
100: | Turn research / a PRD / a goal into a build plan first | `plan-builder` (then `orchestrator`) | cheap |
101: | Keep working until something is provably true — tests green, coverage target, contract met, queue drained, overnight/autonomous, "loop until" | `loop-controller` (it picks the specific loop + primitive) | expensive |
102: | One concrete feature in an existing codebase (single-developer flow) | `feature-dev:feature-dev` (Claude-Code-native; else the matching role skill) | cheap->medium |
103: | Build one component when you already know the role | the role skill (`backend-agent`, `frontend-agent`, `infrastructure-agent`, `db-migration-agent`, ...) | cheap->medium |
104: | Design / rebuild / redesign a UI | `ui-brief` (then `frontend-design:frontend-design` on Claude Code, else `frontend-agent`) | cheap |
105: | Author or audit an integration contract | `contract-author` / `contract-auditor` | cheap |
106: | Review code, security, or deploy-readiness | `code-review-agent` / `security-agent` / `deployment-checklist` | cheap |
107: | Anything about the skills themselves — create, audit, update, sync | `skill-writer` / `skill-review` / `skill-update` / `sync-skills` | cheap |
108: | Just browsing — "what do I have", "I forgot the name", "which skill for X" (no intent to launch yet) | `skill-explorer` | cheap |
109: | Git: commit, PR, address feedback, cleanup | `git-commit` / `git-pr` / `git-pr-feedback` / `git-post-merge-cleanup` | cheap |
110: | Docs / research / deep-dive / wiki / diagram / image | `repo-deep-dive` / `llm-wiki` / `interactive-doc` / `mermaid-charts` / `nano-banana` | cheap |
111: | Onboard / profile / set up a repo or harness | `project-profiler` / `setup-project-skills` / `settings-consolidator` | cheap |
112: 
113: ## The load budget: one skill, or none
114: 
115: Route to **one** skill. Pushy descriptions mean any request touching a shared
116: term ("deploy", "review") makes several skills look plausible — but
117: plausible-on-topic is not the test. Ask which decision is actually *unresolved*
118: and route to the skill that resolves it (the "By unresolved decision" index in
119: `skill-explorer/references/routing-table.md` keys on exactly this). Add a second
120: skill only when the request genuinely contains two distinct open concerns — a
121: sequenced handoff like `plan-builder` -> `orchestrator` is one route with a next
122: step, not a second pick. An accepted token-saver offer (the confirm-gate
123: question below) also doesn't count against the budget: a user-consented
124: `use-pxpipe` side-launch is run infrastructure, not a second routing pick.
125: 
126: And **"no skill" is a legal outcome.** When no decision a skill resolves is
127: open, say so plainly and stop — a wrong-but-plausible launch costs more than
128: "nothing here needs a skill; closest miss is X, and here's the gap." If it looks
129: like a genuinely new pattern, point at `skill-writer`.
130: 
131: ## The confirm gate: cheap goes, expensive confirms
132: 
133: The split exists so a reflexive `/madness` can never *silently* spend an hour or
134: spawn a swarm. Judge by blast radius, not by category label.
135: 
136: **Expensive — confirm with a one-line "...go?" first.** Anything that spawns
137: multiple agents, runs autonomously on its own loop or schedule, kicks off a full
138: multi-component build, or is awkward to unwind: `orchestrator`, every `loops/*`
139: skill (via `loop-controller`), large `plan-builder`->`orchestrator` handoffs that
140: proceed straight into building, anything that pushes commits or opens PRs without
141: you in the seat.
142: 
143: **Cheap — just launch.** Single-skill, read-mostly, fast, and easy to walk back:
144: `skill-explorer`, a single role skill, `ui-brief`, `mermaid-charts`,
145: `contract-author`, `repo-deep-dive`, a `git-*` helper. Confirming these only adds
146: a turn of friction for no safety gain.
147: 
148: When unsure which side a route falls on, treat it as expensive and ask. The cost
149: of one needless confirm is a sentence; the cost of an unwanted swarm is real.
150: 
151: ### The token-saver question (rides the expensive confirm)
152: 
153: This question only applies on Anthropic hosts (Claude Code / Claude API), where
154: `use-pxpipe` can sit in front of the model. On any other host — DeepSeek,
155: Hermes, any non-Anthropic model — skip it entirely and go straight to the route.
156: 
157: The runs `madness` gates as expensive — swarms, autonomous loops, full builds —
158: are exactly the long, token-heavy sessions where the pxpipe proxy pays (it cuts
159: input tokens roughly in half to a third by imaging re-sent bulk; see
160: `use-pxpipe`). So when an expensive route confirms, fold one extra question into
161: the same line: *"enable the token-saver proxy for this run?"* Once per session —
162: a decline holds; don't re-ask on the next route.
163: 
164: Know when **not** to ask, so the courtesy never becomes a nag:
165: 
166: - **Cheap or short routes** — the proxy's own profitability gate passes small
167:   sessions through anyway, so the question is pure friction.
168: - **Byte-exact-critical work** — runs where history would be the sole copy of
169:   secrets, hashes, or exact numbers have the wrong loss profile for imaging.
170: - **Already answered** — the proxy is already wired (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` points
171:   at it) or the user declined earlier this session.
172: - **Model not on the allowlist** — if the session's model isn't allowed per
173:   `model-adaptation`'s *Image-proxy model allowlist*, the proxy would pass
174:   requests through uncompressed; there's nothing to offer.
175: 
176: Suggest-only, never auto-enable. On a yes, launch `use-pxpipe` to do the wiring
177: (the allowlist check and cache-warm verification are its job), then continue
178: into the route. `madness` never sets the env var itself — that would be doing
179: the work instead of routing it.
180: 
181: ## No args or vague intent -> the menu
182: 
183: If you can't route confidently, don't pick at random — show the lay of the land
184: and ask which door. Keep it to the front doors, not the whole library:
185: 
186: ```
187: Where do you want to go?
188:   • Build something — multi-agent (orchestrator) or one feature (feature-dev)
189:   • Plan it first — turn research/PRD/goal into a build plan (plan-builder)
190:   • Run a loop — work until tests pass / coverage / a contract holds (loop-controller)
191:   • Design a UI — brief first, then build (ui-brief)
192:   • The skills themselves — find / create / audit / sync (skill-explorer, skill-writer, ...)
193:   • Just browsing — "what do I have / which one for X" (skill-explorer)
194: ```
195: 
196: Then route the answer through the normal flow (classify -> cost -> launch).
197: 
198: ## madness vs skill-explorer
199: 
200: They are deliberately the two halves of one idea:
201: 
202: - **`skill-explorer`** answers "what do I have / what's it called / which one
203:   fits" and **stops at the name**. Passive. Use it to browse and decide.
204: - **`madness`** takes the intent and **launches** the right front door. Active.
205:   Use it to actually get moving.
206: 
207: So if the user is clearly just orienting ("what skills do I have for testing?"),
208: hand to `skill-explorer` — that's its job, and `madness` shouldn't take it over.
209: If the user wants to *do* the thing ("set up the tests and run them till green"),
210: `madness` routes and launches.
211: 
212: ## When NOT to use madness
213: 
214: - The user already named the skill they want (`"use ui-brief for the dashboard"`)
215:   — just run that skill; routing through `madness` is a pointless hop.
216: - The user is mid-task inside an active skill and asks a domain question — let the
217:   running skill handle it.
218: - The request is a clean, unambiguous match for one skill's own triggers
219:   ("write the OpenAPI contract", "commit this") — those skills should fire
220:   directly. `madness` is for *routing confusion and cold starts*, not for adding a
221:   hop in front of triggers that already work.
222: 
223: ## Host awareness — this skill works on every host
224: 
225: madness runs on Claude Code, Hermes, DeepSeek, and any other harness that loads
226: skills. The routing logic is host-agnostic; only the set of available target
227: skills changes. **"Not Claude Code" is never a reason to skip routing.**
228: 
229: The one host-specific step: **route to skills that actually exist here.** The
230: skills available in this session are listed in your context — check that list
231: before you name a target. Some front doors in the map below are
232: Claude-Code-native (`orchestrator`'s Agent Teams, every `loops/*` skill, the
233: role agents, and the workflows bound to the Artifact tool or `~/.claude`
234: config). On a host where those aren't present or don't run, route to the closest
235: portable skill — the git, planning, docs, review, debugging, and contract
236: workflows work everywhere — and name the gap in one line. Never answer "this is
237: Claude Code only"; pick the nearest live route.
238: 
239: ## Anti-patterns
240: 
241: | Anti-pattern | Why it fails |
242: |---|---|
243: | Doing the task yourself instead of dispatching | `madness` is a router; if it does the work it has become the meta-orchestrator it's meant to replace |
244: | Routing everything to `orchestrator` | That's the exact crutch this skill exists to retire — most intents have a lighter, correcter front door |
245: | Launching an expensive route with no confirm | A reflexive `/madness` must never silently spawn a swarm or start an overnight loop |
246: | Naming the skill but not invoking it | That's `skill-explorer`'s contract; `madness` is the active half — it launches |
