247: | Duplicating the full routing table here | The long-tail map lives in `skill-explorer/references/routing-table.md`; route to front doors and lean on it |
248: | Adding a hop in front of a clean trigger | If a request already fires the right skill on its own, get out of the way |
249: | Routing to several plausible skills at once | The budget is one; a second pick needs a second genuinely distinct open concern, and "none" beats force-fitting the closest miss |
250: | Auto-enabling the token-saver proxy, or re-asking after a no | The proxy question is suggest-once; wiring is `use-pxpipe`'s job after a yes, and repeat-asking turns a courtesy into friction |

=== skills/meta/model-adaptation/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: model-adaptation
3: version: 1.5.0
4: description: |
5:   Adapt prompts, skills, and agent scaffolding when the underlying model changes — currently the Claude 5 family (Fable 5/Mythos 5) vs Opus 4.x, plus the cross-vendor DeepSeek landscape. Stronger models need LESS scaffolding: this skill says what to PRUNE, what backfires (narrating reasoning trips a reasoning_extraction refusal), and what to add for long autonomous runs. Canonical home of the model & effort tiering policy (declared-provider ladder — Anthropic or DeepSeek; FreeLLMAPI carve-out) and the capability-handoff technique — extract an operating manual from a stronger model to run on a cheaper one. Use when migrating a skill to a new model, agents get refused, or picking model/effort per role. Trigger on "migrate to Fable", "Fable 5", "Mythos 5", "deepseek", "model migration", "reasoning_extraction", "tune effort", "model tiering", "long-running agent hygiene", "extract operating manual", "pxpipe", "image proxy".
6: requires_claude_code: false
7: min_plan: starter
8: compatibility: "Claude Code, Claude.ai, or DeepSeek hosts (e.g. Freebuff); reference/advisory skill. No special tools required — WebFetch is optional, only to re-pull the live Anthropic or DeepSeek guide."
9: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Glob", "Edit", "WebFetch"]
10: composes_with: ["skill-writer", "skill-review", "skill-update", "loop-controller", "orchestrator", "use-freellmapi", "use-pxpipe", "claude-api"]

13: 
14: # model-adaptation
15: 
16: > The toolkit's home for **what changes about your prompts, skills, and scaffolding
17: > when the underlying model changes** — currently the Claude 5 family
18: > (Fable 5 + Mythos 5) succeeding Opus 4.x, plus the cross-vendor DeepSeek
19: > landscape (V4 flash/pro). It advises and audits; it does not
20: > build. `skill-writer`/`skill-review` enforce the authoring half, `loop-controller`
21: > the long-run half, and `orchestrator` the multi-agent half — this skill is where
22: > the *why* and the migration checklist live so those enforcement points stay in sync.
23: 
24: ## Why this skill exists
25: 
26: Model-adaptation guidance is the kind that rots. It's true only relative to a model
27: generation, it cuts across every skill, and it has no natural owner — so it survives
28: as scattered asides ("Fable 5 reroutes flagged agents", a stray `/effort` note) that
29: drift out of date the moment a new model ships and nobody remembers to reconcile them.
30: This skill is the single owner. When a new model lands, you update **one** landscape
31: table here and re-run the migration audit, instead of hunting the toolkit for stale
32: assumptions. Everything model-specific is quarantined in the *Current landscape*
33: section below; the patterns are written to outlive it.
34: 
35: Source of record: Anthropic's [Prompting Claude Fable 5](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/prompting-claude-fable-5)
36: and [Prompting best practices](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/claude-prompting-best-practices);
37: for DeepSeek runs, the [DeepSeek API docs](https://api-docs.deepseek.com/quick_start/pricing/)
38: and [Thinking Mode](https://api-docs.deepseek.com/guides/thinking_mode/).
39: Re-fetch these when a new model generation ships — that's the trigger to update this skill.
40: 
41: ## The core move: a capability jump means PRUNE, not ADD
42: 
43: The reflex when a stronger model arrives is to write *more* guidance to exploit it.
44: That reflex is usually wrong. A skill written for a weaker model is a cast made around
45: that model's failure modes — enumerated do's and don'ts, rigid templates, anti-laziness
46: nagging, "show your work" instructions. A stronger model has fewer of those failure
47: modes, so the cast now **constrains** rather than supports it, and Anthropic measures
48: this as a real *degradation* in output quality, not a wash.
49: 
50: So migration is mostly **subtraction**:
51: 
52: - **A brief instruction now beats an enumeration.** Where you once listed every bad
53:   behavior by name ("don't survey options, don't over-explain root causes, don't write
54:   narrating comments…"), one short instruction plus the *reason* now steers the whole
55:   cluster. Fewer words, better result. (This is why `skill-review`'s long-standing
56:   anti-pattern — *"Excessive MUST / NEVER / ALWAYS without explaining why"* in its
57:   audit checklist — was already the right instinct; the newer model rewards it more.)
58: - **Some old instructions now actively backfire.** The sharpest example: telling the
59:   model to reproduce, echo, or explain its internal reasoning *in the response* can trip
60:   a **`reasoning_extraction` refusal** on the Claude 5 family and silently elevate
61:   fallbacks to Opus. A tactic that helped a prior model now costs you the frontier model.
62:   See *The refusal landmine* below.
63: - **Trust defaults before adding a rule.** The migration question is "does the model
64:   already do this well without being told?" If yes, delete the instruction and let the
65:   default carry it. Add scaffolding back only where measured behavior needs it.
66: 
67: The opposite is also true in one direction: long *autonomous* runs need **new**
68: scaffolding the shorter-turn era didn't (async harness, evidence-backed progress, a
69: send-to-user channel). Subtract the prescription; add the long-run hygiene. See
70: `references/long-run-hygiene.md`.
71: 
72: ## Current landscape (update this section when a new model ships)
73: 
74: This is the *only* part meant to age. The patterns below it are durable; these facts are not.
75: 
76: | Model | Role today | What to know for adaptation |
77: |---|---|---|
78: | **Fable 5** | Frontier (Claude 5 family) | Long-horizon autonomy (multi-day runs), strong first-shot correctness, dispatches parallel subagents readily, high bug-finding recall. Runs safety classifiers (offensive cyber, bio/life-sciences, frontier-LLM development, reasoning-extraction) → can return `stop_reason: "refusal"`. Adaptive thinking only; no extended-thinking budgets; summarized-only thinking output. |
79: | **Mythos 5** | Frontier sibling (Claude 5 family) | Same family, same prompting patterns and refusal behavior as Fable 5. Everything here that says "Fable 5" applies to Mythos 5 unless a future note says otherwise. |
80: | **Opus 4.8** | Prior baseline **and the fallback target** | The model a refused Claude 5 request should reroute to. Prompts/skills tuned for it are the ones this skill helps you prune. |
81: 
82: ### DeepSeek landscape (cross-vendor entry)
83: 
84: The toolkit also runs under DeepSeek models — the 2026-08-03 deep-dive vendor review
85: (DV-1–DV-5) was a Freebuff/DeepSeek session, and this is the first vendor this skill
86: covers besides Anthropic. The aging facts (pricing, model versions, effort mapping)
87: live in `references/deepseek-adaptation.md`; the durable shape is here:
88: 
89: | Model | Role today | What to know for adaptation |
90: |---|---|---|
91: | **deepseek-v4-flash** | Cheap tier | Non-thinking + thinking modes; tool calls, JSON output, Anthropic-format endpoint (`api.deepseek.com/anthropic`). Cache-hit input is ~30× cheaper than cache-miss — prompt-cache hits are the dominant cost lever. |
92: | **deepseek-v4-pro** | Top tier | Same feature set, stronger reasoning; the reasoning-gate tier. 1M context / 384K max output across the family. |
93: 
94: On DeepSeek the capability dial is **thinking mode + effort** (`none/low/high/max`;
95: `xhigh` and `medium` both map to `high`), not a model switch — and the chain of
96: thought arrives in `reasoning_content`, a separate channel from the answer. There is
97: **no `reasoning_extraction` refusal classifier** and **no Opus fallback**: refusals
98: retry inside the DeepSeek ladder (toggle thinking, switch flash↔pro). Body triggers
99: that route here but stay out of the description (lint band): **"deepseek-reasoner"**,
100: **"R1"**, **"V4"**, **"thinking mode"**, **"cross-vendor run"**.
101: 
102: When "the next model" (a Mythos successor, an Opus 5, a DeepSeek V5) arrives:
103: re-fetch the vendor's current docs, update this table **and**
104: `references/deepseek-adaptation.md`, and run the *migration audit* at the bottom.
105: 
106: ## The pattern catalog
107: 
108: Each pattern: what changed, the toolkit move, and where it's enforced so it can't drift.
109: Depth lives in the two reference files; this is the map.
110: 
111: ### Bucket A — Authoring hygiene (enforced in `skill-writer` / `skill-review`)
112: 
113: | Pattern | What changed | The toolkit move | Enforced in |
114: |---|---|---|---|
115: | **Prune over-prescription** | Prior-model prescription degrades Claude 5 output | On migration, delete enumerations/rigid templates the model no longer needs; keep the *why*, drop the MUSTs | `skill-review` anti-pattern checklist (*"Excessive MUST/NEVER/ALWAYS"*, *"Overly rigid templates"*) — now flagged as a **model-driven** re-review trigger, not just static smell |
116: | **Reasoning-extraction refusal** | Telling the model to narrate/echo its reasoning as response text now trips a refusal | Never instruct "show your thinking / explain your reasoning in the output"; read structured `thinking` blocks or use a send-to-user tool instead | `skill-review` audit checklist (new anti-pattern) → detail in `references/refusal-and-fallback.md` |
117: | **Brief instruction > enumeration** | One instruction + the reason steers a whole behavior cluster | Prefer a short "why" over naming every behavior; don't carry the *description* field's "pushy / over-enumerate" style into *behavioral* instructions | `skill-review` audit checklist (*"Excessive MUST/NEVER/ALWAYS"* + the prior-model over-prescription trigger); `references/long-run-hygiene.md` has the drop-in brevity instruction |
118: | **Give the reason, not only the request** | The model connects the task to context better when it knows intent | Already a scored `skill-review` rubric dimension (*"explains WHY, not just WHAT"*) — the Claude 5 family rewards it more | `skill-review` deep-review rubric |
119: 
120: The one boundary to hold explicit: the **"pushy / over-enumerate"** philosophy is correct
121: for the frontmatter `description` (the trigger slot — you *want* to over-enumerate
122: contexts to combat under-triggering) and **wrong** for the skill body's behavioral
123: instructions (where over-enumeration is exactly the prescription that degrades the newer
124: model). Same word, opposite slots. `skill-writer` and `skill-review` keep these apart.
125: 
126: ### Bucket B — Long-running agents (enforced in `loop-controller` / the loops)
127: 
128: Full drop-in instructions and where each plugs into the 5-part loop contract:
129: **`references/long-run-hygiene.md`**. Summary:
130: 
131: | Pattern | What changed | The toolkit move |
132: |---|---|---|
133: | **Longer turns by default** | A single request can run minutes; autonomous runs, hours | Structure harnesses to check **asynchronously** (scheduled/poll), not block; lengthen timeouts; add progress indicators |
134: | **Ground progress claims** | Long runs can emit *fabricated* status reports | Instruct: audit each progress claim against an actual tool result before reporting it (distinct from the anti-*gamed-gate* rule loops already have) |
135: | **Don't end a turn on a promise** | Deep in a run, the model can say "I'll now run X" with no tool call, or pause to ask when it has enough | Add a last-paragraph self-check + an autonomous-operation reminder to the loop prompt |
136: | **Context-budget reassurance** | Surfacing a token countdown makes the model prematurely summarize / suggest a new session | Don't show the model raw budget counts; if you must, add "you have ample context, don't stop" |
137: | **Fresh-context verifier** | Fresh verifiers beat self-critique | **Already covered** — `loop-controller`'s GAN / Plan-Generate-Evaluate evaluator is exactly this. Cite it; don't reinvent |
138: | **Send-to-user tool** | Long async agents need to surface verbatim content mid-turn | Give the agent a client-side `send_to_user` tool + elicitation language |
139: | **Effort as the primary dial** | Effort trades intelligence/latency/cost; low effort on Claude 5 can beat xhigh on prior models | Pick effort per task: `high` default, `xhigh` for the hardest, `medium`/`low` for routine |
140: 
141: ### Bucket C — Multi-agent coordination (enforced in `orchestrator`)
142: 
143: | Pattern | What changed | The toolkit move | Enforced in |
144: |---|---|---|---|
145: | **Async, long-lived subagents** | Fable 5 dispatches subagents readily; blocking on the slowest one wastes its strength | Prefer async orchestrator↔subagent comms and long-lived subagents (cache reuse) over a strict block-on-every-reply relay, where the build allows | `orchestrator` coordination rules + its `references/workflow-orchestration.md` |
146: | **Effort per wave/role** | Effort is the main capability dial, not just the ultracode switch | Set `xhigh` for the hardest agent (contracts, adversarial verify), `low`/`medium` for routine (docs, mechanical edits) | `orchestrator` runtime section |
147: | **Refusal reroute contract** | A flagged agent returns `stop_reason: "refusal"` and reroutes to Opus 4.8 | Treat as expected routing; **configure** the server/client fallback rather than assuming it; note the security-agent implication | `orchestrator` + `references/refusal-and-fallback.md` |
148: | **State the boundaries / don't over-refactor at high effort** | At high effort the model may tidy/refactor beyond the ask, or act when only asked to assess | When the ask is a question or a "thinking out loud", the deliverable is the assessment — report and stop; scope refactors to the task | `orchestrator` coordination rules |
149: 
150: ## Model & effort tiering (the cost doctrine)
151: 
152: Buckets B and C treat *effort* as the primary capability dial. This section is the
153: fuller doctrine — **model and effort chosen together, per task, not per project** —
154: and it is canonical here: `orchestrator` (per-role dispatch and Workflow-mode
155: stages), `loop-controller` (Step 6), and `use-freellmapi` point at this section
156: rather than restating it.
157: 
158: **The principle.** Cost leaks when a premium model does bulk work — fan-out
159: crawls, boilerplate, mechanical edits, first drafts — that a cheaper model in
160: the same family does just as well. Reserve the top tier for the load-bearing
161: reasoning: architecture/contract design, adversarial verification, final
162: synthesis, hard debugging. Two dials on the same call: `model` (the cheapest
163: tier that clears the task's quality bar) and `effort` (`low`→`max`; lower it for
164: routine passes, raise it only for the hardest reasoning). Output tokens cost
165: ~5× input across the Anthropic family, so moving bulk work down a tier and
166: trimming output dominate every other cost lever.
167: 
168: **The provider-relativity rule (load-bearing).** One project, one provider
169: ladder — never mix vendors to save tokens. A project *declares* its provider
170: and runs the whole toolkit on that one vendor's ladder:
171: 
172: - **Default = Anthropic-native.** The ladder is Haiku → Sonnet → Opus → Fable,
173:   plus the effort dial. Read a project's declared provider from
174:   `.claude/profile.yaml`; absent that, assume Anthropic.
175: - **DeepSeek-native.** The ladder is `deepseek-v4-flash` → `deepseek-v4-pro`,
176:   with thinking mode + effort (`none/low/high/max`) as the dial within each.
177:   Full facts in `references/deepseek-adaptation.md`.
178: - **The doctrine is a shape** — cheapest-that-clears-the-bar for grunt work, top
179:   tier for the reasoning gate — instantiated with whatever single provider the
180:   project actually runs on, staying inside that provider's own ladder.
181: - **FreeLLMAPI is the only multi-provider carve-out** (see `use-freellmapi`): it
182:   deliberately aggregates free provider tiers behind one endpoint, the scarce
183:   resource is rate/quota rather than dollars, and the aggregation *is* the ladder.
184: 
185: **Task → tier map** (the durable part; the priced ladder lives in the reference):
186: 
187: | Task class | Examples | Model | Effort |
188: |---|---|---|---|
189: | **Mechanical / high-volume** | file transforms, migration edits, formatting, lint-fix application, boilerplate, broad research crawl, first drafts | Haiku, or Sonnet if it needs light reasoning | low/medium (none on Haiku) |
190: | **Standard implementation** | feature code, test authoring, straightforward role-agent build work | Sonnet | medium/high |
191: | **Load-bearing reasoning** | architecture & contract design, adversarial verification / fresh-context evaluator, final synthesis, hard debugging, ambiguity resolution | Opus or Fable | high/xhigh (max only when correctness ≫ cost) |
192: 
193: On a DeepSeek project the same map instantiates as: `deepseek-v4-flash` with
194: thinking off for mechanical work, flash/pro with thinking on for standard
195: implementation, `deepseek-v4-pro` with thinking high/max for load-bearing
196: reasoning — the two-model family *is* the ladder.
197: 
198: **The optimizer/target split.** When one model *authors or optimizes* an
199: artifact (a skill, a prompt, a config) that another model then *executes
200: under*, tier by **role**, not just task difficulty: author/optimize/review on
201: the strong tier, execute/validate on the cheap tier. microsoft/SkillOpt's
202: cross-model study measured why this is the economical direction — the same
203: optimized skill gained roughly **2× more** on a weaker execution model (more
204: headroom), so "cheap deployed target + one strong optimizer" beats "strong
205: everywhere." Detail in `references/model-effort-tiering.md`.
206: 
207: **Guardrails:**
208: 
209: - **No `effort` param on Haiku 4.5** — the API returns a 400. Tier down to
210:   Haiku *or* dial effort down, not both.
211: - **DeepSeek effort semantics differ.** The API maps `xhigh`→`high` and
212:   `medium`→`high`; emit `none/low/high/max` (`none` = thinking off), and don't
213:   bother with `xhigh`. Thinking mode ignores `temperature`/`top_p`/penalties
214:   (accepted, no effect).
215: - **Don't reflexively `max`.** On the Claude 5 family `high`/`xhigh` is the
216:   sweet spot, and `low` effort often matches or beats prior-generation
217:   `xhigh`/`max` — so `low`/`medium` is the correct setting for routine work,
218:   not a compromise.
219: - **Pass `model` and `effort` explicitly on every Agent/Workflow spawn.**
220:   Per-agent defaults resolve to the *session-start* model, which goes stale the
221:   moment the user runs `/model` — the subagent-model footgun. This deliberately
222:   overrides the Workflow tool's generic "omit `opts.model` by default" guidance.
223: - **Conciseness ≠ reasoning suppression.** Output-trimming (`caveman`-style) is
224:   fine; instructing the model to expose its reasoning in the response trips the
225:   `reasoning_extraction` refusal (see the landmine below).
226: 
227: The priced Anthropic ladder (model IDs, $/1M, effort support), the
228: billing-surface table, provider-relative instantiation, and the per-consumer
229: wiring live in **`references/model-effort-tiering.md`** — like the *Current
230: landscape* table above, its model and pricing facts age; update both when a new
231: model ships.
232: 
233: ## Image-proxy model allowlist
234: 
235: `use-pxpipe` puts an image proxy in front of a session: the bulky, re-sent parts
236: of every request are rendered into dense PNGs, because image tokens are priced by
237: pixel area rather than characters. That only works if the model behind the proxy
238: can actually *read* the render — and a model that misreads dense glyphs doesn't
239: error, it produces **confident wrong answers from garbled input**. Because the
240: failure is silent, the gate is governed here, in one place, fail-closed:
241: 
242: - **Only allowlisted models may sit behind an image proxy.** An unlisted or
243:   newly released model defaults to *not allowed*, no matter how capable it is
244:   otherwise — read fidelity on dense renders is a distinct, measured property.
245: - **A model earns its slot by passing pxpipe's ~20-call glyph sweep** — cheap,
246:   minutes of work. Re-run it on every model release, the same trigger that
247:   updates the *Current landscape* table above.
248: - **This axis is independent of the tiering ladder.** A model can be exactly the
249:   right execution tier per the optimizer/target split above and still be barred
250:   from the proxy. Choose model + effort from the tiering doctrine, then check
251:   this allowlist before enabling the proxy — two separate gates.
252: 
253: The current allowlist — which models pass today and their measured read rates —
254: ages with the model generation, so it lives with the other aging facts in
255: **`references/model-effort-tiering.md`** (*Image-proxy allowlist — current
256: state*). `use-pxpipe` treats that list as its safety gate: it owns the wiring,
257: this skill owns the policy.
258: 
259: ## Capability handoff (extract an operating manual)
260: 
261: When a stronger model is leaving the plan — or you want a cheaper model to run
262: with a stronger model's discipline — extract an **operating manual of working
263: procedures** from the strong model and run it as the cheap model's system
264: prompt. The mechanic is real but bounded: a manual ports *discipline*, not
265: *capability* — the prompt-level cousin of the optimizer/target split above.
266: The hardened extractor (`scripts/extract_operating_manual.py`), an
267: already-extracted manual (`references/operating-manual.md`, reusable as-is —
268: on-demand only, never injected into Claude Code sessions), the three
269: refusal/continuation/framing hardenings, and the corrected cost note live in
270: **`references/capability-handoff.md`**.
271: 
272: Also reach for this section on **"fable handoff"**, **"capability handoff"**,
273: **"model leaving plan"**, or **"model is going away"**. These triggers live in
274: the body rather than the description because the description is held under the
275: 950-char lint band — a body hit still routes here once the skill is open.
276: 
277: ## The refusal landmine (read this even if you read nothing else)
278: 
279: For a toolkit that **authors** prompts and skills, the highest-consequence change in the
280: Claude 5 family is a class of instruction that now causes a **refusal**:
281: 
282: > Instructions that tell the model to **echo, transcribe, or explain its internal
283: > reasoning as response text** can trigger the `reasoning_extraction` refusal category
284: > on the Claude 5 family, returning `stop_reason: "refusal"` and silently elevating
285: > fallbacks to Opus 4.8.
286: 
287: The danger is that a skill-authoring toolkit can bake this into *every* skill it produces
288: ("narrate your reasoning", "show your thinking step by step in the output", "explain your
289: chain of thought"). If your app needs reasoning visibility, read the structured `thinking`
290: blocks from adaptive thinking, or surface progress with a send-to-user tool — never ask
291: the model to reproduce its reasoning in the response. `skill-review` now audits for this;
292: the full mechanics, the other classifier domains (offensive cyber, bio/life-sciences,
293: frontier-LLM development), and the fallback configuration are in
294: **`references/refusal-and-fallback.md`**.
295: 
296: **On DeepSeek the classifier doesn't exist, but the instruction is still wrong.**
297: Thinking mode already emits the chain of thought in `reasoning_content` (a separate
298: channel), so asking the model to also narrate its reasoning into the response doubles
299: output tokens and buries the answer — a cost/quality failure instead of a refusal.
300: Read `reasoning_content` (or the harness's structured thinking) when you need the
301: reasoning; and since there is no Opus fallback on DeepSeek, reroute refusals inside
302: the DeepSeek ladder (toggle thinking, switch flash↔pro). See
303: `references/deepseek-adaptation.md`.
304: 
305: ## When a new model lands: the migration audit
306: 
307: Run this checklist against an existing skill/harness (or the whole toolkit) on a model change:
308: 
309: 1. **Re-fetch the guide.** Pull the vendor's current docs — Anthropic's prompting
310:    guide, or the DeepSeek API docs for a DeepSeek project — and update the *Current
311:    landscape* table above **and the priced ladder in
312:    `references/model-effort-tiering.md`** (models, pricing, effort support all age).
313:    New behaviors = new audit items.
314: 2. **Subtract first.** For each skill, ask per instruction: *does the new model already do
315:    this well without being told?* If yes, cut it. Rigid templates, anti-laziness nags, and
316:    long enumerations are the first candidates.
317: 3. **Hunt the refusal landmine.** Run the **audit recipe** in
318:    `references/refusal-and-fallback.md` — that file owns the canonical grep (don't
319:    inline a variant here; divergent copies of the sweep are exactly the drift this
320:    skill exists to prevent). Every hit that routes reasoning to the *response* is a
321:    refusal risk — rewrite it.
322: 4. **Check the long-run scaffolding.** For any loop/autonomous skill, confirm the
323:    `references/long-run-hygiene.md` patterns are wired: evidence-backed progress, the
324:    last-paragraph check, context-budget reassurance, effort selection, send-to-user.
325: 5. **Check the coordination scaffolding.** For the orchestrator, confirm effort-per-wave,
326:    async/long-lived subagents, and the refusal→fallback contract.
327: 6. **Re-benchmark, don't assume.** If the toolkit has evals (`skill-creator`), re-run them
328:    with and without the pruned instructions. Keep the cut only if quality holds or improves —
329:    the model updating its own approach on the fly is often better than the old rule.
330: 
331: ## Reference files
332: 
333: - `references/deepseek-adaptation.md` — the cross-vendor DeepSeek landscape: the
334:   V4 flash/pro family, thinking-mode mechanics (`reasoning_content`, the
335:   Anthropic-format endpoint, tool-call pass-back), the effort mapping, the
336:   provider-relative tiering instantiation, the image-proxy status, and the audit
337:   additions for DeepSeek runs. Read when a project declares DeepSeek, or when
338:   adapting any skill for DeepSeek execution.
339: - `references/refusal-and-fallback.md` — the four Claude 5 classifier domains, the
340:   `reasoning_extraction` landmine in depth (what trips it, the symptom, the fix, how to
341:   audit for it), the `stop_reason: "refusal"` contract, and server-side vs client-side
342:   fallback to Opus 4.8. Read when a skill gets refused, when writing security-agent
343:   prompts, or when auditing authored skills for the landmine.
344: - `references/long-run-hygiene.md` — the long-running-agent behavioral patterns with
345:   drop-in instruction text (verbatim from Anthropic where they published it) and where
346:   each plugs into `loop-controller`'s 5-part contract: async turns, evidence-backed
347:   progress, last-paragraph check, context-budget reassurance, the send-to-user tool,
348:   effort tiers, and user-facing readability. Read when authoring or migrating any loop.
349: - `references/model-effort-tiering.md` — the aging half of the tiering doctrine: the
350:   priced Anthropic ladder (model IDs, $/1M, effort support), the output-token asymmetry
351:   and billing-surface table, provider-relative instantiation, the current image-proxy
352:   allowlist (which models pass the glyph sweep today), and how each consumer
353:   (orchestrator dispatch, Workflow-mode stages, loop-controller Step 6, use-freellmapi,
354:   use-pxpipe) wires the policy in. Read when assigning model+effort to roles/stages/loops,
355:   when enabling the image proxy, or when a new model ships and the ladder needs updating.

=== skills/meta/skill-catalog/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: skill-catalog
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: |
5:   Keep the Skill-Madness catalog in sync with the filesystem. The published
6:   plugin.json `skills` array and every advertised skill count (README, CLAUDE.md,
7:   PLAN.md, START-HERE.md, marketplace.json) are DERIVED from disk by one command,
8:   so they never drift. Use this whenever you add, remove, or rename a skill,
9:   register a skill in plugin.json, notice mismatched skill counts ("47 vs 49"),
10:   hit a CI "catalog drift" failure, ask "how many skills are there", or need
11:   skills symlinked into ~/.claude for global use. The filesystem is the single
12:   source of truth: run `scripts/catalog.sh --sync` (a pre-commit hook also
13:   auto-fixes drift on commit), then `sync-skills` for the global symlinks. Never
14:   hand-edit the counts or the plugin.json array — let the tooling regenerate them.
15: compatibility: Claude Code
16: requires_claude_code: true
17: requires_agent_teams: false
18: min_plan: starter
19: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Bash", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
20: owns:
21:   directories: []
22:   patterns: []
23:   shared_read: ["*"]
24: composes_with: ["sync-skills", "skill-writer", "skill-update", "skill-review"]
25: ---
26: 
27: # Skill Catalog
28: 
29: > **One rule:** the filesystem under `skills/` is the single source of truth for
30: > what skills exist and how many there are. Everything else — the `plugin.json`
31: > manifest, the README badge, the counts in `CLAUDE.md` / `PLAN.md` /
32: > `START-HERE.md` / `marketplace.json` — is *derived* from it. You never edit
33: > those by hand; you run one command and they regenerate.
34: 
35: This skill exists because the alternative — hand-maintaining a skill count in
36: six files plus a manifest array — drifts every single time. A skill gets added,
37: the array isn't updated, three of the six counts get bumped and three don't, and
38: now the repo disagrees with itself. The fix is to stop treating those numbers as
39: facts to maintain and start treating them as *outputs* to generate.
40: 
41: ## The one command
42: 
43: ```bash
44: scripts/catalog.sh --sync     # make every derived count + the plugin array match disk
45: scripts/catalog.sh --check    # verify they match (exit 1 + a drift report if not); CI runs this
46: scripts/catalog.sh --text     # print the per-category table (disk truth)
47: ```
48: 
49: `--sync` is **idempotent** — run it as often as you like; if nothing drifted it
50: changes nothing. It is safe to run any time.
51: 
52: ## What it keeps in sync
53: 
54: | Artifact | What's reconciled |
55: |---|---|
56: | `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` `skills` array | every on-disk skill is registered; stale entries (deleted skills) are removed; your curated order is preserved and new skills are appended to their category group |
57: | `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` description | the "with N skills" count |
58: | `README.md` | badge, all prose counts, the per-category mermaid labels |
59: | `CLAUDE.md` | the "N OSS-publishable skills" line |
60: | `PLAN.md` | the "**N-skill** library" line |
61: | `START-HERE.md` | the "library of **N skills**" line |
62: | `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` | the two "N-skill" / "all N" phrases |
63: 
64: Only the **live** count phrasings are touched. Historical notes — `PLAN.md`'s
65: "catalog reshaped to 47 skills", "across 49 skills", etc. — use different wording
66: on purpose and are left exactly as written. They're a record of what was true
67: then, not an assertion about now.
68: 
69: ## What counts as a skill
70: 
71: A directory directly under a category (`skills/<category>/<skill>/SKILL.md`) or
72: the top-level `skills/orchestrator/SKILL.md`. **Not** a skill: anything under
73: `archive/`, `in-progress/`, or `node_modules/` — including the `SKILL.md`
74: scaffolds that some bundled npm packages (e.g. `playwright-core`) ship deep
75: inside a skill's `scripts/node_modules/`. The scan is depth-bounded so those
76: never inflate the count. (This is why the count used to read "52" locally after
77: an `npm install` but "50" in CI — that bug is gone.)
78: 
79: ## You usually don't run it at all
80: 
81: A `catalog-sync` pre-commit hook (`hooks/scripts/catalog-sync.sh`, registered in
82: `hooks/hooks.manifest.json`) intercepts `git commit`. If — and only if — the
83: catalog has drifted from disk, it runs `--sync` and **re-stages** the corrected
84: files so the commit is self-consistent. On an in-sync repo it does nothing. So
85: in practice: add your skill directory, commit, and the counts + manifest are
86: already correct in that commit. The drift can't land.
87: 
88: CI is the backstop: the "Installer + Catalog" job runs `catalog.sh --check`, so
89: even a commit made outside Claude Code (a terminal commit with the hook profile
90: off) is caught before merge.
91: 
92: ## Workflows
93: 
94: ### Add a skill
95: 
96: 1. Create `skills/<category>/<new-skill>/SKILL.md` (use the `skill-writer` skill
97:    to scaffold it correctly).
98: 2. Commit. The hook reconciles the manifest + counts into your commit. (Or run
99:    `scripts/catalog.sh --sync` first if you want to see the changes before
100:    committing.)
101: 3. Run `sync-skills` to symlink it into `~/.claude/skills/` so it's available
102:    globally — see "Global availability" below.
103: 
104: ### Remove or rename a skill
105: 
106: Delete or rename the directory, then commit (or `--sync`). The reconciler drops
107: the stale `plugin.json` entry and registers the new path; the counts fall or hold
108: automatically. A rename is just a remove + add.
109: 
110: ### "The counts are wrong" / a CI catalog failure
111: 
112: Run `scripts/catalog.sh --check` to see exactly which files disagree and by how
113: much, then `scripts/catalog.sh --sync` to fix all of them at once, and commit.
114: Don't open the files and edit numbers — that's how the drift started.
115: 
116: ## Global availability (symlinks) — `sync-skills`
117: 
118: Catalog sync is about the *repo's* internal consistency. Making the skills
119: actually loadable by Claude Code / Cursor is a separate axis: symlinking the
120: skill directories into `~/.claude/skills/` (and `~/.cursor/skills-cursor/`). That
121: is the `sync-skills` skill's job. The two compose into one "I added a skill"
122: story:
123: 
124: ```bash
125: scripts/catalog.sh --sync     # repo agrees with itself (counts + manifest)
126: # … then, via the sync-skills skill:
127: /sync-skills                  # the skill is linked into the global dirs
128: ```
129: 
130: Run catalog sync first (so the manifest is right), then `sync-skills` (so the
131: filesystem links are right). `sync-skills` also has a `--status` mode to answer
132: "are my skills linked?" the same way `catalog.sh --check` answers "are my counts
133: right?".
134: 
135: ## Anti-patterns
136: 
137: - **Hand-editing a skill count anywhere.** The moment you type a number into
138:   `CLAUDE.md` or `plugin.json`, you've created a second source of truth that will
139:   drift. Run `--sync` instead.
140: - **Hand-adding a `plugin.json` `skills` entry.** Same problem, and easy to
141:   forget (that's the bug where a freshly-added skill silently never publishes).
142:   The reconciler registers it from disk.
143: - **Committing with `--no-verify` or the hook profile off, then wondering why
144:   CI fails on "catalog drift."** The check is there to catch exactly that. Run
145:   `--sync` and recommit.
146: - **Bumping the total but not the per-category breakdown** (the curated
147:   `skills/workflows/ (N) — name, name, …` list in `CLAUDE.md`). The *counts*
148:   sync automatically; that one enumerated prose list is still curated by hand —
149:   update it when you add a workflow skill, or the number and the names will
150:   disagree.
151: 
152: ## Reference
153: 
154: - `scripts/catalog.sh` — the count + invariant engine (bash; the CI gate).
155: - `scripts/sync-catalog-skills.py` — the `plugin.json` `skills` array reconciler.
156: - `hooks/scripts/catalog-sync.sh` — the auto-fix-on-commit hook.
157: - `contracts/installer/catalog-invariant.md` — the catalog-as-CI-invariant contract.

=== skills/meta/skill-explorer/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: skill-explorer
3: version: 1.4.0
4: description: |
5:   Help the user discover, recall, understand, and pick the right skill from the available toolkit. Names the skill; does NOT invoke it. Use when the user is trying to find a skill ("I forgot the name of the one that does X", "what was that skill called"), asking what skills exist ("what skills do I have", "list all my skills", "show me the catalog"), asking what a specific skill does ("what does X do", "explain the X skill"), asking how skills relate ("how do these connect", "what does orchestrator spawn"), or asking for routing help ("which skill for this task", "what should I use to Y"). Also trigger when the user reaches for orchestrator on something that isn't a multi-agent build, or asks any meta-question about the skill ecosystem itself.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["skills/"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Glob", "Bash"]
14: composes_with: ["skill-review", "skill-writer"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Skill Explorer
19: 
20: The user has accumulated a large toolkit (40+ repo skills plus plugin skills loaded into every session). Names blur together, descriptions overlap, and reaching for the wrong entry point (typically `orchestrator`) wastes a turn before getting redirected. This skill is the deliberate entry point for "what do I have / which one is right for this".
21: 
22: **skill-explorer vs its siblings.** Use `skill-explorer` (this skill) to *discover, recall, explain, and route* — it names the right skill and stops, leaving you to fire it. Use `madness` when you want that routing decision *acted on* — it picks the entry point and launches it (confirming first on anything expensive). Use `skill-catalog` when you want the authoritative generated inventory/counts of what is installed, not a recall or routing answer. (There is no `find-skills` skill in this repo; that name belongs to a separately-installed plugin.)
23: 
24: **Host awareness.** This skill runs on any host that loads skills — Claude Code, Hermes, DeepSeek, and others. The routing table and rules below contain plugin-namespaced refs (`superpowers:*`, `claude-mem:*`, `claude-obsidian:*`, `feature-dev:*`, `frontend-design:*`) and Claude Code slash commands that exist only in a Claude Code setup. On any other host, treat those as placeholders: name the closest repo skill that is actually loaded in this session, and say so plainly if none covers it. Never report a plugin skill as available when it isn't.
25: 
26: It answers four kinds of question:
27: 
28: 
29: | Mode        | Triggered by                                                         | Output                                                                                       |
30: | ----------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
31: | **Recall**  | "what was that skill called", "I forgot the name of the one that..." | The skill name, in code, plus one sentence on what it does                                   |
32: | **Catalog** | "what skills do I have", "list all my skills", "show me the toolkit" | Grouped list, one line per skill                                                             |
33: | **Explain** | "what does X do", "tell me about X", "when should I use X"           | Purpose, when it triggers, what it produces, related skills                                  |
34: | **Route**   | "which skill for Y", "I want to do Z", "what should I use to..."     | One recommended skill (in code) + why; alternates only if the request is genuinely ambiguous |
35: 
36: 
37: ## Core principle: name, don't invoke
38: 
39: The user said: *"first, names the skill."* When this skill recommends a tool, **stop after naming it** — do not silently invoke the recommended skill on the user's behalf. The user wants to see the recommendation, decide, and then trigger it themselves (either by typing the trigger phrase or via `/skill-name`). Auto-invoking would turn this into a meta-orchestrator and hide the routing decision.
40: 
41: The exception: if the user's intent is unambiguous AND they explicitly say "go ahead" or "use it" in the same message ("which skill writes UI briefs and use it for the dashboard rebuild"), pass through. Otherwise, name and stop.
42: 
43: ## Data sources, in order
44: 
45: 1. **Session-loaded descriptions** (free) — every skill available in the current session has its name + description already in your context. For most recall, catalog, and routing questions, this is enough. Don't read files just to confirm what you already see.
46: 2. **SKILL.md frontmatter** — read when the user wants deeper detail (composes_with, spawned_by, owns), or when descriptions alone don't disambiguate. Use `head -30` or read with `limit: 30` — frontmatter is small.
47: 3. **SKILL.md body** — read only for "explain in depth" questions or when the user is debugging why a skill triggered/didn't trigger.
48: 
49: For the Skill Madness repo specifically, skills live in:
50: 
51: ```
52: skills/orchestrator/SKILL.md
53: skills/{contracts,git,loops,meta,roles,workflows}/<skill-name>/SKILL.md
54: ```
55: 
56: Plugin skills are host-specific and may be visible in your session context but not in this repo tree — on Claude Code they come from `~/.claude/plugins/`; other hosts have their own equivalents.
57: 
58: ## Output format
59: 
60: Keep responses tight. The user is in a flow and needs a name fast.
61: 
62: ### Recall ("I forgot the name of the one that...")
63: 
64: > You're thinking of `ui-brief` — generates opinionated, design-led briefs for new or rebuilt UIs.
65: > Trigger it with phrases like "write me a UI brief" or "design brief for X".
66: 
67: If you can't pin down a single skill, list 2–3 candidates with one line each.
68: 
69: ### Catalog ("what skills do I have")
70: 
71: Group by category. One line per skill: `` `name` `` — what it does in <12 words.
72: 
73: ```
74: ## Orchestration
75: - `orchestrator` — coordinator for multi-agent contract-first builds
76: 
77: ## Roles (implementation agents)
78: - `backend-agent` — APIs, business logic, data layer
79: - `frontend-agent` — UI, client state, presentation
80: - ...
81: ```
82: 
83: If the user asks for "everything" including plugin skills, separate repo skills from plugin skills clearly so they know what's shipped from this repo vs added by plugins.
84: 
85: ### Explain ("what does X do")
86: 
87: ```
88: **`<name>`** (v<version>) — <one-line summary>
89: 
90: **Triggers on:** <2–3 sample trigger phrases from the description>
91: **Produces:** <output format / artifact>
92: **Composes with:** `<other>`, `<other>`
93: **Spawned by:** `<orchestrator>` (if applicable)
94: 
95: <one paragraph of detail>
96: ```
97: 
98: Pull `version`, `composes_with`, `spawned_by` from frontmatter. Skip fields that are empty.
99: 
100: ### Route ("which skill for...")
101: 
102: ```
103: Use `<skill-name>` — <one sentence on why it fits>.
104: Trigger it with: "<sample phrase>" or `/<skill-name>`.
105: ```
106: 
107: Add an alternate only if the request genuinely could go either way:
108: 
109: ```
110: If you actually mean <reframe>, use `<other-skill>` instead.
111: ```
112: 
113: ### Compose ("how do these connect")
114: 
115: When the user asks how skills relate, render a small mermaid diagram or text tree from `composes_with` / `spawned_by`. Keep it to the skills they asked about plus one degree out — don't dump the whole graph.
116: 
117: ## Routing rules of thumb
118: 
119: These are common confusions. Lean toward the right answer rather than reflecting the user's wording.
120: 
121: - **"I want to build X with multiple agents" / "swarm build" / "team build"** → `orchestrator`. This is its actual job.
122: - **"I just want to write/fix one thing"** → name the role skill directly (`backend-agent`, `frontend-agent`, etc.), not orchestrator. Orchestrator is for *coordinating* a team, not for any task that touches code.
123: - **"Design / rebuild / redesign a UI"** → `ui-brief` first to produce the brief, then `frontend-agent` to build from it (or `frontend-design` if that Claude-Code plugin is present).
124: - **"Make a plan from this research/PRD"** → `plan-builder`, then optionally `orchestrator` to execute the plan.
125: - **"Audit / review my skills"** → `skill-review` (`--scope=all` for bulk, `--scope=<name>` for deep dive).
126: - **"Create a new skill"** → `skill-writer`.
127: - **"Sync skills globally" / "link them"** → `sync-skills`.
128: - **"Commit / branch / PR"** → `git-commit`, `git-pr`, etc.
129: - **Plugin skills** (`superpowers:*`, `claude-mem:*`, `claude-obsidian:*`, `feature-dev:*`, etc.) — Claude-Code plugin namespaces; name them with their full namespace only if they're actually loaded in this session.
130: 
131: When orchestrator would be wrong, **say so explicitly**: "This isn't a multi-agent build, so `orchestrator` would bounce you. Use `<actual-skill>` instead."
132: 
133: ## When NOT to invoke this skill
134: 
135: - The user already named a specific skill they want to run (`"use ui-brief for the dashboard"`) — just run it.
136: - The user is mid-task and asks a domain question that the active skill should handle.
137: - The user wants to *create* a new skill — that's `skill-writer`'s job. (You can mention `skill-writer` as a routing answer, but don't take it over.)
138: - The user wants to *audit* skills — that's `skill-review`. Same deal.
139: 
140: ## Anti-patterns
141: 
142: 
143: | Anti-pattern                                       | Why it fails                                                                                                                         |
144: | -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
145: | Auto-invoking the recommended skill                | The user said "names the skill" — auto-invoking hides the decision and creates a meta-orchestrator                                   |
146: | Reading every SKILL.md to answer "what do I have"  | Descriptions are already in your session context; reading 38 files burns tokens for nothing                                          |
147: | Recommending `orchestrator` for single-skill tasks | Orchestrator is for multi-agent coordination, not generic routing                                                                    |
148: | Listing 5 candidates when one fits                 | Picking is the job. If one skill clearly fits, name only that one                                                                    |
149: | Long preambles before the answer                   | The user wants the name. Lead with `` `skill-name` `` and explain after                                                              |
150: | Inventing skill names                              | If you don't see a skill that fits, say "no skill covers this — closest is X" or "this might warrant a new skill via `skill-writer`" |
151: 
152: 
153: ## References
154: 
155: - `references/routing-table.md` — fuller table of common requests → recommended skill (plus a "By unresolved decision" index for when a shared term like "deploy" or "review" matches several plausible skills), used when the rules of thumb above don't cover the case
156: - `references/troubleshooting.md` — named symptom taxonomy for skill discovery and routing problems (skill not triggering, wrong skill firing, overlapping triggers, instructions not followed)
157: 

=== skills/meta/skill-review/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: skill-review
3: version: 1.3.0
4: argument-hint: skill-name or 'all'
5: description: |
6:   Review skills for quality, consistency, triggering accuracy, and adherence to the 5000-word / 500-line body guideline. Two modes: 'all' (bulk ecosystem-wide scan for ownership conflicts, length outliers, weak triggers, dead xrefs) or a single skill name (deep dive on description quality, body structure, anti-pattern naming, cross-references). Outputs a structured markdown report plus JSON sidecar consumable by skill-update. Trigger on "audit skills", "review this skill", "health check skills", "bulk review", "deep review", "what needs fixing".
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: false
9: min_plan: starter
10: owns:
11:   directories: []
12:   patterns: []
13:   shared_read: ["skills/"]
14: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Glob", "Grep", "Bash", "Write"]
15: composes_with: ["skill-update", "skill-writer", "skill-creator:skill-creator", "model-adaptation"]
16: spawned_by: []
17: ---
18: 
19: # Skill Review
20: 
21: One skill, two modes. Use `--scope=all` for an ecosystem-wide bulk scan; use `--scope=<skill-name>` for a thorough single-skill deep dive. Both modes produce a structured markdown report plus a JSON sidecar that `skill-update` can consume to generate fix plans.
22: 
23: ## When to Use
24: 
25: - Periodic health check across the skill ecosystem (`--scope=all`)
26: - After bulk edits or a new batch of skills lands (`--scope=all`)
27: - Reviewing a single skill before publishing (`--scope=<name>`)
28: - Investigating why one skill won't trigger or produces poor output (`--scope=<name>`)
29: - Quality-gating a skill before it enters the ecosystem (`--scope=<name>`)
30: 
31: ## Inputs
32: 
33: The skill takes a single positional argument:
34: 
35: - `all` — bulk scan across `skills/**/SKILL.md`
36: - `<skill-name>` — deep dive on `skills/**/<skill-name>/SKILL.md`
37: 
38: Optional follow-on context:
39: 
40: - **Focus** — narrow the run to specific checks ("just ownership", "just descriptions")
41: - **Why-now** — what prompted the review (e.g., "it never triggers", "outputs are wrong")
42: 
43: ## Process
44: 
45: ### Phase 0: Parse the Argument
46: 
47: 1. Read the argument. If it equals `all` (case-insensitive), enter Mode A.
48: 2. Otherwise treat the argument as a skill name. Glob for `skills/**/<arg>/SKILL.md`. If exactly one match, enter Mode B. If zero matches, return a list of fuzzy candidates and stop. If multiple matches, ask the user to disambiguate.
49: 
50: ### Mode A — Bulk Scan (`--scope=all`)
51: 
52: Optimize for speed. Score quickly, flag issues, move on. Use subagents to parallelize check categories where available.
53: 
54: #### A1. Discovery
55: 
56: 1. Glob `skills/**/SKILL.md`
57: 2. Parse frontmatter from each
58: 3. Build a skill inventory table with columns: Skill, Category, Version, Description Length, Body Lines, Refs Count
59: 
60: #### A2. Bulk Checks
61: 
62: Run these check categories. Full checklist lives in `references/audit-checklist.md`.
63: 
64: - **Frontmatter consistency** — required fields present, name matches directory, version is valid semver, description starts with action verb and is ≤200 chars
65: - **Spec compliance (FAIL)** — no `<` or `>` in frontmatter field values (security rule); description ≤1024 chars (hard ceiling)
66: - **Spec compliance (WARN)** — reserved-prefix name (`claude-*` / `anthropic-*`). Acceptable as a documented exception when the skill targets the corresponding Anthropic product. Verify the documented exception exists in the skill body.
67: - **Tool field naming** — `allowed-tools` (hyphen) is canonical; `allowed_tools` (underscore) accepted as deprecated alias (warn but don't fail)
68: - **Ownership conflict detection** — collect every `owns.directories` and `owns.patterns` across agent roles. Flag overlaps. Validate against the v1.1 resolved conflicts table in `frontmatter-spec.md`
69: - **Description quality scoring** — has action verb, ≥3 trigger contexts, keyword variants, states exclusions if ambiguous, estimated "pushiness" (low/medium/high)
70: - **Progressive disclosure** — SKILL.md body line + word count, references linked from body, body >5,000 words OR >500 lines flagged (soft warning), references >300 lines without TOC flagged
71: - **Cross-skill consistency** — `composes_with` and `spawned_by` point to real skills, no circular `composes_with` chains, no orphan reference files
72: - **Coverage gaps** — compare inventory against `docs/architecture.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, and the orchestrator's File Ownership Map; flag roles or workflows referenced but not implemented
73: 
74: #### A3. Triage
75: 
76: Highlight the top 3–5 most impactful issues. Suggest which skills warrant a single-skill follow-up (re-run with `--scope=<name>`). Note which issues `skill-update` can address directly.
77: 
78: ### Mode B — Deep Dive (`--scope=<skill-name>`)
79: 
80: Take time. Read every file in the skill's directory tree. Score each rubric dimension 1–5 with specific evidence.
81: 
82: #### B1. Structural Analysis
83: 
84: Read `SKILL.md` and every file in `references/`. Score these dimensions against `references/deep-review-rubric.md`:
85: 
86: 1. **Frontmatter compliance** — required fields, types, semver, optional fields used appropriately
87: 2. **Description quality** — action verb, trigger contexts, keyword variants, length, "pushiness"
88: 3. **Progressive disclosure** — body ≤5,000 words (soft warning past 500 lines), references used appropriately, clear pointers
89: 4. **Instruction clarity** — imperative voice, logical flow, no ambiguity, explains "why" not just "what"
90: 5. **Coordination** — ownership declarations, `composes_with` accuracy, no overlaps
91: 6. **Completeness** — referenced files exist, no dead links, validation checklists where needed
92: 7. **Anti-patterns** — see the anti-pattern checklist in `references/audit-checklist.md`, including the model-adaptation checks (reasoning-extraction refusal risk, prior-model over-prescription) that flag a skill written for a superseded model, and the writing-craft checks (leading-word anchor, no-op-sentence deletion, positive-prompting lint). When a skill scores poorly there or "worked before and got worse," hand off to the `model-adaptation` skill's migration audit.
93: 
94: #### B2. Live Trigger Testing
95: 
96: First, check the skill carries a **triggering test** — at least one example phrasing that must activate it (ideally one near-miss that must not). Flag its absence as an issue. Then verify it actually holds.
97: 
98: If `/skill-creator` is available, use its eval infrastructure to test whether the skill actually triggers:
99: 
100: 1. Generate 3–5 realistic prompts that **should** trigger this skill
101: 2. Generate 2–3 near-miss prompts that should **not** trigger it
102: 3. Run trigger evaluation via skill-creator's description optimization tooling
103: 4. Report should-trigger hit rate and false-positive rate. List any problem triggers.
104: 
105: If `/skill-creator` is unavailable, skip this phase and note it in the report.
106: 
107: #### B3. Output Quality Sampling
108: 
109: For skills that produce structured output (reports, files, configs):
110: 
111: 1. Pick 2 representative test prompts
112: 2. Run them through the skill (or skill-creator's test harness)
113: 3. Compare actual output against the skill's stated format
114: 4. Note gaps between promised and actual output
115: 
116: Optionally (recommended for non-trivial skills), run one prompt with and without the skill and compare — if the skill doesn't measurably improve the output, flag that it may not earn its context cost.
117: 
118: ### Phase 4: Report
119: 
120: Both modes write the report following `references/report-format.md`. Output two artifacts:
121: 
122: - `skill-review-report.md` — structured markdown for humans
123: - `skill-review-report.json` — sidecar consumable by `skill-update`
124: 
125: In Mode A, save to the repo root or a user-specified path. In Mode B, save to `{skill-path}/skill-review-report.{md,json}`.
126: 
127: ## Output Handoff
128: 
129: The report is designed to feed directly into **skill-update**, which consumes the JSON sidecar and produces a prioritized edit plan. After writing the report, tell the user:
130: 
131: > "Review complete. [X] issues found. Feed the JSON sidecar into `/skill-update` to generate a fix plan, or re-run me with `--scope=<skill-name>` for a deeper look at [specific skills]."
132: 
133: ## Guidelines
134: 
135: - Be constructive — every issue gets a concrete suggestion
136: - Score honestly but explain reasoning, especially for low scores. Don't nitpick style if the skill is functionally sound
137: - Weight trigger testing heavily — a skill that doesn't trigger is useless regardless of how well-written it is
138: - When recommending fixes, expect them applied one focused change at a time with re-validation between — don't bundle many unrelated edits into one pass
139: - In Mode A, don't read reference file contents unless checking for orphans or broken links. Frontmatter + body line count is enough for most checks
140: - In Mode B, if the user supplied context (e.g., "it never triggers"), lead with that complaint
141: - If scope is filtered in Mode A, still validate cross-skill references against the full inventory
142: - Use subagents to parallelize independent check categories when available
143: 
144: ## Data source
145: 
146: Before recommending changes, consult real usage signal: run
147: `scripts/skill-health.sh report --json` (P2-C skill-health telemetry). It returns
148: per-skill total invocations, 7-day/30-day success rates, a `declining` flag, and
149: version drift (recorded vs current `SKILL.md` frontmatter `version`). The math is
150: deterministic in code, never a prompt. Treat the signal as coarse and
151: best-effort: the emitter can only attribute a skill on a Skill-tool call and
152: records `outcome:unknown`, so a skill reported as `no-data` means "unobserved",
153: not "broken". Use it to prioritize which skills to deep-review and to back up
154: declining-quality verdicts — never as the sole basis for a low score. See
155: `contracts/installer/skill-health.md`.
156: 
157: ## Reference Files
158: 
159: - `references/audit-checklist.md` — checklist of every per-skill and ecosystem-level check, plus the anti-pattern list
160: - `references/deep-review-rubric.md` — 1–5 scoring criteria for each of the seven deep-review dimensions, with verdict thresholds (SHIP / NEEDS WORK / MAJOR REWORK)
161: - `references/report-format.md` — exact shape of the markdown report and JSON sidecar

=== skills/meta/skill-update/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: skill-update
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: |
5:   Plan and apply changes to an existing skill in one workflow. Reads a skill-review report (bulk or deep-dive) or inline findings, drafts an edit list with the agent's recommended answer attached, walks the edits one at a time, applies them, and re-runs lint and frontmatter checks. Use after skill-review when you're ready to ship the changes. Trigger on "apply the review", "update this skill", "fix the skill", "ship the recommendations", "edit this skill", "apply the plan", "implement the changes".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: []
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
14: composes_with: ["skill-review", "skill-writer", "sync-skills"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Skill Update
19: 
20: Plan and apply changes to an existing skill in a single walk-through. Consumes a review report (or inline findings), drafts one edit per bullet with the agent's recommended answer attached, walks the edits one at a time, applies them, and re-validates the skill.
21: 
22: ## When to Use
23: 
24: - A `skill-review` report exists and the user wants next steps
25: - The user said something like "apply the review", "ship the recommendations", "fix this skill"
26: - A short list of fixes is in the chat and the user wants them applied with confirmation
27: - Simple direct edits where planning and applying is one motion, not two
28: 
29: If there is no review yet, run `skill-review` first and come back.
30: 
31: ## Inputs
32: 
33: - **Review source** — a `skill-review` report (single-skill or `--scope=all`), or inline findings from chat
34: - **Target skill(s)** — SKILL.md path(s) to edit
35: - **Scope (optional)** — priority levels to walk ("just P0", "P0 and P1", "all")
36: 
37: ## Process
38: 
39: ### Step 1: Parse the Feedback
40: 
41: Read every report and note provided. Extract:
42: 
43: - Each issue with its severity, affected skill, file, and dimension
44: - Each recommendation
45: - Trigger-testing results and coverage gaps from `skill-review`
46: 
47: Deduplicate — if the same issue surfaces from both a bulk pass and a deep-dive review, merge them. Group by category:
48: 
49: | Category | Examples |
50: |----------|----------|
51: | Frontmatter fixes | Missing fields, wrong types, bad version |
52: | Description rewrites | Vague triggers, missing keywords, too long/short |
53: | Content restructuring | Body too long, needs reference extraction, missing sections |
54: | Instruction improvements | Ambiguous steps, missing rationale, wrong voice |
55: | Ownership fixes | Overlaps, stale `composes_with`, missing declarations |
56: | New content | Missing reference files, examples, checklists |
57: | Deletions | Duplicate content, orphan files, dead links |
58: 
59: ### Step 2: Draft the Edit Plan
60: 
61: Produce a one-bullet-per-edit list. Each bullet carries an explicit **recommended answer** so the user can accept by nodding.
62: 
63: Score each change:
64: 
65: - **Impact** (1–3): 3 = skill misfires without this, 2 = quality suffers, 1 = polish
66: - **Effort** (1–3): 1 = quick field edit, 2 = rewrite a section, 3 = major rework
67: 
68: Sort by Impact DESC, then Effort ASC. P0 first, P1 next, P2/P3 later.
69: 
70: For each bullet, draft the actual edit text — not "improve the description" but `Rewrite description to: "<new text>"`. For description rewrites, draft the new description in the plan so it can be applied verbatim.
71: 
72: See `references/plan-format.md` for the full bullet schema.
73: 
74: ### Step 3: Walk the User Through Edits
75: 
76: Present the plan, then walk one edit at a time. For each:
77: 
78: 1. State the change — file, what, why, recommended answer
79: 2. Ask: "Apply this edit?" Default is yes
80: 3. On confirm, apply with `Edit` (one logical change per call — never batch unrelated edits)
81: 4. On skip, note why and move on
82: 5. On modify, take the user's revision and apply that
83: 6. Briefly confirm: `Updated <skill-name>: <what changed>`
84: 
85: Rules while applying:
86: 
87: - **Read before editing, always** — never edit a file you have not read this session
88: - For new reference files, create the file first, then edit the parent SKILL.md to link it
89: - For deletions, update any links that pointed to the removed content
90: - Preserve YAML formatting and field order in frontmatter edits
91: - If a planned edit no longer makes sense (file already fixed, context changed), skip it and say why
92: 
93: For batches of 10+ independent edits across different skills, parallel-dispatch with subagents.
94: 
95: ### Step 4: Post-Edit Validation
96: 
97: After all edits in this pass:
98: 
99: - Re-read every modified SKILL.md and validate frontmatter against `skills/meta/skill-writer/references/frontmatter-spec.md`
100: - Confirm body length is within spec guidance — ≤5,000 words and ≤500 lines (soft warnings); when content could move to references, prefer that over inflating the SKILL.md body
101: - Resolve all reference links in modified files
102: - If `owns` fields changed, re-check there are no overlaps with other agent roles
103: - Run markdownlint if available — `.markdownlint.json` is at repo root
104: 
105: See `references/validation-checklist.md` for the full check list and report format.
106: 
107: ### Step 5: Re-Run Review and Sync (optional)
108: 
109: Offer:
110: 
111: > "Re-run `skill-review` to confirm the issues are gone? I can diff before/after."
112: 
113: Then offer sync:
114: 
115: > "All changes applied and validated. Sync to your global skill locations now?"
116: 
117: If yes, invoke `sync-skills`:
118: 
119: ```bash
120: skills/workflows/sync-skills/scripts/sync-skills.sh --to-all
121: ```
122: 
123: If declined, remind: "Repo versions are updated but your global locations still have the old versions — sync when you are ready."
124: 
125: ## Error Handling
126: 
127: - **Edit fails:** report what failed, skip to the next edit, collect failures, present them at the end with retry/manual-fix/skip options
128: - **Validation fails:** name the specific failures, suggest fixes, ask whether to fix now or leave for later — do not auto-fix without confirmation
129: 
130: ## Guidelines
131: 
132: - Be specific in every bullet — vague "improve the description" is not actionable
133: - Attach the recommended answer to every bullet so the user can accept fast
134: - One logical change per `Edit` call
135: - Skip pure style preferences — focus on functional improvements
136: - Note ordering dependencies between changes (e.g. ownership fix in skill A must land before skill B's update)
137: - Never reformat an entire file as a side-effect of a targeted edit
138: 
139: ## Data source
140: 
141: Before drafting the edit list, consult real usage signal: run
142: `scripts/skill-health.sh report --json` (P2-C skill-health telemetry) for the
143: skills you are about to change. It reports per-skill total invocations,
144: 7-day/30-day success rates, a `declining` flag, and version drift (recorded vs
145: current `SKILL.md` frontmatter `version`). The math is deterministic in code,
146: never a prompt. The signal is coarse and best-effort — the emitter records
147: `outcome:unknown` and a `no-data` skill is merely unobserved, not broken — so use
148: it to prioritize and justify edits, not to fabricate problems. A `stale`
149: version-drift row is a strong, signal-free hint that a skill's recorded behavior
150: lags its current definition. See `contracts/installer/skill-health.md`.
151: 
152: ## References
153: 
154: - `references/plan-format.md` — schema for the one-bullet-per-edit plan
155: - `references/validation-checklist.md` — post-edit checks (markdownlint, frontmatter spec, line count, broken xrefs)

=== skills/meta/skill-writer/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: skill-writer
3: version: 1.6.0
4: description: |
5:   Generate new SKILL.md files conforming to the ecosystem's frontmatter spec and structure conventions. Use when creating a new agent role, meta skill, workflow skill, or contract skill — anything that needs a SKILL.md scaffold. Trigger on "create a skill", "new agent", "write a SKILL.md", "scaffold a skill", "add a role to the skill ecosystem".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: []
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with: ["project-profiler", "orchestrator", "skill-review", "skill-update"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Skill Writer
19: 
20: Generate correctly structured SKILL.md files for the Claude Code skill ecosystem. Every skill follows the same frontmatter convention and directory structure.
21: 
22: ## When to Use
23: 
24: - Creating a new agent role skill
25: - Creating a new workflow or meta skill
26: - Reviewing existing skills for spec compliance
27: - Adding a role to an orchestrated build
28: 
29: ## Skill Directory Structure
30: 
31: ```text
32: skill-name/
33: ├── SKILL.md              # Required — frontmatter + instructions
34: └── references/           # Optional — loaded on demand
35:     └── detailed-guide.md
36: ```
37: 
38: ## Progressive Disclosure
39: 
40: Skills use three-level loading:
41: 
42: 1. **Metadata** (~100 tokens) — name + description, always in context
43: 2. **SKILL.md body** (≤5,000 words; soft warning at 500 lines) — loaded when skill triggers
44: 3. **References** (unlimited) — loaded on demand via explicit reads
45: 
46: Keep SKILL.md bodies concise. Move detailed checklists, templates, and reference tables to `references/` with clear pointers.
47: 
48: ## Creating a New Skill
49: 
50: ### Step 1: Choose the Skill Type
51: 
52: | Type | Directory | Purpose |
53: |------|-----------|---------|
54: | Agent role | `roles/{name}/` | Implementation agent for orchestrated builds |
55: | Meta skill | `meta/{name}/` | Tools for the skill ecosystem itself |
56: | Workflow | `workflows/{name}/` | Cross-cutting processes |
57: | Contract | `contracts/{name}/` | Integration contract management |
58: | Orchestrator | `orchestrator/` | Lead coordinator (singleton) |
59: 
60: ### Step 2: Write the Frontmatter
61: 
62: Every SKILL.md starts with YAML frontmatter. See `references/frontmatter-spec.md` for the complete field reference.
63: 
64: Required fields:
65: 
66: - `name` — kebab-case, max 64 chars. `claude-*` / `anthropic-*` prefixes are reserved by Anthropic; use them only as a documented exception when the skill targets the corresponding Anthropic product (e.g., `claude-design-brief`).
67: - `version` — semver (start at 1.0.0), top-level
68: - `description` — `[What] + [When] + [Capabilities]` anatomy (≤200 chars target, 1024 hard ceiling)
69: 
70: The description is the primary trigger mechanism. Write it "pushy" — enumerate contexts where the skill should activate. See `references/description-patterns.md` for templates and the 3-slot anatomy.
71: 
72: **Declare portability explicitly.** Set `requires_claude_code: false` unless the skill genuinely needs Claude-Code machinery (Agent-Teams subagents, the Artifact tool, `~/.claude` config). Default to portable — a host-agnostic skill costs nothing extra, while one marked `true` is skipped entirely when converting for other tools. If the skill is host-specific, say which host in `compatibility` (e.g. `"Claude Code or any host with Bash"`).
73: 
74: ### Step 3: Write the Body
75: 
76: Structure the body around:
77: 
78: 1. **Role statement** — one paragraph defining what this agent/skill does
79: 2. **Inputs** — what parameters it receives
80: 3. **Process** — numbered steps, imperative voice
81: 4. **Coordination rules** — how it interacts with other agents
82: 5. **Guidelines** — principles and common pitfalls
83: 
84: **Writing craft (the four rules that keep a body short and reliable):**
85: 
86: - **Anchor on a leading word.** Hang the skill's behavior on one strong
87:   pretrained concept the model already knows ("adversarial", "triage",
88:   "postmortem") instead of restating a three-part definition at three
89:   different sites. If the same idea appears in the description, the intro, and
90:   a step, collapse it to the one word that carries it.
91: - **Delete no-op sentences whole.** Any line the model already obeys by
92:   default ("be helpful", "write clean code", "consider edge cases") is pure
93:   context cost. Delete the entire sentence — don't trim it to a shorter no-op.
94: - **State the target behavior, not the bare prohibition.** "Don't X" leaves
95:   the model to guess the replacement; "do Y (because Z)" names it. Keep a
96:   prohibition only when the forbidden form itself is the sharpest anchor
97:   (the *Forbidden:* anti-pattern naming convention).
98: - **Price every warning you keep.** A warning that names its real rework cost
99:   ("burns the agent's entire context", "rework surfaces a wave later") gets
100:   weighed; an unpriced one reads as style advice and gets skipped under
101:   pressure. See *Cost-Tagged Anti-Patterns* in `references/patterns.md`.
102: - **Describe capabilities, not tool names.** Write "read the file", "run the
103:   command", "list the directory" — not "use the Read tool" or "use the Bash
104:   tool". Capability language keeps the same body working on every host (Claude
105:   Code, Hermes, DeepSeek, Copilot, Gemini CLI). Reserve tool names for genuinely
106:   host-specific instructions, and scope them ("on Claude Code, use the Task
107:   tool").
108: 
109: For agent role skills, also include:
110: 
111: - **Step 0: Read Contracts** — every role skill should start by reading contracts and domain rules before any implementation
112: - **Ownership** — directories/files owned exclusively, with note that orchestrator prompt takes precedence over frontmatter defaults
113: - **Off-limits** — what this agent must never touch
114: - **Right-sizing** — guidance on adapting to project complexity
115: - **Validation** — link to `references/validation-script-pattern.md` for how to wire a validation step into the new skill's body
116: 
117: ### Step 4: Create Reference Files
118: 
119: Move detailed content to `references/`:
120: 
121: - Validation checklists with specific commands
122: - Templates and examples
123: - Detailed technical guides
124: - Tables longer than 20 rows
125: 
126: Reference files from the body with guidance on when to read:
127: 
128: ```markdown
129: For the complete validation procedure, read `references/quick-checklist.md`
130: before reporting done.
131: ```
132: 
133: ### Step 5: Add a Triggering Test
134: 
135: Every skill must carry a concrete **triggering test** — at least one example user phrasing that MUST activate the skill, and ideally one near-miss that must NOT. This is how you verify the `description` actually triggers (not just that it reads well). Record it in the skill (e.g. a short "Triggering test" note or a row in `references/quick-checklist.md`) so reviewers can re-run it.
136: 
137: ### Step 6: Validate the Skill
138: 
139: Work one focused change at a time — make a single edit, re-validate (lint + the triggering test below), then move to the next. Don't sweep many unrelated changes into one pass.
140: 
141: - [ ] Frontmatter has all required fields
142: - [ ] `name` is kebab-case. If it starts with `claude-` or `anthropic-`, that's a documented exception (skill targets the corresponding Anthropic product)
143: - [ ] No `<` or `>` anywhere in frontmatter
144: - [ ] Description is ≤200 chars (target) and "pushy"; never exceeds 1024 chars (ceiling)
145: - [ ] Body is ≤5,000 words (soft warning past 500 lines)
146: - [ ] File ownership doesn't overlap with existing agents (check v1.1 resolved conflicts)
147: - [ ] Directory ownership takes precedence over pattern ownership
148: - [ ] Reference files are linked from the body
149: - [ ] No duplicate content between body and references
150: - [ ] A triggering test is recorded (≥1 phrasing that must trigger; ideally one near-miss that must not)
151: - [ ] `requires_claude_code` is `false` unless the skill genuinely needs Claude-Code machinery (subagents, Artifact, `~/.claude`)
152: - [ ] Body uses capability language ("read the file"), not tool names ("use the Read tool")
153: - [ ] No Claude-Code-only references (plugin namespaces, `~/.claude` paths, `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`) unless scoped as host-specific
154: 
155: ### Step 7: Earn the Context Cost (optional, recommended for non-trivial skills)
156: 
157: Optionally compare behavior with vs without the skill — a quick baseline eval on one or two representative prompts — to confirm the skill measurably improves the output and earns the tokens it adds to every context. Skip for trivial skills; do it for anything substantial.
158: 
159: ## Common Mistakes
160: 
161: - **Vague descriptions** — "Helps with backend stuff" won't trigger. Be specific.
162: - **Body too long** — Approaching 5,000 words or 500 lines? Move content to references.
163: - **Missing ownership** — Agent roles must declare owned and off-limits files.
164: - **Overlapping ownership** — Two agents can't own the same directory. Directory ownership takes precedence over pattern ownership (see `references/frontmatter-spec.md` §Ownership Resolution Rules).
165: - **Ignoring resolved conflicts** — Check `references/frontmatter-spec.md` §Resolved Conflicts (v1.0 → v1.1) before declaring ownership of `contracts/`, `.claude/handoffs/`, `CLAUDE.md`, `README.md`, or `tests/performance/`.
166: - **Hardcoded project details** — Global skills never change per project. Use profile.yaml.
167: - **Claude-Code-only assumptions** — Writing "use the Bash tool", hardcoding `~/.claude` paths, or assuming Agent Teams / the Artifact tool exist makes the skill bail on non-Claude hosts. Write capability language, default `requires_claude_code` to `false`, and scope any genuinely host-specific step.
168: 
169: ## Reference Files
170: 
171: - `references/frontmatter-spec.md` — Complete field reference with types, rules, and examples
172: - `references/description-patterns.md` — Templates for writing effective trigger descriptions
173: - `references/body-template.md` — Anthropic's recommended SKILL.md body structure, plus per-skill-type deviations (agent roles, meta, orchestrator, workflow)
174: - `references/patterns.md` — Five architectural skill patterns (Sequential Workflow, Multi-MCP Coordination, Iterative Refinement, Context-Aware Tool Selection, Domain-Specific Intelligence)
175: - `references/quick-checklist.md` — Pre-ship checklist: frontmatter, description, body length, triggers, cross-references
176: - `references/performance-notes.md` — When and how to add a Performance Notes section to combat model laziness
177: - `references/validation-script-pattern.md` — How to author and wire a deterministic validation script for a skill

=== skills/orchestrator/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: orchestrator
3: version: 1.18.0
4: description: |
5:   Coordinate multi-agent Claude Code builds end-to-end: read the plan/mission, design integration contracts, dispatch role-agents in parallel, gate on QA, ship. Under ultracode (standing opt-in) or an explicit "workflow" ask, it drives the implement + verify phases with the Workflow tool — fanning out role-agents against the contracts and adversarially verifying instead of hand-spawning agents one message at a time. Use when the user mentions agent teams, parallel/swarm builds, multi-agent work, a MISSION.md file, a multi-phase mission, or splitting work across Claude sessions. Triggers on "agent team", "parallel build", "team build", "multi-agent", "swarm build", "build X with agents", "coordinate the build", "run the mission", "workflow", "dynamic workflows", "ultracode build", "orchestrate with workflows". Does NOT preempt brainstorming, planning, design-brief, or feature-dev — it picks up after those produce artifacts.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: true
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: [".gitignore"]
12:   shared_read: ["contracts/", ".claude/handoffs/"]
