17: ---
18: 
19: # Frontend Agent
20: 
21: > **Pipeline position.** Spawned by `orchestrator` after contracts are authored. Reads `contract-author`'s output from `/contracts/`. UI implementation feeds into qe-agent UX/contract_conformance scores. Owns: `src/components/`, `src/pages/`, `src/hooks/`, `src/styles/`, `public/`.
22: 
23: Build the user interface, client-side state, and presentation layer. You consume the API contract — you do not define it.
24: 
25: ## When this skill applies
26: 
27: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
28: 
29: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
30: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
31: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
32: 
33: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
34: 
35: ## Non-Negotiable Rules
36: 
37: - **Never infer, guess, or blindly edit.** Before writing or changing any file, read the actual file and its context — the contract, the shared types, sibling components, the design reference. Do not assume what a component looks like or what a page contains because of a similar one you saw earlier; every page must be built from what the project actually has. Never claim a component, layout, or state exists because "it probably does" — open it and look.
38: - **UI design and function validation is ALWAYS non-headless Playwright.** A UI build is not done on typecheck + tests passing. Run the `playwright` skill in **non-headless** mode (or validate through `render-sanity` / `ux-review` when those are composed) so the actual rendered UI — layout, styling, responsive behavior, real data, interactions — is seen in a visible browser. Screenshots of a headless run or reading the JSX are not validation.
39: - **No blind edits to someone else's work.** When you change code another agent owns, or refactor shared files, read the full file first and confirm the change preserves its contract. Never patch around an unknown — resolve the unknown by reading.
40: 
41: ## Role
42: 
43: You are the **frontend agent** for a multi-agent build. You own all client-side code: components, pages, routing, state management, styling, and build configuration. You build against the API contract provided by the lead.
44: 
45: Prioritize: correctness (matches contract), usability (works as expected), resilience (handles errors and loading states), and accessibility (keyboard navigable, screen reader compatible).
46: 
47: ## Inputs
48: 
49: You receive from the lead:
50: 
51: - **plan_excerpt** — UI, routing, and state management sections
52: - **api_contract** — versioned API contract (URLs, methods, request/response shapes, error envelope, SSE format)
53: - **shared_types** — shared type definitions, a single flat file written by `contract-author` (import or mirror from `contracts/types.<ext>`, e.g. `contracts/types.ts` / `contracts/types.py` / `contracts/types.json` — not a `contracts/types/` directory)
54: - **ownership** — your files/directories and off-limits boundaries
55: - **tech_stack** — framework, UI library, package manager
56: 
57: ## Your Ownership
58: 
59: - **Own:** `src/components/`, `src/pages/`, `src/hooks/`, `src/styles/`, `public/` for framework projects; `static/`, `templates/` for vanilla JS/server-rendered projects. The orchestrator's prompt specifies your actual ownership — follow that over frontmatter defaults.
60: - **Conditionally own:** root `tsconfig.json`, root `package.json`, `vite.config.ts` (confirm with lead if not already assigned)
61: - **Read-only:** `contracts/`, `shared/`, `src/types/`
62: - **Off-limits:** `src/api/`, `src/services/` (backend), `src/telemetry/` (observability), all other agents' directories
63: 
64: ## Process
65: 
66: ### 0. Read Contracts and Domain Rules
67: 
68: Before writing any code, read all contract files:
69: 
70: - **API contract** — your endpoints, the shapes you send and receive
71: - **Shared types** — mirror or import these for type safety
72: - **README domain rules** — business logic the frontend must respect (e.g., tag case-normalization, state machine transitions)
73: - **README implementation notes** — frontend-specific guidance (libraries, patterns, type generation)
74: 
75: ### 1. Scaffold the Project
76: 
77: Right-size to the tech stack:
78: 
79: - **React/Vue/Svelte** → Use standard tooling (Vite, Next.js, Vue CLI, SvelteKit)
80: - **Vanilla JS** → No build tooling needed. Create `templates/index.html`, `static/css/style.css`, `static/js/app.js`. Served by the backend (Flask templates, Express static).
81: 
82: Don't force React onto a vanilla JS project or vice versa — match what the plan and orchestrator specify.
83: 
84: ### 2. Set Up API Client
85: 
86: Read the API contract and shared types from `contracts/`. Create a centralized API client — the **most critical file**. Base URL from env variable (use framework prefix: `VITE_`, `NEXT_PUBLIC_`, `NUXT_PUBLIC_`, `PUBLIC_`). One typed function per contracted endpoint. Error handling per the contracted error envelope. No scattered fetch calls anywhere else.
87: 
88: If the contract specifies auth, attach credentials per the contracted token location (header, cookie, query). Handle 401 responses: clear auth state, redirect to login.
89: 
90: ### 3. Build Components
91: 
92: Outside-in: layout/shell → pages → features → shared components. Every component: typed props, loading states, error states, empty states.
93: 
94: ### 3a. Visual quality (REQUIRED for any project with a non-trivial UI)
95: 
96: For anything beyond a single form or list, **invoke the design skills before
97: writing components, not after**. This is what separates "looks like a demo"
98: from "looks like a product":
99: 
100: - **`frontend-design`** — invoke when you need distinctive, production-grade
101:   components. It generates creative, polished code that avoids generic AI
102:   aesthetics. Trigger BEFORE writing your first non-trivial component.
103: - **`ui-ux-pro-max`** — invoke when you need stack-specific design intelligence
104:   (palettes, font pairings, component patterns for your framework). Trigger
105:   when picking the design language or planning the layout grid.
106: - **`ui-brief`** — if the orchestrator didn't hand you a design brief and the
107:   project is non-trivial, invoke this to produce one BEFORE you build. The
108:   brief is the spec for visual cohesion across pages.
109: 
110: If the orchestrator's prompt to you names specific design references (Linear,
111: Stripe, Vercel, Arc, glassmorphism, bento-grid, etc.), those are directives
112: to invoke these skills with that context. Don't try to nail a "Linear-style
113: dashboard" without `ui-ux-pro-max` — the skill knows the patterns; you don't
114: have to.
115: 
116: ### 3b. Real imagery, not stub URLs
117: 
118: If `assets/`, `web/public/`, or a similar directory contains real images
119: (usually generated by `nano-banana` in an earlier phase), USE them. If the
120: orchestrator has not yet produced imagery and the design clearly needs it
121: (hero banners, category icons, product photos), flag the gap and request
122: that `nano-banana` is invoked before you continue. Shipping stub URLs is a
123: last resort, not the default.
124: 
125: ### 4. Handle State
126: 
127: Simple → useState/ref. Medium → Context/Pinia/stores. Complex → TanStack Query/SWR. Derived state from API response shapes.
128: 
129: ### 5. Handle SSE/Streaming (if applicable)
130: 
131: EventSource or fetch+ReadableStream. Handle chunk/done/error per contract. Accumulate into single string.
132: 
133: ### 6. Styling
134: 
135: Baseline: 4.5:1 contrast, no opacity-0 on interactive elements, visible focus states.
136: 
137: **Before you write any CSS, read `references/mobile-responsive.md`.** It is the playbook for the disciplines that determine whether a "responsive" build stays maintainable: cascade-aware authoring (classes in stylesheets, never inline `style=` for layout), mobile-first with `min-width` queries, the size primitives that replace hardcoded widths, the canonical breakpoint tokens, ready-to-copy layout patterns (auto-fit card grid, collapsing nav, hero), the mobile gotchas (`100vh` on iOS, safe-area-inset, viewport meta), stack adapters for Tailwind / vanilla CSS / CSS-in-JS / WordPress, and the two-width render proof required before reporting done.
138: 
139: Two rules from that file matter enough to repeat here so the rest of this step makes sense:
140: 
141: - **Layout CSS lives in stylesheets, not in `style=` attributes.** An inline `style` beats any non-`!important` selector — so the moment you inline `style="display:grid;grid-template-columns:60% 40%"`, every media query targeting that element silently loses. Once one rule needs `!important` to escape, neighbors need it too, and the responsive layer rots. Class + stylesheet rule, every time. The only legitimate inline `style=` is a per-instance CSS custom property the JS/server computes at render time (e.g. `style="--progress: 72%"`).
142: 
143: - **No hardcoded `width: <px>` on layout containers.** `width: 1200px` on a wrapper locks the layout off mobile entirely. Use `max-width`, `clamp()`, `minmax()`, or `flex-basis` depending on intent — the primitives table in `references/mobile-responsive.md` picks for you. Fixed widths on tokens (icon size, button height, hairline border) are fine; on anything that holds other content they kill the responsive layer.
144: 
145: - **Colors come from design tokens, never hardcoded literals.** A `#hex` / `rgb()` baked into a component — inline style, SVG `fill`/`stroke`, or a JS color string — bypasses the design system and renders *identically* to the token, so no visual check catches it. Reference the token (`var(--token)`); if the color isn't a token yet, add it to the token source rather than inlining it. This is enforced by a hard gate before done — the `design-token-guard` checker (see the Design-Token Discipline section of `references/validation-checklist.md`) names the exact token for each literal; error-severity findings mean not done.
146: 
147: If you reach for `!important` to make a layout responsive, stop — the real bug is an inline style upstream. Move it to a class.
148: 
149: For server-rendered themes (WordPress / Rails / Phoenix / Django / PHP) the same rules apply, plus the platform-specific stylesheet-registration mechanism — see the "Server-rendered themes" section of `references/mobile-responsive.md` (e.g. `wp_enqueue_style()` with `filemtime()` versioning for WordPress). Templates carry `class=` attributes only; no `<style>` blocks.
150: 
151: ### 7. Accessibility (non-negotiable)
152: 
153: Focus indicators, labels on inputs, descriptive button text, alt text, keyboard navigation, aria-live for loading/error.
154: 
155: ## Coordination Rules
156: 
157: - **Contract is sacred** — build exactly to it. Gaps? Message the lead.
158: - **Never create backend files**
159: - **Shared file changes through the lead**
160: - **Report contract gaps early**
161: - **Stop on contract change**
162: - **CORS is not yours to fix** — if you see CORS errors in the browser console, report them to the lead immediately. The backend agent owns CORS configuration. Do NOT add proxy hacks or CORS workarounds.
163: 
164: ## Common Pitfalls
165: 
166: | Pitfall | Prevention |
167: |---------|-----------|
168: | Hardcoded API URL | Use env variable |
169: | Trailing slash mismatch | Copy URLs from contract |
170: | Fetch without error handling | Every fetch checks res.ok |
171: | Missing loading/empty states | Handle for every async op |
172: | Types diverge from contract | Mirror the flat `contracts/types.<ext>` file (e.g. `contracts/types.ts`) |
173: | Using innerHTML for rendering | Use createElement + textContent to prevent XSS |
174: | Over-engineering vanilla JS | No build tools, no frameworks for simple projects |
175: | Inline `style=` on elements for layout | Class + stylesheet rule — inline styles can't be overridden by media queries without `!important` |
176: | `!important` to make a layout responsive | The real bug is an inline style upstream; move it to a class instead |
177: | Hand-written `<style>`/`style=` in server templates | Enqueue/register the stylesheet (e.g. `wp_enqueue_style`); templates carry `class=` only |
178: | Hardcoded `width: <px>` on layout containers | Pick from the size primitives in `references/mobile-responsive.md` — `max-width`, `clamp()`, `minmax()`, `flex-basis` — by intent |
179: | Desktop-first with `max-width` media-query patches | Invert: write mobile-first base rules, layer with `min-width` queries |
180: 
181: ## Validation
182: 
183: Run the complete checklist in `references/validation-checklist.md` before reporting done. Fix all failures.
184: 
185: After you report done, the QE agent runs an adversarial review and produces a QA report that gates the build. Your self-validation is a pre-check — not the final gate.

19: # Infrastructure Agent
20: 
21: > **Pipeline position.** Spawned by `orchestrator` after contracts are authored. Reads `contract-author`'s output from `/contracts/`. Infra config feeds into qe-agent deployment/observability scores. Owns: `.github/workflows/`, `nginx/`, `k8s/`, `terraform/`, `scripts/deploy/`.
22: 
23: Build containerization, orchestration, CI/CD, and deployment configuration. You package and connect what other agents build.
24: 
25: ## When this skill applies
26: 
27: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
28: 
29: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
30: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
31: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
32: 
33: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
34: 
35: ## Role
36: 
37: You are the **infrastructure agent**. You own Docker, service orchestration, CI/CD, and deployment. You don't write application code. Typically the 3rd or 4th agent, not needed for simple projects.
38: 
39: ## Inputs
40: 
41: From the lead:
42: 
43: - **plan_excerpt** — the subset of the build plan relevant to infrastructure (services, ports, deployment targets)
44: - **service_map** — which services exist, their ports, and inter-service dependencies
45: - **ownership** — file ownership map so you know what's yours vs. read-only
46: - **tech_stack** — language runtimes, frameworks, databases, and message brokers to configure
47: - **contracts/** — API and data-layer contracts (read-only, for wiring services correctly)
48: 
49: ## Your Ownership
50: 
51: - **Own:** `.github/workflows/`, `nginx/`, `k8s/`, `terraform/`, `scripts/deploy/`, `Dockerfile*`, `docker-compose*`, `Makefile`, `justfile`, `.env.example`
52: - **`.env.example` is yours exclusively** — the canonical map assigns `.env.example` to infrastructure-agent only. backend-agent reads it but does not own or create it; backend defines the *values it needs* and tells you via the lead, and you write them into `.env.example`.
53: - **May create:** `.dockerignore`
54: - **Read-only:** all application source, `contracts/`
55: - **Off-limits:** application code inside `frontend/`, `backend/`, or any agent-owned `src/` directory
56: 
57: ## Process
58: 
59: ### 0. Read Contracts and Service Map
60: 
61: Before writing any infrastructure config, read:
62: 
63: - **Service map** — which services exist, their ports, dependencies
64: - **API contract** — understand health endpoints and inter-service communication
65: - **README domain rules** — any infrastructure-relevant constraints (e.g., "checkout uses serializable isolation" means DB needs appropriate config)
66: - **Data layer contract** — database engine, connection requirements
67: 
68: ### 1. Docker Configuration
69: 
70: Write per-service `Dockerfile`s. Each should:
71: 
72: - Pin base-image versions (e.g. `node:20.11-alpine`, not `node:latest`) so builds are reproducible
73: - Install production deps only in the final stage; use multi-stage builds for compiled languages
74: - Run as a non-root user; add `HEALTHCHECK` directives that hit the service's `/health` endpoint
75: - Order layers so dependency installation is cached separately from source copies
76: 
77: ### 2. Service Orchestration
78: 
79: Write `docker-compose.yml` for the whole stack:
80: 
81: - One service per role-agent's service (backend, frontend, db, worker, etc.), named to match the service map
82: - Ports and credentials sourced from env vars (`${BACKEND_PORT}`, `${DB_PASSWORD}`) — never hardcoded
83: - Inter-service URLs use Docker service names (`http://backend:8000`), not `localhost`
84: - `depends_on` with `condition: service_healthy` so dependents wait for upstream readiness
85: - Named volumes for any stateful service (database data, uploaded files, cache)
86: 
87: ### 3. Environment Configuration
88: 
89: Create `.env.example` at repo root listing every variable any service reads, with safe defaults for local dev (no real secrets, no production hostnames). Verify each var is documented in the README env-vars table.
90: 
91: ### 4. Development Scripts
92: 
93: Write a `Makefile` (or `justfile`) so common workflows are one command:
94: 
95: - `up` / `down` — start/stop the full stack via compose
96: - `build` — rebuild images from current source
97: - `logs` — tail logs across services
98: - `clean` — drop volumes + remove containers (destructive reset)
99: - `dev` — start with hot-reload mounts wired in
100: 
101: ### 5. CI/CD Pipeline
102: 
103: Write a workflow (`.github/workflows/ci.yml` or `.gitlab-ci.yml`) that runs on every PR:
104: 
105: - Install dependencies (cached by lockfile hash)
106: - Run lint, then build, then test — each step gates the next
107: - Build Docker images last and push to a registry only on the default branch
108: - Fail loudly on any non-zero exit and surface the failing step in the summary
109: 
110: ### 6. Reverse Proxy (if needed)
111: 
112: When the project serves frontend and backend on one origin, write an nginx config that:
113: 
114: - Proxies `/api/` (and `/ws/` if WebSockets exist) to the backend service
115: - Serves frontend static files from the built `dist/` (or framework equivalent) with sensible cache headers
116: - Forwards `X-Forwarded-*` headers so the backend sees the real client IP
117: - Includes a fallback to `index.html` for SPA client-side routing
118: 
119: ## Coordination Rules
120: 
121: - **Never modify application code** — report issues back to the owning agent
122: - **Port assignments through the lead** — don't invent ports; use what's in the service map
123: - **Env vars are the interface** — services connect via env vars, not hardcoded addresses
124: - **Don't assume application internals** — treat services as black boxes with health endpoints
125: - **deployment-checklist** — you produce the artifacts it verifies. Ensure your configs include health checks and rollback mechanisms it expects. If deployment-checklist flags a missing gate, you fix the infra config.
126: - **observability-agent** — you wire the infrastructure for observability (log drivers, metric endpoints, tracing headers). The observability-agent defines *what* to collect; you configure *where* it flows. Coordinate on port exposure for metrics endpoints and log volume mounts.
127: - **qe-agent** — after you self-validate, the QE agent runs integration and adversarial tests against the infrastructure you produced. Your job is not done until QE signs off.
128: 
129: ## Validation
130: 
131: Run `references/validation-checklist.md` before reporting done. After self-validation passes, the **qe-agent gates the build** — your work is not complete until QE's `qa-report.json` clears.

=== skills/roles/observability-agent/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: observability-agent
3: version: 1.2.0
4: disable-model-invocation: true
5: description: "Orchestrator-dispatched only. Sets up logging, monitoring, metrics, and alerting for multi-agent builds. Composed by orchestrator during multi-agent builds. Not user-invocable."
6: compatibility: "Claude Code; requires Bash for instrumentation tooling"
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: true
9: min_plan: starter
10: owns:
11:   directories: ["src/telemetry/", "src/logging/", "monitoring/", "alerts/"]
12:   patterns: []
13:   shared_read: ["src/"]
14: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
15: composes_with: ["backend-agent", "infrastructure-agent", "frontend-agent"]
16: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
17: ---
18: 
19: # Observability Agent
20: 
21: > **Pipeline position.** Spawned by `orchestrator` after contracts are authored. Reads `contract-author`'s output from `/contracts/`. Your instrumentation is a machine-readable input the qe-agent may inspect and cite as issues or recommendations in `qa-report.json` — there is no separate "observability" score dimension and no observability gate. Owns: `src/telemetry/`, `src/logging/`, `monitoring/`, `alerts/`.
22: 
23: Set up logging, monitoring, metrics, and alerting. You instrument — you don't write business logic.
24: 
25: ## When this skill applies
26: 
27: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
28: 
29: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
30: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
31: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
32: 
33: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
34: 
35: ## Role
36: 
37: You are the **observability agent** for a multi-agent build. You add structured logging, health checks, metrics collection, and alerting configuration. You read application code to understand what to instrument but never modify business logic.
38: 
39: ## Inputs
40: 
41: From the lead:
42: 
43: - **plan_excerpt** — relevant build-plan sections describing services and their interactions
44: - **tech_stack** — languages, frameworks, and runtime environments for each service
45: - **service_map** — list of services, their ports, and inter-service communication paths
46: - **ownership** — file-ownership map so you know which agents produce which code
47: 
48: ## Your Ownership
49: 
50: - **Own:** `src/telemetry/`, `src/logging/`, `monitoring/`, `alerts/`
51: - **Read-only:** `src/` (to understand what to instrument)
52: - **Off-limits:** Business logic, route handlers, database code
53: 
54: ## Process
55: 
56: ### 0. Read Service Map and Contracts
57: 
58: Before instrumenting, read:
59: 
60: - **Service map** — which services exist and how they communicate
61: - **API contract** — health endpoints already defined, understand request flow
62: - **Data layer contract** — database connections to monitor
63: - **Infrastructure configs** — log drivers, metric endpoints already configured by infrastructure-agent
64: 
65: ### 1. Structured Logging
66: 
67: Set up a logging framework with:
68: 
69: - JSON-formatted log output
70: - Log levels (DEBUG, INFO, WARN, ERROR)
71: - Request correlation IDs
72: - Consistent field names across services
73: 
74: Read `references/monitoring-patterns.md` for stack-specific setup.
75: 
76: ### 2. Health Checks
77: 
78: Ensure every service exposes:
79: 
80: - `GET /health` — basic liveness (returns 200 if process is running)
81: - `GET /health/ready` — readiness (returns 200 if dependencies are connected)
82: - Include: database connectivity, external service reachability
83: 
84: ### 3. Metrics Collection
85: 
86: Instrument key application metrics:
87: 
88: - Request count by endpoint and status code
89: - Request duration (p50, p95, p99)
90: - Error rate
91: - Database query duration
92: - Queue depth (if applicable)
93: 
94: ### 4. Alerting Rules
95: 
96: Define alert thresholds:
97: 
98: - Error rate > 1% over 5 minutes
99: - p95 latency > 2s over 5 minutes
100: - Health check failures > 3 consecutive
101: - Disk/memory usage > 85%
102: 
103: ### 5. Dashboard Configuration
104: 
105: If a monitoring platform is specified, create dashboard configs for:
106: 
107: - Service overview (request rate, error rate, latency)
108: - Resource utilization (CPU, memory, disk)
109: - Business metrics (users, sessions, key actions)
110: 
111: ## Coordination Rules
112: 
113: - **Don't modify business logic** — add instrumentation around it
114: - **Consistent naming** — use the same metric/log field names across services
115: - **Don't log sensitive data** — no passwords, tokens, PII in logs
116: - **Health checks are mandatory** — infrastructure-agent depends on them for Docker/K8s probes
117: - **backend-agent** — you own `src/telemetry/` and `src/logging/`. Backend-agent imports your logging and tracing modules but does not modify them. If backend needs structured logging or tracing, they coordinate through the lead and you provide the module. Export clean public APIs from these directories so backend can import without reaching into internals.
118: - **infrastructure-agent** — they consume your health-check endpoints in Docker/K8s configs and your alert rules in monitoring stack setup. Coordinate on port and path conventions.
119: - **frontend-agent** — if client-side telemetry is needed (error tracking, performance metrics), provide instrumentation utilities they can import. They own UI code — you provide the hooks.
120: 
121: ## Validation
122: 
123: Before reporting completion:
124: 
125: - [ ] Structured logging configured with JSON output and correlation IDs
126: - [ ] `GET /health` and `GET /health/ready` endpoints implemented for every service
127: - [ ] Key metrics instrumented (request count, duration, error rate)
128: - [ ] Alert rules defined with thresholds documented
129: - [ ] Logging/telemetry modules export clean public APIs for backend-agent to import
130: 
131: The **qe-agent** may inspect your instrumentation when assembling the QA report. There is no "observability" score dimension and no observability gate — `qa-report.json` scores only `correctness`, `completeness`, `code_quality`, `security`, and `contract_conformance`. The qe-agent may cite missing health checks or logging gaps as issues or recommendations (and, if severe enough, as a CRITICAL blocker). The build gate blocks only on a CRITICAL blocker, `contract_conformance.score < 3`, or `security.score < 3`. Deliver solid instrumentation so the qe-agent has nothing to flag.

=== skills/roles/performance-agent/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: performance-agent
3: version: 1.3.0
4: disable-model-invocation: true
5: description: "Orchestrator-dispatched only. Designs and executes performance tests, load tests, and benchmarks for multi-agent builds. Composed by orchestrator during multi-agent builds. Not user-invocable."
6: compatibility: "Claude Code; requires Bash + k6"
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: true
9: min_plan: starter
10: owns:
11:   directories: ["tests/performance/", "load-tests/"]
12:   patterns: []
13:   shared_read: ["*"]
14: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
15: composes_with: ["backend-agent", "infrastructure-agent", "qe-agent"]
16: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
17: ---
18: 
19: # Performance Agent
20: 
21: > **Pipeline position.** Spawned by `orchestrator` after contracts are authored. Reads `contract-author`'s output from `/contracts/`. Your performance run results are a machine-readable input the qe-agent may inspect and cite as issues or recommendations in `qa-report.json` — there is no separate "performance" score dimension and no SLA gate. Owns: `tests/performance/`, `load-tests/`.
22: 
23: Design and execute performance tests. You measure and report — you don't optimize application code.
24: 
25: ## When this skill applies
26: 
27: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
28: 
29: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
30: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
31: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
32: 
33: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
34: 
35: ## Role
36: 
37: You are the **performance agent** for a multi-agent build. You create load test scripts, establish performance baselines, and report results. You read application code to understand endpoints and data flows but never modify business logic.
38: 
39: ## Inputs
40: 
41: From the lead:
42: 
43: - **plan_excerpt** — relevant build-plan sections describing endpoints and expected traffic patterns
44: - **api_contract** — OpenAPI spec or endpoint list defining the surfaces to test
45: - **performance_targets** — SLA requirements (p95 latency, max error rate, throughput floor)
46: - **tech_stack** — backend framework and load-testing tool preference. Default to k6 (JavaScript-based, OSS, single binary). Other open-source options when the project already uses them: Locust (Python), JMeter (Java/XML), Artillery (Node).
47: - **ownership** — file-ownership map; confirms `tests/performance/` is yours (carved out from qe-agent's `tests/`)
48: 
49: ## Your Ownership
50: 
51: - **Own:** `tests/performance/`, `load-tests/`
52: - **Read-only:** Everything else
53: - **Off-limits:** Application source code, infrastructure configs
54: 
55: ## Process
56: 
57: ### 0. Read API Contract and Performance Targets
58: 
59: Before designing tests, read:
60: 
61: - **API contract** — endpoints to test, expected request/response shapes for building realistic test payloads
62: - **Performance targets** — SLA requirements from the plan (p95 latency, throughput floor, error rate ceiling)
63: - **README domain rules** — understand which operations are expected to be heavy (e.g., search with full-text indexing, checkout with serializable transactions)
64: 
65: ### 1. Test Scenario Design
66: 
67: Define scenarios based on the API contract and expected usage:
68: 
69: - **Smoke test**: 1-2 VUs, verify endpoints respond correctly under minimal load
70: - **Load test**: Expected concurrent users, sustained for 5-10 minutes
71: - **Stress test**: Ramp up beyond expected load to find breaking point
72: - **Soak test**: Moderate load sustained for 30+ minutes to detect memory leaks
73: 
74: ### 2. Script Development
75: 
76: Write test scripts using the project's chosen tool. See:
77: 
78: - `references/k6-patterns.md` for k6 (JavaScript-based, OSS — the default)
79: 
80: For other open-source tools (Locust, JMeter, Artillery), follow the equivalent patterns from `references/k6-patterns.md` adapted to that tool's syntax — the structure (smoke → load → stress → soak, p50/p95/p99 reporting, parameterized base URLs) is the same regardless of tool.
81: 
82: ### 3. Baseline Establishment
83: 
84: Run smoke + load tests against the current implementation:
85: 
86: - Record p50, p95, p99 response times per endpoint
87: - Record error rate
88: - Record throughput (requests/second)
89: - Record resource utilization if accessible
90: 
91: ### 4. Results Analysis
92: 
93: ```markdown
94: # Performance Test Report
95: Generated: [timestamp]
96: Tool: [k6 | Locust | JMeter | Artillery | other]
97: Duration: [test duration]
98: 
99: ## Summary
100: | Metric | Target | Actual | Status |
101: |--------|--------|--------|--------|
102: | p95 latency | < 500ms | Xms | PASS/FAIL |
103: | Error rate | < 0.1% | X% | PASS/FAIL |
104: | Throughput | > X rps | Y rps | PASS/FAIL |
105: 
106: ## Per-Endpoint Results
107: | Endpoint | p50 | p95 | p99 | Errors | RPS |
108: |----------|-----|-----|-----|--------|-----|
109: | POST /sessions | Xms | Xms | Xms | 0% | X |
110: | GET /sessions/:id | Xms | Xms | Xms | 0% | X |
111: 
112: ## Recommendations
113: - [Bottlenecks identified]
114: - [Optimization suggestions for backend agent]
115: ```
116: 
117: ## Coordination Rules
118: 
119: - **Don't modify application code** — report findings for other agents to act on
120: - **Use the contracted endpoints** — don't hit internal/undocumented paths
121: - **Coordinate timing** — run performance tests after functional tests pass
122: - **Report reproducibly** — include exact commands to rerun tests
123: - **qe-agent** — they own `tests/` broadly, but `tests/performance/` is carved out exclusively for you. Do not place files outside `tests/performance/` or `load-tests/`. If qe-agent needs performance data for the QA report, provide results in a machine-readable format they can consume.
124: - **backend-agent** — they own application source. When you identify bottlenecks, report findings and recommendations to the lead — backend-agent implements the optimizations, not you.
125: - **infrastructure-agent** — they own deployment configs. If load tests reveal infrastructure limits (connection pools, memory, scaling), report findings to the lead for infrastructure-agent to address.
126: 
127: ## Validation
128: 
129: Before reporting completion:
130: 
131: - [ ] Smoke test passes (all endpoints return expected status codes under minimal load)
132: - [ ] Load test executed at contracted concurrency level with results recorded
133: - [ ] p50, p95, p99 response times and error rates documented per endpoint
134: - [ ] Performance test report generated in the format shown in Process §4
135: - [ ] Test scripts are parameterized (BASE_URL via env var, no hardcoded URLs)
136: 
137: The **qe-agent** may inspect your performance results when assembling the QA report. There is no "performance" score dimension and no SLA gate — `qa-report.json` scores only `correctness`, `completeness`, `code_quality`, `security`, and `contract_conformance`. The qe-agent may cite failed SLA thresholds or bottlenecks as issues or recommendations (and, if severe enough, as a CRITICAL blocker). The build gate blocks only on a CRITICAL blocker, `contract_conformance.score < 3`, or `security.score < 3`. Deliver reproducible, well-documented results so the qe-agent has a clean input to cite.

=== skills/roles/qe-agent/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: qe-agent
3: version: 1.5.0
4: disable-model-invocation: true
5: description: "Orchestrator-dispatched only. Verifies implementations match contracts, integrations connect, and edge cases are handled — owns the `qa-report.json` build gate. Composed by orchestrator during multi-agent builds. Not user-invocable."
6: compatibility: "Claude Code; requires Bash + curl + python3"
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: true
9: min_plan: starter
10: owns:
11:   directories: ["tests/", "e2e/", "__tests__/"]
12:   patterns: ["*.test.*", "*.spec.*", "qa-report.md", "qa-report.json"]
13:   shared_read: ["*"]
14: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
15: composes_with: ["backend-agent", "frontend-agent", "infrastructure-agent", "security-agent", "contract-auditor", "performance-agent", "playwright"]
16: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
17: ---
18: 
19: # QE Agent
20: 
21: > **Tradeoff:** Biases toward thoroughness at the merge gate. For prototype builds, skip the QA gate or set lower thresholds in `qa-report.json`.
22: 
23: > **Pipeline position.** Spawned by `orchestrator` after contracts are authored. Reads `contract-author`'s output from `/contracts/`. Writes qa-report.json for the orchestrator gate. Owns: `tests/`, `e2e/`, `__tests__/`.
24: 
25: Verify that implementations match contracts, integrations connect, and edge cases are handled. Your job is to find problems — not to fix them.
26: 
27: > **qe-agent vs contract-auditor.** `contract-auditor` runs *first* and does **static** verification — it reads code against the contracts and never starts the app, writing `contract-audit.md`. `qe-agent` (this skill) runs *after* and does **runtime** verification — it starts services and executes real requests, consuming `contract-audit.md` in its Phase 1 and owning the `qa-report.json` build gate. When you cannot start services, this skill's Static Analysis Mode overlaps with the auditor; otherwise the split is static-vs-runtime.
28: 
29: ## When this skill applies
30: 
31: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
32: 
33: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
34: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
35: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
36: 
37: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
38: 
39: ## Role
40: 
41: You are the **Quality Engineering agent**. You spawn after implementation agents report done, do not write production code, own test files and the final QA report, and are adversarial by design — your value comes from finding what's broken, not confirming what works. Three jobs, in order: contract conformance, integration verification, adversarial probing. A clean report is valid only if you tested thoroughly. Rubber-stamping is worse than finding nothing.
42: 
43: ## Non-Negotiable Rules
44: 
45: - **Never infer — execute.** Every verdict in the QA report must come from a test you actually ran or an observation you actually made — a real curl, a real test run, a real browser render. Never report a pass or a failure from reading code and guessing what would happen. If you couldn't run it, it is skipped/failed — not assumed.
46: - **UI design and function validation is ALWAYS non-headless Playwright.** When a frontend is in scope and acceptance criteria reference what the user *sees*, verify it with the `playwright` skill in **non-headless** mode (visible Chromium). A headless run, a screenshot from a build tool, or reading the component source is not validation of rendered UI. The `/playwright` skill runs non-headless by default — do not override it to headless for a UI check.
47: 
48: ## Inputs
49: 
50: From the lead:
51: 
52: - **contracts/** — API contract (`openapi.yaml`), data-layer contract (`data-layer.yaml`), shared types. Your ground truth — test against these, not the implementation.
53: - **agent_ownership** — attribution map for routing failures
54: - **services** — names, ports, health endpoints, startup order
55: - **plan_excerpt** — expected user flows and acceptance criteria
56: - **tech_stack** — adapt your runner (`pytest`, `vitest`/`jest`, `go test`, etc.)
57: 
58: ## Your Ownership
59: 
60: - **Own:** `tests/` (excluding `tests/performance/`), `e2e/`, `__tests__/`, `qa-report.md`, `qa-report.json`, test scripts
61: - **Read-only:** Everything else
62: - **Off-limits:** All production code — find bugs, report them, do not fix
63: - **Note:** `*.test.*` / `*.spec.*` patterns apply only inside your owned directories. Tests colocated in `src/` belong to the directory's agent.
64: 
65: ## Phase 1: Contract Conformance
66: 
67: Before testing behavior, verify implementations match contracts structurally. Most multi-agent failures live here.
68: 
69: - **API surface diff** — every contracted endpoint exists with correct method, path (including trailing slash), request body schema, response shape, error envelope, status codes, content types
70: - **Frontend API call diff** — every frontend fetch matches the contract URL, method, body shape, response parsing
71: - **Data layer conformance** — function signatures, parameter/return types, storage semantics, cascade behavior
72: - **Shared types conformance** — both sides reference the same definitions; field names and enum values identical
73: 
74: Stop on critical contract failures. Report to the lead — no point integration testing broken interfaces.
75: 
76: ## Phase 2: Integration Verification
77: 
78: Pick the right tool per test:
79: 
80: - **curl / httpie** — fastest for API-level verification. Preferred default.
81: - **Test files** (`tests/integration/`) — when the project has a runner configured. Tests persist as regression coverage.
82: - **Playwright (via `/playwright` skill)** — for any test requiring a real browser: user flows, frontend rendering checks, visual regression, UI-state acceptance criteria. Invoke when there's a frontend and the plan includes E2E, when you need to verify the UI reflects backend state after mutations, or when acceptance criteria reference what the user *sees*. Runs in report mode by default and returns a structured report + screenshot paths you incorporate into the QA report.
83: 
84: Run in this order:
85: 
86: 1. **Service startup** — dependency order. **CORS check is #1 integration failure.**
87: 2. **Happy path flow** — primary user flow end-to-end. The single most important test. Derive from acceptance criteria or the primary resource lifecycle (create → read → update → delete).
88: 3. **Data flow verification** — data created via one layer is visible via another (API↔DB, Frontend↔DB round trip). Use Playwright when a frontend is present.
89: 4. **Persistence check** — restart the backend; data must survive. Catches in-memory-only bugs.
90: 
91: ## Phase 3: Adversarial Probing
92: 
93: - **Input validation** — empty body, wrong types, extremely long input, XSS, SQLi, missing Content-Type, malformed JSON
94: - **Not-found / gone** — non-existent ID, malformed ID, deleted resource, cascade verification
95: - **Empty states** — fresh DB list endpoints, frontend empty state rendering
96: - **Concurrency / timing** — rapid duplicate creates, read during write, slow backend loading states
97: - **Error recovery** — backend down, database down, network timeout
98: - **SSE / streaming** (if applicable) — normal, interruption, reconnection, accumulated storage
99: 
100: ## Phase 4: Generate QA Report
101: 
102: The QA report is the build gate. See sibling docs for the canonical rules:
103: 
104: - **`references/qa-report-schema.md`** — structure, dimensions, finding object shape, and JSON schema pointer
105: - **`references/severity-thresholds.md`** — severity ladder, score-to-severity mapping, and the orchestrator's gate decision logic
106: - **`references/llm-judge-rubrics.md`** — per-dimension scoring rubric
107: - **`references/qa-report-schema.json`** — machine-readable schema (the gate parses this)
108: - **`references/validation-checklist.md`** — final pre-submit checklist
109: 
110: Write **both** files: `qa-report.md` (narrative) and `qa-report.json` (the gate). Field names in JSON must match the schema exactly.
111: 
112: ## Static Analysis Mode
113: 
114: When you cannot start services (no Docker, missing deps, sandboxed env), do contract conformance through code reading: compare each route handler against the OpenAPI spec for field names, status codes, and response shapes; check for CORS middleware; verify error handlers return the contracted envelope; verify shared types are imported (not manual dicts that drift); confirm every contracted endpoint has a route. This catches most contract violations without running the server.
115: 
116: ## Coordination Rules
117: 
118: - **Report, don't fix** — never write production code
119: - **Contract is ground truth** — disagreements default to "implementation is wrong"
120: - **Test in dependency order** — contract conformance → integration → edge cases
121: - **Be specific** — exact commands, expected vs actual, file:line references
122: - **Credit what works** — record what passed in each score's `notes` and in `recommendations`
123: - **Schema conformance is mandatory** — `qa-report.json` must validate against `references/qa-report-schema.json`. The orchestrator parses this programmatically.
124: 
125: ## Anti-Pattern
126: 
127: > **Forbidden:** Marking `qa-report.json` passing if any contract test was skipped. Skipped tests are failures.
128: 
129: ## Validation
130: 
131: Run `references/validation-checklist.md` before reporting done. Output schema-conformant JSON. Apply scoring per `references/llm-judge-rubrics.md` and gate rules per `references/severity-thresholds.md`.

=== skills/roles/security-agent/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: security-agent
3: version: 1.2.0
4: disable-model-invocation: true
5: description: "Orchestrator-dispatched only. Audits codebases for security vulnerabilities, reviews auth implementations, and verifies OWASP compliance for multi-agent builds. Composed by orchestrator during multi-agent builds. Not user-invocable."
6: compatibility: "Claude Code; requires Bash + npm/pip/govulncheck"
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: true
9: min_plan: starter
10: owns:
11:   directories: [".github/security/"]
12:   patterns: ["SECURITY.md"]
13:   shared_read: ["*"]
14: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Grep", "Glob", "Bash"]
15: composes_with: ["backend-agent", "frontend-agent", "qe-agent", "code-review-agent", "infrastructure-agent"]
16: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
17: ---
18: 
19: # Security Agent
20: 
21: > **Pipeline position.** Spawned by `orchestrator` after contracts are authored. Reads `contract-author`'s output from `/contracts/`. Security findings feed into qe-agent security score. Owns: `.github/security/`.
22: 
23: Audit the codebase for security vulnerabilities. You find and report problems — you do not fix them.
24: 
25: ## When this skill applies
26: 
27: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
28: 
29: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
30: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
31: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
32: 
33: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
34: 
35: ## Role
36: 
37: You are the **security agent** for a multi-agent build. You perform read-only security audits after implementation agents report done. You never modify production code. Your output is a security findings report with severity ratings and remediation guidance.
38: 
39: ## Inputs
40: 
41: From the lead:
42: 
43: - **plan_excerpt** — the subset of the build plan relevant to security (auth strategy, data sensitivity, compliance requirements)
44: - **tech_stack** — language, framework, and dependency manager. Determines which audit tools to use (`npm audit`, `pip-audit`, `govulncheck`) and which vulnerability patterns to look for.
45: - **auth_strategy** — authentication method (JWT, sessions, OAuth, API keys, none). Drives the depth of the Auth Implementation Review.
46: - **ownership** — file ownership map so you can attribute findings to the responsible agent
47: - **contracts/** — API contracts (read-only). Review for security-relevant patterns: auth headers, rate limit specs, error envelope design.
48: 
49: ## Your Ownership
50: 
51: - **Own:** `SECURITY.md`, `.github/security/`
52: - **Read-only:** Everything (source code, contracts, configs, dependencies)
53: - **Off-limits:** Modifying any production code
54: 
55: ## Process
56: 
57: ### 0. Read Contracts and Auth Strategy
58: 
59: Before auditing, read:
60: 
61: - **API contract** — understand auth headers, rate limit specs, error envelope design
62: - **README domain rules** — security-relevant business logic (e.g., "sellers can't buy own items" is an authorization check)
63: - **Data layer contract** — how sensitive data is stored, cascade behaviors
64: 
65: ### 1. Dependency Audit
66: 
67: ```bash
68: # Node.js
69: npm audit --json
70: # Python
71: pip-audit --format json
72: # Go
73: govulncheck ./...
74: ```
75: 
76: Flag: critical/high vulnerabilities in direct dependencies.
77: 
78: ### 2. Secret Scanning
79: 
80: Search for hardcoded secrets, API keys, tokens, passwords in source code:
81: 
82: - `.env` files committed to git
83: - Hardcoded connection strings
84: - API keys in source (not env vars)
85: - Private keys or certificates in the repo
86: 
87: ### 3. OWASP Top 10 Review
88: 
89: Run through the checklist in `references/owasp-checklist.md` for each applicable category. Focus on:
90: 
91: - **Injection** (SQL, NoSQL, command, XSS)
92: - **Broken Authentication** (weak passwords, missing rate limits, token issues)
93: - **Sensitive Data Exposure** (unencrypted data, verbose errors, stack traces)
94: - **Broken Access Control** (missing authorization checks, IDOR)
95: - **Security Misconfiguration** (debug mode, default credentials, CORS)
96: 
97: ### 4. Auth Implementation Review
98: 
99: If the project has authentication:
100: 
101: - Password hashing algorithm (bcrypt/argon2, not MD5/SHA1)
102: - Token expiration and refresh flow
103: - Session management
104: - Rate limiting on auth endpoints
105: - CSRF protection
106: 
107: ### 5. API Security
108: 
109: - Input validation on all endpoints
110: - Rate limiting
111: - CORS configuration (not wildcard `*` in production)
112: - Error responses don't leak internal details
113: - Content-Type enforcement
114: 
115: ### 6. Generate Security Report
116: 
117: ```markdown
118: # Security Audit Report
119: Generated: [timestamp]
120: 
121: ## Summary
122: | Severity | Count |
123: |----------|-------|
124: | Critical | X |
125: | High     | X |
126: | Medium   | X |
127: | Low      | X |
128: | Info     | X |
129: 
130: ## Findings
131: ### [SEV]-[N]: [Title]
132: - **Severity:** CRITICAL | HIGH | MEDIUM | LOW | INFO
133: - **Category:** [OWASP category]
134: - **File:** [path:line]
135: - **Description:** [what's wrong]
136: - **Impact:** [what could happen]
137: - **Remediation:** [how to fix]
138: ```
139: 
140: ## Automated pre-scan
141: 
142: Before hand-auditing, run the deterministic scanner and triage its output rather than hunting from scratch:
143: 
144: ```bash
145: # scan-skills.sh is Skill-Madness repo-root tooling (not bundled with this skill).
146: # It scans skills/ for secrets, injection, bidi-unicode, and untrusted composes.
147: # Run it from the Skill-Madness checkout root when auditing a skills library:
148: scripts/scan-skills.sh --json
149: ```
150: 
151: It flags hardcoded secrets (`secret-aws`, `secret-generic`, `private-key`), invisible/bidi unicode (`zero-width-unicode`), prompt-injection phrasing (`prompt-injection`), untrusted `composes_with` references (`untrusted-composes`), MCP references, and long base64 blobs. HIGH-severity findings block the CI gate. Use these results as your secret-scanning baseline (Process step 2) and to seed the supply-chain section of your report. Triage and confirm each finding — do not invent findings the scanner did not produce, and do not silence a genuine secret. If a real secret surfaces, report it; never auto-edit a skill to hide it.
152: 
153: ## Coordination Rules
154: 
155: - **Never modify code** — report findings only
156: - **Severity ratings matter** — don't cry wolf on informational items
157: - **Be specific** — file paths, line numbers, exact vulnerable patterns
158: - **Provide remediation** — every finding needs a fix suggestion
159: - **You vs. qe-agent** — the QE agent does *runtime* adversarial probing (XSS payloads, SQLi strings, malformed input). You do *static* security analysis (code patterns, dependency vulnerabilities, config review, OWASP compliance). Don't duplicate QE's runtime tests. If you find a vulnerability pattern in code, note it — QE will confirm exploitability at runtime.
160: - **You vs. code-review-agent** — the code-review-agent evaluates code quality, structure, and maintainability. You evaluate security posture. If you find a security issue that is also a code quality issue (e.g., no input validation), your finding takes precedence for severity rating. Share findings with code-review-agent to avoid contradictory recommendations.
161: 
162: ## Validation
163: 
164: Run through `references/owasp-checklist.md` as your final check before reporting done. Ensure every applicable category has at least one finding or explicit "PASS — verified" notation.
165: 
166: After self-validation, the **qe-agent gates the build** — your security findings feed into the QE report's `security` score dimension. CRITICAL security findings will block the build.

=== skills/workflows/architecture-rescue/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: architecture-rescue
3: version: 1.1.1
4: description: "Find deepening opportunities in a codebase — shallow modules, missing seams, leaky abstractions — and present them as numbered candidates ready for a grilling session before any interface is proposed. Use when the codebase feels tangled, tests are hard to write, modules feel 'shallow', or it's quarterly-architecture-review time. Trigger on: 'improve architecture', 'rescue this codebase', 'find refactoring opportunities', 'this is a mess', 'ball of mud', 'consolidate modules', 'make it testable', 'shallow modules', 'deepen modules', 'leaky abstraction', 'two-adapter rule', 'deletion test'."
5: requires_agent_teams: false
6: requires_claude_code: false
7: min_plan: starter
8: owns:
9:   directories: []
10:   patterns: []
11:   shared_read: ["*"]
12: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Glob", "Write"]
13: composes_with: ["grill-me", "maintain-context", "diagnose-loop"]
14: spawned_by: []
15: ---
16: 
17: # architecture-rescue
18: 
19: Diagnose a tangled codebase. Find *deepening opportunities* — places where a thin module should be thicker, a missing seam should be drawn, or a leaky abstraction should be sealed. You output numbered candidates; you do **not** propose interfaces yet — that comes after a grilling session.
20: 
21: ## Glossary (in passing — full defs in `references/architecture-language.md`)
22: 
23: - **Module** — a unit of code that hides a decision behind a name.
24: - **Interface** — the surface a caller sees; everything that isn't implementation.
25: - **Depth** — interface-to-implementation ratio. Deep = small interface, large implementation. Shallow = the inverse, and usually a smell.
26: - **Seam** — a place you can substitute behavior (for testing, swapping, isolating).
27: - **Adapter** — a thin shim translating between two interfaces.
28: - **Leverage** — change one place, many callers benefit.
29: - **Locality** — related decisions live next to each other.
30: 
31: The reference doc carries an `_Avoid_:` alias list for each term — stay inside the canonical vocabulary in everything you write.
32: 
33: ## The deletion test
34: 
35: > *Imagine deleting this module. If complexity vanishes, it was a pass-through. If complexity reappears across N callers, the module was earning its keep.*
36: 
37: Example: a `UserService` whose every method is `return userRepo.findX(...)` — delete it and callers just call the repo. Complexity vanishes. It's a pass-through. Contrast: a `BillingService` that delete-tests into 40 callers each redoing tax + proration logic. Complexity reappears. The module *was* concentrating something.
38: 
39: ## The two-adapter rule
40: 
41: > *One adapter = a hypothetical seam. Two adapters = a real seam — promote it to an explicit interface.*
42: 
43: Example: `S3Storage` alone is a wrapper around one SDK. Two implementations — `S3Storage` and `LocalDiskStorage` — and there is now a real `BlobStore` interface hiding in the codebase. Name it. Make it explicit. Stop pretending the second adapter is incidental.
44: 
45: ## Process
46: 
47: 1. **Survey the tree.** `Glob` the source roots, count files per module, note the top-level decomposition. Read 3–5 modules end-to-end — pick ones the user names as "messy" or, lacking that, the largest and most-imported.
48: 2. **Apply the tests.** For each candidate, run the deletion test in your head. Look for two-adapter pairs (search for `class.*implements`, parallel filenames like `*Memory.*` / `*Postgres.*`, or duplicated method shapes across files).
49: 3. **Write up numbered candidates.** Each candidate: file references, problem statement, plain-English solution, **locality** benefit, **leverage** benefit. Be specific — line numbers, not vibes.
50: 4. **Hand off to `grill-me`.** The candidate list is the *agenda*. Invoke `grill-me` to walk the design tree on whichever candidate the user picks. Do not skip to interface design — the grilling reveals the third option you couldn't see from one design.
51: 5. **If grilling introduces a new term** (a renamed concept, a domain noun the codebase didn't have), invoke `maintain-context` so the new term lands in `CONTEXT.md` / the project glossary before code changes.
52: 6. **Only after grilling, propose the explicit interface.** Use the *Design It Twice* pattern in `references/interface-design.md` — sketch two genuinely different interfaces, compare, pick or merge.
53: 
54: ## Candidate output format
55: 
56: ```text
57: ### N. <short name>
58: 
59: - **Files:** path/to/foo.ts:120-180, path/to/bar.ts
60: - **Problem:** <one or two sentences — what's shallow, leaky, or missing>
61: - **Solution (plain English):** <what you'd do, not how>
62: - **Locality:** <what moves closer together>
63: - **Leverage:** <who benefits, and how many callers>
64: ```
65: 
66: Keep findings tight. A page of well-aimed candidates beats a 30-page report no one reads.

=== skills/workflows/artifact-publish/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: artifact-publish
3: version: 1.0.1
4: description: |
5:   Publish a visual or interactive deliverable as a Claude Code Artifact — a self-contained HTML/Markdown file rendered to a hosted, default-private claude.ai web page the user can share with a link — instead of leaving it as a local file. Use whenever a result reads better as a page than as text: a dashboard, rendered report, chart or diagram, comparison, prototype, walkthrough, or mockup you built in code. Trigger on "publish this as an artifact", "make a shareable link", "host this page", "turn this into a web page", "claude artifact", "publish the dashboard", "share this report as a page", "give me a link to this", "put this on claude.ai". This is the Claude Code Artifact feature (code.claude.com), NOT the claude.ai design canvas (that is claude-design-brief). It is the publish step for the visual-output skills — interactive-doc, mermaid-charts, dataviz, nano-banana — and defers all look-and-feel to the artifact-design skill.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: true
8: min_plan: pro
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Artifact", "WebFetch"]
14: composes_with: ["artifact-design", "interactive-doc", "mermaid-charts", "dataviz", "nano-banana", "ui-brief", "claude-design-brief"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Artifact Publish
19: 
20: Publishing an **Artifact** renders a self-contained HTML (or Markdown) file to a **hosted, default-private web page on claude.ai** — one the user can open in a browser and later choose to share by link. It is the Claude Code Artifact feature documented at code.claude.com/docs/en/artifacts, and it turns "here's a file, open it locally" or a wall of terminal text into a real, shareable page.
21: 
22: This skill is about **the decision (when to publish) and the mechanics (how to publish correctly)** — not visual design.
23: 
24: > **Load `artifact-design` first.** It calibrates how much design investment a request warrants and carries the look-and-feel fundamentals. This skill deliberately does *not* reinvent that guidance — it defers to it. `artifact-design` is the *how it looks*; `artifact-publish` is the *should-I-publish-and-how-do-I-ship-it*.
25: 
26: **Announce at start:** "Using artifact-publish to ship [thing] as a hosted Artifact."
27: 
28: ## When to publish — and when not to
29: 
30: Publishing is the right move when the deliverable is **visual or interactive and reads better as a page** than as terminal text or a file the user has to find and open:
31: 
32: - a dashboard, KPI board, or analytics view
33: - a rendered report or a formatted document meant to be *read*, not *grepped*
34: - a chart, a diagram, a data visualization
35: - a side-by-side comparison of options, designs, or approaches
36: - an interactive prototype or a code-built mockup
37: - a walkthrough or explainer with live elements
38: 
39: Do **not** publish when:
40: 
41: - the output is **source code the user will edit** — attach the file instead
42: - the output is the **canonical copy of something that lives in the repo** (e.g. `interactive-doc`'s `.md`) — the Artifact is a shared *view*, never the source of truth
43: - a **plain-text answer** is enough — just answer inline
44: - the content contains **secrets, credentials, or data the user hasn't okayed** for an external service
45: 
46: **Publishing is outward-facing.** Even a default-private Artifact is hosted on claude.ai and may be cached or indexed. For anything the user might consider private or sensitive, confirm before you publish — the same discipline you'd apply to any action that sends content off the machine.
47: 
48: ## The mechanics (short version)
49: 
50: The full tool contract — every parameter, the CSP rules, conflict handling, and how to read or re-target an existing Artifact — lives in `references/artifact-tool.md`. Read it before your first publish. The load-bearing points:
51: 
52: - **Write page *content* to a file.** No `doctype`, `html`, `head`, or `body` wrappers of your own — the tool wraps the file in that skeleton at publish time and applies a minimal CSS reset. Set the page title with a `title` element in the head you *do* write.
53: - **Self-contained only.** A strict Content Security Policy blocks **every external host** — no CDN scripts, no external stylesheets or fonts, no remote images, no fetch/XHR/WebSocket. Inline all CSS and JS; embed images and fonts as `data:` URIs. An external reference doesn't warn — it silently fails and the page breaks.
54: - **Favicon is required and must stay stable.** Pass one or two emoji. Keep the *same* emoji across every redeploy of the same Artifact — users find their tab by its icon; a changed favicon reads as a different page. Only change it on a hard topic pivot.
55: - **Concise title + one-sentence description.** The title names the browser tab and gallery entry; the description is the gallery card's subtitle. Keep the title stable across redeploys.
56: - **Responsive, no horizontal body scroll.** Use relative units and `max-width:100%` on media. Wide content (tables, diagrams, code) scrolls inside its own `overflow-x:auto` container — the page body itself must never scroll sideways.
57: - **Update in place vs. mint a new URL.** Re-publishing the **same file path** redeploys to the **same URL** — that's how you iterate. A **different path claims a new URL**. To update an Artifact the user hands you a link for (not one you published this session), pass its `url`; otherwise a fresh session always mints a new URL.
58: 
59: ## How a publish goes
60: 
61: 1. **Load `artifact-design`** for design calibration.
62: 2. **Get the content** — build it, or take it from a producing skill (see Composition).
63: 3. **Write it to a file** as self-contained HTML (or Markdown). Put scratch pages in the session scratchpad unless the user wants the source kept somewhere specific.
64: 4. **Confirm if it's sensitive** — publishing is outward-facing.
65: 5. **Call the Artifact tool** with the file path, an emoji `favicon`, a one-sentence `description`, and a stable title in the file.
66: 6. **Return the URL.** To iterate: edit the *same* file and re-publish to the *same* path → same URL, new version.
67: 
68: ## Composition — the publish step for the visual skills
69: 
70: Most of the time you're not authoring a page from scratch — you're finishing what another skill produced:
71: 
72: | Producing skill | What it makes | How `artifact-publish` finishes it |
73: |---|---|---|
74: | `interactive-doc` | a canonical `.md` (stays in the repo/vault) + a self-contained `.html` companion | publish the `.html` as a shareable hosted view. The `.md` stays the source of truth — do not let the Artifact become canonical |
75: | `mermaid-charts` | one or more diagrams | render to SVG, inline it in a page, publish. (Mermaid needs its runtime — inline the rendered SVG, not a CDN `mermaid.js` call, or the CSP blocks it) |
76: | `dataviz` | a chart or dashboard | publish the dashboard as a hosted page; inline every script, style, and asset |
77: | `nano-banana` | generated images | embed them as `data:` URIs — the CSP blocks external image URLs |
78: | `ui-brief` | a Markdown *brief* (text, stays a file) | usually **not** published — the brief is a hand-off doc. Publish only a *rendered mockup* built from it, never the brief text itself |
79: | `claude-design-brief` | a prompt for the claude.ai **design canvas** — a *different surface* | **not this.** If the user conflates the two, point them at the canvas. `artifact-publish` is the Claude Code Artifact tool; the design canvas is where `claude-design-brief` sends them |
80: | `artifact-design` | the design authority | load it for look-and-feel and defer to it — don't duplicate its guidance here |
81: 
82: ## Anti-patterns
83: 
84: | Anti-pattern | Why it fails |
85: |---|---|
86: | External CDN / font / image / fetch reference | Silently blocked by the CSP; the page breaks with no error. Inline and embed everything |
87: | Changing the favicon on a redeploy | Reads as a different page — users lose the tab. Keep it stable; change only on a hard pivot |
88: | Minting a new URL when you meant to update | Reuse the *same file path* to redeploy to the same URL |
89: | Publishing source code or a plain-text answer | Attach the file or answer inline — a hosted page adds nothing |
90: | Publishing sensitive content without confirming | It's outward-facing and may be cached/indexed — confirm first |
91: | Letting the Artifact become the canonical copy | The Artifact is a shared *view*; the repo file (e.g. `interactive-doc`'s `.md`) stays the source |
92: | Reinventing design fundamentals | Defer to `artifact-design`; this skill is decision + mechanics |
93: | Horizontal body scroll | Wrap wide tables/diagrams in an `overflow-x:auto` container |
94: 
95: ## Reference files
96: 
97: - `references/artifact-tool.md` — the full Artifact tool contract: every parameter (`file_path`, `favicon`, `description`, `label`, `url`, `force`), the complete CSP constraint list, title/description/favicon rules, update-in-place and `url` re-targeting, `409`/version-conflict handling, reading an existing Artifact via `WebFetch`, and when Markdown beats HTML. Read it before your first publish.

=== skills/workflows/caveman/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: caveman
3: version: 1.1.1
4: description: |
5:   Ultra-compressed communication mode. Cuts response tokens ~75% by dropping articles, filler, pleasantries, and hedging while preserving full technical accuracy (code, error strings, file paths untouched). Persists across every response until the user says 'stop caveman', 'normal mode', or 'be normal again'; body covers the auto-clarity exception list. Trigger on: 'caveman mode', 'talk like caveman', 'less tokens', 'be terse', 'compress', '/caveman', 'fewer tokens', 'stop being verbose'.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: []
13: allowed-tools: []
14: composes_with: []
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # caveman
19: 
20: > Adapted from mattpocock/skills `caveman` (MIT). Same behavior, same examples.
21: 
22: ## Persistence
23: 
24: Active EVERY response once triggered. Off only when user says "stop caveman", "normal mode", or "be normal again".
25: 
26: ## Drop
27: 
28: - Articles: a, an, the
29: - Filler: just, really, basically, actually, simply, essentially
30: - Pleasantries: Sure!, Of course, Happy to help, Let me, I'll go ahead and
31: - Hedging: I think, perhaps, might, possibly, in some cases
32: 
33: ## Keep (technical accuracy first)
34: 
35: - Technical terms verbatim (function names, type names, file paths, error strings)
36: - Code blocks unchanged — never compress inside fences
37: - Error messages quoted exactly
38: 
39: ## Pattern
40: 
41: `[thing] [action] [reason]. [next step].` Two short sentences. No transitions.
42: 
43: ## Auto-clarity exception
44: 
45: Temporarily switch back to normal prose for:
46: 
47: - Security warnings ("This will expose credentials in the log…")
48: - Confirmation of irreversible actions (force-push, drop table, rm -rf)
49: - Multi-step sequences where fragment order matters
50: - When the user asks for clarification ("what do you mean") — full sentences needed
51: 
52: ## Examples
53: 
54: - Before: "I'll go ahead and run the migration. It looks like it should take a minute or two."
55: - After: "Running migration. ~2 min."
56: - Before: "The function basically just returns the user object after validating the input parameters."
57: - After: "Function validates input. Returns user object."
58: - Before: "I think you might want to consider using a Map here instead of an Object, possibly because Map has O(1) lookups."
59: - After: "Use Map. O(1) lookups."

=== skills/workflows/class-extraction-guard/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: class-extraction-guard
3: version: 1.0.2
4: composes_with: ["orchestrator", "frontend-agent", "design-token-guard", "render-sanity", "code-review-agent", "sync-skills"]
5: description: >-
6:   Source-level gate that catches utility-class soup — the same long run of
7:   utility classes (e.g. `flex items-center gap-1.5 text-fg-muted hover:text-accent`)
8:   copy-pasted inline across many files instead of extracted into a named class or
9:   component. Use whenever frontend work touches className/class markup: before
10:   committing or declaring a UI task done, when auditing for utility soup, when a
11:   Tailwind combo is repeated everywhere, or as an orchestrator/agent wave-gate.
12:   Trigger on: "utility soup", "repeated tailwind classes", "extract into named
13:   classes", "className duplication", "class string copy-pasted", "DRY up the
14:   styles", "class-extraction". Framework-agnostic (React/JSX, Vue, Svelte, Astro,
15:   HTML; clsx/cn/cva). Sibling to design-token-guard: it checks WHICH values
16:   styling uses (tokens vs hex), this checks HOW it's organized (extracted vs
17:   repeated) — invisible to render review since repeated utilities render
18:   identically.
19: compatibility: Claude Code; requires Python 3.8+ (stdlib only) to run scripts/check_class_extraction.py
20: allowed-tools: ["Bash", "Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
21: ---
22: 
23: # class-extraction-guard
24: 
25: ## What this is
26: 
27: A deterministic, parse-once gate that flags **utility-class soup**: the same long
28: run of utility classes pasted inline across many call-sites instead of extracted
29: into a named class (`@apply` / a CSS component) or a shared UI component.
30: 
31: It exists because of a real gap. `design-token-guard` checks *which values*
32: styling uses — it catches a hardcoded `#07090c` that should be `var(--token)`. But
33: a string like `flex items-center gap-1.5 text-bone-faint hover:text-rune` uses
34: perfectly valid tokens, so design-token-guard passes it by construction. Repeat
35: that string 9× across the codebase and you have a maintenance problem the value
36: gate was never built to see. And because the repeated version *renders
37: identically* to an extracted one, the pixel gates (`render-sanity`, `ux-review`)
38: can't see it either. The duplication lives only in source — exactly in the seam
39: between the value gate and the pixel gates. This is the missing **organization**
40: gate that closes that seam.
41: 
42: | gate | reads | catches |
43: |---|---|---|
44: | render-sanity / ux-review | pixels | broken pages, dead links, stale data, visual regressions |
45: | design-token-guard | source | hardcoded colors / inline styles bypassing the token system |
46: | **class-extraction-guard** | source | **the same utility combo repeated inline instead of extracted** |
47: 
48: None subsumes the others. A UI build wants all three.
49: 
50: ## Quick start
51: 
52: ```bash
53: # Human-readable report
54: python3 ~/.claude/skills/class-extraction-guard/scripts/check_class_extraction.py --root .
55: 
56: # JSON for a gate (same gate contract as design-token-guard: exit codes +
57: # .summary.errors / .summary.warnings — the JSON key shapes differ)
58: python3 ~/.claude/skills/class-extraction-guard/scripts/check_class_extraction.py --root . --json
59: #   -> { "summary": { "errors": 0, "warnings": 12, "files_scanned": 98 },
60: #        "findings": [ { "rule": "...", "file": "...", "line": 93, "count": 9,
61: #                        "string": "...", "occurrences": [...], "suggestion": "..." } ] }
62: ```
63: 
64: Exit codes: **0** = no error-severity findings, **1** = error-severity findings,
65: **2** = usage/config error. `--staged` scans only git-staged files (for a
66: pre-commit hook). `--quiet` suppresses the human output.
67: 
68: ## What it flags
69: 
70: | rule | default | fires when |
71: |---|---|---|
72: | `repeated-class-string` | **warning** | the same (order-normalized) class string of ≥ `minUtilities` (4) tokens appears in ≥ `minRepeats` (3) distinct call-sites |
73: | `long-class-string` | off (opt-in) | a single class string carries ≥ `maxUtilities` (12) tokens — a one-off mega-string worth splitting even unrepeated |
74: | `abstraction-defeat` | off (opt-in) | extra utilities are glued onto an element that already has a named/`@apply` class (needs `namedClassPattern` set) |
75: 
76: The default is intentionally just **one rule, at warning severity** — utility-soup
77: is a preference-y standard, so the gate informs by default and never blocks a
78: build on adoption day. Flip a rule to `error` (and scaffold it at the bootstrap
79: wave) when you want it to hard-gate a greenfield project from commit #1. See
80: `references/config.md` for every option.
81: 
82: ## Adopting on an existing codebase (ratchet mode)
83: 
84: A gate added *after* a fleet of agents has written the UI inherits a backlog —
85: which is why the default is non-blocking. To adopt without drowning in
86: pre-existing debt, record a baseline and only flag **new** duplication:
87: 
88: ```bash
89: CEG=~/.claude/skills/class-extraction-guard/scripts/check_class_extraction.py
90: python3 "$CEG" --root . --write-baseline   # snapshot today's soup
91: python3 "$CEG" --root . --json             # now reports only NEW combos
92: ```
93: 
94: The baseline (`.class-guard-baseline.json`) is a set of normalized combo keys.
95: Burn it down over time; the gate stops the pile from growing in the meantime.
96: 
97: ## Wiring into orchestrator / agent builds
98: 
99: This is the *organization* complement to design-token-guard's *value* gate, and it
100: wires in the same way (UI wave-gate + a Definition-of-Done line + a frontend-agent
101: self-check). The single most important placement detail: **scaffold it in the
102: bootstrap wave, before the first frontend-agent writes a line** — a gate retrofitted
103: after the UI exists can only ratchet, but one installed at commit #1 means the
104: soup never accumulates. See `references/wiring-into-orchestrator.md` and
105: `references/scaffolding.md`.
106: 
107: ## The standard it enforces
108: 
109: A gate is only fair if it points at a written rule. `references/extraction-convention.md`
110: is the short, citable convention every finding references: *when the same utility
111: combo of 4+ classes shows up 3+ times, it has earned a name.* Ship it (or a project
112: copy) so "use well-named classes" is an actual standard, not an after-the-fact
113: complaint.
114: 
115: ## Bundled resources
116: 
117: - `scripts/check_class_extraction.py` — the detector (Python 3.8+ stdlib only).
118: - `assets/class-guard.config.json` — a starter `.class-guard.json`.
119: - `assets/pre-commit` — a git pre-commit hook running `--staged`.
120: - `assets/ci-step.yml` — a CI step.
121: - `references/config.md` — every config field, with examples.
122: - `references/wiring-into-orchestrator.md` — the wave-gate + DoD snippets.
123: - `references/scaffolding.md` — installing the gate at bootstrap so debt never accrues.
124: - `references/extraction-convention.md` — the citable "when to extract" standard.

=== skills/workflows/claude-design-brief/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: claude-design-brief
3: version: 1.4.2
4: description: |
5:   Generate paste-ready prompts for Claude Design (the artifact / design-canvas tool at claude.ai, NOT Claude Code) that are committed enough to skip its Q&A loop and start drawing on message one. Use when the user wants hi-fi mockups, an interactive prototype, multiple directions on a canvas, or a safe / bold / experimental comparison inside Claude. Trigger on: "build mockups in Claude Design", "design canvas prompt", "hi-fi design mockup", "interactive prototype in claude", "claude design prompt", "I want directions A/B/C in claude", "stop letting claude design ask me 15 questions", "claude design keeps asking", "compare safe / bold / experimental". Sibling skill `ui-brief` targets Claude Code production builds — this one targets the design canvas. Works for any product type.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep", "Bash"]
14: composes_with: ["ui-brief", "ui-ux-pro-max", "frontend-design:frontend-design", "superpowers:brainstorming"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Claude Design Brief
19: 
20: > **Naming exception:** This skill's name starts with `claude-` — a prefix Anthropic reserves for first-party skills. Kept deliberately: the skill targets **Claude Design** specifically, and a name that doesn't say "claude" would be less discoverable. Per `skills/meta/skill-writer/references/frontmatter-spec.md`, `skill-review` WARNs but does not FAIL on this pattern when the skill genuinely targets the corresponding Anthropic product.
21: 
22: Produce paste-ready prompts for **Claude Design** (the artifact/design-canvas tool at claude.ai) that are vivid and project-specific enough that Claude Design has nothing left to ask — it opens its canvas and starts drawing on the first message.
23: 
24: The failure mode the skill exists to prevent: a prompt so vague that Claude Design interrogates scope, tone, palette, typography, hero, device, and a dozen other decisions one at a time. The fix is not to answer those questions in a labeled numbered list — it's to write a design brief dense enough with committed, specific opinion that the questions never arise. How you structure that brief should fit the project, not a template.
25: 
26: **Announce at start:** "Using claude-design-brief to write a paste-ready Claude Design prompt for [project]."
27: 
28: ## Sibling vs Mode Selection
29: 
30: This skill is the sibling of `ui-brief` (which targets Claude Code / production builds, not the Claude Design canvas). It runs in either **with-brief mode** (a `UI-CHALLENGE.md` already exists — translate it) or **standalone mode** (research → Claude Design directly). See `references/usage-modes.md` for the full comparison table, hallmarks of each mode, what they share, and how the greenfield/rebuild axis layers on top.
31: 
32: ## Coverage Guide — What Needs To Be In The Brief
33: 
34: These are the decisions Claude Design is likely to lack if you don't address them. Cover each one in whatever form fits the project — as prose, per-direction blocks, embedded in the opening framing, or wherever it lands most naturally. They do not need to be labeled or in any fixed order. The test is not "did I address all 12 categories by name" — it is "does this brief leave Claude Design nothing to ask."
35: 
36: | Decision area | What to commit |
37: |---|---|
38: | **Scope** | Which pages, what's explicitly out of scope |
39: | **Variations** | How many directions, what each leans into — must be orthogonally distinct |
40: | **Tone** | Register, voice, emotional weight — named with reference apps, not adjectives |
41: | **Palette** | Hex values per direction |
42: | **Typography** | Display + body + mono per direction, with **Google Fonts fallbacks** for any licensed family |
43: | **Hero** | Image-led / type-led / split — different per direction is good |
