44: | **Live elements** | Explicit yes/no on tickers, counters, animated stats |
45: | **Photos** | Real, placeholder frames, or AI — commit and state the reason |
46: | **CTAs** | Donation / payment pattern, or explicit absence; newsletter pattern, or explicit absence |
47: | **Interactivity** | Nav scoping rule: Direction A routes only within Direction A's artboards |
48: | **Risks** | Decomposed by sub-risk (see `references/variation-and-risks.md`) — not a generic statement |
49: | **Device** | Primary viewport, secondary viewport policy |
50: 
51: Add anything project-specific that would otherwise become a question.
52: 
53: ## Canvas Constraints, Variation Rubric, Risk Decomposition
54: 
55: Three load-bearing reference files. Read them before writing the prompt:
56: 
57: - `references/canvas-constraints.md` — the 7 non-obvious canvas runtime limits (licensed fonts, image URLs, animation, backends, nav scoping, artboard math, chat-vs-canvas split). Encode all 7 as defaults in every prompt.
58: - `references/variation-and-risks.md` — orthogonal-axes rubric for keeping directions distinct, plus the sub-risk decomposition table for the risk section.
59: - `references/direction-examples/` — worked examples of safe (`safe.md`), bold (`bold.md`), and experimental (`experimental.md`) directions.
60: 
61: ## How To Run
62: 
63: ### Step 1 — Discovery (2–4 minutes)
64: 
65: - **Read the source material.** Research doc, brand brief, prior site URL, brainstorm transcript.
66: - **Check for `UI-CHALLENGE.md`.** If it exists, you are in with-brief mode. If not, standalone.
67: - **Identify the moat** in one sentence.
68: - **Confirm scope and variations.** The two questions you cannot guess. If the user has not specified "4 pages, 3 directions" (or whatever), ask once. Everything else has reasonable defaults.
69: - **Identify named patterns the user has called out** (e.g., "ACU sticky donate", "Kyiv Independent newsletter band"). Quote them verbatim.
70: - **Note explicit user rejections.** Capture in the negative-references section.
71: 
72: ### Step 2 — Decide The Defaults
73: 
74: When the user says "decide for me", **decide**. State the decision, explain the why in one clause. The canvas executes on commitments, not questions.
75: 
76: If you need help picking concrete palette/font values, **invoke `ui-ux-pro-max`** as a sub-step. Run before committing the per-direction palette and typography blocks.
77: 
78: ### Step 3 — Apply The Variation Rubric
79: 
80: Sketch the directions on the orthogonal-axes table in `references/variation-and-risks.md`. If two directions land in the same cell on the same axis, change one.
81: 
82: ### Step 4 — Write The Prompt
83: 
84: `references/prompt-template.md` shows one way to organize the sections — treat it as a reference for what sections tend to exist, not a form to fill in. The opening frame and canvas output spec have fixed load-bearing sentences (the "build immediately" directive and the artboard math) — everything in between should be shaped by what this project needs most.
85: 
86: **File location:** by default, write to `<repo-root>/CLAUDE-DESIGN-PROMPT.md`. If it already exists, name the next one `CLAUDE-DESIGN-PROMPT-v2.md`.
87: 
88: **Length:** 60–150 lines target. Hard ceiling 250. If past 200, you are probably duplicating `ui-brief` work — switch to with-brief mode and reference the brief.
89: 
90: ### Step 5 — Verify Before Returning
91: 
92: - [ ] All coverage-guide decision areas addressed somewhere — not necessarily labeled, but present and committed.
93: - [ ] Per-direction palette and typography specified with concrete hex values and named font families with Google Fonts fallbacks.
94: - [ ] Directions vary on at least three orthogonal axes (per `references/variation-and-risks.md`), not just color.
95: - [ ] All 7 canvas constraints encoded (per `references/canvas-constraints.md`).
96: - [ ] Named patterns the user called out appear verbatim.
97: - [ ] Explicit user rejections appear in the negative-reference section.
98: - [ ] Risk section decomposed into 2–4 sub-risks (per `references/variation-and-risks.md`).
99: - [ ] "What to ship" section enumerates the artboard math and the chat-vs-canvas summary instruction.
100: - [ ] Source Material section: with-brief mode quotes 5–8 rules from `UI-CHALLENGE.md`; standalone mode summarizes or omits.
101: - [ ] Prompt is under 250 lines (60–150 ideal).
102: 
103: Then summarize for the user in 3–5 sentences: which answers were committed, where the prompt lives, what was deferred.
104: 
105: ## Anti-Patterns and Composition
106: 
107: See `references/anti-patterns.md` for the 12 anti-patterns (decide-for-me echoed back, adjective-only tone, 13-slot form structure, three palettes of the same layout, missing font fallbacks, missing chat-vs-canvas instruction, cross-direction nav, generic risk framing, rebuilding `ui-brief` inline, omitting artboard math), how each is prevented, and how this skill composes with `ui-brief`, `brainstorming`, `ui-ux-pro-max`, and `frontend-design`. The output-file convention (self-contained, project-specific, decision-dense, paste-target friendly, canvas-aware) lives there too.
108: 
109: ## Reference Files
110: 
111: - `references/usage-modes.md` — `ui-brief` vs this skill comparison; with-brief vs standalone mode; greenfield/rebuild axis
112: - `references/canvas-constraints.md` — 7 non-obvious canvas runtime limits to encode as defaults
113: - `references/variation-and-risks.md` — orthogonal-axes rubric and risk sub-risk decomposition
114: - `references/direction-examples/safe.md` — credible-default direction with worked "Navy Field" sample
115: - `references/direction-examples/bold.md` — leaned-in direction with worked "Blackout Dossier" sample
116: - `references/direction-examples/experimental.md` — swing-for-fence direction with worked "Bone & Olive" sample
117: - `references/anti-patterns.md` — the 12 anti-patterns and prevention rules, composition notes, output-file convention
118: - `references/prompt-template.md` — sample prompt structure (reference, not a form)
119: 
120: Worked examples live in `references/direction-examples/` — read the safe / bold / experimental files for the variation rubric in action, the four-axis distinct-direction discipline, and the per-direction font-fallback pattern.

=== skills/workflows/context-manager/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: context-manager
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: |
5:   Manage context window usage, compaction strategy, and session handoffs for long-running multi-agent builds. Writes and validates structured handoff files so continuation agents can pick up cleanly. Trigger on: "compact this", "handoff this conversation", "we're running out of context", "save state", "transfer to a new session", "context is too full", "summarize this session for the next one", "continue in a fresh session".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: true
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: [".claude/handoffs/"]
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob"]
14: composes_with: ["orchestrator"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Context Manager
19: 
20: Manage context window usage, compaction strategy, and session handoffs for long-running builds.
21: 
22: This is a **user-invocable** helper. A user reaches for it directly when a conversation is running out of context and needs to be compacted or handed off to a fresh session. It is **not** spawned by the orchestrator during a build.
23: 
24: > **During an orchestrated build, handoff validation is governed by `skills/orchestrator/references/handoff-protocol.md`** (the protocol the orchestrator actually runs). This skill is for user-driven compaction/handoff outside that loop.
25: 
26: ## Role
27: 
28: You help a user manage their context window efficiently when a session is getting too full. When usage approaches limits (~80%), you help produce a structured handoff file so a fresh continuation session can pick up seamlessly. You also **validate handoff quality** — ensuring the handoff file you write contains actionable continuation context before the user transfers to a new session.
29: 
30: ## Your Ownership
31: 
32: - **You own (exclusive):** `.claude/handoffs/` directory
33: - **Shared read:** All project files (read-only)
34: - **Off-limits:** `src/`, implementation code
35: - **Resolved conflict (v1.1):** `.claude/handoffs/` was previously claimed by both orchestrator and context-manager. Context-manager is the definitive owner of user-driven handoffs — you write and validate them. (Build-loop handoffs during an orchestrated build are governed separately by `skills/orchestrator/references/handoff-protocol.md`.)
36: 
37: ## Inputs
38: 
39: - **Context signal** — the current session is approaching ~80% context usage
40: - **Handoff draft (optional)** — the user may provide a draft handoff file for you to validate and improve
41: - **Compaction request (optional)** — the user asks for help compacting the session before resorting to a full handoff
42: 
43: ## When to Act
44: 
45: - Session context usage approaches 80%
46: - A long-running task needs to continue across multiple sessions
47: - The user wants to transfer the conversation to a fresh session
48: 
49: ## Handoff Protocol
50: 
51: When the session needs to hand off, write a structured YAML file to `.claude/handoffs/`. See `references/compaction-guide.md` for the full specification.
52: 
53: ### Handoff File Structure
54: 
55: ```yaml
56: handoff_version: "1.0.0"
57: agent_role: [role name]
58: timestamp: [ISO 8601]
59: session_id: [string]
60: context_usage_pct: [number]
61: 
62: task_state:
63:   assigned_task: [what was assigned]
64:   completion_pct: [honest estimate]
65:   completed_subtasks: [list]
66:   remaining_subtasks: [list]
67:   blockers: [list]
68: 
69: decisions_made:
70:   - decision: [what was decided]
71:     rationale: [why]
72:     affects_files: [which files]
73: 
74: files_modified: [list of relative paths]
75: files_created: [list of relative paths]
76: contracts_consumed: [which contract files were read]
77: 
78: continuation_context: |
79:   [Free-text: what the continuation agent needs to know.
80:    Key variable names, error states, partial work, next action.
81:    ≤500 words.]
82: 
83: suggested_first_action: [exact next step]
84: ```
85: 
86: ### Continuation in a Fresh Session
87: 
88: After the handoff file is written, the user starts a fresh session and points it at the latest file in `.claude/handoffs/`. The continuation session reads `files_modified` and `files_created` to understand current state, then begins with `suggested_first_action`.
89: 
90: > **Inside an orchestrated build, the read/validate/spawn behavior on a handoff is governed by `skills/orchestrator/references/handoff-protocol.md` — not this skill.** This skill covers writing and quality-checking the user-driven handoff file; it does not duplicate the orchestrator's build-loop validation.
91: 
92: ## Context Efficiency Tips
93: 
94: For a session approaching its context limit:
95: 
96: - Avoid re-reading files already in context
97: - Summarize long outputs before storing in context
98: - Focus on the current task, not previously completed work
99: - Use `references/compaction-guide.md` for compaction strategies
100: 
101: ## Coordination
102: 
103: - Handoff files are append-only — never modify a previous handoff
104: - Each handoff gets a unique filename: `{role-or-session}-{timestamp}.yaml`
105: - **Quality gate (your job):** Before declaring a handoff ready, validate that `continuation_context` is specific and actionable (not vague), `suggested_first_action` is an exact next step, and `completion_pct` is an honest estimate. Reject and rewrite vague handoffs.

=== skills/workflows/dependency-coordinator/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: dependency-coordinator
3: version: 1.1.0
4: description: "Orchestrator-dispatched only. Authors the cross-package dependency manifest before parallel implementation agents are dispatched, preventing transitive version drift. Composed by orchestrator during multi-agent builds. Not user-invocable."
5: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob"]
6: disable-model-invocation: true
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: true
9: min_plan: starter
10: owns:
11:   directories: []
12:   patterns: []
13:   shared_read: ["contracts/", ".claude/profile.yaml"]
14: composes_with: ["contract-author", "infrastructure-agent", "contract-auditor", "orchestrator"]
15: spawned_by: ["orchestrator"]
16: ---
17: 
18: # Dependency Coordinator
19: 
20: > **Pipeline position.** Runs after `plan-builder`, before `orchestrator` dispatches parallel agents. Pins inter-agent deps so concurrent work doesn't collide.
21: 
22: Authors the cross-package dependency manifest in a monorepo so parallel implementation agents can fill in their own `package.json` (or equivalent) without producing version drift on shared transitive deps. **Run before any agent that will write a package manifest is dispatched.**
23: 
24: ## When this skill applies
25: 
26: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
27: 
28: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
29: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
30: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
31: 
32: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
33: 
34: ## Why this skill exists
35: 
36: Specification problems cause ~42% of multi-agent failures (per orchestrator skill). The `contract-author` skill prevents that for API/data/event boundaries — but does NOT cover the package manifest. Without a coordinated dep manifest, parallel agents pin conflicting transitive versions (most commonly: esbuild via vite + drizzle-kit + vitest), and `pnpm install` / `pip install` / `cargo build` fails partway through with a postinstall version mismatch.
37: 
38: This skill sits next to `contract-author` in the orchestrator's Phase 4 (contracts), authoring a different kind of contract: **the dependency contract**.
39: 
40: ## Role
41: 
42: You are the **dependency coordinator**. You read the project's tech-stack profile (from `.claude/profile.yaml` or the plan), enumerate every shared transitive dependency that's likely to drift, and emit:
43: 
44: 1. A root manifest with overrides/resolutions pinning shared transitives to one version
45: 2. Per-package manifest templates that consumer agents fill in (rather than authoring from scratch)
46: 3. A human-readable `DEPENDENCIES.md` documenting the version policy
47: 4. A short dispatch advisory the orchestrator pastes into each agent's prompt
48: 
49: ## Inputs
50: 
51: - **Tech stack profile** — from `.claude/profile.yaml` or the plan document. Specifically: language, runtime, package manager, test framework, build tools, ORM, web framework, UI framework.
52: - **Workspace shape** — list of `apps/*` and `packages/*` to be created (from the plan's component list).
53: - **Pinning policy (optional)** — strict (one version per dep) or flexible (caret ranges with overrides only on known-conflict deps). Default: flexible.
54: 
55: ## Process
56: 
57: The detailed recipes for each step live in `references/pinning-strategies.md` — read it before authoring. The flow is:
58: 
59: 1. **Enumerate shared transitives** — for the detected stack, list deps known to drift (see `references/known-conflict-deps.md`).
60: 2. **Pick a target version per transitive** — choose the version satisfying the most demanding consumer; record rationale.
61: 3. **Author the root manifest** — emit the overrides/resolutions block matching the workspace's package manager (pnpm/npm/yarn/poetry/uv/cargo). Stack-specific templates are in `references/pinning-strategies.md`.
62: 4. **Author per-package manifest templates** — one `package.json.template` (or equivalent) per workspace package, with a header comment listing inherited pins.
63: 5. **Author `DEPENDENCIES.md`** — version policy in human-readable form, including rationale, per-package boundaries, escalation procedure, and migration playbook. See `references/dependencies-md-template.md`.
64: 6. **Emit a dispatch advisory** — short string pasted verbatim into every implementation agent's prompt warning them not to modify root overrides.
65: 
66: After steps 1–6, run a dry install in the workspace root before dispatching agents (see `references/pinning-strategies.md` for the per-tool command). A failing dry install means the overrides block is wrong — fix it before dispatching.
67: 
68: ## Right-sizing
69: 
70: Match output complexity to the project:
71: 
72: - **Single-package project** — skip this skill entirely. No cross-agent drift surface exists.
73: - **2–3 package monorepo** — author overrides + DEPENDENCIES.md only; skip per-package templates.
74: - **4+ packages with parallel agent dispatch** — full output: root overrides + per-package templates + DEPENDENCIES.md + dispatch advisory.
75: - **Polyglot monorepo (e.g. TS + Python)** — emit one manifest per language (root `package.json` overrides + workspace-level `pyproject.toml` constraints).
76: 
77: ## Coordination
78: 
79: - **You vs. contract-author** — `contract-author` owns API/data/event boundaries. You own the dependency boundary. Both run in the orchestrator's Phase 4. Run `contract-author` first (it determines which packages exist), then you (you pin their deps).
80: - **You vs. infrastructure-agent** — `infrastructure-agent` owns Dockerfiles, CI, deploy configs. You own the package manifests they install from. Your output is upstream of theirs; they pick up pinned versions automatically.
81: - **Run order:** plan → contract-author → dependency-coordinator → implementation agents → contract-auditor (verifies no agent broke the pin policy).
82: 
83: ## Output
84: 
85: | File | Required when | Format |
86: |---|---|---|
87: | Root `package.json` (or `pyproject.toml`, `Cargo.toml`) with overrides block | Always | Language-native |
88: | `packages/<each>/package.json.template` | 4+ packages | Language-native |
89: | `DEPENDENCIES.md` at workspace root | Always | Markdown |
90: | Dispatch advisory string | Always | Plain text — pasted into agent prompts |
91: 
92: ## Quality checklist
93: 
94: - [ ] Every override has a rationale entry in DEPENDENCIES.md
95: - [ ] Per-package templates exist for every workspace package the orchestrator plans to spawn
96: - [ ] No template hardcodes a version that conflicts with a root override
97: - [ ] DEPENDENCIES.md lists the migration playbook for bumping a pin
98: - [ ] Dispatch advisory mentions: don't modify root, use templates, escalate on conflict
99: - [ ] Run `pnpm install --dry-run` (or equivalent) post-author — must succeed before agents are dispatched
100: 
101: ## Anti-patterns to avoid
102: 
103: - **Empty overrides block** — if you can't identify any shared transitive worth pinning, don't emit an empty block. The skill's value is the pin, not the ceremony.
104: - **Pinning everything strict** — overshoots the goal and creates maintenance burden. Pin only deps that have demonstrated drift in this stack.
105: - **Authoring per-package package.json directly** — that's the implementation agent's job. You author TEMPLATES; agents fill them in.
106: - **Skipping DEPENDENCIES.md** — undocumented overrides are landmines for future maintainers.
107: 
108: ## Reference files
109: 
110: - `references/pinning-strategies.md` — full recipes per stack: enumeration, version-pick logic, root manifest templates for pnpm/npm/yarn/Poetry/uv/Cargo, per-package template patterns, dry-install verification commands
111: - `references/known-conflict-deps.md` — table of frequently-drifting transitives per stack (TS/Python/Go/Rust)
112: - `references/dependencies-md-template.md` — DEPENDENCIES.md template
113: 
114: ## Composes with
115: 
116: - `contract-author` — runs immediately before this skill
117: - `infrastructure-agent` — consumes the pinned manifests
118: - `contract-auditor` — verifies post-hoc that no agent broke the pin policy
119: - `orchestrator` — runs this skill in Phase 4 (contracts)

=== skills/workflows/deployment-checklist/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: deployment-checklist
3: version: 1.2.0
4: description: |
5:   Run pre-deployment verification before pushing to staging or production — build, tests, env config, security, DB, infra, integration — and return a READY / NOT READY verdict. Trigger on: "pre-deploy check", "deployment checklist", "ready to ship", "is this ready for prod", "deploy readiness", "release checklist", "can we deploy", "verify the build", "pre-flight check".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: true
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:

12:   shared_read: ["*"]

17: 
18: # Deployment Checklist
19: 
20: Run pre-deployment verification before pushing to staging or production.
21: 
22: ## Role
23: 
24: You verify that a build is ready for deployment by running through a structured checklist. You check build artifacts, environment configs, security basics, and integration health.
25: 
26: ## Inputs
27: 
28: - **Target environment** — `staging` or `production` (determines which checks apply and which URLs to verify)
29: - **QA report** — the qe-agent's `qa-report.json` must show `gate_decision.proceed: true` before you run; if QA hasn't passed, block deployment
30: - **Infrastructure state** — the infrastructure-agent should have completed Docker builds, health check endpoints, and CI/CD config before this checklist runs
31: - **Project profile** — `CLAUDE.md` / `.claude/profile.yaml` for environment-specific commands and URLs
32: 
33: ## Coordination
34: 
35: - **qe-agent**: Deployment-checklist runs *after* the QE gate passes. If `qa-report.json` shows `gate_decision.proceed: false`, do not proceed — report the blocker to the orchestrator.
36: - **infrastructure-agent**: Infrastructure-agent owns Docker, CI/CD, and deployment configs. Deployment-checklist *validates* their output (Docker builds, health checks, resource limits) but does not modify infrastructure files. Route infrastructure failures back to the infrastructure-agent via the orchestrator.
37: - **Orchestrator**: Report the final `READY / NOT READY` verdict to the orchestrator. Include the structured report so it can be appended to the build record.
38: 
39: ## Process
40: 
41: Before running the checklist, apply the **assumption-audit** move (`references/assumption-audit.md`) to surface any unverified beliefs about the environment. Then run through `references/pre-deploy.md` in order. Each section must pass before moving to the next. Report results as a structured checklist.
42: 
43: ### Quick Reference
44: 
45: 1. **Build Verification** — does it compile/build cleanly?
46: 2. **Test Verification** — do all tests pass?
47: 3. **Environment Config** — are all env vars set?
48: 4. **Security Basics** — no secrets in code, no debug mode?
49: 5. **Database** — migrations applied, rollback tested?
50: 6. **Infrastructure** — Docker builds, health checks pass?
51: 7. **Integration** — services connect, CORS works?
52: 
53: ## Reference Documents
54: 
55: - **`references/pre-deploy.md`** — full ordered checklist (build, tests, env config, security, DB, infra, integration).
56: - **`references/assumption-audit.md`** — thinking move for surfacing unverified assumptions before sign-off.
57: 
58: ## Output
59: 
60: ```markdown
61: # Pre-Deployment Report
62: Environment: [staging | production]
63: Generated: [timestamp]
64: 
65: ## Results
66: | Check | Status | Notes |
67: |-------|--------|-------|
68: | Build | PASS/FAIL | |
69: | Tests | PASS/FAIL | X passed, Y failed |
70: | Env Config | PASS/FAIL | |
71: | Security | PASS/FAIL | |
72: | Database | PASS/FAIL | |
73: | Infrastructure | PASS/FAIL | |
74: | Integration | PASS/FAIL | |

81: ```

=== skills/workflows/design-token-guard/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: design-token-guard
3: version: 1.0.2
4: composes_with: ["orchestrator", "frontend-agent", "render-sanity", "ux-review", "code-review-agent", "sync-skills"]
5: description: >-
6:   Source-level gate that prevents inline styles and hardcoded CSS from
7:   bypassing a project's design-token system. Use whenever frontend work touches
8:   colors, styling, or themes — before committing or declaring a UI task done,
9:   when auditing for hardcoded hex/rgb/inline styles, when setting up enforcement
10:   so inline CSS can't slip in again, or as an orchestrator/agent wave-gate.
11:   Trigger on: "inline CSS", "inline styles", "hardcoded colors", "hardcoded
12:   hex", "style={{}}", "theme/token drift", "design tokens", "we keep shipping
13:   inline styles", "lint for tokens", "why did this get through review".
14:   Framework- and stack-agnostic (React/JSX, Vue, Svelte, Angular, Astro, HTML;
15:   CSS variables, SCSS/Less, JS theme objects, design-token JSON) —
16:   auto-discovers the project's tokens; NOT specific to any one repo or to
17:   Tailwind. Don't skip it because a render review passed: render gates can't see
18:   a hardcoded color — it renders identically to the token.
19: compatibility: Claude Code; requires Python 3.8+ (stdlib only) to run scripts/check_design_tokens.py; ESLint scaffolding step is optional
20: allowed-tools: ["Bash", "Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
21: ---
22: 
23: # design-token-guard
24: 
25: ## The problem this solves
26: 
27: A hardcoded color renders *identically* to the token it should have used.
28: `style={{ background: "#07090c" }}` and `style={{ background: "var(--tl-tooltip)" }}`
29: produce the same pixels. So every **render-level** gate — visual review, e2e,
30: screenshots, "console is clean" — passes a component that has silently bypassed
31: the design system. The violation only exists in **source**.
32: 
33: That is why inline styles and hardcoded CSS accumulate invisibly until someone
34: eyeballs the code weeks later and burns hours on a token refactor. The fix is a
35: **source-level gate**: a deterministic check that reads the diff for styling
36: that bypasses the token system, run *before* the code is declared done, and
37: wired into the repo so it can't regress.
38: 
39: This skill is that gate. It does two things:
40: 
41: 1. **Audit** — run the bundled checker (`scripts/check_design_tokens.py`) to find
42:    every inline style and hardcoded color, each mapped to the exact token to use.
43: 2. **Enforce** — scaffold the check into the repo (config + ESLint rule +
44:    pre-commit hook + `lint:tokens` script) so the gate runs on every commit/CI,
45:    not just when an agent remembers to look.
46: 
47: It is **dynamic**: it auto-discovers whatever token system the project already
48: uses and derives the rules from it. TruthLens and a vanilla Vue app are two
49: *configs* of one skill, not two skills.
50: 
51: ## Step 1 — Audit
52: 
53: Run the checker from the project root. It needs no flags for a first pass:
54: 
55: ```bash
56: python3 <skill-dir>/scripts/check_design_tokens.py --root .
57: ```
58: 
59: What it does automatically:
60: - **Discovers the token source** — scans for CSS custom properties
61:   (`--name: …`), SCSS/Less vars (`$name`, `@name`), JS/TS theme objects, or
62:   design-token JSON. From those it builds a `color-value → token` map.
63: - **Scans the source tree** (`.tsx/.ts/.jsx/.js/.vue/.svelte/.astro/.html`),
64:   skipping `node_modules`, build dirs, config files, and the token files
65:   themselves.
66: - **Reports** each finding as `file:line:col`, the offending snippet, and — when
67:   the literal matches a declared token — the exact fix:
68: 
69:   ```
70:   no-hardcoded-color (error) — 2
71:     src/components/schedule/EventBar.tsx:69:17
72:       fill="#0E1116"
73:       → hardcoded color #0E1116 is var(--tl-bg-0)  ·  use var(--tl-bg-0)
74:   ```
75: 
76: Useful flags:
77: - `--staged` — only git-staged files (this is what the pre-commit hook uses).
78: - `--json` — machine-readable output for a gate/CI to parse (`{ ok, summary, findings }`).
79: - `PATHS…` — limit to specific files/dirs (e.g. just the component you changed).
80: - `--config <path>` — explicit config location.
81: 
82: Exit code is `1` when there are **error**-severity findings, `0` when clean — so
83: it drops straight into a gate or CI step.
84: 
85: ## Step 2 — Interpret and fix
86: 
87: Two outcomes per color finding, and they need different fixes — don't blur them:
88: 
89: - **"… is `var(--token)`"** — the literal duplicates an existing token. Replace
90:   it with the token reference. Mechanical and safe.
91: - **"… is not a declared token"** — the color was never tokenized. This is the
92:   more important signal: either it's a genuinely new design value (add it to the
93:   token source with a real name, then reference it) or it's an off-palette
94:   mistake (use the nearest existing token). Don't paper over it by leaving the
95:   literal — that's how palettes rot.
96: 
97: For `no-inline-style` findings, move the value into a token or a utility class.
98: Inline styles whose values are all dynamic (`var(--…)`, JS expressions) are
99: **allowed** by default — the rule fires on hardcoded literals, not on the
100: mechanism. Use `"inlineStyleMode": "strict"` only if the project bans inline
101: style attributes outright.
102: 
103: ## Step 3 — Enforce (scaffold into the repo)
104: 
105: A one-time audit doesn't stop regression. Wire the gate into the repo so it runs
106: without anyone remembering. Read `references/scaffolding.md` for the full
107: procedure; the short version:
108: 
109: 1. **Write `.design-guard.json`** at the repo root (template:
110:    `assets/design-guard.config.json`). Set `tokenSources` explicitly so
111:    discovery is deterministic in CI, and turn on any project-specific rules.
112: 2. **Add the checker to the repo** — copy `scripts/check_design_tokens.py` into
113:    the project's `scripts/`, or reference the skill path. Add a script:
114:    `"lint:tokens": "python3 scripts/check_design_tokens.py --root ."`.
115: 3. **Pre-commit hook** — install `assets/pre-commit` (runs `--staged`, blocks the
116:    commit on error-severity findings). Wire via Husky/lefthook if present, else a
117:    plain `.git/hooks/pre-commit`.
118: 4. **ESLint editor-time subset** — merge `assets/eslint-tokens.snippet.mjs` into
119:    the repo's flat config (create `eslint.config.mjs` if absent). ESLint gives
120:    in-editor squiggles for the patterns it expresses well (inline-style literals,
121:    forbidden radius/partisan-color classes). The Python checker stays the
122:    authority — it's the only layer that can do the `value → token` mapping, so
123:    pre-commit/CI run *that*, and ESLint is fast feedback, not the source of truth.
124: 5. **CI** — add a `lint:tokens` step to the pipeline (`assets/ci-step.yml` shows a
125:    GitHub Actions example).
126: 
127: Tell the user which layers you installed and which you skipped (e.g. "no Husky
128: here, used a raw git hook").
129: 
130: ## The rule set
131: 
132: Two **universal** rules, on by default (these are the "no inline CSS" core):
133: 
134: | rule | catches | default |
135: |---|---|---|
136: | `no-hardcoded-color` | `#hex`, `rgb()/rgba()`, `hsl()/hsla()` literals anywhere a token belongs — inline styles, SVG `fill`/`stroke`, JS color strings, Tailwind arbitrary `[#…]` | error |
137: | `no-inline-style` | `style={{…}}` / `style="…"` / `:style` / `[style]` carrying hardcoded literals (React/Vue/Svelte/Angular/HTML) | warn |
138: 
139: A **project-specific library**, off by default — opt in via config when a repo
140: has these conventions:
141: 
142: | rule | catches |
143: |---|---|
144: | `no-class-in-svg` | utility/Tailwind classes on `<svg>` primitives (`rect`, `path`, `g`, …) where styling should be token attributes |
145: | `restricted-radius` | border-radius above `maxRadiusPx`, or `rounded-{md,lg,xl,…}` classes |
146: | `forbidden-colors` | configured regexes — banned second accent, partisan red/blue, etc. |
147: | `no-arbitrary-tailwind` | Tailwind arbitrary *values* — `text-[10px]`, `max-w-[1100px]`, `leading-[1.75]` — that bypass the spacing/type scale. Arbitrary *colors* (`border-[rgba(…)]`) are already caught by `no-hardcoded-color`, so this rule skips them. `*-[var(--token)]` is allowed. |
148: 
149: Each rule's severity is `"error" | "warn" | "off"`. Errors fail the gate
150: (exit 1); warnings report but pass. Full config reference and per-rule notes:
151: `references/config.md`.
152: 
153: ## Using it as an agent / orchestrator gate
154: 
155: When a frontend build runs under an orchestrator or agent team, this is a **hard
156: source-level wave-gate**, complementary to the render-level gates (render-sanity,
157: ux-review) — those check pixels, this checks source, and a build needs both:
158: 
159: - A **frontend-agent** runs `--json` against the files it changed *before*
160:   reporting done; error-severity findings mean the task isn't done.
161: - The **orchestrator** runs it at the wave gate alongside typecheck/test. Parse
162:   `summary.errors`; non-zero blocks the wave and routes back to the owning agent.
163: 
164: See `references/wiring-into-orchestrator.md` for the exact gate snippet and how
165: this plugs into the orchestrator's Definition of Done.
166: 
167: ## Reference files
168: 
169: - `references/config.md` — full `.design-guard.json` schema, every field, per-rule notes, and adapting to non-CSS-variable token systems (SCSS, JS theme, Style Dictionary).
170: - `references/scaffolding.md` — step-by-step repo enforcement: config, hook, ESLint, CI, with the Husky/lefthook/raw-hook decision.
171: - `references/wiring-into-orchestrator.md` — the agent self-check + orchestrator wave-gate snippets, and the Definition-of-Done line this closes.

=== skills/workflows/diagnose-loop/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: diagnose-loop
3: version: 1.2.0
4: description: |
5:   Disciplined bug-diagnosis loop where Phase 1 — building a fast, deterministic, binary-signal feedback loop — IS the skill; the rest of the phases mechanically consume that signal. Use whenever the user reports a hard bug, a flaky test, a performance regression, or says they've been "staring at this for an hour." Trigger on: "diagnose this", "debug this", "why is this broken", "it sometimes fails", "performance regression", "I can't reproduce it", "what's wrong with this", "this test is flaky", "intermittent failure".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: []
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
14: composes_with: ["playwright", "qe-agent", "architecture-rescue"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Diagnose Loop
19: 
20: > **Tradeoff caveat.** This skill biases toward thoroughness over speed. For trivial bugs with obvious causes (typos, off-by-one, a missing import the stack trace already points at), skip phases and just fix it. For everything else — flaky tests, performance regressions, "it works on my machine," intermittent failures, anything you've stared at for more than 15 minutes — the discipline is the point. Adapted from mattpocock's `diagnose` pattern.
21: 
22: > **diagnose-loop vs systematic-debugging.** The plugin skill `superpowers:systematic-debugging` is the general "form a hypothesis before you touch a fix" discipline for any bug or unexpected behavior. Reach for `diagnose-loop` (this skill) specifically when the payoff is in *building a fast, deterministic, binary feedback loop first* — flaky tests, performance regressions, hard-to-reproduce or intermittent failures. For a straightforward bug where you just need disciplined hypothesizing, `superpowers:systematic-debugging` is the lighter fit.
23: 
24: ## Phase 1 — Build a feedback loop (THE SKILL)
25: 
26: **Spend disproportionate effort here. Be aggressive. Be creative. Refuse to give up.**
27: 
28: Every later phase mechanically consumes Phase 1's output. A fast deterministic loop turns hypothesizing into a binary search; a slow or noisy loop turns it into guessing. Building the loop is the work — the rest is bookkeeping.
29: 
30: A good loop has three properties: it runs in seconds, it returns a binary pass/fail signal, and it fails for the same reason every time. Optimize for those before doing anything else.
31: 
32: ### Ten ranked ways to construct a loop
33: 
34: In order of cost — pick the cheapest one that actually reproduces the bug.
35: 
36: 1. **Existing failing test** — cheapest possible signal; the harness already exists.
37: 2. **New failing test that captures the bug** — write the assertion, watch it fail, then start.
38: 3. **`curl` / HTTP request that reproduces** — for API and webhook bugs; capture as a shell script so it replays in one line.
39: 4. **CLI command + `diff` against known-good output** — golden-file testing for any tool that emits text.
40: 5. **Visible browser script** (Playwright, non-headless) for UI bugs — compose with the `playwright` skill. Never diagnose UI behavior from a headless run or from reading the component source: rendering and interaction bugs only show up in a real browser. Run Playwright with `headless: false` and keep the visible window part of the loop.
41: 6. **Trace/log replay** — when the bug only happens in production, dump the request and replay it locally.
42: 7. **Throwaway harness script** in the repo's language — a 20-line file that imports the buggy module and exercises it directly.
43: 8. **Property-based / fuzz test** — when the bug is data-dependent and you don't yet know which input triggers it.
44: 9. **`git bisect`** — when the bug is recent and a known-good commit exists; the loop *is* the bisect script.
45: 10. **Differential testing** against a reference implementation — for parser, compiler, or protocol bugs where "correct" is defined elsewhere.
46: 
47: **Last resort:** Human-in-the-loop bash harness — the user runs a step, the agent reads stdout. See `scripts/hitl-loop.template.sh`. Use this only when none of the above are reachable (e.g., bug needs physical hardware, a paid third-party service, or a manual UI step). Concrete snippets for all ten methods live in `references/feedback-loop-recipes.md`.
48: 
49: ## Iterate on the loop itself
50: 
51: Make the loop faster, sharper, and more deterministic *before* using it to investigate. A 30-second loop that fires automatically beats a 5-second loop you have to remember to run. Sharpen by narrowing the test surface, stripping irrelevant setup, and removing log noise that hides the signal.
52: 
53: ## Non-deterministic bugs
54: 
55: If the bug reproduces less than half the time, you don't have a loop yet — you have a coin flip. Raise the reproduction rate from ~1% to ~50% before doing anything else. Methods:
56: 
57: - Tighten the loop (smaller test surface, fewer dependencies)
58: - Run it in parallel (`for i in {1..20}; do ...; done &`) to surface races
59: - Deliberately stress the system: throttled CPU, slow network, constrained memory, deliberate clock skew
60: 
61: ## Phase 2 — Reproduce
62: 
63: Run the loop and confirm it shows *the user's* failure, not a nearby one. Read the failing assertion or output character-by-character against the reported symptom. Two bugs that look alike are still two bugs.
64: 
65: ## Phase 3 — Hypothesize
66: 
67: Generate three to five ranked falsifiable hypotheses. Each one uses the format in `references/hypothesis-format.md`: "If `<X>` is the cause, then `<changing Y>` will make the bug disappear." Show the list to the user before testing any of them — the user often knows which one is most likely from context the agent doesn't have.
68: 
69: ## Phase 4 — Instrument
70: 
71: Test one hypothesis at a time. Change one variable per loop iteration. Tag every debug log, print, or breakpoint with a unique token like `[DEBUG-7f3a]` (any short random string works) so Phase 6 cleanup is a single `grep -r '\[DEBUG-7f3a\]'`. Untagged debug output is how `console.log("here")` ships to production.
72: 
73: ## Phase 5 — Fix and regression test
74: 
75: Write the regression test at the *correct seam* — the smallest scope where the bug is observable. If the natural seam doesn't exist (the test would have to reach into private implementation details to observe the failure), note this in the PR. A missing seam is a signal that the module has an architectural problem; recommend a follow-up invocation of `architecture-rescue` rather than papering over it with a brittle test.
76: 
77: ## Phase 6 — Cleanup and post-mortem
78: 
79: 1. `grep -r '\[DEBUG-xxxx\]' .` — every tagged line comes out.
80: 2. Run the original Phase 1 loop one more time. It must still fail on the pre-fix commit and pass on the post-fix commit.
81: 3. Commit the *hypothesis* — the real root cause — in the PR message, not just "fixed bug." Future-you debugging a regression will thank present-you.
82: 4. If a missing seam blocked Phase 5, recommend `architecture-rescue` as a follow-up issue.
83: 
84: ## Anti-pattern
85: 
86: > **Forbidden:** Skipping Phase 1. If you don't have a fast deterministic loop, no amount of staring at code will find the bug. Reading code without a loop is guessing with extra steps.

=== skills/workflows/find-unknowns/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: find-unknowns
3: version: 1.0.1
4: description: >-
5:   Convert your unknown-unknowns to known — cheaply, before they get expensive to
6:   fix. Owns two moves: a blindspot pass (research an unfamiliar domain and learn
7:   the questions you don't yet know to ask) and a comprehension quiz (after a big
8:   change, explain the diff and verify you understand it before merging). Routes
9:   the rest of the unknowns lifecycle to the owning skills. Use when you're in
10:   unfamiliar territory, can't articulate what "good" looks like, feel like you're
11:   missing something, or want to be sure you understand what got built. Trigger on
12:   "find my unknown unknowns", "blindspot pass", "what am I missing", "I don't know
13:   this domain/codebase", "teach me my blind spots", "map my unknowns", "quiz me on
14:   this change", "do I actually understand this diff", "only merge if I understand
15:   it", "comprehension check". Sibling to grill-me — that interviews you on
16:   decisions you already hold opinions about; this surfaces the ones you don't.
17: requires_agent_teams: false
18: requires_claude_code: false
19: min_plan: starter
20: owns:
21:   directories: []
22:   patterns: []
23:   shared_read: ["*"]
24: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Glob", "Bash", "WebSearch", "WebFetch"]
25: composes_with: ["grill-me", "plan-builder", "maintain-context", "repo-deep-dive", "interactive-doc", "artifact-publish", "superpowers:brainstorming"]
26: spawned_by: []
27: ---
28: 
29: # find-unknowns
30: 
31: The map is not the territory. Your prompt, your plan, your mental model — that's
32: the map. The codebase, the domain, the real constraints — that's the territory.
33: The gap between them is your **unknowns**, and on a capable model the work is
34: bottlenecked less by the model's ability and more by *your* ability to surface
35: those unknowns before they get expensive. Every explainer, brainstorm, interview,
36: and quiz is a cheap way to find out what you didn't know — before it costs you a
37: bad implementation to find out the hard way.
38: 
39: ## Name the four unknowns
40: 
41: When you bring a problem, it breaks down four ways. Naming which kind you're
42: short on tells you which move to reach for:
43: 
44: | Quadrant | What it is | Cheapest way to surface it |
45: |---|---|---|
46: | **Known knowns** | What you put in the prompt | Already written down |
47: | **Known unknowns** | What you know you haven't figured out | An **interview** → `grill-me` |
48: | **Unknown knowns** | So obvious you'd never write it, but you'd know it on sight | A **brainstorm / prototype** to react to |
49: | **Unknown unknowns** | What you haven't considered at all — you don't even know the question | A **blindspot pass** (this skill) |
50: 
51: The dangerous quadrant is the last one: you can't ask about what you don't know
52: exists, and it's the one that surfaces late, deep in implementation, when
53: reverting is expensive. That's the gap this skill is built to close.
54: 
55: ## What this skill owns vs. routes
56: 
57: This skill owns the two ends of the "do I actually understand this?" question —
58: the **before** (blindspot pass) and the **after** (comprehension quiz). The
59: middle of the lifecycle is already well-covered; route to the owner rather than
60: re-doing it here:
61: 
62: | You want to… | Go to |
63: |---|---|
64: | Be interviewed on decisions you already have opinions about | `grill-me` |
65: | Generate directions / a throwaway prototype to react to | `superpowers:brainstorming`, `ui-brief`, `claude-design-brief` |
66: | Turn resolved decisions into a plan (leading with what's most likely to change) | `plan-builder` |
67: | Log deviations you hit mid-build so the next attempt learns | `maintain-context` |
68: | Deeply map an unfamiliar codebase | `repo-deep-dive`, `zoom-out` |
69: | Package the result into a pitch/explainer for buy-in | `interactive-doc`, `artifact-publish` |
70: 
71: Reach for the owner, hand back here for the two moves below.
72: 
73: ## Move 1 — Blindspot pass (before you build)
74: 
75: Use when you're in territory you don't know well: a new module, an unfamiliar
76: domain, a design you can't yet articulate. The problem isn't that you have the
77: wrong answers — it's that you don't know the *questions*. You can't see what good
78: looks like, what's already been tried, or which potholes are waiting.
79: 
80: The move is the inverse of an interview. Instead of asking you what you think,
81: Claude researches the terrain and teaches you your unknown unknowns, then hands
82: you back the questions you're now equipped to answer (or to hand off as
83: requirements).
84: 
85: 1. **Get the starting point — context is load-bearing.** Tell it who you are,
86:    what you already know, and what you're trying to do. "I know nothing about the
87:    auth modules here" produces a very different pass than "I've written auth
88:    before but not in this stack." Say so.
89: 2. **Research the terrain.** Grep/read the relevant code paths; `WebSearch` /
90:    `WebFetch` the domain when the unknowns are conceptual rather than local. The
91:    best reference of all is *source code* — if a library or a sibling module
92:    already does the thing well, point at it and have Claude read how it's built,
93:    not just what it looks like.
94: 3. **Return a map, not a verdict.** Structure the output as: the terrain (the
95:    pieces and how they connect), your grouped unknown unknowns, what "good" looks
96:    like here, the historical context / prior attempts, and the potholes to avoid —
97:    ending with the handful of questions you should now be able to answer or route
98:    to `grill-me`. An HTML artifact is often the clearest form (→ `interactive-doc`
99:    / `artifact-publish`).
100: 
101: This is *not* `grill-me`: grill-me interrogates opinions you already hold and
102: resolves your **known** unknowns; the blindspot pass surfaces the **unknown**
103: ones you didn't know to have opinions about. Run this first, then grill-me.
104: 
105: **Example prompts**
106: 
107: - "I'm adding a new auth provider but I know nothing about the auth modules in
108:   this codebase. Do a blindspot pass — find my unknown unknowns and teach me
109:   enough to prompt you well."
110: - "I need to color-grade this video and don't know what color grading even is.
111:   Teach me my unknown unknowns about it so I can react."
112: - "Before I scope this, search the codebase and tell me what I'm not seeing about
113:   how sessions are currently persisted."
114: 
115: ## Move 2 — Comprehension quiz (after it's built)
116: 
117: Use after a large change, especially an agent-driven one. Reading the diff gives
118: you only a light understanding, because most of the behavior lives in how the new
119: code interacts with existing paths — which the diff doesn't show. Merge on that
120: light understanding repeatedly and you accrue **comprehension debt**: the code
121: outruns your mental model, the system works but nobody knows *why* it's shaped
122: the way it is, and the bill comes due at the next bug. (`loop-controller` names
123: this as the human-side failure mode of autonomous loops; this is its antidote.)
124: 
125: The move: before you merge, have Claude bring you up to the level of the change,
126: then test that it stuck.
127: 
128: 1. **Read the change and what it touches.** The diff *plus* the existing code
129:    paths it now runs through — that interaction is where understanding actually
130:    lives. In Claude Code, `git diff` / `git log`; elsewhere, paste the diff.
131: 2. **Explain it back.** A short report: what changed, why, the intuition behind
132:    the approach, and how it interacts with what was already there.
133: 3. **Quiz on the non-obvious.** Questions aimed at the behavior a reader would
134:    *miss* — edge cases, interactions, the "why this and not the obvious
135:    alternative." Not trivia the diff makes obvious.
136: 4. **Grade honestly and close the gaps.** You merge only when you can pass it. A
137:    miss isn't a failure — it's the exact spot your understanding was thin, so fill
138:    it and re-check.
139: 
140: **Example prompts**
141: 
142: - "Give me an HTML report on this change — what was done, the intuition, how it
143:   hits existing code paths — with a quiz at the bottom I have to pass before I
144:   merge."
145: - "I let an agent refactor the payment flow and the tests are green. Quiz me on
146:   the diff — I only want to merge if I actually understand what it did."
147: 
148: ## Why this is worth the minutes
149: 
150: None of this produces shippable output — it produces *understanding*, and that's
151: the point. A blindspot pass costs a few minutes; discovering the same unknown
152: three days into an implementation costs the implementation. A comprehension quiz
153: costs one round of Q&A; comprehension debt costs a future debugging session on
154: code no one understands. Spend the cheap discovery now so you don't pay the
155: expensive kind later.

=== skills/workflows/grill-me/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: grill-me
3: version: 1.1.2
4: description: |
5:   Get relentlessly interviewed about a plan, design, or change until every branch of the decision tree is resolved — depth-first, one question at a time, recommendation-attached, code-checked before user-asked. Use before any non-trivial change, when scope feels fuzzy, or to stress-test a plan before committing. Trigger on: 'grill me', 'interview me', 'challenge my plan', 'ask me questions', "I'm not sure what I want", 'help me think this through', 'is this the right approach', 'walk me through it', 'stress test this plan'.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: []
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Glob"]
14: composes_with: ["architecture-rescue", "maintain-context", "plan-builder", "find-unknowns"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # grill-me
19: 
20: Walk the design tree depth-first. Resolve the current branch before backing out. Three rules:
21: 
22: 1. **One question at a time.** Wait for the user's response before asking the next. Don't batch.
23: 2. **Recommend, then ask.** Every question takes the form: "I think *<X>* because *<reason>*. Do you agree, or do you want *<alternative>*?" The recommendation is the default — the user accepts with "yes" or steers with an alternative.
24: 3. **Ask code, not user, when possible.** Before asking, check: can `Read`, `Grep`, or `Glob` answer this? If yes, do that and proceed. Only ask the user when the answer is genuinely in their head.
25: 
26: **Exit condition:** every decision in the tree has a chosen answer plus your analysis attached. Output a final `Decisions` section the user can paste into a plan or brief — one bullet per decision, with the chosen answer and a one-line rationale.
27: 
28: **Compose with:** `architecture-rescue` when grilling about an unfamiliar codebase; `maintain-context` to capture decisions as ADRs or glossary entries; `plan-builder` to turn the `Decisions` output into an executable plan. `find-unknowns` is the sibling for the other direction — this skill interrogates the *known* unknowns you already have opinions on; run its blindspot pass first when you don't yet know what to have opinions about.

=== skills/workflows/interactive-doc/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: interactive-doc
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: |
5:   Produce rich documentation as a paired Obsidian-friendly markdown source plus a self-contained HTML companion built from it — the .md is canonical, the HTML is the experience. Two workflows: (A) render an existing research markdown file into an HTML companion (the .md stays read-only by default), or (B) write both for a new doc, .md first as the substantive source and the HTML derived from it. Trigger for architecture deep-dives, module maps, concept explainers with interactive demos, side-by-side repo/approach comparisons, feature walkthroughs, or whenever the user says "interactive doc", "wiki page", "Obsidian doc", "explainer", "render this research", "architecture diagram", or "compare these repos". Output is always a pair: an Obsidian-native `.md` plus a self-contained `.html`.
6: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep", "Bash"]
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: false
9: min_plan: starter
10: composes_with: ["llm-wiki", "wiki-research", "docs-agent", "artifact-publish"]
11: spawned_by: []
12: ---
13: 
14: # Interactive Doc
15: 
16: Pair an Obsidian-friendly markdown source with a self-contained HTML companion. The .md is the canonical source of truth — token-efficient, wiki-linkable, what other agents and tools consume. The HTML is the experience for humans who want to *read* it instead of *grep* it. Same content, two surfaces.
17: 
18: > **Design tokens do not apply here.** These docs are standalone, handmade artifacts that live in a repo or vault, not product UI governed by a project's design-token system — so `design-token-guard` / `class-extraction-guard` are out of scope. The HTML's own theming uses CSS variables in `:root` (see House style below); that local convention is the only "token" layer, and you should *not* try to wire these docs into an application's token pipeline or framework CSS.
19: 
20: ## The two workflows
21: 
22: This skill handles two distinct jobs. Identify which one applies before doing anything else.
23: 
24: ### Workflow A — Render existing research
25: 
26: The user has an existing markdown file (often deep-research notes, an Obsidian vault page, or a long analysis) and wants an HTML companion. The .md is **canonical and read-only by default** — your job is to read it carefully and produce HTML that respects what's there. You may, however, **offer** to suggest edits to the .md if rendering reveals genuine gaps or contradictions; only make those edits if the user says yes.
27: 
28: Steps:
29: 1. Read the source .md in full — frontmatter, body, links, embedded diagrams, the lot. Don't skim.
30: 2. Identify the doc type (architecture map, concept explainer, comparison, or other) from the content. If unclear, ask.
31: 3. Read `references/house-style.md` and the relevant doc-type reference.
32: 4. Build the HTML as a faithful rendering: every section, callout, file ref, and link in the .md has a place in the HTML. Don't invent content the .md doesn't support.
33: 5. If you noticed gaps while rendering (a diagram referenced but not shown, a section that ends mid-thought, a wiki-link to a note that doesn't exist), surface those at the end as "things I noticed" — don't silently fix them.
34: 6. Save the HTML next to the source .md with the same slug.
35: 
36: ### Workflow B — Create both, greenfield
37: 
38: The user wants a new doc. No source exists yet. Write the **.md first** as the substantive document, then derive the HTML from it.
39: 
40: Steps:
41: 1. Establish what's being documented — concrete component names, file paths, real data. Generic examples produce generic docs; ask for specifics if they're missing.
42: 2. Pick the doc type and read the relevant references plus `references/house-style.md` and `references/markdown-document.md`.
43: 3. Write the .md as a real Obsidian-native document — YAML frontmatter, callout blocks, wiki links, Mermaid diagrams, file-referenced code blocks. Full prose. The .md should stand on its own.
44: 4. Write the HTML from the .md. Every section in the HTML traces to a section in the .md. The HTML adds *experience* (interactivity, hand-SVG diagrams, color, layout) but never *content*.
45: 5. Save both files with the same slug.
46: 
47: ## The cardinal rule
48: 
49: > The .md is the source of truth. The HTML never adds content the .md doesn't have.
50: 
51: This isn't aesthetic — it's load-bearing. If the HTML ever drifts ahead of the .md, the .md stops being canonical and the system collapses into "two files that both half-document the same thing." When you find yourself wanting to add something to the HTML that isn't in the .md, the answer is to add it to the .md first and then render it.
52: 
53: The reverse asymmetry is allowed and expected: the .md can have content that the HTML *renders differently* (e.g. a Mermaid block becomes a hand-SVG; a list of `[[wiki-links]]` becomes a sidebar of cards; a fenced code block with a file path becomes a collapsible with a file:line badge). Same content, different rendering.
54: 
55: ## Doc types supported
56: 
57: Three are first-class in v1. A fourth is supported but uses an existing pattern.
58: 
59: - **Architecture / module map** — boxes-and-arrows of how components talk, with hot paths highlighted and entry points listed. Reference: `references/architecture-map.md`.
60: - **Concept explainer** — a thing taught with a live interactive piece (a ring you can add nodes to, a state machine you can step through), plus comparison tables and a glossary. Reference: `references/concept-explainer.md`.
61: - **Side-by-side comparison** — two or three approaches/repos rendered next to each other, with a "what we borrowed from each" synthesis section. Reference: `references/comparison.md`.
62: - **Feature explainer** (TL;DR + collapsible step-through + tabbed code + FAQ) — supported but not first-class. Use the concept-explainer reference and drop the live demo.
63: 
64: If the user's request doesn't fit any of these, you can adapt the house style to other formats (a slide deck, a status report, a post-mortem) but flag the mismatch and confirm.
65: 
66: ## File naming and location
67: 
68: Always paired, same slug:
69: 
70: ```
71: docs/<topic-slug>.md
72: docs/<topic-slug>.html
73: ```
74: 
75: In an Obsidian vault, the .md goes in the appropriate vault folder; the .html goes alongside it (Obsidian ignores it, vault tools tolerate it). For a project repo without a vault, default to a `docs/` folder.
76: 
77: ## The Obsidian-native .md format
78: 
79: Detailed in `references/markdown-document.md`. Quick summary:
80: 
81: **YAML frontmatter** — at minimum `title`, `tags`, `type`, and `html` (path to companion):
82: 
83: ```yaml
84: ---
85: title: How the Hive orchestrator dispatches to agents
86: tags: [hive, architecture, orchestrator]
87: type: architecture-map
88: html: ./orchestrator-dispatch.html
89: date: 2026-05-09
90: ---
91: ```
92: 
93: **Wiki links** — `[[double-bracket]]` form throughout the .md. Obsidian resolves them; at HTML render time, you translate to plain `<a href>` anchors. A link `[[skill-md-explainer]]` becomes `<a href="./skill-md-explainer.html">SKILL.md explainer</a>` in the HTML.
94: 
95: **Obsidian callouts** — for TL;DRs, gotchas, asides:
96: 
97: ```markdown
98: > [!tldr]
99: > The orchestrator builds a task envelope, picks an agent based on
100: > capability tags, and hands off via a SKILL.md contract.
101: 
102: > [!warning] Gotcha
103: > `burst` is bucket capacity, not rate.
104: ```
105: 
106: These render natively in Obsidian and translate to the HTML's `.tldr` / `.callout` components.
107: 
108: **File-referenced code blocks** — fence with both language and path:
109: 
110: ````markdown
111: ```ts orchestrator/dispatch.ts:21
112: function dispatch(envelope: Envelope) { ... }
113: ```
114: ````
115: 
116: In the HTML, the path becomes a small badge under the code block.
117: 
118: **Mermaid for diagrams** — `mermaid` fenced blocks for diagrams in the .md. These render in Obsidian. The HTML uses **independent hand-SVG** drawn from the same concept — *not* auto-converted from the Mermaid, since the two serve different needs (Mermaid is for grep and quick render; hand-SVG is for the polished read).
119: 
120: ## Diagram parity
121: 
122: This is the one place where "single source of truth" gets a careful exception. The .md has Mermaid; the HTML has hand-SVG. They depict the *same conceptual diagram* but are written independently.
123: 
124: Why both:
125: - Mermaid in the .md is essential for Obsidian rendering, grep-ability, and tools that ingest your vault.
126: - Hand-SVG in the HTML is what makes the rendered version feel handmade and not Mermaid-stamped.
127: 
128: The rule that keeps them honest: **same boxes, same connections, same labels, same colors**. If you change one, change the other in the same edit. The Mermaid is the contract; the hand-SVG is the artwork. They must agree on what they depict.
129: 
130: For Workflow A specifically: if the source .md has Mermaid diagrams, draw the hand-SVG to match exactly — same nodes, same edges. If the source .md has no diagrams but the architecture clearly needs one, surface that as a "noticed" gap and *ask* before adding. (The .md is read-only by default.)
131: 
132: ## Workflow choreography in practice
133: 
134: ### Workflow A walk-through
135: 
136: User: "Render this research doc as an interactive HTML." [attaches `hive-orchestrator-research.md`]
137: 
138: You:
139: 1. Read the .md in full.
140: 2. Note: it's an architecture deep-dive on the Hive orchestrator. Type → architecture-map.
141: 3. Read `references/architecture-map.md` and `references/house-style.md`.
142: 4. Build `hive-orchestrator-research.html` — every section in the .md becomes a section in the HTML, the Mermaid diagram becomes a hand-SVG of the same shape, file:line refs become badges, callouts become styled boxes, wiki links become anchors to sibling HTML files.
143: 5. Surface any gaps: "I noticed the .md references a 'capability matcher' component but never defines it — want me to flag that as a follow-up, or leave the HTML as-is?"
144: 6. Save next to the source.
145: 
146: ### Workflow B walk-through
147: 
148: User: "Create a doc explaining how SKILL.md works as an operational contract."
149: 
150: You:
151: 1. Confirm the substance — what does the user know about this? Where does it live in the code? What sources can you draw from? (Check past conversations if available and if it'd help.)
152: 2. Pick type → concept explainer (it's pedagogical, wants a live demo).
153: 3. Read references for concept-explainer, house-style, markdown-document.
154: 4. Write `skill-md-as-contract.md` first — full prose, frontmatter, callouts, wiki links to `[[hive-orchestrator]]` and `[[agent-anatomy]]`, a Mermaid diagram of the dispatch flow, file-referenced code samples.
155: 5. Write `skill-md-as-contract.html` — every section from the .md, the live SKILL.md editor demo (the experience the .md *describes* with a callout but can't *be*), hand-SVG of the same dispatch flow, comparison table, hover-glossary.
156: 6. Save both.
157: 
158: ## House style essentials (always apply)
159: 
160: Full reference in `references/house-style.md`. The non-negotiables:
161: 
162: - **Self-contained HTML.** One file. CSS in `<style>`, JS in `<script>`. CDN imports tolerable for charting libraries but prefer hand-written SVG. Works offline after first load.
163: - **CSS variables for theming.** Every color and font goes through a variable in `:root`. This is what holds the "loose house style" together.
164: - **Typography: serif body, sans UI, mono code.** System stacks. ~70ch measure for body.
165: - **Inline SVG over images.** The agent has a real pen — use it.
166: - **On-page nav at the top** for any doc longer than two screens.
167: - **The "Files read" / "Sources" header** above the H1, listing actual files this doc draws from. Credibility move.
168: - **Color-code by layer or status, not decoration.**
169: 
170: ## Optional: index page
171: 
172: Once the user has 3+ docs, offer to generate `index.html` — a landing page linking all docs, grouped by type, with the same house style. Great for an Obsidian vault folder you want to share externally.
173: 
174: ## Optional: publish the HTML as a hosted Artifact
175: 
176: When the user wants a *shareable link* rather than a file they open locally, the `.html` companion is a ready-made Claude Code Artifact — hand it to `artifact-publish` to render it to a hosted, default-private claude.ai page. The one rule that carries over: **the `.md` stays canonical**; the published Artifact is a shared *view* of it, never the source of truth. (The HTML is already self-contained, which is exactly what the Artifact CSP requires — one fewer thing to fix.)
177: 
178: ## Anti-patterns
179: 
180: - **HTML adding content the .md doesn't have.** Cardinal rule violation. Stop, add to .md first, then render.
181: - **Editing the source .md in Workflow A without permission.** Read-only by default. Surface gaps; don't silently patch them.
182: - **Mermaid and hand-SVG depicting different things.** Same boxes, same connections, same labels. Always.
183: - **Generic stock examples.** "Imagine a service that handles user authentication..." No. Use the user's actual repo, file paths, component names. Ask for specifics if missing.
184: - **Bootstrap / Tailwind / framework CSS.** These docs feel handmade and live forever in a repo. A 200-line `<style>` block beats 100KB of framework.
185: - **Diagrams as screenshots.** Always inline SVG.
186: - **Decoration over information.** Gradients, glassmorphism, animations that don't teach. Restrained on purpose.
187: - **Walls of text inside the HTML.** If a section has 3+ paragraphs in a row with no visual break, add a collapsible, tab, side-by-side, diagram, or callout.
188: - **Wiki links left as `[[double-brackets]]` in the HTML.** Translate at render time to `<a href="./<slug>.html">`. Falling back to the slug as link text is fine if there's no friendlier label.
189: 
190: ## Reference files
191: 
192: Read these as needed — don't read all upfront:
193: 
194: - `references/house-style.md` — CSS tokens, components, typography, .md → HTML mapping (read every time)
195: - `references/markdown-document.md` — Obsidian-native .md conventions (read every time you write a .md)
196: - `references/architecture-map.md` — boxes-and-arrows pattern with SVG techniques (load when type = architecture-map)
197: - `references/concept-explainer.md` — interactive demo pattern with vanilla JS (load when type = concept-explainer)
198: - `references/comparison.md` — side-by-side pattern for repos/approaches (load when type = comparison)

=== skills/workflows/living-plan/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: living-plan
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: |
5:   Document and set up the living-plan convention: a front door (START-HERE.md), a
6:   strategic roadmap, tactical ledger, done-archive, and frontier doc, wired to an
7:   intake loop so reports become tracked entries instead of rotting. The ledger
8:   trio is often named by file — the "remaining" / "completed" / "future" set
9:   (REMAINING-WORK / COMPLETED-WORK / FUTURE). Use when the user says "set up a
10:   living plan", "make this plan a living doc", "stop my docs from rotting", "set
11:   up the remaining/completed/future files", "where are my remaining/completed/future
12:   md files", "my ledger is full of done rows", "sweep done rows to the archive",
13:   or wants a report-to-ledger intake loop. Key disambiguation: if the user asks
14:   for their "remaining / completed / future" files and THIS project lacks them,
15:   set the convention up HERE — never hunt across other repos; reading or editing
16:   one existing file is a normal file operation, not this skill.
17: requires_agent_teams: false
18: requires_claude_code: false
19: min_plan: starter
20: owns:
21:   directories: []
22:   patterns: []
23:   shared_read: ["*"]
24: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep", "Bash"]
25: composes_with: ["plan-intake", "plan-builder", "repo-deep-dive", "work-item-brief"]
26: spawned_by: []
27: ---
28: 
29: # Living Plan
30: 
31: > **Purpose:** Make plans that stay alive. A living plan has a single front door, three
32: > canonical documents, and one intake loop. Reports become tracked work — they never just rot.
33: 
34: ## The Convention
35: 
36: A living plan has **three layers + one front door + one intake loop**.
37: 
38: ### 1. Front door — `START-HERE.md` (one screen)
39: 
40: The single place to land. Contains:
41: 
42: - **Status at a glance** — current milestones or waves and their state. A few lines, updated regularly.
43: - **Ownership map** — which docs are canonical (edit these), which are frozen reference (read, don't edit), and which are archived (history, superseded). One-line description per doc.
44: - **How work flows in** — one sentence pointing to the intake loop.
45: 
46: Keep it to one screen. If it grows longer, something belongs in one of the canonical docs instead.
47: 
48: ### 2. Strategic doc — `BUILD-PLAN.md` or `PLAN.md`
49: 
50: The roadmap: waves or milestones with their scope and exit criteria, plus a **closure log** that records what shipped and when. The strategic doc answers "where are we going and what have we finished?"
51: 
52: ### 3. Tactical ledger — `docs/REMAINING-WORK.md` (+ `docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md`)
53: 
54: Every **open / in-progress** item, ID'd, prioritized, and sourced. Must document its own entry format and ID scheme at the top so new items are formatted consistently. The tactical ledger answers "what exactly needs to be done **next**?"
55: 
56: Entry shape (adapt to project conventions):
57: 
58: | ID | Priority | Wave | Area | Source | Summary | Status | Owner |
59: |----|----------|------|------|--------|---------|--------|-------|
60: 
61: IDs are stable and never reused. Priority (P0–P3 or equivalent), Wave/Phase, and Source are the minimum useful columns.
62: 
63: **Keep the open ledger lean.** Completed rows are the single biggest source of bloat — a mature project accumulates hundreds of `done` rows, and every session that reads the ledger pays for all of them in tokens for zero planning value. So the tactical layer is **two files**:
64: 
65: - `docs/REMAINING-WORK.md` — open + in-progress only (the to-do list, stays short and cheap to load).
66: - `docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md` — the **completed archive**: every `done` row, verbatim, append-only. History is preserved in full; it just lives where it doesn't tax the working doc.
67: 
68: The archive is the *tactical* record (every ID'd row). It complements — does not duplicate — the strategic **closure log** in the build plan (one line per wave/milestone close). Both exist: the closure log is the digest, the archive is the detail. The open ledger's header points to both so nothing feels lost. See **The Completion Sweep** below for how rows move.
69: 
70: ### 4. Frontier doc — `docs/FUTURE.md`
71: 
72: Items explicitly out of scope for the current roadmap. Keeps them from cluttering the tactical ledger while ensuring they aren't lost. Short, updated as scope decisions are made.
73: 
74: ### Small-project collapse
75: 
76: Small projects may collapse layers 2–4 into a single file (e.g. one `PLAN.md` with sections for roadmap, open items, and out-of-scope ideas). Always keep the front door and the intake loop — those are non-negotiable.
77: 
78: ## The Intake Loop (the load-bearing rule)
79: 
80: Reports don't rot here. Any finished report — deep dive, audit, skill-review, QA findings — enters the living plan via this loop:
81: 
82: ```
83: report  →  plan-intake skill  →  proposed entries  →  human approval  →  ledger + closure log
84: ```
85: 
86: The rule: **every report-producing workflow ends by proposing entries.** `repo-deep-dive` invokes `plan-intake` on its gap/integration findings. Audit and skill-review outputs go through `plan-intake` before they're filed. Nothing is "done" until it has either landed in the ledger or been explicitly dismissed.
87: 
88: `plan-intake` is fail-closed: nothing writes to the ledger without explicit human approval.
89: 
90: ## The Completion Sweep (the other load-bearing rule)
91: 
92: Intake is one half of keeping the ledger honest; the **completion sweep** is the other. Intake brings open work *in*; the sweep moves finished work *out*. Without it, the open ledger grows without bound and every future read gets more expensive for less signal — the exact failure the two-file split exists to prevent.
93: 
94: The rule: **when an item reaches `done`, it does not stay in the open ledger.** It moves to the completed archive in the same motion that marks it done.
95: 
96: ```
97: item ships  →  flip status to done  →  move the row to docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md
98:             →  add/confirm a one-line entry in the closure log (BUILD-PLAN.md)
99: ```
100: 
101: Three places update, each with a distinct job:
102: 
103: 1. **`docs/REMAINING-WORK.md`** — the row is *removed* (it's no longer "remaining").
104: 2. **`docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md`** — the row is *appended*, verbatim, in ID order. The full detail (what shipped, commit, caveats) is preserved here, not deleted.
105: 3. **`BUILD-PLAN.md` closure log** — gets the strategic one-liner (date · wave/item · result) if this completion closes a wave or is otherwise worth the digest. Per-item rows don't each need a closure-log line; wave closes do.
106: 
107: **When to run the sweep:** batch it, don't thrash. Sweep at a natural close — when a wave/PR lands, when `plan-intake` runs (it sweeps as a final step — see that skill), or whenever the open ledger has accumulated a handful of `done` rows. A row may sit `done` in the open ledger briefly between ship and sweep; that's fine. What's not fine is `done` rows accumulating there permanently.
108: 
109: **Preserve, don't summarize.** The archive keeps each row *as written* — the same fail-closed honesty as intake. Don't compress a completed item's detail on the way out; that detail is exactly why the archive exists. Trimming happens by *relocation*, never by deletion.
110: 
111: If a project is small enough that one ledger never gets long, the sweep can be deferred — but document that choice, because "the ledger is short" is a property that quietly stops being true.
112: 
113: ## Setting It Up on a New Project
114: 
115: ### Step 1 — Copy the front-door template
116: 
117: ```bash
118: cp template/START-HERE.template.md START-HERE.md
119: ```
120: 
121: Open `START-HERE.md` and fill every `{{PLACEHOLDER}}`. The ownership map is the most important part: list every planning doc and classify it as canonical, frozen reference, or archived. This is the map new contributors (and future Claude sessions) use to navigate.
122: 
123: ### Step 2 — Ensure the canonical docs exist
124: 
125: Check whether `BUILD-PLAN.md` (or `PLAN.md`), `docs/REMAINING-WORK.md`, and `docs/FUTURE.md` exist. If any are missing:
126: 
127: - Use `plan-builder` to create a strategic doc + tactical ledger from scratch when none exist.
128: - Create `docs/FUTURE.md` as a simple markdown list — it doesn't need a formal structure.
129: - `docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md` (the completed archive) may be created lazily — `plan-intake`'s completion sweep creates it on the first sweep that finds `done` rows — but if you're setting up by hand, create it now with a short header pointing back at the open ledger.
130: - If the project already has equivalent docs under different names, use those; update `START-HERE.md` to name them correctly.
131: 
132: ### Step 3 — Point CLAUDE.md and README at START-HERE.md
133: 
134: Add a line near the top of `CLAUDE.md` (and optionally `README.md`) directing new sessions to `START-HERE.md`. Example:
135: 
136: ```markdown
137: > Start here: see `START-HERE.md` for the current status and ownership map.
138: ```
139: 
140: ### Step 4 — Adopt the intake loop
141: 
142: Install or sync the `plan-intake` skill so it's available in the project's skill set. Announce to the team (or to your future self in `START-HERE.md`) that reports go through `plan-intake` before being filed.
143: 
144: ## Reference Implementations
145: 
146: **PetriDishOfMadness** is the canonical implementation of the full convention — including the two-file tactical layer and the completion sweep:
147: 
148: | Role | File |
149: |------|------|
150: | Front door | `START-HERE.md` |
151: | Strategic doc | `BUILD-PLAN.md` (with the closure log) |
152: | Tactical ledger (open) | `docs/REMAINING-WORK.md` |
153: | Tactical archive (done) | `docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md` |
154: | Frontier doc | `docs/FUTURE.md` |
155: | Intake skill | `plan-intake` (invoked after every deep dive or audit; sweeps done → archive) |
156: 
157: Its open ledger's header documents the ID scheme (prefixed, stable, never reused), priority labels (P0–P3), wave labels, and area taxonomy — the exact information `plan-intake` needs to format new entries correctly — and points at the completed archive + closure log so a reader who lands on the open ledger can find finished work without it bloating the to-do list.
158: 
159: **The Hive** (`/Users/johns/Repos/the-hive-ecosystem/The-Hive`) implements the front door, strategic doc, and intake loop, but predates the two-file tactical split — its single ledger keeps `done` rows inline. Read it as an example of the front door + roadmap + intake, not of the sweep.
160: 
161: **Worked example (PetriDishOfMadness, 2026-06-27):** the open ledger had grown to 502 lines / 226 rows, **185 of them `done`** — every session re-read the whole wall of shipped work. The completion sweep relocated those 185 rows (plus the historical status narrative and stale intake notes) to `docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md`, leaving a **89-line** open ledger of just the 41 open/in-progress items. Nothing was lost; the working doc got ~80% cheaper to load.
162: 
163: ## Relation to Other Skills
164: 
165: ```text
166: repo-deep-dive / skill-review / audit
167:               |
168:               v
169:           plan-intake  ←──  living-plan convention
170:               |
171:               v
172:       ledger entries  →  orchestrator / work-item-brief
173: ```
174: 
175: - **`plan-intake`** is the intake loop made executable. `living-plan` documents the convention; `plan-intake` runs it.
176: - **`plan-builder`** creates a strategic doc + ledger from scratch. Use it in Step 2 when neither exists.
177: - **`repo-deep-dive`** ends by calling `plan-intake` on its findings whenever the target project has a ledger.
178: - **`work-item-brief`** expands individual ledger entries into full implementation briefs.

=== skills/workflows/llm-wiki/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: llm-wiki
3: version: 1.2.0
4: description: |
5:   Bootstrap and operate LLM-maintained personal knowledge bases (wikis) for any project or domain — a persistent, compounding artifact, not RAG. Four modes: setup, ingest, query, lint.
