Trigger on "set up a wiki for X", "build me a knowledge base", "create an llm wiki", "organize my notes", "set up my second brain", "maintain a wiki for this project", and (inside an existing wiki) "add this article to the wiki", "what does the wiki say about X", "clean up the wiki", "process this source".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: ["index.md", "wiki/log.md", "wiki/overview.md"]
12:   shared_read: ["raw/", "wiki/"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with: ["wiki-research", "repo-deep-dive", "project-profiler", "mermaid-charts"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # LLM Wiki
19: 
20: Bootstrap and operate a persistent, LLM-maintained knowledge base for any domain.
21: 
22: The key idea: instead of re-deriving answers from raw sources every time, the LLM builds and maintains a structured wiki of markdown files. Sources are ingested once, knowledge is compiled into cross-linked pages, and the wiki compounds with every new source and question. You read it; the LLM writes it.
23: 
24: **Announce at start:** "Using llm-wiki to [set up / operate] your [domain] wiki."
25: 
26: ## Entry Detection
27: 
28: First, check what situation you're in:
29: 
30: | Signal | Mode |
31: |--------|------|
32: | No `CLAUDE.md` with wiki schema present in cwd | **Setup** — bootstrap a new wiki |
33: | `CLAUDE.md` exists with wiki schema AND user wants to add a source | **Ingest** |
34: | `CLAUDE.md` exists with wiki schema AND user is asking a question | **Query** |
35: | `CLAUDE.md` exists with wiki schema AND user wants health check | **Lint** |
36: | `CLAUDE.md` exists with wiki schema AND no specific operation given | Ask: "Ingest a new source, query the wiki, or run a health check?" |
37: 
38: Detect a wiki CLAUDE.md by looking for the `## Wiki Schema` marker in the file.
39: 
40: ---
41: 
42: ## Setup Mode
43: 
44: Bootstrap a new wiki from scratch. Quick interview, then create the structure.
45: 
46: ### Step 1: Interview (3 questions max, combine if possible)
47: 
48: Ask:
49: 1. **Domain and purpose** — What is this wiki for? (topic, project, use case)
50: 2. **Location** — Where should the wiki live? (default: `~/wikis/<domain-name>/` — confirm or let them specify a path)
51: 3. **Sources you already have** — Any existing docs/articles/notes to ingest immediately? (optional — can start empty)
52: 
53: If the user's initial message already answers some of these, don't re-ask. Bias toward action.
54: 
55: ### Step 2: Create directory structure
56: 
57: ```
58: <wiki-root>/
59: ├── CLAUDE.md          ← the wiki schema (LLM operating manual)
60: ├── index.md           ← entry-point content catalog (root; updated on every ingest)
61: ├── raw/               ← immutable source documents (user adds, LLM reads only)
62: │   └── assets/        ← downloaded images and attachments
63: ├── wiki/              ← LLM-generated and LLM-maintained article pages
64: │   ├── log.md         ← append-only operation history
65: │   ├── overview.md    ← evolving synthesis / thesis (created after first ingest)
66: │   └── sources/       ← one summary page per ingested source
67: │       └── .gitkeep
68: └── .gitignore         ← ignore nothing by default (wiki is just markdown)
69: ```
70: 
71: Create these directories and files. The root `index.md` is the entry point; `wiki/` holds the article pages. The `wiki/sources/` directory holds one page per ingested source. Additional subdirectories (`wiki/entities/`, `wiki/concepts/`, etc.) are created as needed during ingest.
72: 
73: ### Step 3: Write the wiki CLAUDE.md
74: 
75: Load `references/wiki-schema-template.md` and fill in the placeholders:
76: - `{{WIKI_NAME}}` — human-readable wiki name (e.g., "AI Research Wiki")
77: - `{{DOMAIN}}` — brief domain description (e.g., "AI safety research papers and blog posts")
78: - `{{WIKI_ROOT}}` — absolute path to the wiki root directory
79: - `{{DATE_CREATED}}` — today's date (YYYY-MM-DD)
80: 
81: Write the filled template to `<wiki-root>/CLAUDE.md`.
82: 
83: ### Step 4: Initialize index.md and log.md
84: 
85: **`index.md`** (root entry point):
86: ```markdown
87: # Index
88: 
89: > Wiki: {{WIKI_NAME}} | Created: {{DATE}} | Sources: 0 | Pages: 0
90: 
91: ## Sources
92: *(none yet — ingest your first source to begin)*
93: 
94: ## Concepts
95: *(none yet)*
96: 
97: ## Entities
98: *(none yet)*
99: ```
100: 
101: **`wiki/log.md`:**
102: ```markdown
103: # Log
104: 
105: Append-only record of wiki operations. Format: `## [YYYY-MM-DD] operation | details`
106: 
107: ---
108: 
109: ## [{{DATE}}] setup | Wiki initialized
110: 
111: Domain: {{DOMAIN}}
112: Wiki root: {{WIKI_ROOT}}
113: ```
114: 
115: ### Step 5: Confirm and offer first ingest
116: 
117: Tell the user:
118: 
119: > "Wiki created at `<path>`. Structure:
120: > - `raw/` — drop your source documents here
121: > - `wiki/` — I maintain this; you read it
122: > - `CLAUDE.md` — the schema that governs how I work in this wiki
123: >
124: > Ready to ingest your first source? Drop a file in `raw/` and tell me, or paste a URL/article directly."
125: 
126: If the user mentioned existing sources in step 1, proceed immediately to Ingest Mode.
127: 
128: ---
129: 
130: ## Ingest Mode
131: 
132: Process a new source into the wiki. This is the core compounding operation.
133: 
134: Read `references/operations.md` § Ingest for the full workflow. Summary:
135: 
136: 1. **Read the source** — the user drops a file in `raw/` or provides content directly
137: 2. **Discuss** — briefly surface the 2–3 most interesting takeaways; invite the user to guide emphasis
138: 3. **Write a source summary page** to `wiki/sources/<slug>.md`
139: 4. **Update or create entity and concept pages** — identify named things (people, orgs, tools, papers) and concepts that warrant their own pages; update cross-references
140: 5. **Update `index.md`** (root entry point) — add the source entry, update page counts
141: 6. **Append to `wiki/log.md`** — one entry: `## [DATE] ingest | <title>`
142: 7. **Update `wiki/overview.md`** — revise the synthesis to incorporate the new source; note if it confirms, contradicts, or extends prior claims
143: 
144: A single source typically touches 5–15 wiki pages. Work through them systematically.
145: 
146: ---
147: 
148: ## Query Mode
149: 
150: Answer questions using the wiki as the knowledge base.
151: 
152: Read `references/operations.md` § Query for the full workflow. Summary:
153: 
154: 1. Read the root `index.md` first to find relevant pages
155: 2. Read the relevant pages
156: 3. Synthesize an answer with wiki citations (link to the wiki pages, not raw sources)
157: 4. Offer to file the answer as a new wiki page if it represents valuable synthesis (comparisons, analyses, discovered connections)
158: 
159: ---
160: 
161: ## Lint Mode
162: 
163: Health-check the wiki and surface improvements.
164: 
165: Read `references/operations.md` § Lint for the full workflow. Summary:
166: 
167: Check for:
168: - Contradictions between pages (newer sources superseding old claims)
169: - Orphan pages (no inbound links from other wiki pages)
170: - Concepts mentioned but lacking their own page
171: - Missing cross-references (entity A mentioned on page B but not linked)
172: - Data gaps (questions the wiki can't answer that a targeted search could fill)
173: 
174: Produce a lint report and offer to fix issues automatically or suggest new sources to investigate.
175: 
176: ---
177: 
178: ## Working Style
179: 
180: The user reads; the LLM writes. Some principles:
181: 
182: - **Never modify files in `raw/`** — they are the immutable source of truth
183: - **Always update the root `index.md` and `wiki/log.md`** on every ingest — these are how the LLM navigates at scale
184: - **Cross-references are the point** — a page with no links to other wiki pages is a missed opportunity
185: - **File good answers back** — if a query produces a valuable synthesis, create a wiki page for it
186: - **Note contradictions explicitly** — when new sources conflict with existing claims, update the relevant pages to flag the tension rather than silently overwriting
187: 
188: When the wiki grows large (100+ pages), suggest adding a search tool like `qmd` for more efficient navigation.
189: 
190: ## Reference files
191: 
192: - `references/wiki-schema-template.md` — the CLAUDE.md template written into new wikis; load during Setup Step 3
193: - `references/operations.md` — detailed ingest, query, and lint workflows; load when executing those operations

=== skills/workflows/maintain-context/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: maintain-context
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: |
5:   Maintain a project's CONTEXT.md domain glossary and docs/adr/ decision records inline as understanding crystallizes — write the glossary entry the moment a term resolves, write an ADR only when the body's three-condition gate fires. Use after any architectural discussion, requirements clarification, or when shared terminology starts to drift. Trigger on: 'update the glossary', 'add to CONTEXT', 'record this as an ADR', 'what do we call this', 'is this the right term', 'we just decided something', 'document this decision'.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: ["docs/adr/"]
11:   patterns: ["CONTEXT.md"]
12:   shared_read: []
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Grep", "Glob"]
14: composes_with: ["grill-me", "architecture-rescue", "setup-project-skills"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # maintain-context
19: 
20: Incrementally maintain two artifacts as conversations happen: `CONTEXT.md` (the project's domain glossary) and `docs/adr/` (decision records). Both compound in value only if you keep them honest, narrow, and rare.
21: 
22: > **Ownership.** This skill owns `docs/adr/` exclusively (as a directory, so it takes precedence over pattern ownership) plus the `CONTEXT.md` glossary file. `docs/adr/` is an explicit carve-out from `docs-agent`'s broader `docs/` ownership — without it, `docs/` would silently swallow the ADR subtree. `docs-agent` reads `docs/adr/` for context but never writes there. See the canonical map in the orchestrator's `references/file-ownership.md`.
23: 
24: ## The three-condition ADR gate
25: 
26: Before writing an ADR, all three must be true. Miss any one and skip — the next ADR will not be a worse decision because this one wasn't recorded.
27: 
28: 1. **Hard to reverse.** Rolling back costs real time or money. Schema migrations, public API shapes, vendor lock-in, contractual commitments. Reversible defaults (a flag, a CSS choice, a folder name) don't qualify.
29: 2. **Surprising without context.** A new contributor would not derive this from the code in front of them. They'd reach for the obvious answer and be wrong. If the code self-documents the choice, no ADR.
30: 3. **Real trade-off involved.** There were viable alternatives, and choosing this one closed some doors. If the alternative was nonsense ("we could have not used HTTPS"), there's no decision to record — only a fact.
31: 
32: Why this gate matters: an ADR folder full of routine choices reads like noise, and people stop opening it. Rarity is the whole point. When all three conditions hit, the ADR is worth writing because it captures information that *will* be lost otherwise.
33: 
34: ## CONTEXT.md is a glossary
35: 
36: Not a spec. Not a scratchpad. Not implementation notes. Each entry is:
37: 
38: - A term (the canonical name this project uses)
39: - Its meaning in this project's vocabulary
40: - An `_Avoid_:` line listing forbidden synonyms — words that mean the same thing in everyday speech but collide with something else here
41: 
42: If the entry isn't disambiguating a term, it doesn't belong in CONTEXT.md. Put it in a README or design doc instead.
43: 
44: ## Inline-update pattern
45: 
46: Capture entries the moment a term resolves. Do not batch glossary updates for end-of-session.
47: 
48: > **Forbidden:** Batching glossary updates for end-of-session. Capture them inline as terms are resolved. Batched updates miss context — the reason a term was forbidden gets lost between the conversation and the writeup.
49: 
50: When the user says "let's call them subscribers, not users" — open `CONTEXT.md` right then, write the entry, confirm the `_Avoid_:` list with the user. The five-second update is worth more than a thirty-minute end-of-session pass that has lost half the nuance.
51: 
52: ## The `_Avoid_:` alias-list pattern
53: 
54: Every term lists forbidden synonyms with a one-clause reason. The reason is what makes the rule stick.
55: 
56: ```markdown
57: ## Subscriber
58: A customer record with `subscription_status = active`. Has access to paid features.
59: _Avoid_: user (too generic), member (legacy term from v1), client (means something else in marketing).
60: ```
61: 
62: See `references/context-format.md` for full structure and three worked examples.
63: 
64: ## Cross-reference with code
65: 
66: When the user states behavior — "Subscribers get a 30-day trial" — verify against the code before recording it. Use `Grep` for the relevant constant or model field. If the code disagrees, stop and ask which is wrong. Do not record a glossary entry or ADR that contradicts the codebase: one of them is going to be wrong, and the artifact that's supposed to disambiguate will become the thing that confuses people.
67: 
68: ## Project layout — read `docs/agents/domain-docs.md` first
69: 
70: Before writing or reading any glossary entry or ADR, check whether the repo has `docs/agents/domain-docs.md`. That file (written by `/setup-project-skills`) declares whether this repo uses a single-context or multi-context layout, which controls where `CONTEXT.md` and `docs/adr/` live.
71: 
72: - **If `docs/agents/domain-docs.md` exists:** read it. A single-context layout puts `CONTEXT.md` at the repo root and ADRs at `docs/adr/`. A multi-context monorepo puts them per-app at `apps/<app>/CONTEXT.md` and `apps/<app>/docs/adr/`. Use the declared paths — do not invent your own.
73: - **If `docs/agents/domain-docs.md` is missing:** default to single-context (root `CONTEXT.md` and `docs/adr/`) and surface one prompt: *"This repo isn't configured for Skill-Madness yet. Run `/setup-project-skills` to make the layout choice durable; I'll use single-context defaults for this entry."* Do not silently assume — the wrong default in a monorepo lands an entry in the wrong vault.
74: 
75: ## Lazy file creation
76: 
77: Do not create `CONTEXT.md` or `docs/adr/` until there is a real entry to put in them. If they don't exist when this skill triggers, ask once: "Should I create `CONTEXT.md` / `docs/adr/` now?" Get explicit consent. Empty scaffolding signals "this project documents things" when it doesn't yet.
78: 
79: ## ADR workflow
80: 
81: When all three gate conditions hit:
82: 
83: 1. Propose the ADR out loud: "This looks ADR-worthy — hard to reverse (schema), surprising (we picked event-sourcing over CRUD), real trade-off (loses ad-hoc queryability). Want me to write it?"
84: 2. On yes, draft per `references/adr-format.md` and save to `docs/adr/NNNN-title.md` with the next sequential number.
85: 3. Status starts as `Accepted` if the decision is live; `Proposed` if still under debate.
86: 
87: For uncertain cases, see `references/three-condition-gate.md` — five scenarios walked through with the right call named.
88: 
89: ## Compose with
90: 
91: - `grill-me` — surfaces decisions and terminology that should be captured here.
92: - `architecture-rescue` — when joining an unfamiliar codebase, this skill is where the recovered understanding lands.

=== skills/workflows/mermaid-charts/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: mermaid-charts
3: version: 2.5.0
4: description: |
5:   Create expert-quality mermaid diagrams — flowcharts, sequence, state, ER, Gantt, mindmap, block-beta and more — including dense 15-30+ node architecture maps that stay readable. Use whenever the user wants to visualize a system, process, workflow, architecture, data model, timeline, or relationship, or when another skill needs an embedded mermaid diagram. Trigger on "diagram this", "draw this", "show me how X works", "map the architecture", "chart the flow", "visualize this", or any request for a technical visual — even without the word "mermaid".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with:
15:   - docs-agent
16:   - backend-agent
17:   - frontend-agent
18:   - skill-writer
19:   - project-profiler
20:   - orchestrator
21:   - infrastructure-agent
22:   - contract-author
23:   - observability-agent
24:   - artifact-publish
25: spawned_by: []
26: ---
27: 
28: # Mermaid Charts
29: 
30: You are an expert at creating clear, well-structured mermaid diagrams that communicate complex systems effectively. Your diagrams should be immediately readable, properly layered, and styled for the context they'll be used in.
31: 
32: ## Core Principle: Diagrams Are Arguments
33: 
34: A diagram isn't a picture — it's an argument about how a system works. Every element should earn its place. Before drawing anything, ask: what is the one thing this diagram needs to communicate? Then ruthlessly cut everything that doesn't serve that point.
35: 
36: A diagram of a three-layer architecture should make the layers obvious. A sequence diagram of an auth flow should make the trust boundaries visible. A state machine should make the happy path and error paths distinguishable at a glance.
37: 
38: ## Choosing the Right Diagram Type
39: 
40: Pick the diagram type that matches the *question* being answered, not just the data shape:
41: 
42: | Question | Diagram Type | Reference |
43: |----------|-------------|-----------|
44: | "How does data/control flow through this?" | `flowchart` | `references/chart-types/flowchart.md` |
45: | "What talks to what, in what order?" | `sequenceDiagram` | `references/chart-types/sequence.md` |
46: | "What states can this be in?" | `stateDiagram-v2` | `references/chart-types/state.md` |
47: | "What are the entities and relationships?" | `erDiagram` | `references/chart-types/er.md` |
48: | "How do these concepts relate?" | `mindmap` | `references/chart-types/mindmap.md` |
49: | Classes, Gantt, block-beta, timeline, pie, journey, gitGraph | various | `references/chart-types/other.md` |
50: 
51: When in doubt between two types, prefer the one with fewer visual elements for the same information. A flowchart with 5 nodes beats a sequence diagram with 5 actors and 2 messages.
52: 
53: ## Workflow
54: 
55: 1. **Identify the question.** State the one thing the diagram must communicate.
56: 2. **Pick the chart type.** Use the table above. Read the relevant `references/chart-types/*.md` for syntax recipes and examples.
57: 3. **Sketch the layout.** Pick direction (TB/LR/BT/RL), identify subgraphs and grouping boundaries.
58: 4. **Draft the diagram.** Use shapes and edges to encode meaning consistently.
59: 5. **Style purposefully.** See `references/styling.md` for `classDef` usage, color discipline, and contrast rules.
60: 6. **Verify against the checklist below** before delivering.
61: 
62: ## Managing Complexity
63: 
64: For diagrams above ~10 nodes, ecosystem maps, or multi-diagram document structure, read `references/complexity-and-output.md`. It covers:
65: 
66: - Visual hierarchy (glance/scan/study levels) for 15-30 node diagrams
67: - Multi-diagram strategies and when to split vs keep together
68: - Ecosystem map patterns for 10+ interconnected systems
69: - Output format (markdown, `.mmd` files, rendered SVG/PNG via `mmdc`)
70: - Common pitfalls (special chars, label lengths, subgraph collisions, keyword IDs)
71: 
72: ## Advanced Patterns
73: 
74: For sophisticated needs — multi-diagram document structure, complex flowcharts with 15-30+ nodes, sequence patterns, `block-beta` system maps, theming/branding, the full rendering pipeline — read `references/advanced-patterns.md`.
75: 
76: ## Sharing a diagram as a hosted page
77: 
78: To hand someone a *link* to a diagram rather than a code block or an image file, render the diagram to SVG, inline that SVG in a self-contained HTML page, and pass it to `artifact-publish` for a hosted, default-private claude.ai page. Inline the **rendered SVG** — do not load `mermaid.js` from a CDN in the page, since the Artifact CSP blocks external scripts.
79: 
80: ## Checklist Before Delivering
81: 
82: - [ ] The diagram answers a clear question (stated in a heading or comment)
83: - [ ] Node count is manageable (5-9 primary elements, subgraphs for more)
84: - [ ] Direction matches the mental model (TB for layers, LR for flows)
85: - [ ] Shapes are used consistently (same meaning throughout)
86: - [ ] Edge labels add information (not just restating what's obvious)
87: - [ ] Styling highlights the important parts (not everything)
88: - [ ] The diagram renders without errors in a mermaid-compatible viewer
89: - [ ] Labels are free of special character issues
90: 
91: ## Reference Files
92: 
93: - `references/chart-types/flowchart.md` — direction, subgraphs, shapes, edges, large-system architecture, anchor pattern, multi-system comparison patterns
94: - `references/chart-types/sequence.md` — actors/participants, activate/deactivate, control flow blocks
95: - `references/chart-types/state.md` — start/end states, transitions, composite states
96: - `references/chart-types/er.md` — cardinality notation, key attributes
97: - `references/chart-types/mindmap.md` — root shape, hierarchy by indent
98: - `references/chart-types/other.md` — class, Gantt, block-beta, timeline, pie, journey, gitGraph; when NOT to diagram
99: - `references/styling.md` — `classDef`, color rules, contrast, color-by-concern
100: - `references/complexity-and-output.md` — visual hierarchy, multi-diagram strategy, ecosystem maps, output formats, pitfalls
101: - `references/advanced-patterns.md` — read when the guidance above isn't enough; covers multi-diagram document structure, complex flowcharts, theming/branding, rendering pipeline (350+ lines of detailed patterns)

=== skills/workflows/nano-banana/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: nano-banana
3: version: 1.5.0
4: description: >
5:   Generate images using Google's Nano Banana (Gemini Image Generation) API and save them to the project. Use whenever
6:   the user asks to generate images, create product photos, hero banners, or lifestyle shots, mentions "Nano Banana" /
7:   "generate an image" / "make a banner", or wants batch image generation. Also trigger for any AI image task using
8:   Gemini models — even casual phrasings like "let's do images" or "make me a picture of".
9: requires_agent_teams: false
10: requires_claude_code: false
11: min_plan: starter
12: owns:
13:   directories: []
14:   patterns: []
15:   shared_read: ["*"]
16: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
17: composes_with: ["frontend-agent", "docs-agent"]
18: spawned_by: []
19: ---
20: 
21: # Nano Banana Image Generation
22: 
23: Generate images using Google's Gemini Image Generation API and save them directly into the project.
24: 
25: ## How It Works
26: 
27: Nano Banana is Google's native image generation built into Gemini models. It accepts text prompts and returns high-quality photorealistic images. The API returns base64-encoded image data which gets decoded and saved to disk.
28: 
29: ### Model
30: 
31: The bundled script uses `gemini-2.5-flash-image` — Google's current image generation model. It handles product photos, hero banners, lifestyle shots, and detail close-ups well at ~$0.04/image.
32: 
33: > **Note:** The script also accepts `--model flash` and `--model pro` for future model tiers as Google releases them. If those fail, fall back to `standard`.
34: 
35: ### Supported Aspect Ratios
36: 
37: - **3:4** — Product cards / catalog grid (768x1024px)
38: - **16:9** — Hero / banner (1920x1080px)
39: - **1:1** — Detail / texture close-ups (1024x1024px)
40: - **4:5** — Lifestyle / editorial (1080x1350px)
41: - Also: 1:4, 1:8, 2:3, 3:2, 4:1, 4:3, 5:4, 8:1, 9:16, 21:9
42: 
43: ## Setup — the key is already wired; do NOT hunt for it or ask for it
44: 
45: **Do not pre-check for the key with `env | grep` or `find`, and never ask the user to supply a key before you have actually run the script.** The script finds the key by itself, and a manual check will lie to you.
46: 
47: Here is why this matters, because it has burned people (and cost real trust): this skill directory is normally a **symlink** into a separate repo — e.g. `~/.claude/skills/nano-banana → ~/Repos/.../Skill-Madness/skills/workflows/nano-banana`. The `GEMINI_API_KEY` lives in that repo's **root `.env`**, which sits *outside* `~/.claude/skills` entirely. The script calls `Path(__file__).resolve()`, so it follows the symlink to the real location and loads that `.env` every single time — it also walks every ancestor directory of both the script and your working directory, so it finds the key across layouts. But if *you* try to "verify" the key first with `find ~/.claude/skills -name .env` or `env | grep GEMINI`, you search the wrong tree, come up empty, and wrongly conclude the key is missing — then ask the user for a key they already set. That is the exact failure this section exists to prevent. Don't reproduce it.
48: 
49: **The workflow is just: run the script.** It is self-sufficient about the key. The *only* trustworthy signal that the key is truly absent is the script exiting with its own `GEMINI_API_KEY ... is not set` error (which also prints the `.env` files it searched). Treat that error — and nothing else — as "key missing." Only then guide the user:
50: 
51: 1. Get a key at https://aistudio.google.com/apikey
52: 2. Add `GEMINI_API_KEY=...` to the skills repo-root `.env` (see `.env.example`), or `export GEMINI_API_KEY=...` in the shell.
53: 
54: ## Generating Images
55: 
56: ### Step 1: Identify What to Generate
57: 
58: When the user asks for image generation, figure out exactly what's needed:
59: 
60: - **Single image**: The user describes what they want. Help them refine the prompt if needed.
61: - **Batch of images**: If the project has a prompts document or image manifest, check it for pending items.
62: - **Custom image**: Help the user craft a detailed prompt (see Prompt Crafting below).
63: 
64: ### Step 2: Find and Run the Script
65: 
66: The script lives alongside this SKILL.md. Locate it by searching for `nano-banana/scripts/generate_image.py`:
67: 
68: ```bash
69: # Find the script path (follows symlinks)
70: find -L ~/.claude/skills -name "generate_image.py" -path "*/nano-banana/*" 2>/dev/null | head -1
71: ```
72: 
73: Then run it:
74: 
75: ```bash
76: python3 <script-path>/generate_image.py \
77:   --prompt "the full prompt text" \
78:   --output "path/to/output.png" \
79:   --aspect-ratio "3:4" \
80:   --model standard \
81:   --resolution 2K
82: ```
83: 
84: **Parameters:**
85: - `--prompt` — The full prompt text (quote it carefully in the shell)
86: - `--output` — Where to save the file (use `.png` — the API returns PNG data)
87: - `--aspect-ratio` — Match the intended use (default: 3:4)
88: - `--model` — `standard` (default), `flash`, or `pro`
89: - `--resolution` — `512`, `1K`, `2K` (default), or `4K`
90: 
91: **For batch generation**, run images sequentially — the API has rate limits so avoid parallel calls.
92: 
93: ### Step 3: Verify and Report
94: 
95: After generation:
96: 
97: 1. **Check the file exists** and has reasonable size (images are typically 1-2MB)
98: 2. **Let the user review** — mention the file path so they can open it
99: 3. **Update any project tracking** if the project maintains an image manifest or prompts doc
100: 
101: ### Step 4: Handle Issues
102: 
103: - **API key missing**: Trust this *only* if the **script itself** printed `GEMINI_API_KEY ... is not set` — never your own `grep`/`find` (see Setup for why that lies). If it genuinely is missing, guide the user to https://aistudio.google.com/apikey and to add it to the repo-root `.env`.
104: - **Rate limited**: Wait a moment and retry, or suggest the user try again shortly
105: - **Bad output**: Re-run with a tweaked prompt. Common fixes:
106:   - Add "Photorealistic" if output looks illustrated
107:   - Be more specific about lighting, materials, and setting
108:   - Try a different aspect ratio if composition feels off
109: - **Model error / timeout**: Fall back to `--model standard` which is the most reliable
110: 
111: ## Prompt Crafting
112: 
113: Good prompts make the difference between generic and stunning output. When helping users write prompts:
114: 
115: **Structure**: Open with shot type and subject, then add material/texture details, setting, lighting, and composition notes.
116: 
117: **Example — Product shot:**
118: > Create a photorealistic editorial product photograph of a leather messenger bag laid on an aged oak table. The bag is made from full-grain vegetable-tanned leather with visible patina and hand-stitched details. Warm side lighting from a tall window casts soft directional shadows. Shot on a medium format camera with natural lighting. No AI artifacts. Photorealistic. Clean composition, suitable for e-commerce product grid.
119: 
120: **Example — Hero banner:**
121: > A dramatic wide-angle photograph of a mountain trail at golden hour. Warm amber sunlight cuts through pine trees, casting long shadows across the rocky path. Atmospheric fog in the valley below creates depth. Cinematic composition, editorial quality. Photorealistic.
122: 
123: **Prompt modifiers** (append as needed):
124: - Higher realism: "Shot on a medium format camera with natural lighting. No AI artifacts. Photorealistic."
125: - Product catalog: "Clean composition, suitable for e-commerce product grid."
126: - Style consistency: "Maintain the same [describe lighting], [describe surface], and [describe photography style]."
127: 
128: The key elements that improve output quality:
129: - **Specific materials** (full-grain leather, rough-woven wool, brushed brass) rather than generic descriptions
130: - **Lighting direction** (side lighting, golden hour, warm torchlight) rather than just "good lighting"
131: - **Setting details** that ground the image (aged oak table, stone courtyard, misty forest)
132: - **Photography framing** (medium format, editorial, cinematic) to set the visual quality bar
133: 
134: ## Reference Files
135: 
136: - `references/imagen-4-prompting.md` — deep-dive prompt engineering guide for Imagen 4 (the model behind Nano Banana). Read this when default output isn't hitting the mark, or when working with text-in-image, complex compositions, or specific photography styles. Covers the SCULPT framework, contextual priming techniques, the `enhancePrompt` parameter, and natural-language patterns that beat keyword soup.
137: - `references/example-project-config.md` — pattern for documenting per-project image conventions (paths, prompt style guide, anti-pattern modifiers, tracking spreadsheet). Suggest this to the user when starting a project that will generate many images, so the project's prompts stay consistent across sessions.

=== skills/workflows/payload-cms/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: payload-cms
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: |
5:   Build, extend, deploy and debug websites on Payload CMS (payloadcms.com): collections, blocks and layout builder, live preview, drafts, plugins, Postgres or Mongo, Vercel, migrations, plus cloning or rebuilding an existing site (including payloadcms.com itself) onto Payload. Use this skill whenever the user mentions Payload, Payload CMS, payload.config.ts, @payloadcms packages, a headless CMS or CMS for their websites, moving a site onto a CMS, cloning payloadcms.com, or asks how to model pages, blocks, a blog, redirects, SEO, forms or preview in Payload, even without saying "Payload". Complements Payload's own official payload skill with the website workflow, version pinning and verified pitfalls.
6: compatibility: "Any coding agent host; needs shell, file access and web access. Browser checks use Playwright."
7: composes_with: ["playwright", "website-walkthrough-video", "design-token-guard", "class-extraction-guard", "render-sanity", "orchestrator", "ui-brief"]
8: ---
9: 
10: # Payload CMS for websites
11: 
12: Payload is a TypeScript-first, code-configured headless CMS that installs inside a Next.js app: one project serves the admin panel, the REST and GraphQL APIs, and your frontend. That shape is why it suits "a CMS for all our websites": a site's content model lives in reviewable code, editors get an admin UI, and the frontend reads content through an in-process Local API.
13: 
14: This skill is a routing layer over verified reference files. The references were written by reading the Payload source and docs at v3.90.2, the `payloadcms/website` repo (the code behind payloadcms.com), and the live site, on 2026-09-24. Payload's own official skill (named `payload`, installed by `create-payload-app`) covers generic app development; this one adds the website workflow, cloning, deployment and the traps found while reading. They coexist.
15: 
16: ## Step 0: establish the version before anything else
17: 
18: Payload's `main` branch is a v4 canary while npm `latest` is v3. Advice for one can silently break the other (v4 flips `overrideAccess`, `defaultDepth`, versions defaults and drops Next 15 and Slate).
19: 
20: 1. Read the project's `package.json` for `payload` and every `@payloadcms/*` version (they must all match exactly).
21: 2. For a new project, default to v3.90.x pinned exactly. Choose v4 only if the user asks and accepts canary churn (`references/v4-delta.md` section 9).
22: 3. Re-check `npm view payload dist-tags` if the work is more than a few weeks after 2026-09-24; the references are a dated snapshot. When a reference and the installed package disagree, trust the installed package's types and docs.
23: 
24: ## Route the task
25: 
26: | The user wants | Read |
27: |---|---|
28: | Build or rebuild a site on Payload, or clone a site (payloadcms.com or their own) | `references/clone-a-site-workflow.md`, then `references/clone-recipes.md` |
29: | How payloadcms.com is actually built (collections, blocks, docs pipeline, styling) | `references/payloadcms-com-anatomy.md` |
30: | Scaffold from the official website template, add a block end to end | `references/website-template.md` |
31: | Config, collections, globals, versions, uploads, auth, localization | `references/config-and-collections.md` |
32: | A field type, blocks, relationships, Lexical rich text, slugs | `references/fields.md` |
33: | Access control, hooks, revalidation | `references/access-and-hooks.md` |
34: | Querying, Local API, REST, GraphQL, seeding | `references/local-api-and-queries.md` |
35: | Drafts, preview button, live preview, scheduled publish | `references/live-preview-and-drafts.md` |
36: | SEO, redirects, nested pages, forms, search, multi-tenant, storage, email | `references/plugins.md` |
37: | Scaffold, env vars, Docker, Vercel, Cloudflare, production checklist | `references/scaffolding-and-deploy.md` |
38: | Database choice, migrations, push vs migrate, transactions | `references/database-and-migrations.md` |
39: | Slow queries, build problems, an error message, "works in dev only" | `references/performance-and-troubleshooting.md` |
40: | v3 vs v4, upgrading, whether to pin | `references/v4-delta.md` |
41: 
42: Load only what the task needs; each file is dense and has its own contents list. Every H2 in a reference names the source it was verified against. Items marked `UNVERIFIED` were not confirmed; say so instead of presenting them as fact.
43: 
44: ## Mental model
45: 
46: - **Config is code.** `payload.config.ts` (`buildConfig`) declares collections (many documents: pages, posts, media, users), globals (one document: header, footer), fields, access rules, hooks, plugins and the database adapter. Types come from `payload generate:types`; the admin's custom-component map comes from `payload generate:importmap`. Regenerate both after schema or component changes.
47: - **Pages are a layout builder.** A `pages` collection with a `hero` group and a `layout` field of type `blocks`. Each block is a schema plus a React renderer chosen by `blockType`. New section = new block config + renderer + map entry + regenerated types.
48: - **The frontend reads with the Local API** (`getPayload({ config })` then `payload.find(...)`) inside Server Components, no HTTP hop. Cache with Next primitives and invalidate in `afterChange` hooks.
49: - **Drafts are separate from published.** With `versions.drafts`, a `_status` field exists, public reads must be limited to published, and preview uses Next draft mode.
50: - **Plugins are config functions** (`(config) => config`). The official ones cover SEO, redirects, nested pages, forms, search, import/export, multi-tenant, storage and email.
51: 
52: ## Rules that prevent the expensive mistakes
53: 
54: Each rule has the reason attached so you can judge edge cases. Details and citations are in the references.
55: 
56: 1. **Pin versions exactly and keep them in lockstep.** Mixed `@payloadcms/*` versions produce admin and hydration breakage that looks unrelated. Upgrades are deliberate.
57: 2. **Local API skips access control by default in v3.** `overrideAccess` defaults to `true` even when you pass `user`. Pass `overrideAccess: false` whenever acting for a user, and write `overrideAccess` explicitly everywhere so code survives v4, where the default flips.
58: 3. **Thread `req` through nested operations in hooks.** Without it the nested call runs outside the transaction and can leave partial data. Guard hook-triggered writes with a `context` flag or the hook re-fires forever.
59: 4. **Public reads of drafted collections must filter `_status: published`** in the access function, or drafts leak through REST, GraphQL and the Local API.
60: 5. **`depth` decides whether relationships are objects or IDs.** Reusable-content and upload blocks render nothing when fetched too shallowly. Set depth per query rather than trusting defaults (v4 changes the default).
61: 6. **Unknown `blockType` renders nothing, silently.** payloadcms.com itself ships a block (`exampleTabs`) registered in the schema with no renderer. Add a parity check so a schema-only block fails CI.
62: 7. **Do not infer a Payload site's blocks from its DOM.** Reusable content inlines other blocks. Read the page document from the API (`clone-recipes.md` section 4), and only use read-only public routes on sites you do not own.
63: 8. **`push` is for development; production uses committed migrations.** Dev push records itself in the migrations table in a way that makes a later `payload migrate` on that database warn about data loss. MongoDB transactions need a replica set.
64: 9. **The website template's "Seed the database" button and any `deleteMany` seed wipe content.** Never run it against a database with real content.
65: 10. **Check the SEO plugin config before trusting auto-generate.** Read from the plugin source (not run): the generate endpoints reject collections that are not in `seoPlugin({ collections })`, and the template's call omits that option. Test the button in the admin.
66: 11. **The default slugify strips everything outside letters, digits, underscore and hyphen.** Legacy URLs with slashes, dots or non-ASCII characters mangle on import. Import slugs verbatim through a custom `slugify` or `unique` fields, and model nested paths with the nested-docs plugin.
67: 12. **Guard every custom endpoint, and never ship `admin.autoLogin` outside development.** payloadcms.com's own repo has sync and redeploy endpoints with no check in code and an unconditional `autoLogin`. Do not copy either.
68: 13. **Preview and revalidation secrets stay server-side**; the preview secret in a URL is visible to any admin, which is acceptable only for admin-only use.
69: 14. **Images do not import themselves.** HTML and Markdown to Lexical converters drop or ignore image references unless you upload first and mark them with the documented attributes or placeholders.
70: 15. **Copy structure, not brand.** A clone of someone else's site takes the information architecture and field names; their copy, logos, customer names and fonts stay theirs. payloadcms/website code is MIT but ships no content and no font license.
71: 
72: ## Standard workflows
73: 
74: **New website on Payload.** Step 0, then scaffold (`npx create-payload-app@latest my-site -t website`), choose the database, then follow `clone-a-site-workflow.md` phases 0-7 with your own design brief instead of a source site (the `ui-brief` skill writes one).
75: 
76: **Clone or rebuild an existing site.** Follow `references/clone-a-site-workflow.md`: inventory the source with Playwright or the API, map sections to blocks and pages to collections, extract tokens from computed styles, build in the documented order, import content, verify parity against the running site, harden.
77: 
78: **Add a block.** `website-template.md` section 17 is the end-to-end checklist (block config, renderer, map entry, types, import map, preview).
79: 
80: **Deploy.** `scaffolding-and-deploy.md`: env vars, build-time database connection, migrations in CI, storage adapter, production checklist. Verify the deployed site, not the dev server: publish a change and confirm it reaches the public page.
81: 
82: **Debug.** Start with the symptom table in `performance-and-troubleshooting.md`. The usual suspects are stale generated types or import map, mismatched package versions, missing `req` in hooks, wrong `depth`, and draft versus published reads.
83: 
84: ## Verification stance
85: 
86: State a Payload fact as true only after tracing it to the installed package, the docs for that version, or the running app. When you cannot, write "unverified". Run the app and look at it (Playwright, visible browser) before reporting UI work done; type-checks and tests do not prove that a page renders its blocks or that preview works. When a user complaint is "smaller" or "tighter", measure before claiming it.
87: 
88: ## Maintenance
89: 
90: The references are a snapshot (Payload v3.90.2, v4.0.0-canary.37, payloadcms/website at commit 023c2a5, checked 2026-09-24). To refresh: re-run the version checks in `v4-delta.md` section 1, then re-verify the sections whose cited paths changed in the Payload repo. Update the snapshot lines and this skill's version together.

11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with: ["orchestrator", "project-profiler", "mermaid-charts", "contract-author"]

17: 
18: # Plan Builder
19: 
20: > **Tradeoff:** Biases toward thoroughness over speed. For simple changes that don't need a plan, skip directly to implementation.
21: 
22: ## When this skill applies
23: 
24: This skill assumes a contract-first multi-agent build model:
25: 
26: - An orchestrator dispatches role-agents in parallel
27: - Each role-agent consumes a machine-readable contract from `/contracts/`
28: - `qe-agent` gates the build via `qa-report.json`
29: 
30: For single-agent or ad-hoc work, this skill is not the right tool.
31: 
32: ## What to do
33: 
34: Transform source material and project goals into orchestrator-ready build plans.
35: 
36: The orchestrator is powerful but needs a well-structured plan to work from. This skill bridges the gap between "I have research and a vision" and "orchestrator, go build it." It handles the thinking that should happen *before* agents are spawned: synthesizing source material, making architectural decisions, mapping content to components, and producing a plan the orchestrator can immediately act on.
37: 
38: **Announce at start:** "Using plan-builder to create a structured project plan."
39: 
40: ## When This Skill Runs vs Others
41: 
42: - **Brainstorming** explores what to build when the idea is vague. It asks many questions, one at a time, to refine intent. If you arrive here with only a vague idea and no source material, invoke brainstorming first, then come back with the spec it produces.
43: - **Plan-builder** (this skill) synthesizes known inputs into a build plan. It works when you have source material, a clear goal, or a spec from brainstorming. It asks at most 3 clarifying questions before producing a draft — bias toward action.
44: - **Writing-plans** produces TDD-level implementation detail (exact file paths, test code, commit messages) for single-agent sequential execution. Plan-builder produces architecture-level plans for multi-agent parallel execution.
45: - **Orchestrator** consumes the plan this skill produces. It sizes teams, authors contracts, and spawns agents.
46: - **Living-plan** is for *maintaining* an existing plan over time — a front door, a tactical ledger, and a report-intake loop so work stays tracked instead of rotting. Use plan-builder (this skill) to *create* a plan from source material once; use living-plan when the project already has a plan and you need to keep it alive as new reports and work items arrive.
47: 
48: ```text
49: brainstorming (vague idea) → plan-builder (structured plan) → orchestrator (agent army)
50:                               ↑ you are here
51: ```
52: 
53: ## Entry Detection
54: 
55: Assess what the user has brought:
56: 
57: | Signal | Entry Path |
58: |--------|-----------|
59: | @-mentioned files, attached docs, research artifacts, Compass exports | **Path A** — Artifact Ingestion |
60: | Spec from brainstorming, PRD, requirements doc | **Path A** — treat as artifact |
61: | Clear goal but no artifacts ("build me a dashboard for X") | **Path B** — Goal-Driven |
62: | Vague idea, no artifacts ("I want to do something with AI") | **Redirect** → invoke brainstorming |
63: | Existing codebase + new source material | **Path A+** — Augmentation variant |
64: 
65: ## Path A: Artifact Ingestion
66: 
67: When the user provides research documents, Compass artifacts, or reference material alongside a build request, follow the four-step extraction in `references/research-extraction.md`:
68: 
69: 1. **Synthesize source material** — content domains, natural sections, data points, implied features, volume
70: 2. **Map content to architecture** — produce a source-to-component table; research-heavy projects need information architecture before code architecture
71: 3. **Check existing codebase (Path A+)** — only if working in an existing project; respect existing conventions
72: 4. **Confirm with user** — present the content map + key decisions concisely; ask at most 3 clarifying questions total
73: 
74: Then produce the plan (see `references/plan-format.md`).
75: 
76: ## Path B: Goal-Driven (No Artifacts)
77: 
78: When the user has a clear goal but no source material:
79: 
80: 1. Ask: **What are you building, who is it for, and are there constraints?** (tech stack, timeline, existing code, deployment target). This is one question — don't split it into three messages.
81: 2. If the answer is still vague after one round, invoke brainstorming rather than asking more questions. Plan-builder is for synthesis, not ideation.
82: 3. If the goal is clear enough to plan, proceed straight to plan production.
83: 
84: ## Behavior Rules
85: 
86: - **Reasoning first, plan second.** Always produce Section 1 (Architecture Reasoning) before Section 2 (Build Plan). Thinking through the *why* produces better plans than jumping straight to the *what*.
87: - **Surface ambiguity, don't bury it.** If source material is contradictory or a decision could go either way, say so in Architecture Reasoning. Silent assumptions become integration bugs.
88: - **Bias toward action.** Three clarifying questions maximum. After that, draft the plan and let the user correct it. A wrong draft you can fix is more useful than a perfect question you haven't asked yet.
89: - **Right-size for orchestrator.** Plans should be specific enough that the orchestrator can size teams and author contracts, but not so detailed that agents have no autonomy. Component responsibilities yes, implementation pseudocode no.
90: - **Flag scale.** If the plan would require more than 6 parallel agents, suggest phasing: build the core in phase 1, extend in phase 2. Large teams need proactive context management (handoffs, phased spawning) to maintain quality.
91: - **Trace second-order effects for consequential decisions.** When a decision affects systems beyond the immediate build scope or has a time horizon longer than 30 days, apply the **second-order-effects** move (`references/second-order-effects.md`) before finalizing that section of the plan.
92: - **Respect existing code.** When augmenting an existing project, follow its conventions. Don't propose a React rewrite of a Vue app just because you prefer React.
93: 
94: ## Handoff
95: 
96: After the plan is saved:
97: 
98: > "Plan saved to `docs/plans/YYYY-MM-DD-<name>-plan.md`. Ready to build?"
99: >
100: > - **Orchestrated build:** I'll invoke the orchestrator skill to spawn the agent team
101: > - **Solo build:** I'll invoke writing-plans to expand this into TDD implementation steps
102: > - **Review first:** Take a look at the plan and let me know what to change
103: 
104: Wait for the user's choice. Do not auto-invoke the orchestrator.
105: 
106: ## Anti-Pattern
107: 
108: > **Forbidden:** Padding the plan with phases for the sake of structure. If three steps suffice, write three steps.
109: 
110: ## Supporting Info
111: 
112: ### Reference Documents
113: 
114: - **`references/plan-format.md`** — the section-by-section structure of the plan document (Architecture Reasoning + Build Plan, with templates for each section).
115: - **`references/research-extraction.md`** — how to pull a plan out of research docs (synthesize, map to architecture, check existing code, confirm with user).
116: - **`references/second-order-effects.md`** — thinking move for tracing downstream ripple consequences of a plan decision (use when the decision scope extends beyond 30 days or touches shared systems).
117: 
118: ### Output Location
119: 
120: Save the plan to `docs/plans/YYYY-MM-DD-<project-name>-plan.md` unless user preferences override the location.

=== skills/workflows/plan-intake/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: plan-intake
3: version: 1.3.0
4: description: |
5:   Turn any report (repo-deep-dive output, audit, skill-review, QA findings, design audit) into approved entries in a project's living-plan ledger. Use when the user says "intake this report", "add findings to the plan", "turn this audit into work items", "update the ledger from this report", "feed the deep-dive into the plan", or has a finished report and wants it tracked instead of rotting. Format-agnostic: adopts the target project's existing entry format.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep", "Bash"]
14: composes_with: ["plan-builder", "repo-deep-dive", "skill-review", "work-item-brief"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Plan Intake
19: 
20: > **Purpose:** Reports become tracked work instead of rotting. Plan-intake is the bridge between a finished report and a project's living tactical ledger.
21: 
22: ## When this skill applies
23: 
24: Use this skill when:
25: 
26: - A `repo-deep-dive`, audit, `skill-review`, QA findings doc, or any other report exists and its findings are not yet reflected in the project's ledger.
27: - The user wants findings ingested as first-class tracked work items, not left as a one-off doc.
28: - The project already has a living ledger (e.g. `docs/REMAINING-WORK.md`) with its own entry format and ID scheme.
29: 
30: **This skill is not for creating a plan from scratch.** For that, use `plan-builder`. Plan-intake appends to an existing living plan — it never replaces or restructures the ledger's existing organization.
31: 
32: ## What to do
33: 
34: Translate a finished report into approved, consistently-formatted entries in the target project's tactical ledger.
35: 
36: **Announce at start:** "Using plan-intake to turn the report into approved ledger entries."
37: 
38: ## Inputs
39: 
40: 1. **Report file** — a markdown document produced by `repo-deep-dive`, a design audit, `skill-review`, QA findings, or any other structured analysis. The user will @-mention or point to the file.
41: 2. **Target ledger** — the project's living tactical ledger. Default: `docs/REMAINING-WORK.md`. The user may specify a different path.
42: 3. **Strategic companion** — the project's high-level closure log or roadmap. Default: `BUILD-PLAN.md`. The user may specify a different file or indicate none exists.
43: 
44: ## Workflow
45: 
46: ### Step 1 — Read the ledger's own rules first
47: 
48: Before touching any content, open the target ledger and read its header section. Most well-maintained ledgers document:
49: 
50: - Entry format (fields, column order, required vs optional)
51: - ID scheme (prefix conventions, numeric ranges, or slug patterns; IDs that are stable and never reused)
52: - Status vocabulary (open, in-progress, done, deferred, etc.)
53: - Priority and wave/phase labels
54: - Area or category taxonomy
55: 
56: Adopt that project's format exactly. Never impose this skill's own format onto the ledger. If the ledger has no documented format, infer the format from the existing entries and note the inference in your proposal.
57: 
58: Example: The Hive's `REMAINING-WORK.md` "How this is organized" section defines Priority P0–P3, Wave A/B/C/— , Area, Source, Status, and Owner columns, plus a stable never-reused ID prefix scheme. A report from a Glass UI audit would get fresh IDs in the `G`-prefixed namespace (or whatever prefix the report source calls for), formatted to match every existing entry in that table.
59: 
60: ### Step 2 — Extract findings from the report
61: 
62: Read the full report and identify every distinct actionable finding. Each finding that could stand alone as a work item becomes one candidate entry. Non-actionable observations (summaries, background, rationale) are context only — do not convert them into entries.
63: 
64: Criteria for a candidate entry:
65: 
66: - It describes something that could be done or fixed.
67: - It is scoped tightly enough to be assigned and tracked.
68: - It is not already implied by a broader entry already in the ledger.
69: 
70: ### Step 3 — Deduplicate against the existing ledger
71: 
72: For each candidate entry, grep the ledger for existing entries covering the same subject, the same source link, or the same affected file or component. Flag likely duplicates — do not propose them as new entries. When in doubt, flag and let the human decide; do not silently drop candidates.
73: 
74: ### Step 4 — Propose a review table
75: 
76: Present all non-duplicate candidates as a table for human review before writing anything. The table serves **the approver**, not the ledger — so it carries two summary columns, not one:
77: 
78: | Proposed ID | Priority | Wave/Phase | Area | Source | What it does / what you'd notice | Ledger summary (one line) |
79: 
80: - **What it does / what you'd notice** — one jargon-free sentence stating the *observable change* if this row ships, written for someone who has not read the report. No insider shorthand, no bare symbol names, no ticket codes — not "off-replay `computeBuildingMesh` CGA split" but "building shapes are computed once and reused, so the map loads faster." This column is the approval gate's real content: if the approver can't tell what a row would change from this sentence alone, the row is not ready to propose. **Required for every row.**
81: - **Ledger summary** — the terse, project-native one-liner that actually gets written on approval (Step 6). It may use the ledger's house shorthand; the plain-language column is what makes it reviewable.
82: - Assign proposed IDs using the project's ID scheme. For a new report source, use a fresh never-reused prefix bundle (check existing IDs to avoid collisions).
83: - If the ledger uses additional columns (Owner, Status, etc.), include them with appropriate defaults (e.g. Status: open, Owner: —).
84: - Annotate any entry flagged as a possible duplicate with a note pointing to the existing entry.
85: 
86: **Over-build pass (YAGNI).** Before presenting, run each candidate through one filter: *does this need to exist yet?* Don't file speculative or "might-want-later" directions at the same weight as concrete fixes. For each such candidate, either mark it **`[speculative]`** in the table (so the approver weighs it as a maybe, not a commitment) or route it to the project's deferred/parked backlog (e.g. `docs/FUTURE.md`) instead of the active ledger. A report proposing eleven items where two are cheap-and-certain and four overlap already-tracked work should reach the approver as *"2 to commit, 4 dups flagged, 5 speculative"* — not eleven equal rows. This is the `caveman`/`yagni-gate` lazy-senior-dev lens (adapted from the upstream ponytail project): reuse-or-defer beats build-because-listed.
87: 
88: ### Step 5 — Gate: get explicit human approval
89: 
90: Present the proposal table and **wait for explicit approval before writing anything.** This skill is fail-closed: no approval, no writes.
91: 
92: The approver decides from the **What it does / what you'd notice** column — that plain-language sentence is what makes this gate real rather than a rubber-stamp. If any row's observable-change sentence is missing or still reads as shorthand, fix it *before* asking for approval: an approver who can only say "sure" or "no" to jargon is not actually gating.
93: 
94: The human may:
95: 
96: - Approve the full table as-is.
97: - Approve a subset (strike individual rows).
98: - Edit priorities, waves, or IDs before approving.
99: - Reject and ask for a revised proposal.
100: 
101: Do not proceed until approval is unambiguous. If the response is ambiguous, ask once for clarification.
102: 
103: ### Step 6 — Write approved entries into the ledger
104: 
105: On a feature branch (never directly on the default branch):
106: 
107: 1. Insert each approved entry into the correct Area section of the ledger, maintaining the existing sort order within that section (typically by priority, then by ID). Write the **Ledger summary** (the project-native one-liner); the review table's plain-language column was an approval aid — fold its observable-change sentence into the entry's detail only if the ledger's format has room for it. The ledger's house format (Step 1) governs what gets written, not this skill's review table.
108: 2. If the project uses a strategic companion (e.g. `BUILD-PLAN.md`), and any approved entry is P0 or P1 priority (or the project's equivalent of high-urgency), add a brief note in the companion's open-items or closure-log section referencing the new IDs.
109: 3. Never modify documents marked frozen, archived, or read-only (e.g. a spec doc with a "Status: Frozen" header). If the report source is one of those docs, read it for findings but do not edit it.
110: 4. Source links must be openable by anyone with the repo. If an entry's source report is audit-class (functional audit, skill-review, QA findings) and lives in a gitignored or machine-local location (e.g. a local `audit/` working tree), do not cite that path — republish the cited report into the project's committed audit-evidence projection first (e.g. `docs/audit-evidence/`, following its README's typed-header convention) and cite the in-repo path.
111: 
112: ### Step 7 — Report what landed
113: 
114: After writing, report back:
115: 
116: - How many entries were proposed, how many approved, how many written.
117: - The IDs of every entry written (list them explicitly so the human can verify).
118: - The branch name and which files were modified.
119: - Any candidates that were dropped as duplicates, with pointers to the existing entries they matched.
120: 
121: ### Step 8 — Sweep completed items out of the open ledger
122: 
123: Intake adds open work; the same pass should remove finished work, so the open ledger never bloats with `done` rows (the `living-plan` **completion sweep**). After writing the new entries, scan the open ledger for rows whose status is `done`:
124: 
125: 1. If the project keeps a completed archive (`docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md` or the path its ledger header names), **move** each `done` row there, verbatim, in ID order — append-only, never summarized. Remove it from the open ledger.
126: 2. If no archive exists yet but the open ledger has accumulated `done` rows, propose creating one (same fail-closed gate as intake — show the human the plan: "N done rows would move to `docs/COMPLETED-WORK.md`"). Don't invent an archive silently.
127: 3. Confirm the strategic closure log (`BUILD-PLAN.md`) carries the wave/milestone one-liner for what shipped; add it if a wave closed and it's missing.
128: 
129: This is pure relocation — reversible, git-tracked, no detail lost — so it doesn't need the per-row approval that *creating* entries does; just **report what moved** (how many rows, their IDs, source → destination). If the ledger explicitly documents a "leave done rows in place" convention, honor it and skip this step.
130: 
131: ## Behavior rules
132: 
133: - **Fail-closed.** No approval from the human, no writes to the ledger. This is non-negotiable.
134: - **Approver-readable proposals.** Every proposed row carries a jargon-free "what you'd notice" sentence written for someone who has not read the report. The fail-closed gate is only real if the approver can tell what each row would change; shorthand-only rows are not ready to propose.
135: - **Filter for over-build.** Run candidates through "does this need to exist yet?" before proposing. Flag speculative directions as `[speculative]` or route them to the parked backlog rather than filing them at the same weight as concrete, certain work.
136: - **Format-agnostic.** This skill has no opinion about what a ledger entry should look like. It adopts the project's existing format exactly. Two different projects may produce completely different entry shapes; that is correct.
137: - **Never touch frozen docs.** A report source marked frozen, archived, or read-only is read-only for findings extraction. Do not edit it.
138: - **Cite in-repo evidence only.** A ledger source link that points into a gitignored or machine-local directory is not a citation — nobody else can open it, and the finding can't be re-checked against a later revision. For audit-class sources, republish the cited report into the project's audit-evidence projection (e.g. `docs/audit-evidence/`) and link that path instead (Step 6.4).
139: - **Feature branch only.** All writes happen on a feature branch. Never write directly to the default branch (main/master).
140: - **Infer, don't invent.** If the ledger has no documented format, infer from existing entries and state the inference explicitly in the proposal table. Do not silently impose a format.
141: - **Conservative deduplication.** When unsure if a candidate duplicates an existing entry, flag it rather than silently dropping it. The human sees the flag and decides.
142: - **One skill, one ledger.** Process one target ledger per invocation. If the user wants findings distributed across multiple ledgers, run the skill once per ledger.
143: - **Sweep on the way out.** Keep the open ledger lean: relocate `done` rows to the completed archive as a final step (Step 8) so finished work never bloats the to-do list. Relocation preserves rows verbatim and is reversible, so it's reported, not gated — but creating a new archive *is* gated, like any other write.
144: 
145: ## Relation to other skills
146: 
147: ```text
148: repo-deep-dive / skill-review / audit   →   plan-intake   →   ledger entries
149:                                                                       ↓
150:                                                               orchestrator consumes
151: ```
152: 
153: - **`plan-builder`** creates a plan from scratch. `plan-intake` appends to an existing plan. Use plan-builder when there is no ledger yet; use plan-intake when the ledger already exists.
154: - **`repo-deep-dive`** produces the reports that plan-intake most commonly consumes.
155: - **`skill-review`** produces skill-quality findings that plan-intake can route into a skill-collection ledger.
156: - **`work-item-brief`** produces detailed briefs for individual items; plan-intake creates the items in the ledger that work-item-brief would then expand.

=== skills/workflows/playwright/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: playwright
3: version: 1.6.0
4: description: |
5:   Run browser-based E2E tests, capture screenshots, and validate user flows using Playwright with visible Chrome. Use this skill when testing a web UI end-to-end, capturing screenshots for visual review, checking responsive layouts, or auditing accessibility in a real browser. Trigger on: "e2e test", "screenshot the UI", "click through the app", "responsive layout check", "accessibility audit". Also invoke when qe-agent needs browser-level integration testing.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with: ["qe-agent", "frontend-agent", "deployment-checklist"]
15: spawned_by: ["orchestrator", "qe-agent"]
16: ---
17: 
18: # Playwright
19: 
20: Run browser-based E2E tests with visible Chrome, capture screenshots at each interaction point, and produce structured reports or interactive spot-check sessions.
21: 
22: ## Non-Negotiable Rules
23: 
24: - **UI design and function validation is ALWAYS non-headless.** When the task is validating what a user sees — layout, styling, responsiveness, real rendered content, or that interactions work — the browser must be visible (`headless: false`). Headless runs are only acceptable as fast signal loops for pure logic (diagnose-loop Phase 1 recipes), never as evidence that a UI's design or function is correct.
25: - **Never infer — observe.** If a page's appearance or behavior is in question, load it in the browser and look. Never guess what a page shows from its source code, never assume a fix worked because tests passed, and never judge design from a headless screenshot. The rendered page is ground truth.
26: - **No blind edits.** Screenshots and reports document what exists; they do not authorize changing code without reading it. If a validation failure points at a component, read the component's actual source before concluding anything about it.
27: 
28: ## Two Modes
29: 
30: ### Report Mode (default)
31: 
32: Automated test execution that produces a timestamped run directory with screenshots, a structured JSON report, and a human-readable summary. Use this when running as part of qe-agent's integration phase, CI-adjacent verification, or any time you need a documented record of test results.
33: 
34: ### Spot-Check Mode
35: 
36: Interactive session where the user watches the visible browser and approves each step in real time. Triggered by "let me watch", "spot check", or "walk me through". Pause after each navigation or interaction, describe what's on screen, and wait for the user's go-ahead. Screenshots still land in the timestamped run directory. Full workflow in `references/screenshot-workflow.md`.
37: 
38: ## Setup
39: 
40: Before running any tests, verify Playwright is available. Read `references/setup.md` for the full installation and configuration flow.
41: 
42: Quick check:
43: 
44: ```bash
45: # Check if Playwright is installed
46: npx playwright --version 2>/dev/null || echo "NOT_INSTALLED"
47: ```
48: 
49: If not installed, install it along with the Chromium browser:
50: 
51: ```bash
52: npm init -y 2>/dev/null  # ensure package.json exists
53: npm install -D @playwright/test
54: npx playwright install chromium
55: ```
56: 
57: ## Capture Bounds
58: 
59: Screenshot cost scales with page *height*, not viewport size, and a long page will exhaust system memory before anything warns you. **Measure before you capture:**
60: 
61: ```js
62: const h = await page.evaluate(() => document.documentElement.scrollHeight)
63: ```
64: 
65: Then apply these rules:
66: 
67: - **Under ~8,000px** — `fullPage: true` is fine.
68: - **Over ~8,000px** — do **not** use `fullPage`. Scroll in viewport-sized tiles and capture each one. A list page can be 30,000–50,000px tall; at 1280px wide, 31,000px decodes to ~150 MB of raw RGBA per image.
69: - **Reuse one page per viewport.** Never create a browser context per screenshot — contexts are spawned faster than they are reclaimed.
70: - **Never hold more than one decoded image pair.** Pixel-diffing (`pixelmatch`, `odiff`) needs uncompressed RGBA: two inputs plus an output buffer is *three* copies. On a 31,000px page that is ~460 MB for a single comparison.
71: - **Capture first, diff in a second pass**, freeing each buffer as you go.
72: 
73: Sampling beats exhaustiveness on very long pages: comparing at 25%, 50%, 75%, and bottom catches real regressions at a fraction of the cost. Bound the run before launching it — the failure mode is a stalled machine and lost user work, not a clean error.
74: 
75: ## Workflow
76: 
77: 1. **Determine what to test.** Translate inputs (plan excerpt, acceptance criteria, user request) into a list of user flows. See `references/selectors-guide.md` for how to structure each flow as navigate → interact → assert → screenshot.
78: 
79: 2. **Write the test script** in non-headless Chromium so screenshots show the real rendered UI. See `references/selectors-guide.md` for the standard `test.use({ headless: false })` block, key patterns (`waitForLoadState('networkidle')`, descriptive screenshot names, multi-viewport runs), and the accessibility quick-check snippet.
80: 
81: 3. **Execute the run.** The standard `RUN_ID=$(date +%Y-%m-%d_%H-%M-%S)` command for both `@playwright/test` runner and standalone `npx tsx` execution is in `references/selectors-guide.md`.
82: 
83: 4. **Lay out the outputs.** Everything from a run lives under a single gitignored root: `.playwright/<run-id>/`, with `screenshots/` (PNGs) and `results/` (traces, raw JSON, logs, the test script) beneath it. One root keeps a project's working tree from sprouting `playwright-screenshots/`, `playwright-results/`, `.playwright-mcp/`, and `test-results/` side by side. See `references/screenshot-workflow.md` for the exact directory layout and naming convention.
84: 
85: 5. **Produce the report.** Report mode emits two files at the run-dir top level: `.playwright/<run-id>/report.json` (structured) and `report.md` (human-readable). See `references/screenshot-workflow.md` for the exact JSON schema and markdown template.
86: 
87: ## Coordination with QE Agent
88: 
89: When spawned by qe-agent during Phase 2 (Integration Verification): qe-agent provides base URL + flows + acceptance criteria; you return the run-dir path (`.playwright/<run-id>/`) — its `report.json` plus the `screenshots/` directory; qe-agent incorporates the findings into the overall QA report. Full handoff details — and standalone invocation — are in `references/screenshot-workflow.md`.
90: 
91: ## Troubleshooting
92: 
93: Common issues and fixes are in `references/screenshot-workflow.md`; the full troubleshooting guide is in `references/setup.md`. Quick checklist:
94: 
95: - "Browser closed unexpectedly" → missing system deps; `npx playwright install-deps chromium`
96: - "Navigation timeout" → service isn't running; verify the URL first
97: - "No usable sandbox" (Linux) → `chromiumSandbox: false` (non-production only)
98: - Blank screenshots → add `waitForLoadState('networkidle')` before capture
99: - Machine stalls / swaps during a run → `fullPage` on a very tall page, a context per screenshot, or a pixel-diff holding several decoded images. See "Capture Bounds" above and tile instead.
100: 
101: ## Reference Files
102: 
103: - `references/setup.md` — installation and configuration
104: - `references/selectors-guide.md` — test-script patterns, key Playwright idioms, accessibility quick-check, run commands
105: - `references/screenshot-workflow.md` — directory layout, JSON + Markdown report formats, spot-check workflow, QE handoff, troubleshooting

=== skills/workflows/project-profiler/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: project-profiler
3: version: 1.2.0
4: description: |
5:   Analyze a codebase and generate a project profile (CLAUDE.md + profile.yaml) so agents have shared context. Use when onboarding a new project, generating CLAUDE.md, or profiling tech stack and conventions. Trigger on: "profile this project", "generate CLAUDE.md", "onboard this codebase", "describe this repo", "set up this repo for agents", "scan the codebase for me".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: true
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: ["CLAUDE.md", ".claude/profile.yaml"]
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with: ["skill-writer", "contract-author", "orchestrator"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Project Profiler
19: 
20: Analyze a codebase and generate a project profile that agents can consume. You produce two files: `CLAUDE.md` (human-readable, ≤200 lines) and `.claude/profile.yaml` (machine-readable).
21: 
22: **Pipeline position:** This skill is user-invocable — a user runs it to onboard or describe a project. It is not spawned by the orchestrator. Its output is *consumed by* downstream skills: the orchestrator reads `CLAUDE.md` for project context, deployment-checklist reads it for environment-specific commands and URLs, and dependency-coordinator reads `.claude/profile.yaml` for the tech-stack profile. Think consumed-by, not spawned-by.
23: 
24: ## Role
25: 
26: You are the **project profiler**. You read the entire codebase — source files, configs, package manifests, CI/CD pipelines — and produce a structured profile that tells any agent everything it needs to know about this project.
27: 
28: ## Your Ownership
29: 
30: - **You own (exclusive):** `CLAUDE.md`, `.claude/profile.yaml` (pattern-based ownership)
31: - **Shared read:** All project files (read-only for analysis)
32: - **Off-limits:** `src/`, implementation code (you analyze but never modify)
33: - **Resolved conflict (v1.1):** `CLAUDE.md` was previously claimed by both orchestrator and project-profiler. Project-profiler is the definitive owner — you generate and maintain it. The orchestrator reads it for project context.
34: 
35: ## Inputs
36: 
37: - **Codebase access** — full read access to the project repository
38: - **Existing profile (optional)** — if `CLAUDE.md` or `.claude/profile.yaml` already exist, read them first and update rather than overwrite
39: - **Orchestrator context (optional)** — the orchestrator may provide specific focus areas or questions about the project
40: 
41: ## Process
42: 
43: ### 1. Detect Tech Stack
44: 
45: Scan for indicators:
46: 
47: ```text
48: package.json → Node.js (check for React, Vue, Svelte, Next.js, Express)
49: requirements.txt / pyproject.toml → Python (check for FastAPI, Django, Flask)
50: go.mod → Go
51: Cargo.toml → Rust
52: *.csproj → .NET
53: docker-compose.yml → containerized
54: prisma/ → Prisma ORM
55: alembic/ → SQLAlchemy + Alembic
56: ```
57: 
58: ### 2. Map Directory Structure
59: 
60: Identify which directories contain what:
61: 
62: - API routes/handlers
63: - Business logic/services
64: - Data models
65: - UI components
66: - Tests
67: - Configuration
68: - Documentation
69: 
70: ### 3. Detect Conventions
71: 
72: Scan code for patterns:
73: 
74: - Naming convention (camelCase, snake_case, PascalCase)
75: - Import style (absolute vs relative)
76: - Error handling patterns
77: - Logging approach
78: - Test organization
79: - Linter/formatter configuration
80: 
81: ### 4. Identify Auth Pattern
82: 
83: Look for:
84: 
85: - JWT middleware
86: - Session management
87: - OAuth callbacks
88: - API key validation
89: - Auth provider SDKs (Auth0, Firebase, Azure AD)
90: 
91: ### 5. Map CI/CD
92: 
93: Check for:
94: 
95: - `.github/workflows/` → GitHub Actions
96: - `.gitlab-ci.yml` → GitLab CI
97: - `Jenkinsfile` → Jenkins
98: - Build, test, deploy commands
99: 
100: ### 6. Generate profile.yaml
101: 
102: Follow the schema in `references/profile-schema.yaml`. Fill in every field that can be determined from the codebase. Mark unknowns as `null`.
103: 
104: ### 7. Generate CLAUDE.md
105: 
106: Structure as follows (≤200 lines):
107: 
108: - What This Is (1-2 sentences)
109: - Tech Stack (bullet list)
110: - How to Run (install, dev, test, lint commands)
111: - Directory Map (table with owner agent assignments)
112: - Auth Pattern (1 paragraph)
113: - Coding Conventions (5-10 bullet points)
114: - Do NOT (forbidden patterns)
115: - CI/CD (3-5 lines)
116: - Agent Notes (quirks, active migrations, rate limits)
117: 
118: ## Quality Checklist
119: 
120: - [ ] profile.yaml conforms to the structure in `references/profile-schema.yaml`
121: - [ ] CLAUDE.md is ≤200 lines
122: - [ ] All "How to Run" commands are verified to work
123: - [ ] Directory map matches actual structure
124: - [ ] Tech stack versions are accurate (from lockfiles/manifests)
125: - [ ] No sensitive data (tokens, passwords) in either file

=== skills/workflows/prose-slop-guard/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: prose-slop-guard
3: version: 1.0.0
4: composes_with: ["orchestrator", "docs-agent", "code-review-agent", "git-pr", "wiki-research", "interactive-doc", "llm-wiki", "skill-writer"]
5: description: >-
6:   Source-level gate that catches AI-slop prose ("claudenese") before it ships:
7:   em dashes, AI vocabulary (delve, leverage, seamless, tapestry), negative
8:   parallelism ("it's not X, it's Y"), throat-clearing openers, sycophantic
9:   filler, rule-of-three rhythm, bullet soup. Use whenever writing or reviewing
10:   outward prose — READMEs, docs, announcements, blog posts, PR descriptions,
11:   wiki pages, marketing copy — before committing or declaring a writing task
12:   done, when setting up enforcement so slop can't ship again, or as an
13:   orchestrator/docs-agent wave-gate. Trigger on: "sounds like AI",
14:   "reads as AI-written", "humanize this", "make it sound human", "de-AI this",
15:   "too ChatGPT", "AI tells", "AI slop", "claudenese", "stop writing like a
16:   bot". A deterministic lexical checker plus a judgment rewrite pass; don't
17:   skip it because it "looks fine" — self-grading is the failure mode this
18:   guards.
19: compatibility: Claude Code or any host with Bash; requires Python 3.8+ (stdlib only) for scripts/check_prose_slop.py
20: allowed-tools: ["Bash", "Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep"]
21: ---
22: 
23: # prose-slop-guard
24: 
25: ## The problem this solves
26: 
27: AI-slop prose passes every existing gate. Tests don't read English. Render
28: review checks pixels. Code review checks logic. A doc full of em dashes,
29: "delve", and "it's not X, it's Y" ships green — and readers clock it as
30: machine-written in two sentences, which costs the text its credibility no
31: matter how correct it is.
32: 
33: Worse, the author-model can't self-grade: the same distribution that produced
34: the tells rates them as fine. Community consensus (HN, on anti-slop skills)
35: is blunt about this — filtered output can still read as slop when the filter
36: and the writer are the same pass.
37:
