38: So the fix is the same shape as `design-token-guard` and
39: `class-extraction-guard`: a **source-level gate** with a deterministic layer a
40: hook can run, plus a **fresh-eyes judgment pass** for the tells no regex can
41: catch. The canonical spec is Wikipedia's
42: [Signs of AI writing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing)
43: page — the same ground truth the Humanizer skill family builds on — mirrored
44: and condensed in `references/slop-patterns.md`.
45: 
46: ## Layer 1 — Lexical audit (deterministic)
47: 
48: Run the bundled checker from the project root:
49: 
50: ```bash
51: python3 SKILL_DIR/scripts/check_prose_slop.py --root .
52: ```
53: 
54: What it catches (the mechanical tells):
55: 
56: | rule | catches | default |
57: |---|---|---|
58: | `no-em-dash` | em/en dashes in prose (use ` - `, comma, or parentheses) | error |
59: | `banned-phrases` | throat-clearing openers, "not only... but also", "it is worth noting", "a testament to", "let's dive in", "ever-evolving" | error |
60: | `sycophancy` | "great question", "you're absolutely right", "I hope this helps" | error |
61: | `banned-vocabulary` | delve, leverage, seamless, tapestry, unleash, harness, elevate, landscape, game-changer, paradigm, synergy, empower, streamline, unprecedented, testament, boasts, furthermore... (each with a plain replacement hint) | warn |
62: | `negative-parallelism` | "it's not just...", "isn't merely..." setups | warn |
63: 
64: Scoping is prose-aware: fenced code blocks, inline code, link targets, URLs,
65: YAML frontmatter, and blockquotes are skipped (quoted content keeps its
66: author's punctuation — an em dash inside a quote is not your finding).
67: 
68: Useful flags:
69: - `--staged` — only git-staged files (what the pre-commit hook runs).
70: - `--stdin` — pipe a commit message or PR body through the same rules:
71:   `git log -1 --format=%B | python3 .../check_prose_slop.py --stdin`
72: - `--json` — `{ ok, summary, findings }` for a gate/CI to parse.
73: - `PATHS…` — limit to the files you just wrote.
74: 
75: Exit code `1` on error-severity findings, `0` when clean — drops straight into
76: a hook or CI step.
77: 
78: ## Layer 2 — Judgment pass (model, fresh eyes)
79: 
80: The regex layer cannot see rhythm, structure, or voice. After (or instead of,
81: for a quick review) the lexical audit, read `references/slop-patterns.md` and
82: sweep the text for the judgment-level categories:
83: 
84: - **Rule-of-three rhythm** — triplet lists as a crutch ("fast, simple, and
85:   powerful"). One is fine; a pattern is a tell.
86: - **Bullet soup / formatting inflation** — bullets, bold, and headers where a
87:   paragraph would carry the argument. Structure should encode information, not
88:   decorate it.
89: - **Vague attribution** — "experts say", "studies show", "many users report"
90:   with no named source. Name it or cut it.
91: - **Inflated symbolism** — "stands as", "serves as a reminder", "underscores
92:   the importance of". State the fact; skip the ceremony.
93: - **Superficial -ing analysis** — trailing participial glosses ("...,
94:   highlighting the need for...", "..., showcasing its versatility").
95: - **Hedged everything-ness** — "whether you're a beginner or an expert".
96: - **Sycophantic or performative openers/closers** in context the regexes miss.
97: 
98: Rewrite toward the require-list: short active sentences with varied length,
99: direct "you/your" address, concrete examples over abstractions, specific
100: attribution, direct claims with evidence, prose paragraphs by default.
101: 
102: **Who runs the judgment pass matters.** If you wrote the text this session, do
103: not grade it yourself — dispatch a fresh-context reviewer (a subagent that gets
104: only the text plus `references/slop-patterns.md`, not your drafting context) or
105: run the lexical checker plus a self-sweep and say plainly that the judgment
106: pass was self-graded. This is the same evaluator principle as
107: `loop-controller`'s fresh-context evaluator: the writer's context contaminates
108: the grade.
109: 
110: ## Interpret and fix
111: 
112: Two kinds of findings, different fixes — don't blur them:
113: 
114: - **Mechanical** (em dash, banned word, banned phrase): replace per the hint.
115:   Safe, fast, no meaning change.
116: - **Judgment** (rule of three, vague attribution, bullet soup): these usually
117:   signal a missing fact, not a wrong word. "Studies show" means *go find the
118:   study or delete the claim*. A triplet means *pick the one that matters*.
119:   Rewriting the surface while keeping the emptiness is how "humanized" text
120:   still reads as slop.
121: 
122: **Legitimate exceptions** — leave these alone and, where recurring, encode them
123: in config: quoted material (already exempt), upstream changelog text,
124: vocabulary that is a real term of art in the domain ("harness" in an agent
125: harness, "landscape" in ecology), and any project whose house style explicitly
126: allows em dashes (`"rules": {"no-em-dash": "warn"}`). The guard serves the
127: project's voice; it does not impose one.
128: 
129: ## Enforce (scaffold into the repo)
130: 
131: A one-time cleanup doesn't stop regression — the next generated README
132: reintroduces everything. Wire the gate in:
133: 
134: 1. **Write `.prose-guard.json`** at the repo root (template:
135:    `assets/prose-guard.config.json`). Tune rules, add domain terms to
136:    `allowVocabulary`, scope `extensions`/`skipPaths`.
137: 2. **Add a script entry** — `"lint:prose": "python3 scripts/check_prose_slop.py --root ."`
138:    (copy the checker into the project's `scripts/` or reference the skill path).
139: 3. **Pre-commit hook** — run `check_prose_slop.py --staged`; block on errors.
140:    Wire via Husky/lefthook if present, else a plain `.git/hooks/pre-commit`.
141: 4. **CI step** — run `lint:prose` next to lint/typecheck.
142: 5. **Standing style rules** — for repos where agents generate prose routinely,
143:    add the CLAUDE.md block from `references/wiring-into-agents.md` so the rules
144:    apply at generation time, not just at gate time. Prevention beats filtering.
145: 
146: Tell the user which layers you installed and which you skipped.
147: 
148: ## Using it as an agent / orchestrator gate
149: 
150: - A **docs-agent** (or any agent producing READMEs, docs, PR bodies) runs the
151:   checker with `--json` against the files it changed *before* reporting done;
152:   error findings mean the task isn't done.
153: - The **orchestrator** runs it at the wave gate alongside typecheck/test;
154:   non-zero `summary.errors` blocks the wave and routes back to the owning
155:   agent.
156: - **git-pr** pipes the drafted PR body through `--stdin` before `gh pr create`.
157: - The **judgment pass** at gate time goes to a fresh-context reviewer, never
158:   the agent that wrote the text.
159: 
160: Exact snippets: `references/wiring-into-agents.md`.
161: 
162: ## Reference files
163: 
164: - `references/slop-patterns.md` — the full pattern catalog by category
165:   (Vocabulary, Phrases, Structure, Voice, Sycophancy, Rhythm, Specificity,
166:   Formatting), each with why it reads as slop and what to write instead;
167:   provenance and upstream sources.
168: - `references/wiring-into-agents.md` — agent self-check, orchestrator
169:   wave-gate, git-pr `--stdin` check, the fresh-context evaluator dispatch, and
170:   the copy-paste CLAUDE.md standing-rules block.

=== skills/workflows/railway-deploy/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: railway-deploy
3: version: 1.3.1
4: description: >
5:   Deploy projects to Railway — Dockerfile creation, railway.toml config, environment variables,
6:   multi-service setups (web + worker), and deployment via CLI or GraphQL API. Trigger on "deploy to
7:   Railway", "ship it on Railway", or "set up Railway hosting", and on questions about Railway
8:   configuration, health checks, deployment status, or env var management.
9: requires_agent_teams: false
10: requires_claude_code: false
11: min_plan: starter
12: owns:
13:   directories: []
14:   patterns: ["railway.toml"]
15:   shared_read: ["*"]
16: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
17: composes_with: ["infrastructure-agent", "deployment-checklist"]
18: spawned_by: []
19: ---
20: 
21: # Railway Deployment
22: 
23: Deploy projects to Railway with proper Dockerfile, config, and multi-environment support.
24: 
25: ## How Railway Works
26: 
27: Railway builds and runs your app from a Dockerfile (or auto-detects with Nixpacks). Each project can have multiple **services** (web, worker, cron) across multiple **environments** (dev, production). Railway assigns a dynamic `PORT` env var that your app must listen on.
28: 
29: ## Prerequisites
30: 
31: **Railway credentials** (for GraphQL API deployments) go in the current project's repo-root `.env` file — see `.env.example` for the full list. Get your API token at https://railway.app/account/tokens.
32: 
33: **Railway CLI** should be installed and authenticated. Verify:
34: 
35: ```bash
36: railway --version    # Should show v4.x+
37: railway whoami       # Should show logged-in user
38: ```
39: 
40: If not installed: `brew install railway` (macOS) or `npm i -g @railway/cli`, then `railway login`.
41: 
42: ## Deployment Approaches
43: 
44: Railway supports two deployment methods. Choose based on the project's needs:
45: 
46: ### Approach 1: Railway CLI (simple projects)
47: 
48: Best for quick deploys, single-service apps, and projects where you want Railway to auto-detect the build.
49: 
50: ```bash
51: # Link to existing project (or create new)
52: railway link          # Interactive — select project + environment
53: # OR
54: railway init          # Create new project
55: 
56: # Deploy
57: railway up            # Builds and deploys from current directory
58: 
59: # Check status
60: railway status
61: railway logs
62: ```
63: 
64: ### Approach 2: GraphQL API + Deploy Script (multi-service, CI/CD)
65: 
66: Best for projects with multiple services (web + worker), automated deployments, or when you need programmatic control. See `references/deploy-script.md` for a ready-to-use deploy script pattern.
67: 
68: ## Setting Up a New Project for Railway
69: 
70: 1. **Detect the stack.** Read `package.json` / `requirements.txt` / `Cargo.toml` / `go.mod` to identify language, framework, entry point, and system deps. See `references/dockerfile-recipes.md` for the full stack detection checklist.
71: 
72: 2. **Create the Dockerfile.** Pick the recipe matching your stack — `references/dockerfile-recipes.md` has Python/FastAPI, Node.js/Express/Next.js, and Astro templates, plus Dockerfile best practices.
73: 
74: 3. **Create `railway.toml`.** The standard template with `startCommand`, `healthcheckPath`, `restartPolicyType` is in `references/dockerfile-recipes.md`.
75: 
76: 4. **Create `.dockerignore`.** Exclude dev deps, local DBs, build artifacts. Template in `references/dockerfile-recipes.md`.
77: 
78: 5. **Add a health check endpoint.** Railway uses it to know when the app is ready. Python/FastAPI and Node/Express snippets are in `references/dockerfile-recipes.md`.
79: 
80: > **Optional fallback:** Railway prefers Dockerfile but falls back to a Procfile if present — only relevant when you can't ship a Dockerfile. See the Procfile section in `references/dockerfile-recipes.md`.
81: 
82: ## Environment Variables, Multi-Service, Troubleshooting
83: 
84: For env var management (`railway variables set`), multi-service setups (web + worker on the same project), the full deployment checklist, and troubleshooting common deploy failures (build errors, crash loops, health check timeouts, 502 errors, port binding), read `references/multi-service-setup.md`.
85: 
86: ## Reference Files
87: 
88: - `references/dockerfile-recipes.md` — per-language Dockerfile templates (Python, Node, Astro), railway.toml, .dockerignore, health check endpoints, Procfile
89: - `references/multi-service-setup.md` — env vars, multi-service (web + worker), deployment checklist, troubleshooting
90: - `references/deploy-script.md` — Python deploy script pattern using Railway's GraphQL API

=== skills/workflows/render-sanity/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: render-sanity
3: version: 1.2.0
4: description: |
5:   Lightweight visual sanity check that catches failure modes passing
6:   "tests green + dev server boots + 0 console errors" but visibly breaking
7:   the app when a human clicks — stale mock IDs on "live" pages, lone `?` /
8:   `—` / `undefined` / `Loading…` where data should be, dead links from
9:   list pages, and "Couldn't load X / Unauthorized" dead-end shells. Runs
10:   four objective checks BEFORE the build is declared done: smell scan,
11:   click-through every list, signed-out matrix, signed-in matrix. Use when
12:   a build is wrapping up, after ux-review or qe-agent finishes, when the
13:   user says "is it actually working", "broken pages", "dead links", or
14:   pastes a screenshot showing `?`, `undefined`, or a "Not found" page.
15:   Also invoke proactively when a frontend agent rewires mocks → real
16:   backend, when auth was added, or when seed data is regenerated.
17: compatibility: Claude Code; requires Playwright MCP tools
18: requires_claude_code: true
19: requires_agent_teams: false
20: min_plan: starter
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep",
22:   "mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__browser_navigate",
23:   "mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__browser_snapshot",
24:   "mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__browser_click",
25:   "mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__browser_evaluate",
26:   "mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__browser_console_messages",
27:   "mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__browser_network_requests"]
28: owns:
29:   directories: []
30:   patterns: []
31:   shared_read: ["*"]
32: composes_with: ["ux-review", "orchestrator", "frontend-agent", "feature-dev:feature-dev", "playwright"]
33: spawned_by: ["orchestrator", "ux-review"]
34: ---
35: 
36: # Render Sanity
37: 
38: > **Why this exists:** "Tests pass, dev server boots, console is clean" is a process bar. It's not the same as "the app works." This skill is the missing semantic check between those two — it catches failure modes that render plausibly but are quietly broken.
39: 
40: This skill is **not** subjective. It does not evaluate visual hierarchy, typography, or polish — those are `ux-review`'s job. It hunts four specific, objectively-verifiable failure modes that ship past every other gate.
41: 
42: **Announce at start:** "Using render-sanity to click through [N routes] and check for stale data, placeholder text, dead links, and auth dead-ends."
43: 
44: ## Non-Negotiable Rules
45: 
46: - **UI design and function validation is ALWAYS non-headless.** These checks exist because "the app renders" is not "the app works" — so they must be run in a **visible** Playwright browser. Never run the route passes in headless mode, never validate via curl/source-reading, and never skip the browser because the smoke tests passed.
47: - **Never infer — observe.** Every finding must be a route you actually navigated and content you actually saw in the browser. If you haven't clicked it, you haven't verified it. Do not guess what a page renders from its component source, from the router, or from a passing test suite.
48: - **No blind edits.** render-sanity is read-only — it reports failures, it does not fix them. When a finding points at a component, read the actual source before describing the cause; don't hypothesize from the symptom.
49: 
50: ## The Four Checks
51: 
52: Each produces concrete file/route/text evidence — not "feels off."
53: 
54: ### Check 1 — Visible-text smell scan
55: 
56: The app may render. The text it renders may be garbage. For every page in the sitemap, grab `document.body.innerText` and grep for known smell patterns (lone `?`, persistent `Loading…`, `undefined`, `Couldn't load`, repeated generic fallbacks, leaked mock IDs).
57: 
58: The full pattern table — universal smells plus how to derive project-specific patterns from the project's mocks/fixtures/seed files — lives in `references/smell-patterns.md`. Read it before running this check.
59: 
60: ### Check 2 — Click-through every list
61: 
62: Pages that render a list of links (feed, catalog, search results, dashboard rows, recent items, leaderboards, threads, files — anything iterating a collection into `<a>` tags) are the most common silent-failure surface. The list renders fine; the items link to dead targets.
63: 
64: For every list page:
65: 
66: 1. Snapshot the page.
67: 2. Identify the first list-item link (`<a>` inside the card / row / item container).
68: 3. Read its `href`.
69: 4. Navigate to that href.
70: 5. Confirm the destination renders real content — not a generic 404, not the project's missing-resource page, not an empty shell.
71: 
72: Clicking the first item is sufficient to catch the systemic "all our list IDs are stale mocks" bug. If the first works and you have time, sample one from the middle and one from the end. This catches "every link in the list is dead because the data source is wrong," not "this one particular item happens to be missing."
73: 
74: ### Check 3 — Signed-out matrix
75: 
76: For every route, navigate with no session and record the outcome:
77: 
78: | Outcome | Verdict |
79: |---|---|
80: | Public page renders real content | Pass |
81: | Redirect to `/login` (or equivalent) | Pass |
82: | Empty shell with persistent "Couldn't load X · Unauthorized" / "Failed to fetch" / blank ledger / `—` everywhere | **Critical** — pick one: gate with a real auth wall (redirect) OR fall back to a public read-only view. A dead-end-but-still-rendered page is the worst of both. |
83: | Console errors but no visible error state | Critical — the fetch is throwing, the page is silently broken |
84: | 500 / unhandled exception in network log | Critical — server bug, not UX |
85: 
86: ### Check 4 — Signed-in matrix
87: 
88: If the project has any way to log in (seed creds, demo button, OAuth with a test account, magic-link in dev), sign in **once** with a known seeded user and re-walk every auth-gated route.
89: 
90: What to verify:
91: 
92: - **User-scoped data views** (profile, account, balance, inbox, dashboard, "your X" pages) must reflect WHO is signed in. If the seed gives this user known activity, the page must show it. "Logged in but the page is empty / zeroed / generic" when the seed says otherwise = Critical.
93: - **User-scoped lists** (your items, followers, conversations, orders) must show entries belonging to this user. Wrong user's data or "0 items" when the seed says otherwise = Critical.
94: - **Counterparty / participant labels** (the other end of any two-party relationship — thread participant, task assignee, post author, resource owner) must resolve to real names/handles — not the generic-fallback label from the placeholder vocabulary captured in Check 1.
95: - **Empty states are FINE** when the seed legitimately has no data for this user. The bar is coherence: "Follow some people to see activity here" is good; a 401-shaped error on an authed page is bad.
96: 
97: **Finding seed credentials.** Read seed/fixture files (`db/seed.*`, `fixtures/`, `scripts/seed.*`, `prisma/seed.*`, `factories/`, `.env.example`, README "Demo accounts" sections). If the project ships a LoginPage with hardcoded demo defaults or a "demo" button, use them — but verify they work against the running auth endpoint first (a quick curl POST is cheaper than discovering it at click-time).
98: 
99: **If there is no way to sign in** — that itself is a Critical. File as "Cannot enter the app as any user — sign-in path is broken or undocumented." Check 4 cannot be performed without one.
100: 
101: **If the project has roles** (admin/member, buyer/seller, teacher/student), sign in as at least one user per role with distinct UI affordances. The "admin sees nothing where a regular user sees a dashboard" case is real.
102: 
103: ## Workflow
104: 
105: ### Step 1 — Build the route inventory
106: 
107: Read the router file (`App.tsx`, `app/`, `pages/`, `src/routes/`, etc.) and write down every route. Mark each as **public**, **auth-gated**, or **role-gated** based on `<RequireAuth>` / `requireAuth` / middleware patterns. Do this from code, not by clicking — a route in the router but not linked from any nav still counts.
108: 
109: If the inventory is large (>20 routes), prioritize:
110: 
111: 1. Routes linked from the navbar (highest signal — a real user lands here)
112: 2. Routes that render lists or accept `:id` params (highest bug density)
113: 3. Auth-gated routes (highest "looks fine but silently broken" risk)
114: 
115: ### Step 2 — Confirm the dev stack is actually up
116: 
117: ```bash
118: # Probe common dev ports (extend if the project uses something exotic)
119: for p in 3000 3001 4000 4321 5173 8000 8080; do
120:   if lsof -i :$p -t > /dev/null 2>&1; then
121:     echo "Port $p is listening"
122:   fi
123: done
124: # Hit the URL and confirm 200
125: curl -fsS http://localhost:<port>/ > /dev/null && echo "Frontend responsive"
126: ```
127: 
128: If nothing is listening, **stop**. Don't run render-sanity against a dead port and call it a pass. Either bring up the stack (`pnpm dev` / `npm run dev` from the project root, or whatever the workspace's `dev` script is) or report "Cannot run — dev server not responding."
129: 
130: ### Step 3 — Run the four checks
131: 
132: For each route in the inventory:
133: 
134: 1. Navigate (Playwright)
135: 2. Snapshot + console + network
136: 3. Run Check 1 (smell scan) — uses patterns from `references/smell-patterns.md`
137: 4. If the page renders a list, run Check 2 (click first item, verify destination)
138: 5. Record outcome in the signed-out matrix (Check 3)
139: 
140: Then sign in as a seed user (or hit the demo button) and re-walk auth-gated routes for Check 4.
141: 
142: ### Step 4 — Write the report
143: 
144: Save to `docs/render-sanity-YYYY-MM-DD.md` using the template in `references/report-template.md`. The template's structure is fixed so a reviewer can scan any render-sanity report and find the same sections in the same order.
145: 
146: ### Step 5 — Decide pass/fail
147: 
148: - **PASS**: zero critical findings across all four checks.
149: - **FAIL**: one or more critical findings. The report names them; the build cannot be declared done until they're fixed and render-sanity is re-run.
150: 
151: A FAIL is a gate, not a recommendation. The orchestrator's Definition of Done depends on render-sanity returning PASS on a UI build.
152: 
153: ## What this skill is NOT
154: 
155: - **Not visual review.** "The spacing feels off" / "the gradient is harsh" — those belong to `ux-review`. This skill has no opinion about aesthetics.
156: - **Not accessibility audit.** Heading hierarchy, ARIA labels, keyboard nav — `ux-review` or a11y tooling.
157: - **Not performance.** Bundle size, LCP, hydration — `performance-agent`.
158: - **Not contract conformance.** Whether the API matches the OpenAPI spec — `qe-agent` / `contract-auditor`.
159: - **Not test coverage.** Whether the unit tests cover this code — `qe-agent`.
160: 
161: This skill catches one specific failure mode: **the app renders, but renders broken content that humans can see and machines couldn't tell from the test suite alone.** Keep it focused.
162: 
163: ## When invoked by other skills
164: 
165: - **`orchestrator`** is the primary invoker. It calls render-sanity during post-build verification, BEFORE `ux-review` — render-sanity catches broken-content failures; ux-review then assesses polish on a known-good shell. A render-sanity FAIL blocks the build's Definition of Done.
166: - **`ux-review`** MAY invoke render-sanity as a precondition. When it does, the render-sanity report becomes the "Critical Issues" section of the ux-review report.
167: - **`feature-dev:feature-dev`** SHOULD invoke render-sanity after a feature is wired end-to-end, before declaring "the feature works."
168: - **The user** can invoke this skill directly any time they want a fast objective answer to "is the UI actually working" — typically after a build claims done, after a refactor, after auth was added, or after seeing a screenshot with `?` / `Couldn't load` / dead links.
169: 
170: ## Key principles
171: 
172: - **Concrete evidence beats subjective judgment.** Every finding is a tuple of `(route, pattern, matched text)` or `(source list, first link, destination, outcome)`. No "feels broken."
173: - **Click, don't just look.** Lists that render but link to nowhere are this skill's primary catch. Snapshots and screenshots don't catch them. Clicking does.
174: - **Both auth states.** "It works when I'm logged in" is half a test. "It works when I'm signed out" is the other half. A skill that only walks one state misses the half its build session happened to be in.
175: - **Treat mock-ID leakage as a P0.** The "frontend imports mocks.ts directly into a 'live' page" bug class is silent, common, and embarrassing. A mock ID on a live page = "page is wired to fake data" Critical, not a polish item.
176: - **Refuse to pass a dead stack.** If the dev server isn't listening, this skill must not say "passed." Either bring it up or report that you couldn't run.
177: 
178: ## Reference files
179: 
180: - `references/smell-patterns.md` — Check 1's universal smell pattern table plus project-specific derivation guidance
181: - `references/report-template.md` — the markdown skeleton for Step 4's report + pass/fail decision rule

=== skills/workflows/repo-deep-dive/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: repo-deep-dive
3: version: 1.5.1
4: description: >-
5:   Perform a comprehensive technical deep dive on an open-source repository, combining
6:   a deep-research markdown document with hands-on codebase analysis to produce a structured
7:   12-14 document reference series. Use this skill whenever the user wants to deeply analyze
8:   a repo, do a deep dive on a project, reverse-engineer a codebase, create a technical
9:   reference for an open-source tool, understand how a project works inside and out, or
10:   compare another project's architecture against a reference project. Also trigger when the
11:   user mentions "deep dive", "deep research", "analyze this repo", "break down this codebase",
12:   "technical reference", "how does this project work", or has a deep-research markdown
13:   alongside a cloned repo ready for analysis.
14: requires_agent_teams: false
15: requires_claude_code: true
16: min_plan: starter
17: owns:
18:   directories: []
19:   patterns: []
20:   shared_read: ["*"]
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep", "Agent"]
22: composes_with: ["project-profiler", "plan-builder", "mermaid-charts", "wiki-research", "llm-wiki"]
23: spawned_by: []
24: ---
25: 
26: # Repo Deep Dive
27: 
28: > **Tradeoff:** Biases toward exhaustive documentation. For quick familiarization, use Explore subagent or grep instead.
29: 
30: Turn a Claude Deep Research document and a locally cloned repository into a comprehensive, structured technical reference — the kind of document set that lets someone understand a 100k+ LoC codebase in an afternoon.
31: 
32: ## First: the repo you're in IS the target — dive in, don't go hunting
33: 
34: You were launched in a specific working directory for a reason: that repo is the one to deep-dive. Read that context **before asking a single question** — interrogating the user for what you could have read yourself is the friction to avoid, and interrogating the *filesystem* to guess is worse.
35: 
36: - **Check the working directory: run `git rev-parse --show-toplevel`.** If it succeeds, that repo is the target. Words like "this", "this repo", "this codebase", "the lessons in this", "another repo from `<person>`", or no explicit target at all — all mean *the repo you're in*. Take it literally and proceed.
37: - **State the assumption in one line and keep going** — e.g. "Deep-diving the repo you're in: `<name>` — tell me if you meant a different one." A single correctable statement respects the user's time; a blocking multiple-choice menu does not.
38: - **Never scan the filesystem to guess a target.** If the user's words seem to gesture at some other repo but give you no path, that is *not* license to `find`/`ls`/`git log` across their other repos hunting for a match — that machine-interrogation is the same friction as menu-interrogation, and it burns a turn guessing. You have exactly two resolutions: the repo you're in, or a *specific* path/URL the user actually named. Nothing concrete to resolve means nothing to hunt for.
39: - **Only one situation earns a question:** the user names a *different* project you cannot resolve to a path, or the working directory isn't a git repo at all. Then ask **once** — a single direct question with the current repo pre-filled as the default — rather than going and guessing.
40: - **"Compare against X" / "what can Y learn from this" is a lens, not a second target to find.** A deep dive may compare the target against a reference project, but that reference is the *framing goal*, not a repo you go looking for — and it never demotes the repo you're in to "the reference" and sends you elsewhere for "the real target." The repo you're in is the target; the comparison is what you do with it.
41: 
42: The rule of thumb: the current repo is the target — state that assumption and proceed. Reserve a question for the one situation above; never a filesystem scavenger hunt.
43: 
44: ## What You Need
45: 
46: 1. **A deep-research document** — markdown from any deep-research session about the project (Claude Deep Research, ChatGPT Deep Research, Gemini Deep Research, or a hand-written brief). This provides landscape context, community perspective, and high-level understanding that code analysis alone can't give you.
47: 
48: 2. **A locally cloned repo** — the actual source code to trace, measure, and analyze. **Default to the repo you're invoked in** (see the section above); only ask when the target is genuinely elsewhere.
49: 
50: 3. **An output directory** — where the deep dive's document series should land. There is no built-in default — naming the location explicitly keeps deep dives from accumulating in a folder the user forgets about. **But don't just ask blind: first run vault detection (Phase 0).** Many users keep a dedicated deep-research / Obsidian "second-brain" vault with an established convention for where deep dives live. If one exists, offer it as the *recommended* target rather than asking from scratch. You still ask — detection sets a smart default, it doesn't override the user.
51: 
52: The deep-research document is critical — it grounds the analysis in why the project exists and where it sits in the landscape, not just what the code does. **But look before you ask:** check whether one already exists — scan the repo's own `docs/` and any detected vault's research/sources area (see Phase 0) for a matching markdown brief. Ask the user only if none turns up. If the user doesn't have one, suggest they run a deep-research session first (it takes ~5 minutes and dramatically improves the output quality).
53: 
54: ## The Process
55: 
56: Six phases (Phase 0 and Phase 5 are conditional). Read `references/phases.md` for the detailed instructions and code snippets, and `references/vault-integration.md` for the Phase 0 detection heuristic + the Phase 5 wiki page schema.
57: 
58: 0. **Phase 0 — Locate the vault (before asking for output_dir).** Detect whether the user keeps a deep-research / Obsidian knowledge vault. If so, propose its convention as the recommended output target. See `references/vault-integration.md`.
59: 1. **Phase 1 — Orient.** Read the deep-research doc, extract claims, gather hard numbers from the repo (LoC, commits, contributors).
60: 2. **Phase 2 — Map the Architecture.** Trace from entry points inward. Use parallel subagents to explore subsystems concurrently. Produce a mermaid architecture diagram.
61: 3. **Phase 3 — Deep Dive Each Subsystem.** One focused document per major subsystem (6-10 typical). Read the actual code, not just docs.
62: 4. **Phase 4 — Compare and Assess.** Comparison + convergence/frontier documents. This is the strategic payoff.
63: 5. **Phase 5 — Wiki integration (only if the target is a knowledge vault).** Synthesize the deep dive into the vault's wiki layer — source page, entity/concept pages, a comparison page — and update the vault's index/log per its own conventions. This is what makes `composes_with: llm-wiki` real. See `references/vault-integration.md`.
64: 
65: ## Output Structure
66: 
67: All output goes in `{output_dir}/{project}_deepdive/source-material/`.
68: 
69: `{output_dir}` is required. Phase 0 detection sets a recommended default (a detected vault's deep-dive convention); otherwise the user supplies it. Either way, confirm the location before generating any files — silently dropping a 12-document series into a location the user didn't confirm wastes their time finding it later. **If the target is a detected vault, also run Phase 5** to integrate the series into its wiki layer rather than leaving it as an unlinked island.
70: 
71: ### Document Progression
72: 
73: Read `references/document-template.md` for the full template with per-document guidance.
74: 
75: The consistent structure across all deep dives:
76: 
77: | # | Document | Purpose |
78: |---|----------|---------|
79: | 00 | INDEX.md | Table of contents, generation metadata, reading guide |
80: | 01 | project-overview.md | What it is, by the numbers, landscape position |
81: | 02 | architecture.md | High-level system design, layers, key decisions |
82: | 03-09 | [subsystem docs] | Deep technical dives — one per major subsystem |
83: | 10+ | comparison.md | How it compares to a reference project and related tools |
84: | 11+ | convergence-analysis.md | What each project has that the other lacks |
85: | 12+ | frontier-assessment.md | What's novel, what's table stakes, what to build |
86: 
87: The exact number and naming of subsystem docs (03-09) varies by project. A project with a complex agent system gets `agent-system.md`. A project with a custom database gets `storage-engine.md`. Name them for what they cover, not by a fixed template.
88: 
89: ### Document Quality Standards
90: 
91: - **80-300 lines per document** — long enough to be thorough, short enough to read in one sitting
92: - **Code paths, not code dumps** — reference specific files and functions, don't paste large blocks
93: - **"By the numbers" tables** — readers love hard data, give them precise counts
94: - **Mermaid architecture diagrams** — use the `mermaid-charts` skill for flowcharts, sequence diagrams, and system maps instead of ASCII art. Prefer `flowchart TB` with subgraphs for layer-cake architectures, `sequenceDiagram` for request flows
95: - **Cross-references** — link between documents when one subsystem touches another
96: - **Honest assessments** — note gaps, limitations, and production readiness issues candidly
97: 
98: ## Parallelization, Scope, and Working With the Research Doc
99: 
100: Read `references/parallelization.md` for: the subagent strategy across phases, how to adapt scope to codebase size (6-8 / 10-12 / 12-14 docs), how to reconcile contradictions between the research doc and the code, and what makes a great deep dive vs a mediocre one.
101: 
102: ## Anti-Pattern
103: 
104: > **Forbidden:** Generating a deep dive into an unconfirmed location. Phase 0 may *propose* a detected vault as the default, but you still confirm before writing. Silently dropping a 12-doc series anywhere — default folder or detected vault — without confirmation wastes the user's time.
105: 
106: > **Forbidden:** Dropping the series into a detected knowledge vault but skipping Phase 5. An unlinked deep-dive folder inside a wiki is an orphan — if the target is a vault, integrate it (source/entity/concept/comparison pages + index + log) per the vault's own conventions.
107: 
108: ## Final step: feed findings into the living plan
109: 
110: After producing the reference series, if the target project has a living plan/ledger
111: (look for `START-HERE.md`, `BUILD-PLAN.md`, or `docs/REMAINING-WORK.md`), invoke the
112: `plan-intake` skill on the integration/gap findings so they become tracked entries
113: rather than a static report. Skip only if the project has no ledger.
114: (For skill-creator / skill-review reports, run `plan-intake` manually on the report — same loop.)
115: 
116: ## Reference Files
117: 
118: - `references/phases.md` — detailed instructions for Phases 1-4 with measurement commands and subagent dispatch patterns
119: - `references/document-template.md` — full per-document template (INDEX, overview, architecture, subsystem skeletons, comparison, convergence, frontier)
120: - `references/parallelization.md` — subagent strategy, scope adaptation, reconciling research vs code, hallmarks of a great deep dive
121: - `references/vault-integration.md` — Phase 0 vault-detection heuristic + Phase 5 wiki integration (page schema, index/log updates)

=== skills/workflows/settings-consolidator/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: settings-consolidator
3: version: 1.3.0
4: description: >
5:   Scan every .claude/settings.local.json across the user's home directory,
6:   deduplicate permissions, collapse supersets, and merge into the global
7:   ~/.claude/settings.local.json with a categorized report. Also bootstraps
8:   permissions for autonomous/unattended sessions using a 350+ command baseline
9:   (shell operators, Claude tools, safety deny list). Trigger on "consolidate
10:   settings", "merge permissions", "stop prompting me", "bootstrap permissions",
11:   "autonomous mode", "unattended session", "overnight build setup", "I want
12:   to go to sleep and let this run", or upgrading colon-wildcards to
13:   space-wildcards across Claude Code permissions.
14: requires_agent_teams: false
15: requires_claude_code: true
16: min_plan: starter
17: owns:
18:   directories: []
19:   patterns: ["settings.local.json"]
20:   shared_read: ["~/.claude/", "**/.claude/"]
21: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob"]
22: composes_with: ["sync-skills"]
23: spawned_by: []
24: ---
25: 
26: # Settings Consolidator
27: 
28: Scan every `.claude/settings.local.json` across your home directory, deduplicate the permissions, collapse supersets, and merge the result into your global `~/.claude/settings.local.json`. This saves you from clicking "allow" on the same commands over and over as you move between projects. The output is a categorized report showing exactly what was merged, what was collapsed, and what was flagged as project-specific.
29: 
30: ## Bootstrap Mode
31: 
32: When the user wants autonomous sessions — "stop prompting me", "I want to go to sleep and let this run", "bootstrap permissions", "set up for unattended builds", or "I keep getting permission prompts" — use this workflow instead of (or before) the consolidation workflow.
33: 
34: ### Why Sessions Get Interrupted
35: 
36: Permission prompts in long-running sessions come from three gaps, in order of how often they bite:
37: 
38: 1. **Missing shell operators.** Commands like `git log --oneline | head -5` or `npm test && npm run build` use pipes, chains, and redirects. Without `Bash(* | *)`, `Bash(* && *)`, `Bash(* > *)`, etc., every compound command prompts. This is the #1 cause of interruptions and the one most people miss entirely.
39: 
40: 2. **Missing Claude tool permissions.** Spawning subagents (`Agent(*)`), editing files (`Edit(**)`), entering plan mode (`EnterPlanMode(*)`), using worktrees (`EnterWorktree(*)`), or managing tasks (`Task(**)`) all need explicit permissions. Without them, multi-agent and orchestrated workflows stall.
41: 
42: 3. **Colon-wildcard gaps.** `Bash(git:*)` is narrower than `Bash(git *)`. Claude Code generates colon-wildcards when a user clicks "allow" on a specific invocation, but space-wildcards are strictly broader and catch command variations that colon-wildcards miss. Always prefer `Bash(cmd *)` over `Bash(cmd:*)`.
43: 
44: ### Bootstrap Workflow
45: 
46: 1. **Back up.** Copy `~/.claude/settings.local.json` to `~/.claude/settings.local.json.bak.YYYY-MM-DD-HHMMSS`.
47: 
48: 2. **Load the baseline.** Read `references/autonomous-permissions.json` — a comprehensive template with ~350 pre-approved commands (all using space-wildcards), all shell operators, all Claude Code tool permissions, and a safety deny list.
49: 
50: 3. **Merge.** Add baseline permissions to the existing global settings, deduplicating against what's already there. Don't remove existing permissions — the baseline is additive.
51: 
52: 4. **Upgrade colon-wildcards.** Scan the merged result for `Bash(cmd:*)` entries. For each one, if a `Bash(cmd *)` (space-wildcard) entry exists in the baseline or the merged result, drop the colon-wildcard — the space-wildcard already covers it.
53: 
54: 5. **Add MCP permissions.** Check `~/.claude/settings.json` for `enabledPlugins`. For each enabled plugin, add its tool permissions. Common patterns:
55:    - Playwright: all `mcp__plugin_playwright_playwright__browser_*` tools
56:    - Other MCP servers: `mcp__<server>__<tool>` format
57: 
58: 6. **Report.** Show a summary: how many permissions added, how many colon-wildcards upgraded, the deny list, and any MCP tools added.
59: 
60: After bootstrapping, optionally run the consolidation workflow below to merge in any project-specific permissions on top.
61: 
62: ## Permission Syntax and Categorization
63: 
64: Claude Code settings use three Bash permission syntaxes (space-wildcard, colon-wildcard, literal) with strict collapsing rules. The category scheme used in the report (File Ops, Git & GitHub, Package Managers, Network, etc.) and the rules for collapsing supersets, detecting literal one-offs, and flagging project-specific entries live in `references/permission-categories.md`.
65: 
66: ## Consolidation Workflow
67: 
68: ### Step 1: Scan
69: 
70: Run:
71: 
72: ```bash
73: find ~ -name "settings.local.json" -path "*/.claude/*" \
74:   -not -path "*/node_modules/*" \
75:   -not -path "*/.git/*" \
76:   -not -path "*/.claude/plugins/*" \
77:   -not -path "*/Library/*" \
78:   -not -path "*/.Trash/*"
79: ```
80: 
81: Exclude the global file (`~/.claude/settings.local.json`) from the results — it is the merge target, not a source. Report how many project files were found and list their paths.
82: 
83: If no project files are found, report "no project settings found" and stop. If only the global file exists, report "nothing to consolidate" and stop.
84: 
85: ### Step 2: Read & Parse
86: 
87: Read each discovered file. Extract `permissions.allow[]` and `permissions.deny[]` arrays. Track which project each permission came from — you'll need this for flagging later.
88: 
89: If a file contains malformed JSON, skip it and report it as unreadable. Don't let one bad file block the whole run.
90: 
91: ### Step 3: Deduplicate
92: 
93: Remove exact string duplicates across all files. Matching is case-sensitive because permissions are case-sensitive. Treat colon-wildcard and space-wildcard as distinct entries at this stage — collapsing happens next.
94: 
95: ### Step 4: Collapse, Detect Literals, Flag Project-Specific
96: 
97: See `references/permission-categories.md` for the full rule sets:
98: 
99: - Superset collapsing (5 rules, applied in order)
100: - Literal one-off detection (heuristic for when to keep vs flag)
101: - Project-specific flagging (7 rules, including absolute paths, project scripts, `Bash(./*)`, WebFetch domains, accidental `#` comments)
102: 
103: ### Step 5: Categorize & Sort
104: 
105: Group consolidated permissions into the categories listed in `references/permission-categories.md`. Sort entries alphabetically within each category.
106: 
107: ### Step 6: Backup, Merge, Report
108: 
109: The merge procedure (backup naming, preserving top-level keys, handling deny/allow conflicts where deny wins) and the exact report format live in `references/safety-deny-list.md`. That file also covers edge cases (no project files, malformed JSON, repeated runs) and what this skill explicitly does NOT do.
110: 
111: ## Reference Files
112: 
113: - `references/autonomous-permissions.json` — 350+ command baseline for bootstrap mode (space-wildcards, shell operators, Claude tools, safety deny list)
114: - `references/permission-categories.md` — three syntaxes, collapsing rules, category table, literal/project-specific detection
115: - `references/safety-deny-list.md` — backup/merge procedure, report format, deny/allow conflict handling, edge cases, scope boundaries

=== skills/workflows/setup-project-skills/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: setup-project-skills
3: version: 1.1.1
4: description: |
5:   Bootstrap the per-repo config other Skill-Madness skills consume: domain doc layout (CONTEXT.md, ADRs), contract format (OpenAPI / Pydantic / TS / JSON Schema), and work-item tracker (Beads / GitHub / GitLab / local). Writes docs/agents/ plus an "## Agent skills" block in CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md (never both, never overwriting). Downstream skills fail loud if this config is missing. Run once per repo. Trigger on "setup project skills", "configure project skills", "bootstrap this repo", "/setup-project-skills".
6: disable-model-invocation: true
7: requires_agent_teams: false
8: requires_claude_code: false
9: min_plan: starter
10: owns:
11:   directories: ["docs/agents/"]
12:   patterns: []
13:   shared_read: []
14: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
15: composes_with: ["project-profiler", "sync-skills"]
16: spawned_by: []
17: ---
18: 
19: # setup-project-skills
20: 
21: Explicit-invocation only. Run once per repository.
22: 
23: > **What this writes:** a `docs/agents/` directory containing three config files (`domain-docs.md`, `contract-format.md`, `work-item-tracker.md`) plus an `## Agent skills` block appended to either `CLAUDE.md` or `AGENTS.md`. Never both. Never overwrites an existing `## Agent skills` block without explicit confirmation.
24: 
25: > **Ownership:** this skill owns `docs/agents/` exclusively. It is carved out of `docs-agent`'s broader `docs/` ownership — `docs-agent` reads `docs/agents/` for context but never writes there. See the canonical map in the orchestrator's `references/file-ownership.md`.
26: 
27: This skill bootstraps the per-repo configuration that the rest of the Skill-Madness toolkit reads at runtime. Downstream skills like `maintain-context`, `contract-author`, and the orchestrator look in `docs/agents/` for these files and fail loud if they are missing.
28: 
29: ## The three questions
30: 
31: Ask one at a time. For each question, look at the repo first and recommend an answer; then ask the user to confirm or override. Do not present the menu cold — recommend, then ask.
32: 
33: ### Q1 — Domain doc layout
34: 
35: Single-context project or multi-context monorepo? Where does `CONTEXT.md` live?
36: 
37: - **Single-context** (default for most repos) — `CONTEXT.md` at repo root, ADRs in `docs/adr/`.
38: - **Multi-context monorepo** — `CONTEXT.md` per app at `apps/<app>/CONTEXT.md`, ADRs at `apps/<app>/docs/adr/`.
39: 
40: Recommend `single-context` unless you see an `apps/`, `packages/`, or `services/` directory with multiple sibling projects.
41: 
42: ### Q2 — Contract format preference
43: 
44: Which contract format does this repo use for integration boundaries?
45: 
46: - **OpenAPI** — REST APIs, YAML/JSON specs
47: - **Pydantic** — Python services, models as contracts
48: - **TypeScript interfaces** — TS-only repos, types as contracts
49: - **JSON Schema** — language-agnostic, event schemas, config validation
50: 
51: Recommend based on detected stack: Python project → Pydantic; TS-only → TypeScript; mixed or HTTP-heavy → OpenAPI; event-driven → JSON Schema.
52: 
53: ### Q3 — Work-item tracker
54: 
55: Where does this project track work items?
56: 
57: - **Beads** — `bd` CLI present or already in use
58: - **GitHub issues** — repo lives on GitHub, no other tracker visible
59: - **GitLab issues** — repo lives on GitLab
60: - **Local markdown** — `briefs/` or `tasks/` directory, no remote tracker
61: 
62: Recommend by checking remote URL (`git remote -v`), presence of `.beads/` or `bd` CLI, and existing `briefs/` or `tasks/` directories.
63: 
64: ## Output
65: 
66: For each answer, copy the matching template from `references/templates/` into `docs/agents/`:
67: 
68: - Q1 → `references/templates/domain-docs-{single|multi}.md` → `docs/agents/domain-docs.md`
69: - Q2 → `references/templates/contract-format-{openapi|pydantic|ts|jsonschema}.md` → `docs/agents/contract-format.md`
70: - Q3 → `references/templates/work-item-tracker-{beads|github|gitlab|local}.md` → `docs/agents/work-item-tracker.md`
71: 
72: After copying, fill in repo-specific paths if the template has placeholders.
73: 
74: ## Update CLAUDE.md or AGENTS.md
75: 
76: Append an `## Agent skills` block. Choose the target file by this rule, in order:
77: 
78: 1. If only one of `CLAUDE.md` or `AGENTS.md` exists, use it.
79: 2. If both exist, ask the user which one to update. Never write to both.
80: 3. If neither exists, ask the user which to create. Default to `AGENTS.md` (host-agnostic).
81: 
82: If the target file already has an `## Agent skills` heading, stop and ask: "An `## Agent skills` block already exists in `<file>`. Replace it, leave it alone, or merge new entries?" Never silently overwrite.
83: 
84: The block to append:
85: 
86: ```markdown
87: ## Agent skills
88: 
89: This repository is configured for the Skill-Madness toolkit. Agent skills read configuration from `docs/agents/`:
90: 
91: - `docs/agents/domain-docs.md` — where `CONTEXT.md` and ADRs live
92: - `docs/agents/contract-format.md` — preferred contract format
93: - `docs/agents/work-item-tracker.md` — work-item tracker for this repo
94: 
95: To re-run setup, invoke `/setup-project-skills`.
96: ```
97: 
98: ## Failure-loud contract for downstream skills
99: 
100: Skills that consume this config MUST fail loud when it is missing. Pattern for downstream skill authors:
101: 
102: ```text
103: This action needs docs/agents/<config-file>. Run /setup-project-skills first.
104: ```
105: 
106: Example downstream uses:
107: 
108: - `maintain-context` reads `docs/agents/domain-docs.md` to know whether `CONTEXT.md` lives at the root or per-app.
109: - `contract-author` reads `docs/agents/contract-format.md` to pick the right template.
110: - `orchestrator` reads `docs/agents/work-item-tracker.md` to know where to log work items.
111: 
112: Do not silently fall back to defaults. Surface the missing config and name this skill in the error.
113: 
114: ## Idempotence
115: 
116: Running this skill twice on the same repo must not destroy existing config.
117: 
118: 1. Before writing any file in `docs/agents/`, check if it exists.
119: 2. If it does, read it and ask: **keep / replace / update specific values**.
120: 3. For the `## Agent skills` block in `CLAUDE.md` / `AGENTS.md`, apply the same rule: never silently overwrite. Ask first.
121: 
122: A second run on an already-configured repo should produce zero file changes if the user picks "keep" for every prompt. That is the success condition for idempotence — the skill is safe to invoke repeatedly without surprises.
123: 
124: ## Compose with
125: 
126: - `project-profiler` — run it first if `CLAUDE.md` does not yet exist; it generates the stack profile this skill annotates.
127: - `sync-skills` — once `docs/agents/` is configured, sync skills globally so they can read this config from any project.

=== skills/workflows/skill-creator/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: skill-creator
3: version: 1.0.0
4: description: |
5:   Create new skills, modify and improve existing skills, and measure skill performance. Use when users want to create a skill from scratch, edit, or optimize an existing skill, run evals to test a skill, benchmark skill performance with variance analysis, or optimize a skill's description for better triggering accuracy.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: allowed-tools:
10:   - Read
11:   - Write
12:   - Edit
13:   - Glob
14:   - Grep
15:   - Bash
16: composes_with:
17:   - skill-review
18:   - skill-update
19:   - skill-writer
20: spawned_by: []
21: ---
22: 
23: # Skill Creator
24: 
25: A skill for creating new skills and iteratively improving them using Hermes Agent.
26: 
27: At a high level, the process of creating a skill goes like this:
28: 
29: - Decide what you want the skill to do and roughly how it should do it
30: - Write a draft of the skill
31: - Create a few test prompts and run Hermes-with-access-to-the-skill on them
32: - Help the user evaluate the results both qualitatively and quantitatively
33:   - While the runs happen in the background, draft some quantitative evals if there aren't any (if there are some, you can either use as is or modify if you feel something needs to change about them). Then explain them to the user (or if they already existed, explain the ones that already exist)
34:   - Use the `eval-viewer/generate_review.py` script to show the user the results for them to look at, and also let them look at the quantitative metrics
35: - Rewrite the skill based on feedback from the user's evaluation of the results (and also if there are any glaring flaws that become apparent from the quantitative benchmarks)
36: - Repeat until you're satisfied
37: - Expand the test set and try again at larger scale
38: 
39: Your job when using this skill is to figure out where the user is in this process and then jump in and help them progress through these stages. So for instance, maybe they're like "I want to make a skill for X". You can help narrow down what they mean, write a draft, write the test cases, figure out how they want to evaluate, run all the prompts, and repeat.
40: 
41: On the other hand, maybe they already have a draft of the skill. In this case you can go straight to the eval/iterate part of the loop.
42: 
43: Of course, you should always be flexible and if the user is like "I don't need to run a bunch of evaluations, just vibe with me", you can do that instead.
44: 
45: Then after the skill is done (but again, the order is flexible), you can also run the skill description improver, which we have a whole separate script for, to optimize the triggering of the skill.
46: 
47: Cool? Cool.
48: 
49: ## Communicating with the user
50: 
51: The skill creator is liable to be used by people across a wide range of familiarity with coding jargon. If you haven't heard (and how could you, it's only very recently that it started), there's a trend now where the power of AI agents is inspiring plumbers to open up their terminals, parents and grandparents to google "how to install npm". On the other hand, the bulk of users are probably fairly computer-literate.
52: 
53: So please pay attention to context cues to understand how to phrase your communication! In the default case, just to give you some idea:
54: 
55: - "evaluation" and "benchmark" are borderline, but OK
56: - for "JSON" and "assertion" you want to see serious cues from the user that they know what those things are before using them without explaining them
57: 
58: It's OK to briefly explain terms if you're in doubt, and feel free to clarify terms with a short definition if you're unsure if the user will get it.
59: 
60: ---
61: 
62: ## Creating a skill
63: 
64: ### Capture Intent
65: 
66: Start by understanding the user's intent. The current conversation might already contain a workflow the user wants to capture (e.g., they say "turn this into a skill"). If so, extract answers from the conversation history first — the tools used, the sequence of steps, corrections the user made, input/output formats observed. The user may need to fill the gaps, and should confirm before proceeding to the next step.
67: 
68: 1. What should this skill enable Hermes to do?
69: 2. When should this skill trigger? (what user phrases/contexts)
70: 3. What's the expected output format?
71: 4. Should we set up test cases to verify the skill works? Skills with objectively verifiable outputs (file transforms, data extraction, code generation, fixed workflow steps) benefit from test cases. Skills with subjective outputs (writing style, art) often don't need them. Suggest the appropriate default based on the skill type, but let the user decide.
72: 
73: ### Interview and Research
74: 
75: Proactively ask questions about edge cases, input/output formats, example files, success criteria, and dependencies. Wait to write test prompts until you've got this part ironed out.
76: 
77: Research in parallel via subagents if needed — come prepared with context to reduce burden on the user.
78: 
79: ### Write the SKILL.md
80: 
81: Based on the user interview, fill in these components:
82: 
83: - **name**: Skill identifier (kebab-case, max 64 chars)
84: - **description**: When to trigger, what it does. This is the primary triggering mechanism — include both what the skill does AND specific contexts for when to use it. All "when to use" info goes here, not in the body. Make descriptions "pushy" — enumerate contexts where the skill should activate. Stay under 1024 characters.
85: - **version**: Start at 1.0.0
86: - **allowed-tools**: Tools the skill needs
87: - **the rest of the skill :)**
88: 
89: #### Skill Directory Structure
90: 
91: ```text
92: skill-name/
93: ├── SKILL.md (required)
94: │   ├── YAML frontmatter (name, description required)
95: │   └── Markdown instructions
96: └── Bundled Resources (optional)
97:     ├── scripts/    - Executable code for deterministic/repetitive tasks
98:     ├── references/ - Docs loaded into context as needed
99:     └── assets/     - Files used in output (templates, icons, fonts)
100: ```
101: 
102: #### Progressive Disclosure
103: 
104: Skills use a three-level loading system:
105: 
106: 1. **Metadata** (name + description) — Always in context (~100 words)
107: 2. **SKILL.md body** — In context whenever skill triggers (<500 lines ideal)
108: 3. **Bundled resources** — As needed (unlimited, scripts can execute without loading)
109: 
110: These word counts are approximate and you can feel free to go longer if needed.
111: 
112: **Key patterns:**
113: 
114: - Keep SKILL.md under 500 lines; if you're approaching this limit, add an additional layer of hierarchy along with clear pointers about where the model using the skill should go next to follow up.
115: - Reference files clearly from SKILL.md with guidance on when to read them
116: - For large reference files (>300 lines), include a table of contents
117: 
118: #### Writing Patterns
119: 
120: Prefer using the imperative form in instructions.
121: 
122: **Defining output formats** — You can do it like this:
123: 
124: ```markdown
125: ## Report structure
126: ALWAYS use this exact template:
127: # [Title]
128: ## Executive summary
129: ## Key findings
130: ## Recommendations
131: ```
132: 
133: **Examples pattern** — It's useful to include examples. You can format them like this (but if "Input" and "Output" are in the examples you might want to deviate a little):
134: 
135: ```markdown
136: ## Commit message format
137: **Example 1:**
138: Input: Added user authentication with JWT tokens
139: Output: feat(auth): implement JWT-based authentication
140: ```
141: 
142: #### Writing Style
143: 
144: Try to explain to the model why things are important in lieu of heavy-handed MUSTs. Use theory of mind and try to make the skill general and not super-narrow to specific examples. Start by writing a draft and then look at it with fresh eyes and improve it.
145: 
146: ### Test Cases
147: 
148: After writing the skill draft, come up with 2-3 realistic test prompts — the kind of thing a real user would actually say. Share them with the user: [you don't have to use this exact language] "Here are a few test cases I'd like to try. Do these look right, or do you want to add more?" Then run them.
149: 
150: Save test cases to `evals/evals.json`. Don't write assertions yet — just the prompts. You'll draft assertions in the next step while the runs are in progress.
151: 
152: ```json
153: {
154:   "skill_name": "example-skill",
155:   "evals": [
156:     {
157:       "id": 1,
158:       "prompt": "User's task prompt",
159:       "expected_output": "Description of expected result",
160:       "files": []
161:     }
162:   ]
163: }
164: ```
165: 
166: See `references/schemas.md` for the full schema (including the `assertions` field, which you'll add later).
167: 
168: ## Running and evaluating test cases
169: 
170: This section is one continuous sequence — don't stop partway through.
171: 
172: Put results in `.workspaces/<skill-name>/` at the repo root. Within the workspace, organize results by iteration (`iteration-1/`, `iteration-2/`, etc.) and within that, each test case gets a directory (`eval-0/`, `eval-1/`, etc.). Don't create all of this upfront — just create directories as you go.
173: 
174: ### Step 1: Spawn all runs (with-skill AND baseline) in the same turn
175: 
176: For each test case, spawn two subagents in the same turn — one with the skill, one without. This is important: don't spawn the with-skill runs first and then come back for baselines later. Launch everything at once so it all finishes around the same time.
177: 
178: **With-skill run:**
179: 
180: ```text
181: Execute this task:
182: - Skill path: <path-to-skill>
183: - Task: <eval prompt>
184: - Input files: <eval files if any, or "none">
185: - Save outputs to: <workspace>/iteration-<N>/eval-<ID>/with_skill/outputs/
186: - Outputs to save: <what the user cares about — e.g., "the .docx file", "the final CSV">
187: ```
188: 
189: **Baseline run** (same prompt, but the baseline depends on context):
190: 
191: - **Creating a new skill**: no skill at all. Same prompt, no skill path, save to `without_skill/outputs/`.
192: - **Improving an existing skill**: the old version. Before editing, snapshot the skill (`cp -r <skill-path> <workspace>/skill-snapshot/`), then point the baseline subagent at the snapshot. Save to `old_skill/outputs/`.
193: 
194: Write an `eval_metadata.json` for each test case (assertions can be empty for now). Give each eval a descriptive name based on what it's testing — not just "eval-0". Use this name for the directory too. If this iteration uses new or modified eval prompts, create these files for each new eval directory — don't assume they carry over from previous iterations.
195: 
196: ```json
197: {
198:   "eval_id": 0,
199:   "eval_name": "descriptive-name-here",
200:   "prompt": "The user's task prompt",
201:   "assertions": []
202: }
203: ```
204: 
205: ### Step 2: While runs are in progress, draft assertions
206: 
207: Don't just wait for the runs to finish — you can use this time productively. Draft quantitative assertions for each test case and explain them to the user. If assertions already exist in `evals/evals.json`, review them and explain what they check.
208: 
209: Good assertions are objectively verifiable and have descriptive names — they should read clearly in the benchmark viewer so someone glancing at the results immediately understands what each one checks. Subjective skills (writing style, design quality) are better evaluated qualitatively — don't force assertions onto things that need human judgment.
210: 
211: Update the `eval_metadata.json` files and `evals/evals.json` with the assertions once drafted. Also explain to the user what they'll see in the viewer — both the qualitative outputs and the quantitative benchmark.
212: 
213: ### Step 3: As runs complete, capture timing data
214: 
215: When each subagent task completes, the task notification includes timing data. Save this data immediately to `timing.json` in the run directory:
216: 
217: ```json
218: {
219:   "total_tokens": 84852,
220:   "duration_ms": 23332,
221:   "total_duration_seconds": 23.3
222: }
223: ```
224: 
225: Process each notification as it arrives rather than trying to batch them.
226: 
227: ### Step 4: Grade, aggregate, and launch the viewer
228: 
229: Once all runs are done:
230: 
231: 1. **Grade each run** — spawn a grader subagent (or grade inline) that reads `agents/grader.md` and evaluates each assertion against the outputs. Save results to `grading.json` in each run directory. The grading.json expectations array must use the fields `text`, `passed`, and `evidence` (not `name`/`met`/`details` or other variants) — the viewer depends on these exact field names. For assertions that can be checked programmatically, write and run a script rather than eyeballing it — scripts are faster, more reliable, and can be reused across iterations.
232: 
233: 2. **Aggregate into benchmark** — run the aggregation script from the skill-creator directory:
234: 
235:    ```bash
236:    python -m scripts.aggregate_benchmark <workspace>/iteration-N --skill-name <name>
237:    ```
238: 
239:    This produces `benchmark.json` and `benchmark.md` with pass_rate, time, and tokens for each configuration, with mean +/- stddev and the delta. If generating benchmark.json manually, see `references/schemas.md` for the exact schema the viewer expects.
240:    Put each with_skill version before its baseline counterpart.
241: 
242: 3. **Do an analyst pass** — read the benchmark data and surface patterns the aggregate stats might hide. See `agents/analyzer.md` (the "Analyzing Benchmark Results" section) for what to look for — things like assertions that always pass regardless of skill (non-discriminating), high-variance evals (possibly flaky), and time/token tradeoffs.
243: 
244: 4. **Launch the viewer** with both qualitative outputs and quantitative data:
245: 
246:    ```bash
247:    nohup python <skill-creator-path>/eval-viewer/generate_review.py \
248:      <workspace>/iteration-N \
249:      --skill-name "my-skill" \
250:      --benchmark <workspace>/iteration-N/benchmark.json \
251:      > /dev/null 2>&1 &
252:    VIEWER_PID=$!
253:    ```
254: 
255:    For iteration 2+, also pass `--previous-workspace <workspace>/iteration-<N-1>`.
256: 
257:    If there's no display available, use `--static <output_path>` to write a standalone HTML file instead of starting a server. Feedback will be downloaded as a `feedback.json` file when the user clicks "Submit All Reviews". After download, copy `feedback.json` into the workspace directory for the next iteration to pick up.
258: 
259:    Note: use generate_review.py to create the viewer; there's no need to write custom HTML.
260: 
261: 5. **Tell the user** something like: "I've opened the results in your browser. There are two tabs — 'Outputs' lets you click through each test case and leave feedback, 'Benchmark' shows the quantitative comparison. When you're done, come back here and let me know."
262: 
263: ### What the user sees in the viewer
264: 
265: The "Outputs" tab shows one test case at a time:
266: 
267: - **Prompt**: the task that was given
268: - **Output**: the files the skill produced, rendered inline where possible
269: - **Previous Output** (iteration 2+): collapsed section showing last iteration's output
270: - **Formal Grades** (if grading was run): collapsed section showing assertion pass/fail
271: - **Feedback**: a textbox that auto-saves as they type
272: - **Previous Feedback** (iteration 2+): their comments from last time, shown below the textbox
273: 
274: The "Benchmark" tab shows the stats summary: pass rates, timing, and token usage for each configuration, with per-eval breakdowns and analyst observations.
275: 
276: Navigation is via prev/next buttons or arrow keys. When done, they click "Submit All Reviews" which saves all feedback to `feedback.json`.
277: 
278: ### Step 5: Read the feedback
279: 
280: When the user tells you they're done, read `feedback.json`:
281: 
282: ```json
283: {
284:   "reviews": [
285:     {"run_id": "eval-0-with_skill", "feedback": "the chart is missing axis labels", "timestamp": "..."},
286:     {"run_id": "eval-1-with_skill", "feedback": "", "timestamp": "..."},
287:     {"run_id": "eval-2-with_skill", "feedback": "perfect, love this", "timestamp": "..."}
288:   ],
289:   "status": "complete"
290: }
291: ```
292: 
293: Empty feedback means the user thought it was fine. Focus your improvements on the test cases where the user had specific complaints.
294: 
295: Kill the viewer server when you're done with it:
296: 
297: ```bash
298: kill $VIEWER_PID 2>/dev/null
299: ```
300: 
301: ---
302: 
303: ## Improving the skill
304: 
305: This is the heart of the loop. You've run the test cases, the user has reviewed the results, and now you need to make the skill better based on their feedback.
306: 
307: ### How to think about improvements
308: 
309: 1. **Generalize from the feedback.** The big picture thing that's happening here is that we're trying to create skills that can be used a million times (maybe literally, maybe even more who knows) across many different prompts. Here you and the user are iterating on only a few examples over and over again because it helps move faster. The user knows these examples in and out and it's quick for them to assess new outputs. But if the skill you and the user are codeveloping works only for those examples, it's useless. Rather than put in fiddly overfitty changes, or oppressively constrictive MUSTs, if there's some stubborn issue, you might try branching out and using different metaphors, or recommending different patterns of working. It's relatively cheap to try and maybe you'll land on something great.
310: 
311: 2. **Keep the prompt lean.** Remove things that aren't pulling their weight. Make sure to read the transcripts, not just the final outputs — if it looks like the skill is making the model waste a bunch of time doing things that are unproductive, you can try getting rid of the parts of the skill that are making it do that and seeing what happens.
312: 
313: 3. **Explain the why.** Try hard to explain the **why** behind everything you're asking the model to do. Today's LLMs are *smart*. They have good theory of mind and when given a good harness can go beyond rote instructions and really make things happen. Even if the feedback from the user is terse or frustrated, try to actually understand the task and why the user is writing what they wrote, and what they actually wrote, and then transmit this understanding into the instructions. If you find yourself writing ALWAYS or NEVER in all caps, or using super rigid structures, that's a yellow flag — if possible, reframe and explain the reasoning so that the model understands why the thing you're asking for is important. That's a more humane, powerful, and effective approach.
314: 
315: 4. **Look for repeated work across test cases.** Read the transcripts from the test runs and notice if the subagents all independently wrote similar helper scripts or took the same multi-step approach to something. If all 3 test cases resulted in the subagent writing a `create_docx.py` or a `build_chart.py`, that's a strong signal the skill should bundle that script. Write it once, put it in `scripts/`, and tell the skill to use it. This saves every future invocation from reinventing the wheel.
316: 
317: This task is pretty important (we are trying to create billions a year in economic value here!) and your thinking time is not the blocker; take your time and really mull things over. I'd suggest writing a draft revision and then looking at it anew and making improvements. Really do your best to get into the head of the user and understand what they want and need.
318: 
319: ### The iteration loop
320: 
321: After improving the skill:
322: 
323: 1. Apply your improvements to the skill
324: 2. Rerun all test cases into a new `iteration-<N+1>/` directory, including baseline runs. If you're creating a new skill, the baseline is always `without_skill` (no skill) — that stays the same across iterations. If you're improving an existing skill, use your judgment on what makes sense as the baseline: the original version the user came in with, or the previous iteration.
325: 3. Launch the reviewer with `--previous-workspace` pointing at the previous iteration
326: 4. Wait for the user to review and tell you they're done
327: 5. Read the new feedback, improve again, repeat
328: 
329: Keep going until:
330: 
331: - The user says they're happy
332: - The feedback is all empty (everything looks good)
333: - You're not making meaningful progress
334: 
335: ---
336: 
337: ## Advanced: Blind comparison
338: 
339: For situations where you want a more rigorous comparison between two versions of a skill (e.g., the user asks "is the new version actually better?"), there's a blind comparison system. Read `agents/comparator.md` and `agents/analyzer.md` for the details. The basic idea is: give two outputs to an independent agent without telling it which is which, and let it judge quality. Then analyze why the winner won.
340: 
341: This is optional, requires subagents, and most users won't need it. The human review loop is usually sufficient.
342: 
343: ---
344: 
345: ## Description Optimization
346: 
347: The description field in SKILL.md frontmatter is the primary mechanism that determines whether Hermes invokes a skill. After creating or improving a skill, offer to optimize the description for better triggering accuracy.
348: 
349: ### Step 1: Generate trigger eval queries
350: 
351: Create 20 eval queries — a mix of should-trigger and should-not-trigger. Save as JSON:
352: 
353: ```json
354: [
355:   {"query": "the user prompt", "should_trigger": true},
356:   {"query": "another prompt", "should_trigger": false}
357: ]
358: ```
359: 
360: The queries must be realistic and something a Hermes user would actually type. Not abstract requests, but requests that are concrete and specific and have a good amount of detail. For instance, file paths, personal context about the user's job or situation, column names and values, company names, URLs. A little bit of backstory. Some might be in lowercase or contain abbreviations or typos or casual speech. Use a mix of different lengths, and focus on edge cases rather than making them clear-cut (the user will get a chance to sign off on them).
361: 
362: Bad: `"Format this data"`, `"Extract text from PDF"`, `"Create a chart"`
363: 
364: Good: `"ok so my boss just sent me this xlsx file (its in my downloads, called something like 'Q4 sales final FINAL v2.xlsx') and she wants me to add a column that shows the profit margin as a percentage. The revenue is in column C and costs are in column D i think"`
365: 
366: For the **should-trigger** queries (8-10), think about coverage. You want different phrasings of the same intent — some formal, some casual. Include cases where the user doesn't explicitly name the skill or file type but clearly needs it. Throw in some uncommon use cases and cases where this skill competes with another but should win.
367: 
368: For the **should-not-trigger** queries (8-10), the most valuable ones are the near-misses — queries that share keywords or concepts with the skill but actually need something different. Think adjacent domains, ambiguous phrasing where a naive keyword match would trigger but shouldn't, and cases where the query touches on something the skill does but in a context where another tool is more appropriate.
369: 
370: The key thing to avoid: don't make should-not-trigger queries obviously irrelevant. "Write a fibonacci function" as a negative test for a PDF skill is too easy — it doesn't test anything. The negative cases should be genuinely tricky.
371: 
372: ### Step 2: Review with user
373: 
374: Present the eval set to the user for review using the HTML template:
375: 
376: 1. Read the template from `assets/eval_review.html`
377: 2. Replace the placeholders:
378:    - `__EVAL_DATA_PLACEHOLDER__` -> the JSON array of eval items (no quotes around it — it's a JS variable assignment)
379:    - `__SKILL_NAME_PLACEHOLDER__` -> the skill's name
380:    - `__SKILL_DESCRIPTION_PLACEHOLDER__` -> the skill's current description
381: 3. Write to a temp file (e.g., `/tmp/eval_review_<skill-name>.html`) and open it: `open /tmp/eval_review_<skill-name>.html`
382: 4. The user can edit queries, toggle should-trigger, add/remove entries, then click "Export Eval Set"
383: 5. The file downloads to `~/Downloads/eval_set.json` — check the Downloads folder for the most recent version in case there are multiple (e.g., `eval_set (1).json`)
384: 
385: This step matters — bad eval queries lead to bad descriptions.
386: 
387: ### Step 3: Run the optimization loop
388: 
389: Tell the user: "This will take some time — I'll run the optimization loop in the background and check on it periodically."
390: 
391: Save the eval set to the workspace, then run in the background:
392: 
393: ```bash
394: python -m scripts.run_loop \
395:   --eval-set <path-to-trigger-eval.json> \
396:   --skill-path <path-to-skill> \
397:   --model <current-model-id> \
398:   --max-iterations 5 \
399:   --verbose
400: ```
401: 
402: Use the model ID from your system prompt (the one powering the current session) so the triggering test matches what the user actually experiences.
403: 
404: While it runs, periodically tail the output to give the user updates on which iteration it's on and what the scores look like.
405: 
406: This handles the full optimization loop automatically. It splits the eval set into 60% train and 40% held-out test, evaluates the current description (running each query 3 times to get a reliable trigger rate), then calls the model to propose improvements based on what failed. It re-evaluates each new description on both train and test, iterating up to 5 times. When it's done, it opens an HTML report in the browser showing the results per iteration and returns JSON with `best_description` — selected by test score rather than train score to avoid overfitting.
407: 
408: ### How skill triggering works in Hermes
409: 
410: Understanding the triggering mechanism helps design better eval queries. Skills appear in Hermes' available_skills list with their name + description, and the agent decides whether to consult a skill based on that description. The important thing to know is that the agent only consults skills for tasks it can't easily handle on its own — simple, one-step queries like "read this PDF" may not trigger a skill even if the description matches perfectly, because the agent can handle them directly with basic tools. Complex, multi-step, or specialized queries reliably trigger skills when the description matches.
411: 
412: This means your eval queries should be substantive enough that the agent would actually benefit from consulting a skill. Simple queries like "read file X" are poor test cases — they won't trigger skills regardless of description quality.
413: 
414: ### Step 4: Apply the result
415: 
416: Take `best_description` from the JSON output and update the skill's SKILL.md frontmatter. Show the user before/after and report the scores.
417: 
418: ---
419: 
420: ## Reference files
421: 
422: The agents/ directory contains instructions for specialized subagents. Read them when you need to spawn the relevant subagent.
423: 
424: - `agents/grader.md` — How to evaluate assertions against outputs
425: - `agents/comparator.md` — How to do blind A/B comparison between two outputs
426: - `agents/analyzer.md` — How to analyze why one version beat another
427: 
428: The references/ directory has additional documentation:
429: 
430: - `references/schemas.md` — JSON structures for evals.json, grading.json, etc.
431: 
432: ---
433: 
434: Repeating one more time the core loop here for emphasis:
435:
