436: - Figure out what the skill is about
437: - Draft or edit the skill
438: - Run Hermes-with-access-to-the-skill on test prompts
439: - With the user, evaluate the outputs:
440:   - Create benchmark.json and run `eval-viewer/generate_review.py` to help the user review them
441:   - Run quantitative evals
442: - Repeat until you and the user are satisfied
443: - Package the final skill and return it to the user.
444: 
445: Please add steps to your todo list, if you have such a thing, to make sure you don't forget. Specifically put "Create evals JSON and run eval-viewer/generate_review.py so human can review test cases" in your todo list to make sure it happens.
446: 
447: Good luck!

=== skills/workflows/sync-skills/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: sync-skills
3: version: 2.1.0
4: description: |
5:   Sync skills between this repo and the global skill directories for Claude Code (~/.claude/skills/) and Cursor (~/.cursor/skills-cursor/) using symlinks (default) or copies. Use when the user wants to link, sync, publish, push, or copy skills globally, check sync status, unlink, or pull a skill from a global location back into the repo. Trigger on "sync skills", "link skills", "publish skills", "skill status", "/sync-skills", "are my skills linked", "unlink skills".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: true
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: ["skills/workflows/sync-skills/"]
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["skills/"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Bash"]
14: composes_with: ["skill-update", "skill-review"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Sync Skills Between Repo and Global Locations
19: 
20: Link or copy skills between this repo and the global skill directories that Claude Code and Cursor read from. Symlinks are the default — edits in the repo are instantly available everywhere without copying.
21: 
22: ## Skill Locations
23: 
24: | Location | Path | Used by |
25: | -------- | ---- | ------- |
26: | Repo (source of truth) | `skills/` | This workspace |
27: | Claude Code (global) | `~/.claude/skills/` | All Claude Code projects |
28: | Cursor (global) | `~/.cursor/skills-cursor/` | All Cursor workspaces |
29: 
30: The repo organizes skills into category directories (`contracts/`, `meta/`, `roles/`, `workflows/`, `orchestrator/`, `git/`). For Claude Code, symlinks are **flattened** — each individual skill is linked directly under `~/.claude/skills/` (no category subdirs) because Claude Code only discovers skills at `~/.claude/skills/<skill-name>/SKILL.md`. For Cursor, symlinks are created at the category level.
31: 
32: ### Excluded directories
33: 
34: Two top-level directories under `skills/` are excluded from discovery and never get symlinked:
35: 
36: - `skills/archive/` — retired skills kept as reference-only audit trail
37: - `skills/in-progress/` — drafts under active development
38: 
39: The exclusion list lives in `SKIP_CATEGORIES` near the top of `scripts/sync-skills.sh`. Add a new entry there if another staging directory ever gets introduced. The corresponding paths must also be kept out of `.claude-plugin/plugin.json`'s `skills` array — otherwise the plugin would load them even though `sync-skills` doesn't.
40: 
41: ## Quick Reference
42: 
43: ```bash
44: SCRIPT="skills/workflows/sync-skills/scripts/sync-skills.sh"
45: 
46: # Link all repo skill categories to both Claude Code and Cursor
47: $SCRIPT --link --to-all
48: 
49: # Check what's linked, copied, or missing
50: $SCRIPT --status
51: 
52: # Remove broken symlinks (e.g. after deleting a skill from the repo)
53: $SCRIPT --clean
54: 
55: # Link just one category to Claude Code
56: $SCRIPT --link --to-claude meta
57: 
58: # Copy instead of link (for machines without repo access)
59: $SCRIPT --copy --to-all
60: 
61: # Remove symlinks (restore independence)
62: $SCRIPT --unlink --to-all
63: 
64: # Pull a skill from Cursor into the repo
65: $SCRIPT --from-cursor shell
66: 
67: # Preview what would happen
68: $SCRIPT --dry-run --link --to-all
69: ```
70: 
71: ## Modes
72: 
73: ### Link Mode (default for `--to-*`)
74: 
75: Creates symlinks from global locations pointing to repo directories. This is the development workflow — edit skills in the repo and they're instantly live in Claude Code and Cursor.
76: 
77: - **Claude Code**: Skills are **flattened** — each individual skill gets its own symlink directly under `~/.claude/skills/` (e.g., `~/.claude/skills/skill-review` → `repo/skills/meta/skill-review`). This is required because Claude Code only discovers skills at `~/.claude/skills/<skill-name>/SKILL.md`.
78: - **Cursor**: Symlinks are created at the **category level** (e.g., `~/.cursor/skills-cursor/meta` → `repo/skills/meta`)
79: - Non-repo skills in global locations (e.g., `~/.claude/skills/builtWithAgent/`) are untouched
80: - If a copy already exists where a symlink would go, the script reports it and replaces the copy with a symlink (use `--dry-run` to preview first)
81: 
82: ### Copy Mode (`--copy`)
83: 
84: Copies skill directories instead of symlinking. Use this when:
85: 
86: - Deploying skills to a machine that doesn't have the repo cloned
87: - You need a frozen snapshot that won't change with repo edits
88: - The target location is on a different filesystem that doesn't support symlinks
89: 
90: ### Pull Mode (`--from-cursor`, `--from-claude`)
91: 
92: Copies skills FROM global locations INTO the repo. Always copies (not symlinks) since the repo is the destination. Useful for importing skills created outside this repo.
93: 
94: ## Script Flags
95: 
96: | Flag | Purpose |
97: | ---- | ------- |
98: | `--link` | Create symlinks (default for `--to-*` operations) |
99: | `--copy` | Copy files instead of symlinking |
100: | `--unlink` | Remove symlinks to repo (restores global locations to independent state) |
101: | `--to-cursor` | Target `~/.cursor/skills-cursor/` |
102: | `--to-claude` | Target `~/.claude/skills/` |
103: | `--to-all` | Target both Claude Code and Cursor |
104: | `--from-cursor` | Pull from Cursor into repo |
105: | `--from-claude` | Pull from Claude Code into repo |
106: | `--from-all` | Pull from both |
107: | `--status` | Show what's linked, copied, or missing across all locations |
108: | `--clean` | Remove broken symlinks from global locations |
109: | `--dry-run` | Preview what would happen without making changes |
110: | `-h, --help` | Show help |
111: 
112: Append category or skill names after flags to target specific ones:
113: 
114: ```bash
115: $SCRIPT --link --to-claude meta roles    # Link only meta/ and roles/
116: $SCRIPT --from-cursor shell              # Pull only the shell skill
117: ```
118: 
119: ## How It Works
120: 
121: **Linking:** For Claude Code, discovers every individual skill within category directories and creates a flattened symlink for each (e.g., `~/.claude/skills/skill-review` → `repo/skills/meta/skill-review`). For Cursor, creates category-level symlinks. If the target already exists as a real directory, warns before replacing.
122: 
123: **Status detection:** Checks each expected location and reports whether it's a symlink (and where it points), a copy, or missing. Also detects broken symlinks.
124: 
125: **Non-repo skills are safe:** The script only manages categories that exist in this repo. Skills like `~/.claude/skills/builtWithAgent/` or Cursor's native skills are never touched.
126: 
127: ## After Linking
128: 
129: Once linked, skills are available automatically:
130: 
131: - **Claude Code**: Skills in `~/.claude/skills/` are picked up by new sessions
132: - **Cursor**: Skills in `~/.cursor/skills-cursor/` appear in all workspaces
133: 
134: Edit any skill in the repo and the change is live immediately — no sync step needed.
135: 
136: To verify: `$SCRIPT --status`

=== skills/workflows/ui-brief/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: ui-brief
3: version: 1.2.1
4: description: |
5:   Generate opinionated, design-led briefs for building or rebuilding UIs so they lead with the product's actual moat instead of converging on a generic shadcn-default SaaS dashboard. Use whenever the user wants a detailed UI prompt or spec to hand off — greenfield OR rebuild. Trigger on "write me a UI brief", "the current UI sucks, redesign it", "make this not look like every shadcn admin", "I need a prompt for the frontend", "design brief for X", "the design feels generic", "build a UI for [project] like [reference]", or before non-trivial UI work when the user names a reference app or named visual style. Output is a standalone Markdown file with positioning, design-language rules, page-by-page treatment, component primitives, motion discipline, and a verifiable Definition of Done.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Glob", "Grep", "Bash"]
14: composes_with: ["ui-ux-pro-max", "frontend-design:frontend-design", "superpowers:brainstorming", "ux-review", "frontend-agent", "orchestrator", "playwright"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # UI Brief
19: 
20: > **Tradeoff:** Biases toward design opinionation. For greenfield work where the user wants exploration, use claude-design-brief instead.
21: 
22: Produce opinionated, design-leading UI briefs that survive contact with implementation.
23: 
24: The default failure mode for LLM-driven UI work is converging on the same shadcn-default-card-grid for every product. This skill exists to write a brief so opinion-dense and reference-specific that the implementing agent cannot drift back into that default. The output is a standalone Markdown file the user (or an orchestrator + frontend-agent) can paste into a fresh Claude Code session and execute against.
25: 
26: **ui-brief vs its siblings.** Reach for `ui-brief` when the deliverable is a written spec to hand to a *Claude Code production build*; use `claude-design-brief` instead when you want hi-fi mockups or A/B/C directions drawn on the *Claude Design canvas* at claude.ai, and `frontend-design` (or `frontend-agent`) when you want the UI actually built rather than briefed.
27: 
28: **Announce at start:** "Using ui-brief to write an opinionated design brief for [project]."
29: 
30: ## Two Modes — Greenfield vs Rebuild
31: 
32: The skill produces both flavors. Pick the right entry path:
33: 
34: ### Greenfield (no UI exists yet)
35: 
36: The user is building from scratch. Examples: a new product, a fresh app, a feature with no prior UI surface. The brief sets **target style references** and primitives upfront — there is nothing to diagnose, only failure modes to anticipate.
37: 
38: Hallmark phrases: "build me a UI for X", "I want to build [thing] and want it to look like [reference]", "design a dashboard for [new product]", "make a frontend for [API/backend that exists]".
39: 
40: ### Rebuild (UI exists, needs replacement)
41: 
42: The user has a working UI that buries the product's value or feels generic. The brief leads with a **diagnosis** of the current state, then prescribes the replacement.
43: 
44: Hallmark phrases: "the current UI sucks", "redesign this dashboard", "make this not look like every shadcn admin", "the design feels generic", "rebuild the frontend".
45: 
46: Both modes share most of the brief's structure. The differences:
47: 
48: | | Greenfield | Rebuild |
49: |---|---|---|
50: | Section 2 — Opening | "The Vision" — what this UI is for, the target reference language | "The Problem" — what is wrong with the current UI, with screenshot reference |
51: | Discovery focus | Stack + adjacent products + target style references | Stack + current screenshot + current routes + libraries underused |
52: | DoD screenshot diff | "Side-by-side vs target reference (or design mockup)" | "Side-by-side vs old screenshot — visibly different product, not a recolor" |
53: 
54: The opinion density and structure are otherwise identical.
55: 
56: ## When To Use This vs Other Skills
57: 
58: - **`ui-brief`** *(this)* — produces a brief / spec / prompt. Output is a Markdown file. No code is written.
59: - **`ui-ux-pro-max`** — design intelligence (palettes, fonts, component patterns) consumed *during* implementation.
60: - **`frontend-design`** — the implementation skill that builds distinctive frontends.
61: - **`frontend-agent`** — the role agent that does the actual building.
62: - **`ux-review`** — reviews a *built* UI in a real browser. Use after the build/rebuild ships.
63: - **`orchestrator`** — consumes this brief if the build is large enough to need a multi-agent team.
64: 
65: ```
66: ui-brief (write the spec)
67:         ↓
68: orchestrator + frontend-agent (build it)
69:         ↓
70: ux-review + playwright (verify it)
71: ```
72: 
73: If the user has only a vague idea ("I want a better dashboard but I do not know what for"), invoke `brainstorming` first, then come back here with the spec it produces.
74: 
75: ## What Makes A Good Brief
76: 
77: The briefs that work follow a specific shape. Each section earns its presence:
78: 
79: 1. **The Opening** — *Greenfield*: state the vision and the target reference language up front. *Rebuild*: name what is wrong with the current UI vividly, referencing the screenshot file.
80: 2. **The Thesis** — extract the product's actual moat in one paragraph. Every design decision will be evaluated against it.
81: 3. **Design Language** — concrete reference points appropriate to the domain. Examples of the *shape*: `Linear × Notion × Arc` for a productivity tool; `Stripe × Vercel × Posthog` for a developer marketing site; `bento-grid × glassmorphism × claymorphism` for a playful consumer product; `Datadog × Grafana × Linear` for ops tooling. Pick what fits — never copy-paste these. State both **positive** references AND **negative** references — what to actively avoid is at least as load-bearing as what to emulate. Greenfield briefs lean into target styles; rebuild briefs often lean against the failure mode.
82: 4. **Hard Rules** — 8–12 numbered, opinionated rules. Anchor numbers (32px row height, 2s pulse, 60/40 split). Frame them as judgment-cementing starting points, not laws.
83: 5. **Page-by-Page Brief** — every primary route gets a layout treatment with what belongs and what does not.
84: 6. **Component Primitives** — name 8–12 reusable pieces with implied APIs (e.g. `<MetricNumber>`, `<DenseTable>`).
85: 7. **Motion / Sound / Notifications** — usually missing from briefs; including this section prevents drift toward over-animation or zero animation.
86: 8. **Accessibility & Responsive Baseline** — explicit, not aspirational. axe AA, specific viewports (default 375 × 667 mobile + 1440 × 900 desktop), mobile-first with `min-width` queries, the canonical breakpoint tokens (sm/md/lg/xl/2xl), no hardcoded `width: <px>` on layout containers, no inline `style=` for layout. These are contract clauses for the implementing agent — see `skills/roles/frontend-agent/references/mobile-responsive.md` for the playbook they will execute against.
87: 9. **Implementation Discipline** — which skills compose during the build (brainstorming → orchestrator → frontend-agent → playwright → ux-review).
88: 10. **Definition of Done** — verifiable items. Must include "loads in a browser with zero console errors" and a screenshot-diff item (vs old screenshot for rebuilds, vs target reference for greenfield). **And — whenever the product's moat depends on live data, a real service, or an integration — a "the moat is real" item that exercises the actual value path end-to-end against the real thing once (a real call, real data flowing), not a mock of it.** The whole brief leads with the moat (§2); a DoD that only checks render + screenshots is a DoD a mock can satisfy, and a mock-satisfiable DoD ships a fiction — a "watch real LLMs compete" product that never calls an LLM, a live dashboard wired to a fixture. Mocks are the right way to *build* the UI before the backend lands; the DoD's job is to force graduating from them. If the brief deliberately scopes the backend out, mark it a **SHELL spec** in one line at the top — so a green shell is never mistaken for a finished product.
89: 11. **Notes for the Operator** — guardrails to prevent drift back to generic defaults during the build.
90: 
91: The voice matters. Brief bullet points and bare specifications produce bland UIs. Opinion-dense paragraphs with rationale produce good ones. **Explain the why** for every non-obvious decision.
92: 
93: ## How To Run
94: 
95: ### Step 1 — Discovery (2–5 minutes of reading)
96: 
97: Before writing a single line of the brief, ground the work in the actual project. Generic briefs come from skipping this step.
98: 
99: - **Read the README, CLAUDE.md, and package.json.** What is the stack? What libraries are already installed? Which ones are barely used? Libraries-already-installed-but-underused is gold — recommending them costs the project nothing and proves you read the file.
100: - **List the routes / pages** if any exist yet. `find <frontend>/src/app -type d -maxdepth 3` (Next.js) or equivalent for the framework in play.
101: - **List the existing components and hooks.** Which primitives already exist that the brief should reuse vs replace?
102: - **Look for screenshots in the repo root or `docs/`.** Use the Read tool on PNG/JPG files — Claude Code can see them. For rebuilds the current UI is the most important artifact you have. For greenfield, look for design mockups, Figma exports, mood-board files.
103: - **For greenfield with no project yet**, the user must provide either target reference apps or a written vision. If neither is present, ask one targeted question.
104: 
105: ### Step 2 — Diagnose or Vision
106: 
107: **Rebuild mode** — answer two questions in writing (your own scratch, before the brief):
108: 
109: 1. **What is wrong with the current UI?** Be specific. "Generic" is not a diagnosis — *why* is it generic? Same visual weight on every section? Hero card hides the product? No information density? Wrong chart library for the domain? Cards on a navy background that looks like every B2B SaaS panel from 2023?
110: 2. **What is the product's actual moat?** Read the README's "core thesis" / "what is this" section, the marketing copy, the CLAUDE.md. Reduce to one sentence. The brief's hero element will be whatever makes this moat visible.
111: 
112: **Greenfield mode** — answer two different questions:
113: 
114: 1. **What is the product's actual moat or differentiator?** Same question, different source — read the plan, the brainstorm, the user's vision. The hero element will surface this.
115: 2. **What is the target style language?** From the user's stated references, the product's gravity (playful vs serious vs operational), and the space (consumer vs prosumer vs operator). State it in 2–3 reference apps + 1–2 named styles drawn from the design intelligence vocabulary (`ui-ux-pro-max` recognizes 67 styles — pick from there: bento-grid, glassmorphism, claymorphism, neumorphism, brutalism, minimalism, skeuomorphism, etc.).
116: 
117: If you cannot answer the moat question from the project's own files, ask the user one targeted question — do not guess. The whole brief hangs on this answer.
118: 
119: ### Step 3 — Pick References
120: 
121: Choose 2–3 positive references and 2–3 negative references. Concrete apps/products and named styles, not adjectives.
122: 
123: | Project type | Positive references that often fit | Negative references to call out |
124: |---|---|---|
125: | Trading / finance / data ops | Bloomberg Terminal, Linear, TradingView, Datadog | Vercel/Stripe SaaS, generic admin templates, bento grids |
126: | Developer tools | Linear, Vercel dashboard, Sentry, Raycast | Bento-grid portfolio sites, glassmorphism, hero-card SaaS |
127: | Creative / playful consumer | Arc Browser, Figma, Notion, Pitch | Buttoned-up enterprise palettes, dense data-grid feel |
128: | Operations / monitoring | Datadog, Grafana, Linear | Generic shadcn dashboards, marketing-style hero cards |
129: | Marketing / content sites | Stripe, Vercel, Notion, Posthog blog | Bento-grid playfulness if the product is serious |
130: | Marketplace / social / commerce | Stripe checkout, modern Etsy, modern eBay | Web 2.0 density, gradient-heavy retail aesthetics |
131: 
132: For greenfield with target styles already stated by the user (e.g. "I want bento-grid item cards, glassmorphism trust badges, dark mode default"), **lean into them** — they are the brief's design vocabulary, not the failure mode. Negative references in greenfield mode usually call out the *adjacent* failure modes (the generic version of what they are asking for).
133: 
134: These tables are starting points, not laws. Pick what fits the product's gravity. **Always include negatives** — naming what to avoid is half the brief's job.
135: 
136: ### Step 4 — Write The Brief
137: 
138: Use `references/brief-template.md` as the structural skeleton. **Do not fill it in mechanically** — the template is a checklist of sections, not a template for the prose. Each section needs project-specific opinion density.
139: 
140: **File location:** by default, write to `<repo-root>/UI-CHALLENGE.md`. If `UI-CHALLENGE.md` already exists, name the next one `UI-CHALLENGE-v2.md`. The user can rename. For greenfield builds embedded inside a larger build challenge, the UI brief may be a section of a larger document (like THE-GAUNTLET.md) rather than a standalone file — ask the user if unclear.
141: 
142: **Length:** typically 200–350 lines of Markdown. Short enough to paste into a fresh session, long enough to be opinion-dense. Ruthlessly cut sections that do not earn their keep — but do not cut for length alone.
143: 
144: ### Step 5 — Verify Before Returning
145: 
146: Before reporting done, check:
147: 
148: - [ ] The brief mentions 2+ libraries already installed in the project (proves discovery happened, where applicable).
149: - [ ] The brief has both positive AND negative reference points (proves opinion).
150: - [ ] The DoD includes "loads in a browser with zero console errors" and a screenshot-diff item — vs old screenshot for rebuilds, vs target reference / mockup for greenfield.
151: - [ ] If the moat depends on live data / a real service / an integration, the DoD has a "the moat is real" item that exercises the real path once (not a mock). If the brief is deliberately UI-only, it declares itself a SHELL spec at the top.
152: - [ ] The brief lists which skills should compose during the build.
153: - [ ] The brief is under 400 lines (or you have a defensible reason for going longer).
154: 
155: Then summarize for the user in 3–5 sentences: the diagnosis-or-vision, the chosen references, where the brief lives. Show, do not narrate.
156: 
157: ## Anti-Patterns
158: 
159: | Anti-Pattern | Prevention |
160: |---|---|
161: | "Generic" as the diagnosis | Name the specific failure mode — same visual weight, buried hero, missing density, wrong chart library. |
162: | Adjective-only design language ("clean", "modern", "polished") | Replace with 2–3 concrete reference apps appropriate to the domain plus 1–2 named styles from the design vocabulary (bento-grid, glassmorphism, claymorphism, brutalism, etc.). |
163: | Spec without opinion | Every numbered hard rule should be defensible. If you cannot explain *why* 32px, change it or drop it. |
164: | Long bullet lists with no prose | The implementing agent will skim and pattern-match to defaults. Use prose for design-language sections. |
165: | Skipping the diagnosis section in rebuild mode | Without naming what is broken, the new brief is just preferences. The diagnosis is what makes the rebuild land. |
166: | Skipping target references in greenfield mode | Without concrete style references, greenfield briefs converge on the same shadcn default. |
167: | Treating the brief as a contract | It is a *brief*. The implementing agent should bring judgment. Anchor numbers are starting points — say so. |
168: | Library-blind | If you do not read package.json, you will recommend installing things that are already there. The user will lose trust. |
169: | Forgetting the screenshot-diff DoD | "Tests pass" is not the bar for UI work. Side-by-side comparison vs current (or vs target) is the only proof the brief produced what was asked for. |
170: | A DoD a mock can satisfy | When the moat is live/real-data and the DoD only checks render + console + screenshots, a fixture-backed shell passes every item — "it renders" gets mistaken for "the moat works." Add the "moat is real" item (a real call / real data, observed), or, if the build is intentionally UI-only, declare the brief a SHELL spec. A mock is a build accelerator, not a finish line. |
171: | One-size-fits-all reference points | A reference app that fits one product fits another badly — Bloomberg suits a trading dashboard but not a kids' drawing app; Arc suits a consumer browser but not an enterprise admin. Pick references that match the product's gravity. |
172: | Writing in passive voice | Briefs that work read like an opinionated colleague telling you what to build. Active voice. First-person plural ("we", "the user") sparingly. |
173: 
174: ## Output File Convention
175: 
176: The brief is a standalone Markdown file. It must be paste-ready into a fresh Claude Code session. That means:
177: 
178: - **Self-contained** — no "see [other doc] for X." If a referenced doc is critical, inline its key points.
179: - **Project-specific** — references the actual stack, the actual routes (or the planned ones), the actual screenshots or target references.
180: - **Operator-aware** — includes a "Notes for the Operator" section for guardrails during build.
181: - **Skill-coverage list at the end** — which skills should fire during the build (so the operator can audit coverage after).
182: 
183: ## Examples Of Briefs That Worked
184: 
185: Two reference briefs are linked from this skill's parent repo (when running inside the Skill Madness context):
186: 
187: - **Greenfield** — `THE-GAUNTLET.md` at the Skill Madness repo root. Single-prompt greenfield stress test for "Bazaar" (eBay × PayPal × Twitter). Called for bento-grid item cards, glassmorphism trust badges, dark-mode default, full responsive. The UI section is one phase of a larger build brief — the design language was opinion-dense even without an existing UI to diagnose.
188: - **Rebuild** — `UI-CHALLENGE.md` at the MarketsBeRigged repo root. Diagnoses the existing trading dashboard's "generic shadcn admin grid" failure mode and prescribes a Bloomberg × Linear × TradingView replacement that leads with the LLM-reasoning moat.
189: 
190: Both followed the structure above. Read them when in doubt about voice. The greenfield one shows how to lean into target styles; the rebuild one shows how to diagnose and replace.

=== skills/workflows/use-freellmapi/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: use-freellmapi
3: version: 1.4.0
4: description: |
5:   Wire any project or coding agent to FreeLLMAPI — a local proxy aggregating ~30 free LLM provider
6:   tiers behind one endpoint — so you can prototype without paying for API calls. Use to switch a
7:   project off paid OpenAI/Anthropic/etc. onto free models, point an app or CLI agent at a local LLM
8:   proxy, or stand up FreeLLMAPI itself. Speaks OpenAI, Anthropic Messages, native Gemini, and Ollama
9:   wire formats, covering Claude Code, Codex CLI, Gemini CLI, Cline, Aider, Continue, Zed, and
10:   JetBrains AI. Detects the project's LLM client, ensures the proxy is running, rewires base_url +
11:   api_key behind an env toggle, and verifies with a live test call. Trigger on "use freellmapi",
12:   "switch this to free models", "point this at freellmapi", "stop paying for openai here", "free llm
13:   proxy", "prototype without api costs", "run claude code on free models", "point claude
14:   code/codex/gemini cli at freellmapi", "free models for my coding agent".
15: requires_agent_teams: false
16: requires_claude_code: false
17: min_plan: starter
18: owns:
19:   directories: []
20:   patterns: []
21:   shared_read: ["*"]
22: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
23: composes_with: ["project-profiler", "model-adaptation", "use-pxpipe"]
24: spawned_by: []
25: ---
26: 
27: # use-freellmapi
28: 
29: Point a project at **FreeLLMAPI** — a local proxy that aggregates the free tiers of ~30 LLM providers
30: (Google, Groq, Cerebras, NVIDIA, Mistral, OpenRouter, GitHub Models, Cohere, Cloudflare, Z.ai,
31: ModelScope, Ollama, Kilo, OVH, AI Horde, and more) behind a single endpoint — roughly **4B tokens/month
32: across 251 model families / 358 endpoints**. A router picks the best available model per request and
33: fails over when one is rate-limited. The payoff: prototype against real models for free, with zero code
34: changes beyond a base URL and a key.
35: 
36: > **Don't hardcode the roster or the numbers into advice.** Providers come and go, the catalog
37: > self-updates from a signed feed, and free installs run on a 30-day catalog trail. Read the live
38: > `/v1/models` and the dashboard rather than trusting any list — including this one.
39: 
40: > **Tiering note:** a FreeLLMAPI project is the toolkit's one sanctioned multi-provider setup. The
41: > model & effort tiering policy (`model-adaptation`, *Model & effort tiering*) is provider-relative —
42: > within a normal build you pick tiers inside ONE provider's ladder and never mix vendors to save
43: > tokens — and FreeLLMAPI is its explicit carve-out: here the aggregated free tiers ARE the ladder,
44: > and the scarce resource is rate/quota headroom, not dollars.
45: 
46: ## The whole integration, in three facts
47: 
48: FreeLLMAPI is an **OpenAI-compatible** proxy. Wiring a project to it is almost always just three values:
49: 
50: | What | Value |
51: | --- | --- |
52: | **base_url** | `http://localhost:3001/v1` (default; `PORT` may differ). **Anthropic, Gemini, and Ollama clients use the bare origin** — `http://localhost:3001`, no `/v1` |
53: | **api_key** (bearer) | `freellmapi-…` — the install's *unified key* — or better, a **per-client key** (`sk-cp-…`) minted for this project |
54: | **model** | `"auto"` — let the router pick and fail over. Steer it per request with `"auto:fast"` / `"auto:smart"` / `"auto:reliable"` / `"auto:<profile-name>"`; pin one like `"gemini-2.5-flash"`; or `"fusion"` to blend a panel of models into one answer |
55: 
56: Because the surface is OpenAI-compatible, any OpenAI client library works unchanged — chat, streaming,
57: tool calling, vision, and embeddings all route through the same endpoint. But it is **not only**
58: OpenAI-shaped: the proxy also speaks the **native Anthropic Messages API** (`/v1/messages` — Claude Code
59: and the Anthropic SDKs), the **native Gemini API** (`/v1beta` — Gemini CLI), and an opt-in **Ollama**
60: surface (`/api/*` — Zed, JetBrains AI). Point each client at the wire format it already speaks instead
61: of forcing everything through chat completions. Everything past this point is about doing the swap
62: *cleanly, reversibly, and verified* — not about anything exotic.
63: 
64: > Read `references/capabilities.md` for the exact supported surface (all six wire formats, the virtual
65: > models `auto`/`fusion` and `auto:*` steering, embeddings families, vision, tools, structured outputs,
66: > media, the `/mcp` server, prompt compression, the response cache, analytics) and the short list of
67: > things it still does **not** do (moderation, `n > 1`, multi-tenant auth). Check it before promising a
68: > capability — and when it matters, confirm against `GET /v1/docs` on the user's own install, since
69: > older pinned images lag the catalog.
70: 
71: ## Workflow
72: 
73: Work top to bottom. Steps 1–2 stand up the proxy and confirm it can actually serve a request; steps
74: 3–5 rewire the project and prove it works end to end. Don't skip the verify — "it should work" is not
75: the same as a 200 with a completion in it.
76: 
77: ### Step 1 — Make sure the proxy is running
78: 
79: Probe the unauthenticated liveness endpoint:
80: 
81: ```bash
82: curl -fsS http://localhost:3001/api/ping
83: # {"status":"ok","timestamp":"..."}  → something is answering, but see the check below
84: ```
85: 
86: > **A 200 on `/api/ping` does not prove the *container* answered.** A stray host-side `npm run dev`
87: > from a source checkout binds `*:3001` on the IPv6 wildcard, which the OS prefers over the container's
88: > `127.0.0.1:3001` when resolving `localhost`. That dev server answers `/api/ping` happily but has no
89: > built client (`ENOENT … client/dist/index.html`, with a **host** path in the error) and its own empty
90: > database (a bogus "create an account" page) — which reads exactly like a wiped install after a
91: > rebuild. If the dashboard looks freshly-installed or errors on refresh, check who owns the port
92: > **before** touching any data:
93: >
94: > ```bash
95: > lsof -nP -iTCP:3001 -sTCP:LISTEN   # a `node` row next to `com.docker` is the bug
96: > ```
97: >
98: > Kill the `npm run dev` parent (not just the node child — `tsx watch` respawns it), then re-probe.
99: > Never reach for `docker compose down -v` to "reset" this; that is what actually destroys keys.
100: 
101: If nothing answers, install and start it with the official one-liner (needs Docker running):
102: 
103: ```bash
104: curl -fsSL https://freellmapi.co/install.sh | bash
105: ```
106: 
107: This sets up `~/freellmapi`, generates an at-rest `ENCRYPTION_KEY`, pulls
108: `ghcr.io/tashfeenahmed/freellmapi:latest`, starts the container on `:3001`, and waits for `/api/ping`.
109: Re-running is safe — it preserves the existing `.env`. When it finishes, probe `/api/ping` again to
110: confirm. If Docker isn't available, the project also ships a **desktop app** bundling the same server
111: (macOS, Windows installer or zip, Linux AppImage/deb/tar.xz — the repo's Releases page), or fall back
112: to a source install (`git clone` + `npm install` + `npm run dev`); see the *Local development* section
113: of the [repo README](https://github.com/tashfeenahmed/freellmapi).
114: 
115: If the user already runs it on a non-default port or another host, use that `base_url` everywhere below.
116: 
117: ### Step 2 — Get the unified key and make sure a provider can serve requests
118: 
119: **Unified key** (`freellmapi-…`): the install-wide bearer token. Get it from:
120: 
121: - the **Keys page header** at `http://localhost:3001` (the dashboard), or
122: - the first-run container logs:
123:   `cd ~/freellmapi && docker compose logs 2>&1 | grep -i "unified api key"`
124: 
125: Since v0.6.9 the Keys page can also mint **per-client keys** (`sk-cp-…`) — one per downstream app or
126: tool, independently revocable, each with an optional system prompt the proxy enforces server-side.
127: For wiring a single project, prefer minting one: analytics attribute traffic by key, and revoking it
128: later doesn't touch any other client's credential. Everything below works the same with either key.
129: 
130: **A fresh proxy has no provider keys, so chat requests fail until at least one provider is enabled.**
131: This is the one part you can't do for the user — adding keys and toggling providers happens in the
132: browser dashboard. Make it explicit and offer to wait. Two paths:
133: 
134: - **Fastest, zero-key smoke test:** on the dashboard, enable a **keyless** provider — as of v0.6.9
135:   that's **Kilo `:free`**, **OVH**, or **AI Horde**, which need no API key at all. Good enough to prove
136:   the pipe end to end in under a minute. (**Pollinations is no longer keyless** — it validates a key
137:   now, so don't offer it as the zero-key path. AI Horde is queue-based and can take tens of seconds.)
138: - **Real prototyping:** add free provider keys on the **Keys** page (Google AI Studio, Groq, Cerebras,
139:   etc. — each has a free signup), then reorder the **Fallback Chain** to taste.
140: 
141: Hold here until the user confirms at least one provider is enabled — otherwise Step 5 will fail with a
142: "no model available" error and look like a wiring bug when it isn't. The Keys page's **provider
143: checklist** (status icons per provider, flagging any that need a key but have none) is the quickest
144: way to confirm, beating guesswork about which toggle actually took.
145: 
146: ### Step 3 — Detect the target: coding agent, or application code?
147: 
148: **First ask which kind of target this is — the two paths diverge completely.**
149: 
150: **If the target is a coding agent or CLI** (Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Cline, Aider, Continue,
151: OpenCode, Goose, Qwen, Roo, Kilo, Crush, Cursor, Zed, JetBrains AI) — **do not hand-edit its config.**
152: The proxy ships generators that read the live catalog, merge with existing config, and write a
153: timestamped backup first:
154: 
155: ```bash
156: npx freellmapi setup-claude --url http://localhost:3001 --dry-run   # show the diff
157: npx freellmapi setup-claude --url http://localhost:3001             # apply, with backup
158: ```
159: 
160: Read `references/agent-clients.md` for the full command table, the per-client base URLs, and the traps
161: that waste the most time (Claude Code needs the **origin** not `/v1`, and `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` **not**
162: `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or it refuses to start; Ollama emulation is off by default and `open-loopback`
163: doesn't work in Docker). Then skip to Step 5 and verify — Step 4's env-toggle pattern is for
164: application code, not agent config.
165: 
166: **If the target is application code**, continue here. Find how the project talks to an LLM today so you
167: pick the right recipe and the smallest diff:
168: 
169: ```bash
170: # language + client library + where the client is built
171: # (quote the --include globs — zsh expands bare *.py before grep sees it and the command fails)
172: grep -rniE "openai|anthropic|langchain|llama_?index|@?ai-sdk|base_?url|api_?key" \
173:   --include="*.py" --include="*.ts" --include="*.tsx" --include="*.js" --include="*.mjs" . \
174:   | grep -viE "node_modules|/dist/|/build/" | head -40
175: ```
176: 
177: Determine: the language (Python / JS-TS), the client library, the file(s) where the client is
178: constructed, and where its `base_url` / `api_key` / `model` come from (env vars vs hardcoded). Then open
179: `references/recipes.md` and read **only the section for the detected stack** — it has copy-paste wiring
180: for OpenAI (Python/Node), LangChain, LlamaIndex, Vercel AI SDK, Continue, raw HTTP/curl, and the
181: Anthropic-SDK-via-`x-api-key` case.
182: 
183: ### Step 4 — Rewire it, reversibly (the env-toggle pattern)
184: 
185: The point of prototyping with free models is that you'll flip *back and forth* — free models for cheap
186: iteration, the paid provider for a quality check or a demo. A hardcoded swap forces a code edit every
187: time and risks losing the original config. So **drive the three values from environment variables and
188: keep the original provider as a documented fallback**, rather than editing call sites.
189: 
190: Concretely:
191: 
192: 1. Introduce (or reuse) env vars for the base URL, key, and model. Many OpenAI clients already read
193:    `OPENAI_BASE_URL` / `OPENAI_API_KEY` from the environment, so reusing those names is ideal. But "no
194:    code change at all" only holds when **both** are true: (a) the client is built with no explicit
195:    `base_url`/`api_key` args, **and** (b) something actually loads `.env` into the process — a shell
196:    `set -a; source .env`, a process manager's `env_file`, or a dotenv loader the app already calls.
197:    Plain Python and plain Node do **not** auto-read a `.env` file. If neither is true (very common — a
198:    prototype with the key hardcoded, and no dotenv), you will edit the call site and/or add a loader;
199:    that's expected, not a failure. Each recipe in `references/recipes.md` notes how its stack loads env.
200: 2. Point the client constructor at those env vars instead of literals (only if it wasn't already).
201: 3. In `.env`, set them to the FreeLLMAPI values and **keep the original provider's values commented
202:    right above them**, labeled, so flipping back is uncommenting two lines:
203: 
204:    ```dotenv
205:    # --- FreeLLMAPI (free, local proxy) — active ---
206:    OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:3001/v1
207:    OPENAI_API_KEY=freellmapi-xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx
208:    LLM_MODEL=auto
209: 
210:    # --- Original provider (paid) — flip back by swapping which block is active ---
211:    # OPENAI_BASE_URL=https://api.openai.com/v1
212:    # OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-...
213:    # LLM_MODEL=gpt-4o-mini
214:    ```
215: 
216: 4. Default the model to `"auto"` so the router chooses and fails over, but keep the option to pin a
217:    specific model where a call needs a known capability (e.g. a vision model).
218: 5. Write or update a committed **`.env.example`** with the same two labeled blocks but placeholder
219:    values (`freellmapi-xxxx`, `sk-...`). Without it, moving a hardcoded key into `.env` leaves the repo
220:    with no record of which env vars it now needs — the example is the documentation.
221: 
222: Show the user the `.env` block and the (usually tiny) code diff. Never write a real provider key into a
223: file that's tracked by git — confirm `.env` is gitignored, and if a key was previously hardcoded in
224: source, move it to `.env` as part of this step.
225: 
226: ### Step 5 — Verify with a live call
227: 
228: Prove the pipe works before declaring success. First a direct smoke test (substitute the real key):
229: 
230: ```bash
231: curl -i -s http://localhost:3001/v1/chat/completions \
232:   -H "Authorization: Bearer freellmapi-xxxx" \
233:   -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
234:   -d '{"model":"auto","messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Reply with the single word: ok"}]}'
235: ```
236: 
237: A healthy response is a `200` with a chat completion, and an **`X-Routed-Via: <platform>/<model>`**
238: header telling you which provider actually served it. Then run the **project's own** code path (its
239: test, its entry script, a representative call) so you've confirmed the app — not just curl — talks to
240: the proxy.
241: 
242: Map failures to causes instead of guessing:
243: 
244: | Symptom | Cause | Fix |
245: | --- | --- | --- |
246: | `401` invalid API key | wrong/missing unified key | re-copy it from the dashboard Keys header |
247: | `4xx` no model / no provider | no provider enabled on the proxy | back to Step 2 — enable a provider |
248: | `429` | the picked key is rate-limited | router fails over; add more provider keys for headroom |
249: | connection refused | proxy not running / wrong port | back to Step 1; check `PORT` |
250: 
251: ### Step 6 — Hand off
252: 
253: Tell the user, briefly:
254: 
255: - **How to flip back** — uncomment the original `.env` block (or re-run the agent's own config backup).
256: - **Where the dashboard is** (`http://localhost:3001`) and what's on it: **Models** (Chat / Embeddings /
257:   Image / Audio / Fusion tabs, with editable intelligence-and-speed ranks and per-model rate limits),
258:   **Playground** (now accepts image and text-file attachments), **Keys** (provider checklist,
259:   per-client `sk-cp-…` keys, per-key model scopes and proxy overrides, bulk key actions), **Agents**
260:   (per-tool setup blocks, Anthropic/Gemini family maps, Ollama emulation mode, URL tokens),
261:   **Analytics**, and **Premium**. Named **routing profiles** auto-sort a chain by
262:   intelligence/speed/budget and are selectable per request as `auto:<profile>`. Settings live in a
263:   sidebar dialog and include an automatic update check with release notes; a forgotten dashboard
264:   password is recoverable with a code printed in the server logs. The UI ships in 60 languages.
265: - **What the model values do** — `auto` routes and fails over, `auto:fast` / `auto:smart` /
266:   `auto:reliable` steer one request without touching the dashboard, `fusion` blends a panel of models
267:   for a quality bump on hard prompts.
268: - **What analytics will tell them later** — p50/p95 latency, time-to-first-token, success rate,
269:   estimated savings, and **pin-honor rate** (the number that answers "I asked for model X, why did I get
270:   Y?"), plus per-request failover traces.
271: - **The relevant not-supported caveats** from `references/capabilities.md` — moderation, `n > 1`, and
272:   multi-tenant auth won't route, so those calls still need the real provider. Legacy
273:   `/v1/completions`, image generation, text-to-speech, and fusion tool-calling **do** work on current
274:   releases; only image *input* to `/v1/responses` and to `fusion` is still missing.
275: - **If the catalog looks stale** — free installs run on a 30-day catalog trail (the live signed feed is
276:   the paid tier), so a missing new model or a provider whose free tier changed is usually catalog lag,
277:   not a wiring bug.
278: - **Optionally, prompt compression** — off by default, but worth mentioning for long agent sessions:
279:   it shrinks re-sent context before routing so more small-context models stay eligible.
280: 
281: ## References
282: 
283: - **`references/recipes.md`** — per-framework, copy-paste env-toggle wiring for **application code**.
284:   Read the one section that matches the detected stack; ignore the rest.
285: - **`references/agent-clients.md`** — wiring for **coding agents and CLIs**: the `npx freellmapi
286:   setup-*` generator table, Claude Code / Codex / Gemini CLI / Ollama-client / headerless setups, the
287:   MCP server, and the per-client traps. Read this whenever the target is a tool rather than a codebase.
288: - **`references/capabilities.md`** — exact supported surface across all six wire formats (chat,
289:   streaming, tools, vision, structured outputs, embeddings + families, Responses, legacy completions,
290:   media, transcription, Anthropic Messages, native Gemini, Ollama, `/mcp`, URL tokens, ops endpoints),
291:   plus routing behavior and `auto:*` steering, prompt compression, the response cache, analytics, the
292:   not-supported list, and the gotchas (sticky sessions, fresh-install-has-no-keys, which providers are
293:   actually keyless, catalog lag, the `X-Routed-Via` / `X-Fallback-Trail` / `X-Request-ID` headers and
294:   their percent-encoding trap).

=== skills/workflows/use-pxpipe/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: use-pxpipe
3: version: 1.0.1
4: description: |
5:   Wire the Claude Code agent harness to pxpipe — a local, loopback-only proxy that renders re-sent
6:   bulk (system prompt, tool docs, older history) into dense PNGs so long sessions cost roughly half
7:   to a third as much in input tokens, with a live savings dashboard and a one-line off switch. Use
8:   when the user wants to cut Claude Code's own token bill, enable the token-saver proxy for a long or
9:   multi-agent run, point the harness at pxpipe, or check whether compression is actually paying.
10:   Opt-in and reversible: ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL behind an env toggle, model scope deferred to
11:   model-adaptation's allowlist, verification that compression fired AND the prompt cache stayed warm.
12:   Trigger on "use pxpipe", "token saver proxy", "compress my context", "cut session input tokens",
13:   "optical context compression", "my Claude Code sessions are expensive". Sibling use-freellmapi
14:   rewires an app's LLM client; this one rewires the agent harness itself.
15: compatibility: "Claude Code; requires Bash + Node/npx (proxy runs via npx pxpipe-proxy)"
16: requires_agent_teams: false
17: requires_claude_code: true
18: min_plan: starter
19: owns:
20:   directories: []
21:   patterns: []
22:   shared_read: ["*"]
23: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Grep", "Glob"]
24: composes_with: ["model-adaptation", "use-freellmapi", "madness"]
25: spawned_by: []
26: ---
27: 
28: # use-pxpipe
29: 
30: Point the **agent harness** at [pxpipe](https://github.com/teamchong/pxpipe) — a local proxy on
31: `127.0.0.1:47821` that rewrites the bulky, re-sent parts of every Claude Code request (system
32: prompt, tool docs, collapsed older history) into dense PNG image blocks before the request leaves
33: the machine. An image's token cost is fixed by pixel area, not character count, so token-dense
34: material (code, JSON, logs) carries roughly 3× more characters per token as pixels than as text.
35: Measured end to end on real sessions: **~59–70% fewer input tokens** (one live A/B: 856k → 277k).
36: Responses stream back untouched — pxpipe compresses the request, never the model's output.
37: 
38: This is the harness-level sibling of `use-freellmapi`, one level up the stack:
39: 
40: | | `use-freellmapi` | `use-pxpipe` |
41: | --- | --- | --- |
42: | What gets rewired | The **app's** LLM client (base_url + key in source) | The **agent harness** (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` env) |
43: | Goal | Prototype the app for free | Cut the coding *session's* input tokens |
44: | Loss profile | None (real models, just free) | Lossy gist on old bulk; recent turns stay text |
45: | Flip back | Env toggle in the app | `unset ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` — one line |
46: 
47: > **Opt-in, never vendored.** Upstream is young (~8 weeks of history, v0.8.x). This skill wires it
48: > as a reversible environment in front of the harness; it never embeds pxpipe code into a build or
49: > makes it a project dependency. If upstream breaks, one `unset` restores stock behavior.
50: 
51: ## The whole integration, in three facts
52: 
53: | What | Value |
54: | --- | --- |
55: | **base_url** | `http://127.0.0.1:47821` → `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` (Claude Code) |
56: | **model scope** | `PXPIPE_MODELS` — default `claude-fable-5` only; widen ONLY per the allowlist in `model-adaptation` |
57: | **dashboard / kill switch** | `http://127.0.0.1:47821/` — live savings, passthrough toggle (`POST /api/compression`) |
58: 
59: ## Workflow
60: 
61: ### Step 1 — Check the model allowlist first
62: 
63: pxpipe images context that the model must then *read back from pixels*, and models differ sharply
64: at that: the technique is safe only for models measured to read production-density renders. The
65: governed allowlist lives in **`model-adaptation`** (the toolkit's model-and-effort authority) —
66: read its **"Image-proxy model allowlist"** section, and the current list with measured read rates
67: in its `references/model-effort-tiering.md` (*Image-proxy allowlist — current state*), before
68: wiring anything, and never widen `PXPIPE_MODELS` beyond it. Imaging a model that misreads the render produces confident wrong
69: answers, not errors. The upstream default (`claude-fable-5` only) is the measured-safe floor; if
70: the session's model isn't on the allowlist, pxpipe passes requests through uncompressed
71: (`reason: unsupported_model`) — harmless, but pointless, so stop here and say so.
72: 
73: ### Step 2 — Make sure the proxy is running
74: 
75: ```bash
76: curl -fsS http://127.0.0.1:47821/api/stats.json > /dev/null && echo "pxpipe up"
77: ```
78: 
79: If nothing answers, start it (Node 18+):
80: 
81: ```bash
82: npx pxpipe-proxy
83: ```
84: 
85: It binds `127.0.0.1:47821` and serves the dashboard at `/`. Leave it running in its own terminal
86: or a background process; re-probe the stats endpoint to confirm.
87: 
88: **Security posture — loopback only.** The dashboard is unauthenticated and serves captured request
89: context plus a kill switch. Never set `HOST=0.0.0.0` on a shared machine; off-host exposure is
90: deliberate opt-in upstream and logs a warning. (A Cloudflare Workers deployment exists but requires
91: `PXPIPE_WORKER_SECRET`; it is out of scope for this skill — local wiring only.)
92: 
93: ### Step 3 — Wire the harness behind a one-line-reversible toggle
94: 
95: Set the env var in the shell that launches Claude Code — never in checked-in project config:
96: 
97: ```bash
98: # on — this shell's Claude Code sessions route through pxpipe
99: export ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:47821
100: 
101: # off — one line, full stock behavior restored
102: unset ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL
103: ```
104: 
105: Scope it to the session: an `export` in the launching shell (or a wrapper alias the user opts
106: into) beats writing it into `~/.zshrc`, because the whole point is that any given session chooses.
107: If the user wants it durable, put the pair in their shell profile as two labeled lines they can
108: comment-swap — same pattern as `use-freellmapi`'s `.env` blocks.
109: 
110: **Byte-exact escape hatch:** work that must be verbatim-faithful can run on a subagent model
111: outside the image scope — e.g. `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL=claude-sonnet-5` (the Mid tier in
112: `model-adaptation`'s tiering ladder) routes that subagent's traffic through the proxy untouched —
113: a genuine passthrough as long as that model stays off the image-proxy allowlist, so re-check the
114: allowlist when picking the hatch model.
115: 
116: ### Step 4 — Verify: compression fired AND the cache stayed warm
117: 
118: "It's wired" is not the claim; "it's saving" is. Two checks, both against
119: `~/.pxpipe/events.jsonl` or the dashboard — read `references/verification.md` for the exact
120: fields and the failure signatures:
121: 
122: 1. **Compression fired.** After a couple of real turns, recent event rows must show compression
123:    applied — not passthrough with `reason: unsupported_model` (wrong model scope) or a gate
124:    decline (session too small; fine, it will kick in as history grows).
125: 2. **The prompt cache stayed warm.** A mis-set history boundary re-keys Anthropic's prompt cache
126:    every turn and pays the 1.25× `cache_create` rate on the whole history repeatedly — the
127:    historical **−250% "savings"** regression. Warm looks like: `history_image_sha8` stable across
128:    consecutive turns, `cache_read_tokens` dominating `cache_create_tokens` after the first turn.
129:    The dashboard reports savings **unfloored** — a negative number is honest and means turn the
130:    toggle off and investigate, not "close enough".
131: 
132: ### Step 5 — Hand off
133: 
134: Tell the user, briefly:
135: 
136: - **Dashboard:** `http://127.0.0.1:47821/` — live per-session savings, image previews, model-scope
137:   chips (chips mutate scope in-memory only; a restart reverts to `PXPIPE_MODELS`/default).
138: - **Kill switches, fastest first:** `unset ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` (hard off, this shell);
139:   dashboard passthrough toggle or `PXPIPE_DISABLE=1` (proxy stays up, compression off, no
140:   restart); `PXPIPE_MODELS=off` (scope-level off).
141: - **The gist-only caveat — say it every time:** imaged bulk is safe to navigate by *gist*
142:   (decisions, paths, names, state) but must **never be the sole copy of anything needed
143:   byte-exact** — secrets, hashes, exact IDs, numeric ledgers. Vision is not OCR: misreads surface
144:   as confident plausible values, not errors. pxpipe's defenses (recent turns stay text; a text
145:   "factsheet" of exact identifiers rides beside each image) mitigate but do not repeal this.
146:   Byte-exact work belongs on the text tail or a non-imaged model (Step 3's escape hatch).
147: 
148: ## When NOT to use it
149: 
150: - **Byte-exact-critical workloads** where imaged history would be the only copy of secrets,
151:   hashes, or exact numbers — the loss profile is wrong for them.
152: - **Short sessions** — the profitability gate needs enough history to amortize; tiny sessions pass
153:   through anyway, so the wiring is pure overhead.
154: - **Prompts already ~98% cache-read** — there's little uncached bulk left to image.
155: - **Latency-sensitive one-shot calls** — PNG encoding adds noticeable latency to a single large
156:   request; it disappears into long agent sessions.
157: 
158: ## Triggering test
159: 
160: - MUST fire: "enable the token-saver proxy for this overnight build" · "my Claude Code sessions
161:   are burning tokens, compress the context" · "point the harness at pxpipe".
162: - Must NOT fire: "switch this project to free models" (that's `use-freellmapi` — it rewires the
163:   app's client, not the harness).
164: 
165: ## References
166: 
167: - **`references/verification.md`** — the events.jsonl fields to check, warm-vs-shredded cache
168:   signatures, the −250% regression story, the full kill-switch matrix, and how to read the
169:   dashboard's savings numbers honestly (pctInput vs pctTotal, unfloored negatives, refusal-flagged
170:   rows).

=== skills/workflows/website-walkthrough-video/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: website-walkthrough-video
3: version: 1.2.0
4: description: |
5:   Generate a smooth scrolling walkthrough video of an entire website — capture
6:   every page full-length at desktop and mobile widths, then render an mp4 that
7:   pans down each page, stitched into one continuous tour. Use whenever the user
8:   wants a video tour, demo reel, screen-recording, walkthrough, "show me the
9:   site as a video", a marketing/launch clip, a before/after of a redesign, or a
10:   desktop + mobile walkthrough mp4 — even if they don't say the word "video" but
11:   describe wanting to show the whole site scrolling. Triggers on "walkthrough
12:   video", "video tour of the site", "record the site", "demo video of all the
13:   pages", "scrolling screen recording", "make a reel of the site", "capture the
14:   whole site as a video", "smooth scroll-through". Also reach for it after a
15:   build or redesign wraps. Prefer it over hand-driving the Playwright MCP
16:   screenshot-by-screenshot — the bundled scripts capture and pan
17:   deterministically.
18: compatibility: Claude Code; requires ffmpeg + Node.js + Playwright (Chromium)
19: requires_claude_code: true
20: requires_agent_teams: false
21: min_plan: starter
22: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Edit", "Bash", "Glob", "Grep"]
23: owns:
24:   directories: []
25:   patterns: []
26: ---
27: 
28: # Website Walkthrough Video
29: 
30: Turn a running website into a polished, smooth scrolling walkthrough video — one
31: continuous mp4 per device (desktop + mobile) that pans down each page in turn, the
32: way a person would scroll through it, with a small `// PAGE | MODE` label in the
33: corner.
34: 
35: ## What it produces
36: 
37: For a site with N routes you get `walkthrough-desktop.mp4` and
38: `walkthrough-mobile.mp4` in your output dir. Each is N clips concatenated: per page,
39: a 1-second hold at the top, a smooth linear scroll to the bottom, a 1-second hold,
40: then on to the next page. Pages that fit in one viewport just hold for a few seconds.
41: 
42: ## How it works (two stages)
43: 
44: 1. **Capture** (`scripts/capture.mjs`, Playwright): for each device width and each
45:    route, set the viewport, navigate, force lazy `<img>` elements to load eagerly,
46:    force `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` to defeat scroll-reveal animations,
47:    scroll the whole page once so remaining lazy images and on-scroll animations
48:    fire, return to top, take a **full-page** screenshot (tall — the entire page,
49:    not just the fold), and wait for all images to finish loading. It also samples
50:    the page background color so the video never flashes black. Writes the PNGs
51:    plus a `manifest.json`.
52: 
53: 2. **Render** (`scripts/build.mjs`, ffmpeg): for each screenshot, build a clip that
54:    overlays the tall image on a viewport-sized window and animates its vertical
55:    position from top to bottom — that pan *is* the simulated scroll. Then concatenate
56:    the clips into one video per device. The smoothness comes from 60fps + CRF 18 +
57:    lanczos scaling + a pixel-floored pan offset (no sub-pixel shimmer on text).
58: 
59: `scripts/walkthrough.mjs` runs both stages from one config file.
60: 
61: ## Prerequisites
62: 
63: - **ffmpeg / ffprobe** on PATH (`ffmpeg -version`). On macOS: `brew install ffmpeg`.
64: - **Node.js** (any recent version).
65: - **Playwright + Chromium**. Install the npm package once inside the skill:
66: 
67:    ```bash
68:    cd <this-skill>/scripts && npm install
69:    ```
70: 
71:    Chromium is often already cached from other Playwright use; if capture complains
72:   about a missing browser, run `npx playwright install chromium`.
73: 
74: ## Workflow
75: 
76: Follow these steps. The only real thinking is step 2 — getting the route list right.
77: 
78: ### 1. Make sure the site is running and reachable
79: 
80: You need a live URL (local dev server, Docker container, or a public URL). Confirm
81: it responds, e.g. `curl -sI http://localhost:8080 | head -1`. Note the base URL.
82: 
83: ### 2. Discover the routes and write a config
84: 
85: The config is the whole interface. Minimal version — just the base URL, where to put
86: output, and the ordered list of pages:
87: 
88: ```json
89: {
90:   "title": "Sovereign Sampson",
91:   "baseUrl": "http://localhost:8080",
92:   "outDir": "./walkthrough",
93:   "routes": [
94:     { "path": "/", "label": "Home" },
95:     { "path": "/about/", "label": "About" },
96:     { "path": "/dispatches/", "label": "Dispatches" },
97:     { "path": "/contact/", "label": "Contact" }
98:   ]
99: }
100: ```
101: 
102: To find the routes, in order of preference: ask the user if they have a page list;
103: read the site's nav/menu (fetch the homepage and pull the primary nav links); check a
104: `sitemap.xml`; or look at the route definitions in the codebase. Put them in the order
105: you want them to appear in the tour (usually nav order, home first). `label` is what
106: shows on screen — keep it short. `slug` is optional (derived from the path).
107: 
108: **Route completeness is the whole job — verify it before capturing.** A "walkthrough of
109: the site" means EVERY route class, including detail pages behind dynamic routes
110: (`/designs/[id]`, `/blog/[slug]`, product pages): include at least one representative
111: instance of each. Cross-check the route list against the codebase's route definitions
112: (e.g. `src/pages/**` in Astro/Next) — nav links alone under-count, and a "walkthrough"
113: missing whole page types has caused real (and justified) anger. State the route count
114: and list before rendering so any gap is visible up front, not after delivery.
115: 
116: `outDir` is resolved relative to the config file. A path the project already
117: gitignores is a good choice (videos are large and regenerable).
118: 
119: See `assets/example-site.json` for a starting point. The full set of overridable
120: fields (device widths, pan speed, fps, fonts, custom modes) is documented in
121: `references/tuning.md` — you rarely need them for a first pass.
122: 
123: ### 3. Run it
124: 
125: ```bash
126: cd <this-skill>/scripts
127: node walkthrough.mjs --config /abs/path/to/site.json
128: ```
129: 
130: Capture takes a few seconds per page per device; rendering is fast. Output lands in
131: `outDir` as `walkthrough-desktop.mp4` and `walkthrough-mobile.mp4`, alongside the
132: screenshots and `manifest.json`.
133: 
134: `--base-url <url>` and `--out-dir <dir>` override the config at the command line — handy
135: for pointing the same route list at local/staging/prod, or for a project wrapper script
136: that reads the base URL from its own env (e.g. a repo's `.env`) and supplies an absolute
137: output path. With `--base-url` set, the config file can omit `baseUrl`.
138: 
139: ### 4. Review the result
140: 
141: **Actually watch the videos** (or open them for the user) before declaring success —
142: smoothness and framing are visual judgments a file size can't confirm. Check: does
143: each page scroll smoothly end to end, is text legible, are the holds long enough to
144: read, is anything cut off, do any pages 404 or render half-loaded?
145: 
146: ### 5. Iterate fast with `--skip-capture`
147: 
148: Tuning the motion doesn't require re-screenshotting. Edit `speed` / `maxPan` /
149: `fontSize` in the generated `manifest.json` (or in the config and re-capture), then:
150: 
151: ```bash
152: node walkthrough.mjs --config /abs/path/to/site.json --skip-capture
153: ```
154: 
155: This re-renders from the existing PNGs in seconds. Common adjustments:
156: 
157: | Symptom | Fix |
158: |---|---|
159: | Scroll feels too fast | Lower `speed` (e.g. desktop 200 → 130) |
160: | Long pages drag on | Lower `maxPan` (caps the longest clip) |
161: | Label too small/large | Adjust `fontSize` per mode |
162: | Black bars on short pages | Set `bg` to the page color, e.g. `"0xFFFFFF"` |
163: | A page came out blank/half-loaded | Bump `timeout`, or that route needs auth/data |
164: 
165: Everything tunable, plus the device-mode schema and ffmpeg internals, is in
166: `references/tuning.md`.
167: 
168: ## Notes
169: 
170: - **`reducedMotion` defaults ON.** The capture script always sends
171:   `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` unless `config.reducedMotion` is explicitly
172:   set to `false`. This defeats scroll-reveal / IntersectionObserver animations
173:   that leave whole sections at opacity:0 in the full-page shot. Previously this
174:   was handled only by the opt-in `injectCss` hatch, which silently did nothing
175:   when omitted — so the blank bands came back every time. Pass `reducedMotion:
176:   false` when you are deliberately capturing motion.
177: - **Lazy images are forced eager.** The script flips all `<img loading="lazy">`
178:   to `eager` + `decoding=sync` before and after scrolling, then waits up to 20s
179:   for every image to report `complete && naturalWidth > 0`. This prevents empty
180:   boxes in the screenshot from images near the bottom of long pages.
181: - **Full-page screenshots with sticky/fixed headers** can repeat the header down the
182:   page in some sites. If you see that, see `references/tuning.md` for the CSS-injection
183:   knob to neutralize sticky positioning before capture.
184: - **Auth-gated pages**: capture runs a fresh browser context with no login. For pages
185:   behind auth you'll need to extend `capture.mjs` to set cookies/storage state — out of
186:   scope for the default flow; capture the public pages and note the gap to the user.
187: - **Output is large.** mp4s and full-page PNGs are big and regenerable — keep `outDir`
188:   gitignored.

=== skills/workflows/wiki-research/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: wiki-research
3: version: 2.2.0
4: description: |
5:   Read the project's Obsidian-style wiki (index.md + wiki/) BEFORE any codebase exploration, repo-deep-dive, or raw source reading. Reading 3-4 wiki pages (~2,000 tokens) typically replaces crawling raw source directories (~100,000-500,000 tokens). Always invoke when orchestrator, a role agent, or code-review needs project context, architecture, component knowledge, or design decisions before touching files. Trigger on "how does X work", "understand the architecture", "review this code", or "build X" when project context matters. Skip only for purely mechanical tasks (rename a variable, fix a typo) where zero project understanding is needed.
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["wiki/", "index.md"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Glob", "Grep"]
14: composes_with: ["repo-deep-dive", "llm-wiki", "project-profiler"]
15: spawned_by: ["orchestrator", "code-review-agent"]
16: ---
17: 
18: # Wiki-First Research Protocol
19: 
20: An Obsidian-style wiki is a compiled, cross-linked knowledge base synthesized from raw source material. Reading it is an order of magnitude cheaper than re-reading raw sources — and faster than re-deriving understanding the hard way.
21: 
22: ## Why Wiki-First
23: 
24: | Source | Typical token cost | When to use |
25: |--------|-------------------|-------------|
26: | `index.md` scan | ~300–600 tokens | Always — find what's covered |
27: | 1 wiki entity/concept page | ~300–800 tokens | Targeted knowledge |
28: | 1 wiki source page | ~600–1,500 tokens | Provenance + raw doc locations |
29: | 1 raw source directory (10–14 docs) | ~30,000–200,000 tokens | Only when wiki gaps exist |
30: | Full codebase exploration | 200,000–500,000+ tokens | Last resort |
31: 
32: Reading the wiki index + 3 pages costs roughly the same as scanning a single mid-sized source file. It almost always covers what you need.
33: 
34: ## Step 1 — Detect the Wiki
35: 
36: Check if a wiki exists before doing anything else:
37: 
38: ```bash
39: # Fast check: does wiki structure exist at the project root?
40: ls index.md wiki/ 2>/dev/null | head -5
41: 
42: # Or check CLAUDE.md for a wiki path declaration
43: grep -i "wiki" CLAUDE.md 2>/dev/null | grep -i "path\|root\|location" | head -3
44: ```
45: 
46: If the wiki lives outside the current working directory (a separate research repo, a sibling directory), the project's `CLAUDE.md` should declare the wiki root — see "Pointing at an external wiki" below.
47: 
48: If no wiki exists → skip this protocol entirely and proceed with normal exploration.
49: If a wiki is found → continue.
50: 
51: ## Step 2 — Read index.md
52: 
53: `index.md` is the navigation layer. It contains one-line summaries of every wiki page, organized by category. Reading it takes seconds and tells you exactly which pages are relevant — don't skip this.
54: 
55: Look for entries matching:
56: - The systems/components your task involves (entities)
57: - The patterns/principles that apply (concepts)
58: - Any known source collections you'd otherwise re-read (sources)
59: 
60: ## Step 3 — Read Targeted Pages (2–4 pages max)
61: 
62: Based on index.md summaries, read the pages most relevant to your task:
63: 
64: **Entity pages** (`wiki/entities/<name>.md`) — for specific systems, platforms, or components
65: **Concept pages** (`wiki/concepts/<name>.md`) — for patterns, principles, and architectural ideas
66: **Overview** (`wiki/overview.md`) — only if you need broad ecosystem context
67: **Source pages** (`wiki/sources/<name>.md`) — only if you need research provenance or raw doc pointers
68: 
69: Each page ends with a `## Related` section. Follow those links only if they're directly relevant — don't spider the entire wiki. Stop when your question is answered.
70: 
71: ## Step 4 — Escalate Only When Needed
72: 
73: After reading targeted pages, one of three situations applies:
74: 
75: **Wiki covers it** → You're done. Proceed with your task using wiki knowledge.
76: 
77: **Wiki has a gap** (page flags "not yet documented", contradiction exists, or your specific question wasn't covered):
78: - When synthesizing 3+ sources, apply the **contradiction-finding** move (`references/contradiction-finding.md`) before writing your summary.
79: - Check the relevant source page for raw doc pointers
80: - Read the specific raw files identified — not entire directories
81: - Return to your task once the gap is filled
82: 
83: **Topic isn't in the wiki at all**:
84: - Check `CLAUDE.md` for a source map or directory pointer
85: - Proceed with targeted exploration (grep/glob first, not broad reads)
86: 
87: ## Standard Lookup Cost
88: 
89: ```text
90: 1. Read index.md            →  ~500 tokens   (mandatory)
91: 2. Read 2–3 targeted pages  →  ~1,500 tokens (pick relevant ones)
92: 3. Source page if needed    →  ~1,000 tokens (optional)
93: ──────────────────────────────────────────────
94: Total:  ~3,000 tokens  ←→  1 small source file
95: ```
96: 
97: ## Wiki Page Structure
98: 
99: Every page should follow this format — knowing this helps you skim efficiently:
100: 
101: ```markdown
102: ---
103: title: Page Title
104: type: entity | concept | comparison | source | overview
105: tags: [...]
106: sources: [...]
107: ---
108: 
109: # Page Title
110: One-paragraph summary.         ← Read this. Usually enough.
111: 
112: ## Sections                    ← Read what's relevant to your task.
113: 
114: ## Related
115: - [[linked-page]] — why linked  ← Scan for follow-up reads.
116: ```
117: 
118: The opening paragraph is often sufficient. Read sections selectively.
119: 
120: ## Standard Wiki Directory Layout
121: 
122: ```text
123: <wiki-root>/
124: ├── index.md          ← TOC, start here always
125: └── wiki/
126:     ├── overview.md   ← Ecosystem synthesis and roadmap
127:     ├── entities/     ← Named systems, platforms, components
128:     ├── concepts/     ← Architectural patterns and principles
129:     ├── comparisons/  ← Side-by-side analyses
130:     └── sources/      ← Per-source research summaries
131: ```
132: 
133: This skill assumes that layout. If a project uses a different convention (a flat `docs/` folder, GitBook, MkDocs, Docusaurus), the protocol still applies but the file paths change — read the project's `CLAUDE.md` or top-level docs index to learn the structure first.
134: 
135: ## Pointing at an external wiki
136: 
137: Many projects keep their research wiki in a separate repository so source code stays clean. When that's the case, the project's `CLAUDE.md` should declare the external root, for example:
138: 
139: ```markdown
140: ## Wiki
141: 
142: This project's research wiki lives at `~/Research/MyProjectWiki/`.
143: - Index: `~/Research/MyProjectWiki/index.md`
144: - Wiki pages: `~/Research/MyProjectWiki/wiki/`
145: - Raw source archives: `~/Research/MyProjectWiki/sources/`
146: ```
147: 
148: When a wiki is external, swap the Step 1 detection commands for `ls <wiki-root>/index.md <wiki-root>/wiki/` against the path the project declares.
149: 
150: ## Multi-cluster wikis
151: 
152: A single wiki can house multiple unrelated knowledge clusters — for example, a research repo that documents one team's platform alongside another team's product. Each cluster typically has its own `index.md` section and its pages don't link across clusters. When working on a task in one cluster, restrict your reading to that cluster's pages — cross-cluster wandering wastes tokens.
153: 
154: If the project uses this pattern, its `CLAUDE.md` should call out which cluster contains what, with any "looks like X but is actually Y" gotchas listed up front.
155: 
156: ## Anti-Patterns
157: 
158: | Anti-Pattern | Why It Fails | Do This Instead |
159: |---|---|---|
160: | Reading raw source directories first | Burns 100k+ tokens before knowing what you need | Always check the wiki first; raw source is escalation only |
161: | Skipping `index.md` | You read the wrong pages and miss key context | Always start with `index.md` — it costs ~500 tokens |
162: | Following every `## Related` link | Token spiral; loses thread of original task | Follow only links directly relevant to current task |
163: | Ignoring the wiki when CLAUDE.md mentions one | Defeats the entire optimization | If wiki is declared, use it — escalate to raw source only on gaps |
164: 
165: ## Reference Documents
166: 
167: - **`references/contradiction-finding.md`** — thinking move for surfacing where sources disagree before writing a synthesis (use when combining 3+ sources).

=== skills/workflows/work-item-brief/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: work-item-brief
3: version: 1.1.1
4: description: |
5:   Produce a durable, agent-ready work-item brief that captures intent precisely enough for an autonomous agent to grab and finish unattended. The brief excludes brittle references (file paths, line numbers, "the file we just edited") and instead names behavioral acceptance criteria, types/signatures, and explicit out-of-scope. Pairs with Beads or any ticket tracker. Use after grill-me or plan-builder, before dispatching. Trigger on "make a work-item brief", "write the agent brief", "agent-ready ticket", "make this dispatchable", "package this for an agent", "brief this".
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: ["briefs/**/*.md"]
12:   shared_read: []
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Write", "Grep", "Glob"]
14: composes_with: ["grill-me", "plan-builder", "contract-author"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # Work-Item Brief
19: 
20: Produce a durable, agent-ready brief that an autonomous agent can grab and finish without a human in the loop. The brief is the *context* artifact. The work-item (Beads ID, status, owner, deadline) is the *contract* — it lives elsewhere and updates on a different cadence.
21: 
22: **Announce at start:** "Using work-item-brief to package this for an agent."
23: 
24: ## The Forbidden List
25: 
26: Durable briefs MUST NOT contain:
27: 
28: - File paths (`src/billing/cancel.ts`) — files get renamed, moved, deleted
29: - Line numbers (`line 142`) — code shifts on every commit
30: - Ephemeral references ("the file we just edited", "the function above", "as discussed")
31: - Snapshots of code blocks copied from the current tree
32: - Dates relative to "now" ("yesterday's change", "the recent refactor")
33: 
34: These all go stale fast and turn the brief into a lie. Use **concept-level references** instead: type names, interface names, function signatures, behavior. The agent finds the files by grepping for the names. See `references/brief-format.md` for the canonical template.
35: 
36: ## Required Sections
37: 
38: Every brief has exactly these sections, in this order:
39: 
40: 1. **Title** — one line, declarative, no ticket prefix
41: 2. **Intent** — 1-2 sentences naming the user problem this solves
42: 3. **Behavioral acceptance criteria** — bulleted; what the system DOES after this lands, observable from outside
43: 4. **Key interfaces** — types and signatures touched, by name only
44: 5. **Out of scope** — explicit list of things this brief does NOT include
45: 6. **AFK/HITL classification** — one of two, declared at the bottom
46: 
47: Missing sections are a defect. An agent reading a brief without out-of-scope will expand scope.
48: 
49: ## Key Interfaces Pattern
50: 
51: Name the types and signatures. Do not name files. Example:
52: 
53: ```text
54: Key interfaces:
55: - `CancellationPolicy` (existing type) — gains a `gracePeriodDays: number` field
56: - `applyCancellation(subscriptionId, policy): CancellationOutcome` — new function
57: - `CancellationOutcome` — new sum type: `Refunded | GraceStarted | Denied`
58: ```
59: 
60: The agent runs `grep -r CancellationPolicy` to find the file. The brief stays valid through every rename, restructure, and refactor. **Do not write paths — write names.**
61: 
62: ## AFK/HITL Classification
63: 
64: Every brief declares one:
65: 
66: - **AFK-ready** — the agent can finish unattended. Acceptance criteria are objectively verifiable (tests pass, output shape matches, behavior is observable). No design ambiguity.
67: - **HITL-required** — a human must review at least one decision. Triggered by: design ambiguity, security risk, UX judgment, irreversible data migration, public API shape choices.
68: 
69: If you can't decide, it's HITL-required. The full rubric with worked examples lives in `references/afk-hitl-rubric.md`.
70: 
71: ## Out-of-Scope as Concept Files
72: 
73: When a related decision keeps surfacing but doesn't belong in this brief, write a separate concept-level file (e.g., `out-of-scope/refund-window-policy.md`). One concept per file. Future triage uses similarity to surface these when a new ticket touches the same concept. Do not bury concepts inside ticket bodies — they get lost. Pattern in `references/out-of-scope-pattern.md`.
74: 
75: ## Process
76: 
77: 1. Read the grilling output, plan, or conversation that produced this brief.
78: 2. Draft the six required sections.
79: 3. Strip every path, line number, and ephemeral reference. Replace with type/function names.
80: 4. Classify AFK or HITL. If unsure, HITL.
81: 5. Save to `briefs/<short-slug>.md`.
82: 6. If out-of-scope items have weight, spawn concept files alongside.
83: 
84: ## Composition
85:
