86: - After `grill-me` (the user has interrogated the idea) → write the brief.
87: - After `plan-builder` (a multi-component plan exists) → write one brief per discrete work item.
88: - Before `contract-author` (interfaces still fuzzy) → the brief seeds the contract; contract-author makes it machine-readable.
89: 
90: ## Closing Reminder
91: 
92: **Forbidden, again: no file paths, no line numbers, no "the file we just touched."** The brief outlives the current tree. If the agent needs to find code, it grep's for the type name. This rule is load-bearing — every brief that violates it becomes a stale ticket within a sprint.

=== skills/workflows/yagni-gate/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: yagni-gate
3: version: 1.0.1
4: description: >-
5:   Gate every build behind a persistent YAGNI ladder — the laziest senior dev in
6:   the room, adapted from DietrichGebert/ponytail (MIT). Before any code: does
7:   this need to exist, can something already present do it, can it be one line —
8:   only then the minimum that works. Never lazy about trust-boundary validation,
9:   data-loss error handling, security, accessibility, or understanding the
10:   problem first; every non-trivial change leaves one runnable check. Persists
11:   until 'stop yagni-gate' or 'normal mode'. Use when code keeps getting
12:   over-built, a tiny ask balloons into scaffolding, or you want a standing
13:   minimal-code gate on a session. Trigger on: 'yagni-gate', '/yagni-gate',
14:   'yagni mode', 'YAGNI', 'stop over-building', 'over-engineered', 'keep it
15:   minimal', 'minimum code', 'lazy mode', 'don't gold-plate', 'ponytail', 'less
16:   code'.
17: requires_agent_teams: false
18: requires_claude_code: false
19: min_plan: starter
20: owns:
21:   directories: []
22:   patterns: []
23:   shared_read: ["*"]
24: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Glob"]
25: composes_with: ["caveman", "architecture-rescue", "plan-intake"]
26: spawned_by: []
27: ---
28: 
29: # yagni-gate
30: 
31: > Adapted from `DietrichGebert/ponytail` (MIT) — the "laziest senior dev in the
32: > room" ruleset. Same ladder, same guardrails; examples adapted to this toolkit.
33: 
34: The build-discipline sibling of `caveman`: caveman compresses what you *say*,
35: yagni-gate compresses what you *build*. The best code is the code never written.
36: 
37: ## Persistence
38: 
39: Active for EVERY code-producing response once triggered. Off only when the user
40: says "stop yagni-gate", "normal mode", or "be normal again". Composes with
41: `caveman` — run both for minimum talk and minimum build.
42: 
43: ## First: understand the problem
44: 
45: The ladder runs *after* you understand the problem, not instead of it. Read the
46: task fully and trace the real flow end-to-end before picking a rung — a
47: wrong-rung shortcut ships a minimal solution to the wrong problem, and that
48: rework costs more than every line the shortcut saved.
49: 
50: ## The ladder
51: 
52: Before writing code, stop at the FIRST rung that holds:
53: 
54: 1. **Does this need to exist at all?** No → skip it (YAGNI)
55: 2. **Already in this codebase?** Reuse the helper/util/pattern — don't rewrite it
56: 3. **Stdlib does it?** Use it
57: 4. **Native platform feature covers it?** Use it
58: 5. **Already-installed dependency solves it?** Use it
59: 6. **Can it be one line?** Make it one line
60: 7. **Only then:** write the minimum that works
61: 
62: Corollaries: no unrequested abstractions; no new dependency when a higher rung
63: holds; shortest working diff wins; deletion beats addition; when two options are
64: the same size, take the one the stdlib already made edge-case-correct. Bug fixes
65: target the root cause — fix the shared function once, don't patch each caller
66: (three caller-side guards are three future bugs wearing bandages).
67: 
68: ## When NOT to be lazy
69: 
70: The load-bearing half: the ladder without these is just an excuse to
71: under-build. Lazy means efficient, never negligent — these are never on the
72: chopping block:
73: 
74: - Input validation at trust boundaries
75: - Error handling that prevents data loss
76: - Security and accessibility
77: - Understanding the problem first (the section above)
78: - Anything the user explicitly asked for — YAGNI gates *speculative* scope,
79:   never requested scope
80: 
81: ## One runnable check
82: 
83: Lazy code without its check is unfinished: every non-trivial change leaves ONE
84: runnable check behind — the smallest thing that fails if the logic breaks (an
85: assert-based self-check or one small test file; no frameworks, no fixtures).
86: Trivial one-liners need none.
87: 
88: ## Mark deliberate simplifications
89: 
90: When a rung deliberately skips something real, leave a `yagni:` comment naming
91: the limitation and the upgrade path — the shortcut then reads as a decision,
92: not an oversight, and nobody re-litigates it in review. (Upstream marks these
93: `ponytail:`; the marker is renamed with the skill.)
94: 
95: ## Examples
96: 
97: - Ask: "add a date picker." Over-built: install flatpickr, write a wrapper
98:   component, add a stylesheet, discuss timezones. Minimal: `<input type="date">`.
99: - Ask: "dedupe this list." Over-built: a `Deduplicator` class with a strategy
100:   interface. Minimal: `[...new Set(items)]`.
101: - Bug: three callers crash on null. Over-built: null-guard each caller.
102:   Minimal: fix the shared function once.
103: 
104: ## Siblings
105: 
106: - `architecture-rescue` — post-hoc: finds what over-building already did to the
107:   codebase (the deletion test). yagni-gate is the up-front reflex that keeps
108:   that cleanup from being needed.
109: - `plan-intake`'s over-build (YAGNI) pass — the same lens applied to *work
110:   items*; yagni-gate applies it to *code*.
111: - `caveman` — the same discipline applied to response tokens.
112: 
113: ## Triggering test
114: 
115: - Must trigger: "yagni mode", "stop over-building this", "keep it minimal — YAGNI".
116: - Must NOT trigger: "improve architecture" (that's `architecture-rescue`),
117:   "be terse" (that's `caveman`).

=== skills/workflows/zoom-out/SKILL.md ===
1: ---
2: name: zoom-out
3: version: 1.1.1
4: description: "User-invoked only, via /zoom-out — never auto-fires. Steps back from the current code to give a higher-level perspective: which modules are involved, how do they connect, what is this change touching that isn't obvious? Reads the project's CONTEXT.md / domain glossary when available. Invoke it deliberately when you feel stuck in detail, when a change feels bigger than expected, or before making a structural decision and you want a map first."
5: disable-model-invocation: true
6: requires_agent_teams: false
7: requires_claude_code: false
8: min_plan: starter
9: owns:
10:   directories: []
11:   patterns: []
12:   shared_read: ["*"]
13: allowed-tools: ["Read", "Grep", "Glob"]
14: composes_with: ["maintain-context"]
15: spawned_by: []
16: ---
17: 
18: # zoom-out
19: 
20: Go up a layer. Map the modules involved in this change. Read the domain glossary (`CONTEXT.md`, configured via `setup-project-skills`) if one exists.
21: 
22: Output a numbered list of modules, one line per module describing its role, with arrows (`→`) showing connections between them. Example shape:
23: 
24: ```text
25: 1. AuthService — validates JWTs → 2. UserRepo
26: 2. UserRepo — loads/persists users → 3. SessionStore
27: 3. SessionStore — refresh-token cache → AuthService
28: ```
29: 
30: Do not propose changes. The user asked for orientation, not a fix — if they wanted a fix they'd have invoked a different skill.
